### [1] [MintFlow: Minimal Trajectory Intervention for Constrained Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02260)
*Yesom Park,Kelvin Kan,Qifan Chen,Thomas Flynn,Hayden Schaeffer. Xihaier Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MintFlow is a training-free constrained sampling framework for flow matching models that enforces constraints via minimal perturbation of the pretrained flow trajectory, using an adjoint formulation for a closed-form perturbation and adaptive intervention timing, achieving strong constraint satisfaction with better preservation of the pretrained distribution than SOTA constrained methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many downstream applications require flow matching samples to satisfy prescribed constraints such as observed measurements and physical laws. However, existing constrained samplers face a trade-off: enforcing constraints substantially displaces samples from the pretrained data distribution.

Method: MintFlow formulates constraint enforcement as a minimal intervention on the pretrained flow trajectory. It seeks the minimal perturbation of an intermediate flow state so that its subsequent evolution under the pretrained flow field satisfies the target constraint, keeping the pretrained flow field unchanged. An adjoint formulation yields a closed-form expression for this perturbation, avoiding iterative optimization. MintFlow also adaptively selects the intervention time to balance perturbation magnitude against its amplification by the remaining flow.

Result: Across a range of tasks in generative vision and physical system modeling, MintFlow achieves competitive constraint satisfaction while preserving the pretrained generative distribution substantially better than state-of-the-art constrained methods.

Conclusion: MintFlow is a training-free constrained sampling framework that enforces constraints with minimal deviation from the pretrained distribution, offering a closed-form, optimization-free solution with adaptive intervention timing and superior distribution preservation over state-of-the-art constrained methods.

Abstract: Flow matching models excel at generative modeling, and many downstream applications require their samples to satisfy prescribed constraints, such as observed measurements and physical laws. However, existing constrained samplers often face a trade-off: \textit{enforcing constraints can substantially displace samples from the pretrained data distribution}. To address this trade-off, we introduce \textbf{MintFlow}, a training-free constrained sampling framework that formulates constraint enforcement as a minimal intervention on the pretrained flow trajectory. MintFlow seeks the minimal perturbation of an intermediate flow state such that its subsequent evolution under the pretrained flow field satisfies the target constraint. By minimally perturbing the flow state while keeping the pretrained flow field unchanged, MintFlow enforces the constraint while minimizing unnecessary deviation from the pretrained distribution. An adjoint formulation yields a closed-form expression for this perturbation, eliminating expensive iterative optimization. Furthermore, MintFlow adaptively selects the intervention time to balance the required perturbation magnitude with its amplification by the remaining flow. Across a range of tasks in generative vision and physical system modeling, MintFlow achieves competitive constraint satisfaction while preserving the pretrained generative distribution substantially better than state-of-the-art constrained methods.

</details>


### [2] [Fast Models, Slow Evidence: A Paired and Self-Audited Evaluation of System-1 Decision Models for LLM Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02267)
*Jiawei Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paired evaluation of two System-1 decision models (open-weight Laya, hosted Jev) across 11 agent decision points: Jev wins 9/11 (+10.8 to +46.0 pp), both fail at zero-shot model routing, and the authors' own pipeline audit reveals four errors that inflated deployment claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: System-1 decision models promise large cost and latency savings over LLM calls for the many small typed decisions agent harnesses make (model routing, tool selection, relevance gating, injection detection), but their reliability at these decision points is unclear.

Method: A paired evaluation of an open-weight (Laya) and a hosted (Jev) System-1 model on 11 agent decision points drawn from 18 public sources: 7,283 base cases plus 6,640 robustness variants, using byte-identical inputs, paired tests, and cross-hardware and cross-day reproducibility checks. The authors additionally audit their own analysis pipeline.

Result: Jev is significantly more accurate on 9 of 11 decision points (+10.8 to +46.0 pp). Neither model beats chance on zero-shot model routing, and they tie on RAG relevance gating. Laya changes 30% of answers under option-order reversal and degrades sharply with many or similar candidates (31% at 50 nearest-neighbour tools, vs. 98% for Jev when a unique correct tool exists). The pipeline audit found three analysis errors and one design confound: omitted pre-screen cost (reported 23.9% saving vs. actual 4.3%), gate accuracy reported as end-to-end quality (58% vs. 98%), in-sample thresholds (5% target vs. up to 17% held-out misses), and a "channel effect" on injection false positives that vanishes with channel-native content. Two other suspected confounds did not change conclusions.

Conclusion: System-1 decision models are not uniformly reliable: performance is strongly model- and task-dependent, with open-weight Laya particularly brittle to option ordering and candidate-set size. Careful auditing matters, as several plausible-sounding deployment claims were artifacts of analysis errors and a design confound, while two other suspected confounds proved benign. All cases, raw outputs, and analysis code are released for reuse.

Abstract: Agent harnesses make many small, typed decisions per task: which model to call, which tool to use, whether retrieved text is relevant, whether an input carries an injection. System-1 decision models answer such questions in a single forward pass with class probabilities, promising large cost and latency savings over LLM calls. We present a paired evaluation of an open-weight (Laya) and a hosted (Jev) System-1 model on 11 agent decision points built from 18 public sources: 7,283 base cases plus 6,640 robustness variants, with byte-identical inputs, paired tests, and cross-hardware and cross-day reproducibility checks. Jev is significantly more accurate on 9 of 11 decision points (+10.8 to +46.0 pp). Neither model beats chance on zero-shot model routing, and they tie on RAG relevance gating. Laya changes 30% of its answers when the option order is reversed and degrades sharply with many or similar candidates (31% at 50 nearest-neighbour tools, vs. 98% for Jev on items with a unique correct tool). We also audit our own pipeline. Three analysis errors and one design confound distorted headline deployment claims: an omitted pre-screen cost (reported 23.9% saving, actual 4.3%), gate accuracy reported as end-to-end quality (58% vs. 98%), in-sample thresholds (5% target, up to 17% held-out misses), and a "channel effect" on injection false positives that vanishes with channel-native content. Two other suspected confounds did not change the conclusions. All cases, raw outputs and analysis code are available at https://github.com/David-DL-Space/sys1-eval.

</details>


### [3] [The AI Risk Observatory: What Can We Learn from AI Disclosures in Annual Reports About Societal Resilience?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02281)
*Bart Jaworski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A two-stage LLM classification pipeline applied to 9,821 UK annual reports (2020–2025) shows that AI risk and adoption disclosure rose sharply, but most AI risk disclosure is not substantive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Societal resilience research needs useful, actionable data, motivating the question of whether annual reports processed at scale with LLMs provide a useful signal about how companies disclose their response to AI.

Method: A reproducible two-stage classification pipeline is applied to 9,821 annual reports from 1,362 UK listed companies (2020–2025, with partial 2026 data). The method is validated against 474 human-annotated passages, and a substantiveness classification is developed to assess disclosure quality.

Result: Validation shows high recall and moderate label-level agreement. Empirically: (i) AI risk mentions rose from 2.8% to 41.2% and AI adoption disclosure from 13.8% to 45.2% (2020–2025), with named vendor mentions clustering around a few major providers led by Microsoft; (ii) disclosure varies by Critical National Infrastructure sector and market segment—AIM reports disclose AI risk far less than Main Market reports, and Energy and Data Infrastructure sectors lag behind; (iii) harm disclosures are near-absent (seven reports corpus-wide). In 2025, 41.2% of reports mention AI as a risk but only 4.3% contain substantive AI risk disclosure.

Conclusion: Annual reports processed at scale with LLMs yield a useful signal on corporate AI disclosure, but reported AI risk disclosure is largely non-substantive, complicating its use for societal resilience assessment.

Abstract: Societal resilience research relies on access to useful and actionable data, which motivates our main research question: Can annual reports, processed at scale with LLMs, provide a useful signal about how companies disclose their response to AI? We test this by applying a reproducible two-stage classification pipeline to 9,821 annual reports from 1,362 UK listed companies (2020-2025, with partial 2026 data). We first validate the method against 474 human-annotated passages, finding high recall and moderate label-level agreement. We then report three empirical patterns: (i) between 2020 and 2025, the share of reports mentioning AI risk rose from 2.8% to 41.2%, while AI adoption disclosure also rose, from 13.8% to 45.2%, and named vendor mentions cluster around a small set of major providers led by Microsoft; (ii) disclosure varies substantially by Critical National Infrastructure sector and market segment: AIM reports disclose AI risk at far lower rates than Main Market reports, and sectors such as Energy and Data Infrastructure lag behind the rest in AI risk disclosure; and (iii) harm disclosures are near-absent (seven reports across the entire corpus). We develop a substantiveness classification to assess the quality of the disclosure and find that most AI risk disclosure is not substantive: in 2025, 41.2% of all reports mention AI as a risk, but only 4.3% contain AI risk disclosure we classify as substantive.

</details>


### [4] [Keep It CALM: Analyzing the Limits of Global Unsafety in Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02300)
*NaHyeon Park,Minhyun Lee,Hyunjung Shim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CALM replaces global unsafe-signal removal in training-free T2I safeguards with prompt-local counterfactual correction, improving unsafe suppression while preserving benign utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-free safeguards assume a single reusable global unsafe signal (an unsafe direction or global toxic subspace) applies across all prompts. The paper argues this global-unsafety assumption is flawed, exposing a coverage-selectivity trade-off: compact unsafe subspaces cannot cover heterogeneous unsafe semantics, while broader aggregation distorts safety-adjacent benign prompts.

Method: A controlled geometric analysis of the global-unsafety assumption motivates CALM (Counterfactual Adaptive Local Modulation), a training-free safeguard. Using matched unsafe-benign anchors, CALM routes each prompt to its active unsafe categories, minimally edits only violating token representations toward the safe side, and suppresses positively aligned unsafe residual components, rather than applying uniform global removal.

Result: Across broad evaluation, CALM significantly improves unsafe content suppression while preserving benign utility.

Conclusion: Local counterfactual correction provides a more selective alternative to global unsafe signal removal for training-free text-to-image safeguards.

Abstract: Training-free safeguards for text-to-image generation often rely on a reusable safety signal, such as an unsafe direction or global toxic subspace, applied broadly across prompts. We provide a controlled geometric analysis of this global-unsafety assumption and reveal a consistent coverage-selectivity trade-off: compact unsafe subspaces fail to cover heterogeneous unsafe semantics, whereas broader aggregation increasingly distorts safety-adjacent benign prompts. Motivated by this finding, we propose CALM (Counterfactual Adaptive Local Modulation), a training-free safeguard that replaces uniform global removal with prompt-local counterfactual correction. Using matched unsafe-benign anchors, CALM routes each prompt to active unsafe categories, minimally edits only violating token representations toward the safe side, and suppresses positively aligned unsafe residual components. Across broad evaluation, CALM significantly improves unsafe content suppression while preserving benign utility, demonstrating that local counterfactual correction provides a more selective alternative to global unsafe signal removal.

</details>


### [5] [Choosing Before Acting: Comparative Value Estimation for Long-Horizon Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02330)
*Yu Li,Zheng Zhang,Xin Liu,Shengtian Yang,Guangfeng Cai,Lei Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes CITA (Comparative Inference for Tool-use Agents), which trains a Comparative Inference Model (CIM) to estimate the long-horizon value of candidate next tool invocations under the current context, using paired supervision from observed behavior, a Bayesian tool-graph simulator, and LLM-based semantic comparison, improving Tool F1 and task success on three tool-use benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs handle complex tasks via long-horizon tool invocation sequences, where each call changes the task state and conditions later decisions. Final-outcome rewards give weak credit assignment over long traces, while step-level rewards require expensive human/LLM judgment or extra rollouts to estimate downstream effects.

Method: The authors argue agents should estimate the long-horizon value of a candidate next tool call before executing it, which demands comparative supervision over alternative invocations in the same context—yet logged trajectories only record the invocation taken. CITA therefore trains a Comparative Inference Model (CIM) on paired signals combining observed tool behavior, scalable supervision from a Bayesian tool-graph simulator, and semantic judgments from LLM-based comparison. The CIM predicts how likely a candidate next invocation is to support final task success given the current context.

Result: Across three tool-use benchmarks and multiple backbone LLMs, CITA consistently improves Tool F1 and task success. Further analysis shows the CIM learns accurate step-level value estimates for comparative tool choices.

Conclusion: Comparative, value-based step-level supervision—derived without costly rollouts—is an effective way to train tool-use agents for long-horizon tasks, yielding consistent gains in tool selection and task completion.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) rely on long-horizon tool invocation sequences for complex tasks, where each invocation can alter the task state and condition subsequent decisions. In long-horizon tool use, final-outcome rewards provide weak credit assignment over long interaction traces. Step-level rewards can offer more targeted feedback, but obtaining reliable step supervision often requires human or LLM judgment, or additional rollouts to estimate the downstream effect of an intermediate decision. In this paper, we argue that effective tool-use agents should estimate the long-horizon value of a possible next tool invocation before executing it. This objective requires comparative supervision over alternative invocations under the same context, while logged trajectories only contain the invocation that was actually taken. Therefore, we propose Comparative Inference for Tool-use Agents (CITA). CITA trains a Comparative Inference Model (CIM) from paired signals that combine observed tool behavior, scalable supervision from a Bayesian tool-graph simulator, and semantic judgments from LLM-based comparison. The resulting CIM learns to estimate how likely a possible next tool invocation is to support final task success under the current context. Across three tool-use benchmarks and multiple backbone LLMs, CITA consistently improves Tool F1 and task success. Additional analysis shows that CIM learns accurate step-level value estimates for comparative tool choices.

</details>


### [6] [World Editing: Intervening on Executable Worlds at Increasing Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02331)
*Max Ku,Nok-Kan Law,Yu-Chien Tang,Shih-Ying Yeh,Ping Nie,Andy Zheng,Tat Hei Lai,Fei-Yueh Chen,Nikko Yu,Wei-Chieh Sun,Suzy Huang,Chiao-Wei Hsu,Chih-Chuan Huang,Chak-Wing Mak,Ho Yin Sam Ng,Edisy Kin Wai Chan,Min-Hung Chen,Ho Kei Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formulates "world editing" as intervening on an existing executable world while preserving invariants, introduces intervention depth as a coupling axis, and releases IGMWorld + IGMBench (110 tasks, 1.1K+ criteria) over Minecraft/Terraria. Frontier coding agents reach 78.2% strict task-level and 94.8% criterion-level success, but reliability drops with intervention depth and visual consistency stays below 50%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interactive world models can generate and act within environments, but deliberately editing an existing executable world is underexplored. There is no formulation of editing as intervention-with-preservation, no notion of how deeply an edit couples world entities, dynamics, and systems, and no rigorous benchmark to measure this capability separately from world generation or interaction.

Method: The authors formulate world editing as intervening on an existing world while preserving properties that should remain unchanged, and introduce intervention depth as an axis capturing how strongly an edit couples world entities, dynamics, and systems. They instantiate this capability via industry-grade game modding, presenting IGMWorld and IGMBench: 110 tasks with over 1.1K executable state and behavioral criteria across Minecraft and Terraria. Tasks span property, entity, dynamics, and system interventions, evaluated through deterministic executability, behavioral, preservation, and visual checks.

Result: Frontier coding agents already exhibit substantial world-editing capability: the strongest configuration solves 78.2% of tasks under a strict task-level criterion, with criterion-level performance reaching 94.8%. Reliability generally decreases with intervention depth, a pattern that persists even among tasks with similar numbers of evaluation criteria. Most failed edits still build and load successfully, indicating the main difficulty is making the edited world behave as requested rather than compiling. Visual consistency is a separate weakness: all evaluated configurations fall below 50% joint visual pass rate.

Conclusion: World editing is a distinct capability from world generation and interaction, and executable games provide a practical, rigorous testbed for studying it. The observed depth-dependent reliability drop and visual-consistency gap highlight it as a meaningful frontier for future coding-agent research.

Abstract: Interactive world models are increasingly capable of generating environments and acting within them, yet deliberately editing an existing executable world remains underexplored. We formulate world editing as intervening on an existing world while preserving properties that should remain unchanged, and introduce intervention depth as an axis describing how strongly an edit couples world entities, dynamics, and systems. We instantiate this capability through industry-grade game modding and introduce IGMWorld, together with IGMBench, a benchmark of 110 tasks and over 1.1K executable state and behavioral criteria across Minecraft and Terraria. The tasks span property, entity, dynamics, and system interventions and are evaluated through deterministic executability, behavioral, preservation, and visual checks. Frontier coding agents already exhibit substantial world-editing capability: the strongest configuration solves 78.2% of tasks under a strict task-level criterion, while criterion-level performance reaches 94.8%. Reliability generally decreases with intervention depth, and this pattern persists even among tasks with similar numbers of evaluation criteria. Most failed edits still build and load successfully, suggesting that the main difficulty is making the edited world behave as requested. Visual consistency remains a separate weakness, with all evaluated configurations below 50% joint visual pass rate. These results show that world editing is a distinct capability from world generation and interaction, and that executable games provide a practical testbed for studying it.

</details>


### [7] [A Multi Method Importance and Performance Efficiency Analysis of Topological Metrics for Natural Visibility Graph Based Cyber Attack Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02342)
*Ali Melih Kanca,Ilker Turker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A consensus-ranking feature selection over 21 Natural Visibility Graph topological metrics shows that a compact Top3 subset (avg clustering coefficient median/std/mean) outperforms the full set for cyber-attack classification on CICIDS2018, achieving 97.148% accuracy and cutting runtime by 96.06%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NVG-based traffic analysis yields many topological descriptors, but descriptors differ in discriminative value and large metric sets inflate computational cost. The authors ask whether a compact subset can retain or improve classification capability while reducing cost.

Method: The study evaluates 21 NVG-derived topological metrics. Four importance methods—SHAP, grouped Permutation Importance, Boruta, and Recursive Feature Elimination (RFE)—are combined via a Consensus Ranking. Configurations Full21, Top15, Top10, Top7, Top5, and Top3 are tested on CICIDS2018 with a CNN classifier and stratified 5-fold cross validation.

Result: The top three metrics are avg_clustering_coeff_median, avg_clustering_coeff_std, and avg_clustering_coeff_mean. Top3 attained the highest observed mean performance: 97.148% accuracy, 97.055% weighted F1, MCC 0.9675, versus 95.999%, 95.521%, and 0.9549 for Full21. Total runtime dropped from 14,961.39 s to 589.22 s (96.06% reduction).

Conclusion: Importance-guided metric reduction can produce a compact NVG representation with higher observed mean predictive performance and substantially lower computational cost under the evaluated setting.

Abstract: Natural Visibility Graph (NVG) based analysis characterizes network traffic through topological descriptors reflecting different structural properties. However, not all descriptors contribute equally to cyber-attack classification, and extracting a large metric set can increase computational cost. This study evaluates 21 NVG derived topological metrics and investigates whether a compact subset can preserve classification capability while improving computational efficiency. Four importance analysis methods SHAP, grouped Permutation Importance, Boruta, and Recursive Feature Elimination (RFE) are integrated through a Consensus Ranking strategy. Based on this ranking, Full21, Top15, Top10, Top7, Top5, and Top3 configurations are evaluated using the CICIDS2018 dataset, a CNN classifier, and stratified 5 fold cross validation. The three highest ranked metrics are avg_clustering_coeff_median, avg_clustering_coeff_std, and avg_clustering_coeff_mean. Top3 achieved the highest observed mean performance, with 97.148% accuracy, 97.055% weighted F1 score, and an MCC of 0.9675, compared with 95.999%, 95.521%, and 0.9549 for Full21, respectively. It also reduced total runtime from 14,961.39 s to 589.22 s (96.06%). These results indicate that importance guided metric reduction can provide a compact NVG representation with higher observed mean predictive performance and substantially lower computational cost under the evaluated setting.

</details>


### [8] [DeReAct: Decomposed Reasoning and Acting for Reliable AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02351)
*Ajay Vohra,Tao Chen,Neeti Narayan,Caron Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DeReAct is a modular ReAct-based agent architecture that externalizes action validation and completion control into two gating policies—a Critic and a Context Manager—improving Pass@1, especially for weaker LLM backbones, while producing better-grounded trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ReAct agents couple action proposal, environment interaction, and completion judgment within a single LLM policy. This coupling prevents independent enforcement of action authorization and completion control, causing errors to propagate and unsupported completion claims to prematurely terminate tasks. The authors aim to decouple these responsibilities so that gating can be enforced separately from action generation.

Method: DeReAct is a modular architecture that externalizes two gating policies on top of a ReAct agent: (1) a Critic that validates proposed actions before execution, and (2) a Context Manager that reconstructs an environment-supported State and certifies task completion. The architecture is evaluated on GAIA and SWE-bench Verified, with trajectory and ablation analyses.

Result: On GAIA and SWE-bench Verified, DeReAct improves Pass@1 most for weaker Brain models: +6.5–7.0 points for Qwen3-Coder-480B and +4.2–5.2 points for Claude Sonnet 4.5, with gains diminishing as Brain capability increases. With Claude Opus 4.5, Pass@1 remains comparable to ReAct, but DeReAct yields more evidence-complete and constraint-satisfying trajectories. Trajectory and ablation analyses show external gating is effective when targeted failures are prevalent and the gating policy is itself sufficient.

Conclusion: DeReAct improves weaker agents by decoupling and externalizing action validation and completion control, while retaining grounding benefits as models strengthen. Completion control can trade earlier termination for stronger grounding, making DeReAct beneficial across a range of model capabilities.

Abstract: ReAct-based agents typically rely on a single LLM policy to propose actions, interact with the environment, and decide when a task is complete. This coupling makes action authorization and completion control difficult to enforce independently, allowing errors to propagate and unsupported completion claims to terminate execution. We introduce DeReAct, a modular agent architecture that externalizes two gating policies: a Critic that validates proposed actions before execution, and a Context Manager that reconstructs an environment-supported \textsc{State} and certifies task completion.
  Across GAIA and SWE-bench Verified, DeReAct improves Pass@1 most for weaker Brain models, with gains of 6.5--7.0 points for Qwen3-Coder-480B and 4.2--5.2 points for Claude Sonnet~4.5; gains diminish as Brain capability increases. Trajectory and ablation analyses show that external gating is effective when targeted failures are sufficiently prevalent and the gating policy is itself sufficient. With Claude Opus~4.5, Pass@1 remains comparable to ReAct, while DeReAct produces more evidence-complete and constraint-satisfying trajectories, indicating that completion control can trade earlier termination for stronger grounding. Overall, DeReAct improves weaker agents while retaining grounding benefits as models strengthen.

</details>


### [9] [Traversing the Satisfaction-Diversity Frontier in Text-to-Image Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02372)
*Kevin Zhai,Siva Rajesh Kasa,Soumya Roy,Sumit Negi,Mubarak Shah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reformulates text-to-image batch generation as a satisficing problem—each image must meet a reward floor while the batch meets a diversity cutoff—and proposes SatisDive, a training-free inference-time method that traverses the resulting reward-diversity Pareto frontier via a batch-relative reward cutoff.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multiple images generated from the same prompt are only useful if each reflects user preferences (measured by a learned reward) and simultaneously differs visually from the others (diversity). Existing approaches either treat reward and diversity separately or merge them into a single aggregate score, which allows high diversity to compensate for low rewards—meaning some candidates can be arbitrarily poor.

Method: The authors formulate generation as satisficing: every candidate must satisfy a reward floor and the batch must satisfy a diversity cutoff, with the reward floor controlling the trade-off between worst-candidate reward and batch diversity and defining a Pareto frontier. To traverse this frontier, they introduce SatisDive, a training-free inference-time method that uses a batch-relative reward cutoff to distinguish lower- from higher-reward candidates: candidates below the cutoff are pushed toward reward improvement, while diversity is emphasized among those above it.

Result: On Pick-a-Pic, at matched DreamSim, SatisDive improves worst-candidate reward over FK steering by up to 0.43 with FLUX.1-dev as base model and HPSv3 as reward, and by up to 0.70 with SANA-1.6B as base model and ImageReward as reward. Across their overlapping DreamSim ranges, SatisDive's satisfaction-diversity curve Pareto-dominates FK steering's curve in every setting.

Conclusion: Framing batch text-to-image generation as satisficing yields a principled reward-diversity Pareto frontier, and SatisDive traverses it effectively without training, Pareto-dominating prior steering-based methods on the reward-diversity trade-off.

Abstract: Text-to-image generation enables users to explore several images generated from the same prompt. For these generated images to be useful, each one must reflect the user's preferences, measured by a learned reward, and differ visually from the others to maintain diversity. Existing methods are limited: they either address reward and diversity separately or combine them in one aggregate score, enabling high diversity to offset low rewards. In this paper, we address these limitations by formulating generation as satisficing: every image (candidate) must satisfy a reward floor and the batch of images must satisfy a diversity cutoff. The reward floor controls the balance between worst-candidate reward and batch diversity; we show that varying this floor defines a Pareto frontier. To traverse this frontier, we introduce SatisDive, a training-free inference-time method. SatisDive uses a batch-relative reward cutoff to distinguish lower- from higher-reward candidates, emphasizing reward improvement for candidates below the cutoff and diversity among candidates above it. On Pick-a-Pic, at matched DreamSim, SatisDive improves worst-candidate reward over FK steering by up to 0.43 with FLUX.1-dev as the base model and HPSv3 as the reward, and by up to 0.70 with SANA-1.6B as the base model and ImageReward as the reward. More broadly, across their overlapping DreamSim ranges, SatisDive's satisfaction-diversity curve Pareto-dominates FK steering's curve in each setting.

</details>


### [10] [THPL: A Vision-to-Language Decision Support Framework for Rainbow Trout Feeding Management in RAS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02378)
*Meng Liang,Guanbo Feng,Haozhuang Chi,Shilong Zhao,Zhixin Xiong,Yuhang He,Wenfeng Han,Tianhao Zhao,Zhihong Ma,Ying Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: THPL is a generative feeding decision framework for rainbow trout in RAS that aligns fish behavior with management knowledge by encoding fish trajectories into dual-evidence "physical" and "soft" tokens, then fine-tuning an LLM with LoRA and counterfactual mDPO, achieving 96.67% decision accuracy and improved reasoning quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precision feeding in Recirculating Aquaculture Systems is critical for lowering costs and improving fish welfare, but existing methods lack cognitive alignment between fish behaviors and management knowledge, which hinders the translation of behavior signals into executable and interpretable feeding decisions.

Method: THPL combines three components. (1) Fishsort extracts trajectories and establishes an Activity Coefficient (AC) quantifying feeding intensity. (2) A Hierarchical Behavior Encoder (HBE) models individual temporal progression and collective dynamics via Temporal and Set Transformers, converting trajectory tensors into dual-evidence representations of explicit physical tokens and implicit soft tokens. (3) These tokens, together with environmental parameters, metadata, and expert rules, fine-tune an LLM through LoRA, followed by counterfactual multimodal Direct Preference Optimization (mDPO) to reinforce causal reasoning.

Result: AC shows a statistically significant monotonic positive correlation with expert-annotated feeding intensity (Spearman ρ = 0.925, p < 0.001). Ablations show decision accuracy rises from 33.33% (text-only baseline) to 93.33% with dual-evidence tokens, indicating spatiotemporal tokens supply needed physical grounding. Counterfactual mDPO further improves accuracy from 93.33% to 96.67%, raises METEOR from 58.10% to 85.30%, reduces Self-BLEU-2 from 58.79% to 52.88%, and increases Distinct-3 from 6.68% to 7.81%, suppressing templating and actuation biases while reinforcing causal consistency and operational safety.

Conclusion: By integrating continuous fish kinematics with LLM reasoning, the work offers a novel decision-support paradigm for precision aquaculture, yielding more accurate, interpretable, and causally consistent feeding decisions than text-only or standard LoRA baselines.

Abstract: In Recirculating Aquaculture Systems (RAS), precision feeding is critical for minimizing costs and improving fish welfare. However, existing methods lack cognitive alignment between fish behaviors and management knowledge, impeding translation into executable, interpretable feeding decisions. To address this, we propose THPL, a generative feeding decision framework tailored for rainbow trout (Oncorhynchus mykiss) in RAS. First, Fishsort extracts trajectories to establish an Activity Coefficient (AC) quantifying feeding intensity. Second, a Hierarchical Behavior Encoder (HBE) models individual temporal progression and collective dynamics using Temporal and Set Transformers, transforming trajectory tensors into dual-evidence representations of explicit physical and implicit soft tokens. Finally, these tokens are integrated with environmental parameters, metadata, and expert rules to fine-tune an LLM via LoRA, followed by counterfactual multimodal Direct Preference Optimization (mDPO) to reinforce causal reasoning. Results show that AC exhibits a statistically significant monotonic positive correlation with expert-annotated feeding intensity (Spearman $ρ= 0.925$, $p < 0.001$). Ablations indicate that decision accuracy improves from 33.33% (text-only baseline) to 93.33% with dual-evidence tokens, confirming that continuous spatiotemporal tokens provide necessary physical grounding for LLMs. Compared with standard LoRA, counterfactual mDPO elevates decision accuracy from 93.33% to 96.67%, advances METEOR from 58.10% to 85.30%, reduces Self-BLEU-2 from 58.79% to 52.88%, and increases Distinct-3 from 6.68% to 7.81%, suppressing templating and actuation biases while reinforcing causal consistency and operational safety. Overall, by integrating continuous kinematics with LLM reasoning, this study provides a novel decision support paradigm for precision aquaculture.

</details>


### [11] [FlashSinkhorn 2: Block-Sparse Entropic Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02395)
*Felix X. -F. Ye,Yu Chin Fabian Lim,Naigang Wang,Davis Wertheimer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FlashSinkhorn 2 (FS2) is a single-GPU solver for large discrete entropic optimal transport (EOT) with squared-Euclidean cost on low-dimensional point clouds. It couples a coarse centroid-based stage with a block-sparse fine stage to reach a prescribed marginal residual, scaling to 1.34×10^8 particles in under 2.5 hours on one A100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming GPU EOT solvers such as FlashSinkhorn avoid storing the dense kernel but still evaluate all n×m point pairs in every Sinkhorn iteration, which is prohibitive for very large problems. There is a need for a solver that reduces per-iteration point-pair work while maintaining accuracy to a prescribed marginal residual on a single GPU.

Method: FS2 combines two stages: (1) a coarse stage that solves EOT on cell centroids, lifts potentials to all points, and—when a sampled marginal check rejects the lift—continues on centroids, thereby replacing most point-level updates; and (2) a block-sparse fine stage that corrects the centroid error left by coarse updates. Its Morton-ordered blocks enable screening and fused tensor-core execution, with a threshold based on block masses bounding each omitted tile's contribution to every row and column.

Result: On synthetic benchmarks, FS2 reaches the target residual on all 32 problems, whereas GeomLoss multiscale reaches it on 10. On one A100, FS2 solves discrete EOT between two 1.34×10^8-particle measures from a cosmological N-body simulation, at an entropic blur equal to the mean interparticle distance, to an all-particle marginal residual below 0.01 in under 2.5 hours—claimed as the largest discrete EOT problem solved to this accuracy within hours.

Conclusion: FS2 provides a streaming, single-GPU approach that makes very large discrete EOT problems tractable to high accuracy, combining coarse centroid updates with a screened block-sparse fine correction. An open-source implementation is released for reproducibility at https://github.com/ot-triton-lab/flash-sinkhorn.

Abstract: Streaming GPU solvers for entropic optimal transport (EOT), such as FlashSinkhorn, avoid storing the dense kernel but still evaluate all $n\times m$ point pairs in every Sinkhorn iteration. We present \textbf{FlashSinkhorn~2} (FS2), a solver for squared-Euclidean cost on low-dimensional point clouds that solves large discrete EOT problems to a prescribed marginal residual on a single GPU by coupling two stages. A coarse stage solves on cell centroids, lifts the potentials to every point and, when a sampled marginal check rejects the lift, continues on the centroids, replacing most point-level updates. A block-sparse fine stage then removes the centroid error that coarse updates cannot. Its Morton-ordered blocks support screening and fused tensor-core execution, and a threshold set by the block masses bounds each omitted tile's contribution to every row and column. On synthetic benchmarks, FS2 reaches the target residual on all 32 problems and GeomLoss multiscale on 10. On one A100, FS2 solves discrete EOT between two $1.34\times10^8$-particle measures from a cosmological $N$-body simulation, at an entropic blur equal to the mean interparticle distance, to an all-particle marginal residual below 0.01 in under 2.5 hours. To our knowledge, it is the largest discrete EOT problem solved to this accuracy within hours. For reproducibility, we release an open-source implementation at https://github.com/ot-triton-lab/flash-sinkhorn

</details>


### [12] [When Terminal-Agent Training Stalls: Demystifying Data Generation and Verification Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02405)
*Xi Qin,Isabel Kurth,Xin Cui,Elin Park,Alexander Schaefer,Yaad Oren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A meta-agent pipeline for generating terminal agent RL training tasks reveals three failure classes (benchmark invalidity, harness brittleness, reward misalignment), showing that solvability bands are model-specific and require calibration, verifier audits, and infrastructure error accounting as primary evaluation criteria.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier models like Claude Opus are increasingly used as meta-agents to generate terminal tasks and verifiers for RL training, but runnable Docker images and executable test suites do not guarantee a faithful end-to-end training pipeline. The paper addresses this gap by diagnosing failures in the meta-agent pipeline.

Method: The authors present a meta-agent pipeline and diagnose three failure classes: benchmark invalidity, harness brittleness, and reward misalignment. They apply prompt redesign and context extension, then test a 9B model on Claude Opus-generated tasks, and introduce hard tasks to probe the solvability band without altering the training configuration.

Result: Prompt redesign and context extension raise baseline solvability 5.6 times. A 9B model saturates at 81.3% mean pass@2 within 20 steps on Claude Opus-generated tasks. Adding hard tasks reduces mean pass@2 to 20.6% without changing the training configuration, providing strong evidence that the solvability band is model-specific.

Conclusion: Meta-agent reliability requires solvability-band calibration, verifier audits, and infrastructure error accounting as first-class evaluation criteria rather than post-hoc diagnostics.

Abstract: Using a frontier model like Claude Opus as a meta-agent to generate terminal tasks and verifiers for RL training is increasingly common. Yet a runnable Docker image and executable test suite do not guarantee a faithful end-to-end pipeline for terminal agent training. We present a meta-agent pipeline motivated by this gap, diagnosing three classes of failure: benchmark invalidity, harness brittleness, and reward misalignment. Prompt redesign and context extension raise baseline solvability 5.6 times, but a 9B model saturates at 81.3% mean pass@2 within 20 steps on Claude Opus-generated tasks. Adding hard tasks reduces mean pass@2 to 20.6% without changing the training configuration, a strong evidence that the solvability band is model-specific. These findings demonstrate that meta-agent reliability requires solvability-band calibration, verifier audits, and infrastructure error accounting as first-class evaluation criteria, not post-hoc diagnost.

</details>


### [13] [Reinforcement Learning Techniques for the Optimization of Target Polarization in Nuclear Physics Scattering Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02452)
*Armen Kasparian,Torri Jeske,Monibor Rahman,Chris Keith,James Maxwell,Thomas Britton,Malachi Schram,David Lawrence*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A data-driven control framework combining surrogate modeling and reinforcement learning automatically tunes microwave frequency to optimize polarization in APOLLO cryogenic nuclear physics targets, achieving nearly 2x improvement over expert manual operation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operating dynamically polarized targets requires continuous microwave frequency tuning to compensate for radiation damage and evolving material properties. This is traditionally done by expert operators through manual trial-and-error, which is labor-intensive, subjective, and suboptimal.

Method: Surrogate models (multilayer perceptrons and Gaussian process regression) are trained on APOLLO cryogenic target operational data to predict polarization from microwave frequency, beam current, and accumulated radiation dose. A Gaussian process approximation with a standardized simulation environment enables learning across multiple target samples, and a reinforcement learning agent is trained with a lower-confidence-bound reward balancing performance and uncertainty.

Result: Gaussian process-based models yield calibrated uncertainty estimates and reliably detect out-of-distribution regions, whereas MLPs show limited sensitivity to distributional shift. The RL agent achieves almost a 2x improvement over operators' actions.

Conclusion: Surrogate modeling with uncertainty-aware reinforcement learning provides an effective automated control strategy for target polarization, outperforming manual expert operation and offering a scalable path for dynamic polarization control.

Abstract: The operation of dynamically polarized targets in nuclear physics experiments relies on continuous tuning of the microwave frequency to compensate for radiation damage and evolving material properties, a task that is traditionally performed through manual trial-and-error by expert operators. This work presents a data-driven control framework that combines surrogate modeling with reinforcement learning to optimize the target polarization. Using operational data from the APOLLO cryogenic target system, we train and evaluate multilayer perceptron and Gaussian process regression models to predict polarization as a function of microwave frequency, beam current, and accumulated radiation dose. We show that Gaussian process-based models provide calibrated uncertainty estimates and reliably identify regions outside the training distribution, while MLPs exhibit limited sensitivity to distributional shift. To enable learning and control across multiple target samples, we introduce a Gaussian process approximation and embed the surrogate model within a standardized simulation environment. A reinforcement learning agent is trained using a lower-confidence-bound reward formulation that balances performance maximization against uncertainty. We are able to show an almost 2x improvement on the operators actions utilizing our RL agent.

</details>


### [14] [Tropical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02478)
*Arip Asadulaev,Aladin Djuhera,Karim Salta,Holger Boche,Fakhri Karray,Martin Takac*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Tropical Reinforcement Learning and the TROPIC algorithm, replacing the standard sum over solution probabilities with a max operation (tropical semiring), to improve compositional reasoning in LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL for LLMs maximizes expected return by summing probabilities of successful trajectories. This only reports how often the policy succeeds, not which solution worked. Since probabilities sum to one, reinforcing one solution can cause forgetting of another never proven wrong. This makes expected return ill-suited for compositional reasoning, where a valid solution must be assembled from steps produced across separate, often failed, attempts.

Method: The authors change the underlying algebra: instead of summing probabilities of alternative solutions, they take the maximum, yielding the tropical semiring. The value of a state becomes the log-probability of its most likely verified solution, along with an explicit replayable, reusable path. This enables composition by joining the best prefix and best suffix at a shared state, even when drawn from different rollouts. They instantiate this as TROPIC, a training algorithm for deterministic, resettable environments with verifiable outcomes.

Result: On four agentic tasks (Sokoban, Countdown, FrozenLake, WebShop), TROPIC outperforms the strongest on-policy baselines by up to 16 percentage points.

Conclusion: Changing the algebra of reinforcement learning, rather than only its estimators, can substantially improve compositional reasoning in language models.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for large language models typically maximizes expected return, adding up the probabilities of all successful trajectories. However, the classical sum formulation can only report how often the model policy succeeds, not which solution actually worked, and because probabilities sum to one, reinforcing one solution can make the model forget another that was never shown to be wrong. This makes expected return a poor fit for compositional reasoning, where a solution must be assembled from reasoning steps that the model produces in separate, often failed, attempts but rarely produces together. To address this, we propose Tropical Reinforcement Learning, which rests on a simple change of algebra: instead of adding the probabilities of alternative solutions, we take their maximum, which yields the tropical semiring. The value of a state then becomes the log-probability of its most likely verified solution, together with an explicit path that can be replayed and reused. This enables true composition, since the best prefix and the best suffix meeting at a shared state can be joined even when they come from different rollouts. To put this into practice, we introduce TROPIC, a training algorithm for deterministic, resettable environments with verifiable outcomes. On four agentic tasks (Sokoban, Countdown, FrozenLake, WebShop), TROPIC outperforms the strongest on-policy baselines by up to 16 percentage points. Changing the algebra of reinforcement learning, not just its estimators, can thus substantially improve compositional reasoning in language models

</details>


### [15] [MEA: A Reward-Driven Multi-Agent System for Faithful Model Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02480)
*Yuyang Cheng,Raghav Kaushik Ravi,Srivarshinee Sridhar,Sriparna Saha,Akash Ghosh,Chirag Agarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MEA is a multi-agent framework that removes the explanation-knowledge barrier by having a Proposer agent select/configure explanation tools and an Actor agent optimized end-to-end against faithfulness to produce natural-language explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML models are widely deployed in high-stakes domains but remain opaque. Post-hoc explanation methods demand expertise most domain experts lack: navigating high-dimensional outputs, selecting the best explanations, and synthesizing evidence across disparate tools.

Method: A Proposer agent selects and configures explanation tools based on question and modality, while an Actor agent is optimized end-to-end against faithfulness, transforming outputs into natural-language explanations grounded in model behavior across tabular, text, and vision modalities. The work introduces diverse question types (feature attribution, counterfactual reasoning, spurious feature detection), each paired with a perturbation-based faithfulness metric, optimizing against faithfulness rewards augmented with a modality-adaptive penalty.

Result: Frontier LLMs systematically produce unfaithful explanations. MEA consistently outperforms post hoc explainers, agentic, and closed-source baselines across six datasets, with reward-driven optimization yielding faithfulness gains of +28% (tabular), +21% (text), and +34% (vision) over the untrained backbone.

Conclusion: AI agents can serve as a scalable, adaptable interface to ML explainability, opening a path toward natural-language explainability that generalizes beyond the fixed, single-purpose tools that have long defined the field.

Abstract: Recent years have seen the employment of a plethora of machine learning (ML) models in high-stakes domains, but they remain largely opaque to the practitioners who act on their predictions. While post-hoc explanation methods offer a lens into this model behavior, wielding them effectively demands expertise most domain experts lack: navigating high-dimensional outputs, selecting the best explanations, and synthesizing evidence across disparate tools. To this end, we present MEA, a multi-agent framework that removes the explanation knowledge barrier entirely: a Proposer agent selects and configures explanation tools based on the question and modality, while an Actor agent is optimized end-to-end against faithfulness, transforming the outputs into natural language explanations grounded in model behavior across tabular, text, and vision modalities. Further, we introduce diverse question types spanning feature attribution, counterfactual reasoning, and spurious feature detection, each paired with a perturbation-based faithfulness metric. We find that frontier LLMs systematically produce unfaithful explanations. By optimizing against faithfulness rewards augmented with a modality-adaptive penalty, MEA consistently outperforms post hoc explainers, agentic, and closed-source baselines across six datasets, with reward-driven optimization yielding faithfulness gains of +28% (tabular), +21% (text), and +34% (vision) over the untrained backbone. More broadly, our findings suggest that AI agents themselves can serve as a scalable, adaptable interface to ML explainability, opening a path toward natural-language explainability that generalizes beyond the fixed, single-purpose tools that have long defined the field.

</details>


### [16] [What Does a Token Cost? A Mixture-of-Agents Measurement of Sufficient Per-Token Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02491)
*Zhixu Du,Weijia Han,Hai Helen Li,Yiran Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper measures the actual computation each token requires using a Mixture-of-Agents panel and shows that token difficulty is highly skewed, enabling better routing and drafting controllers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs spend uniform computation per token regardless of difficulty, and existing methods like speculative decoding and model routing assume much computation is wasted, yet per-token computation requirements have never been measured.

Method: They use a Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) lens: fifteen language models of increasing capacity from three families, each reproducing a reference sequence token by token conditioned on correct preceding tokens. The inference cost of the smallest agent that succeeds defines the token's "sufficient compute," an upper bound on what the token requires. Applied across Qwen, OLMo, and R1-distilled panels on three core benchmarks.

Result: A 0.5B agent reproduces 92-95% of reference tokens. The most expensive 10% of tokens account for 64-80% of estimated FLOPs. On MATH-500, MoA-derived routing reduces projected latency from 7.59s to 5.12s while slightly improving accuracy over confidence-routing baselines. The MoA-map enables drafting with 32.6% fewer draft tokens and ~20% lower projected latency than fixed-window drafting at similar accuracy.

Conclusion: Per-token computation requirements are highly skewed and measurable, revealing significant allocation headroom and motivating controllers that exploit sufficient-compute structure.

Abstract: Large language models spend the same amount of computation on every token they generate, regardless of how difficult each token is to produce. Methods such as speculative decoding and model routing are built on the premise that much of this computation is unnecessary, yet the computation an individual token actually requires has not been measured. We measure it through a Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) lens: a panel of fifteen language models of increasing capacity, drawn from three families, in which every agent attempts to reproduce a reference sequence token by token, conditioned on the correct preceding tokens. We define the inference cost of the smallest agent that succeeds as the token's sufficient compute, which upper-bounds what the token requires. On three core benchmarks, a 0.5B agent reproduces 92--95\% of reference tokens. Across Qwen, OLMo, and R1-distilled panels, the most expensive 10\% account for 64--80\% of estimated FLOPs. On all 500 MATH-500 problems, the MoA-derived map helps model routing reduce projected latency from 7.59 to 5.12 seconds while slightly improving accuracy, relative to the best confidence-routing baseline. The MoA-map helps drafting use 32.6\% fewer draft tokens and approximately 20\% lower projected latency than fixed-window drafting at similar accuracy. These comparisons reveal remaining allocation headroom, motivating controllers that exploit sufficient-compute structure.

</details>


### [17] [Right Order, Wrong Scale: Auditing LLM Judges for Occupational AI Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02492)
*Harry Lyu,Neil Thompson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: O*NET-BENCH, a 4,501-rating audit derived from 45,796 worker ratings, shows that 33 LLM judge configurations can rank responses similarly to workers yet disagree wildly on acceptance rates (3.0%-97.9% vs. 61.1%) and occupational aggregates, so ranking agreement alone is insufficient for occupational measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges are increasingly used to assess whether AI outputs meet workplace requirements, often validated by agreement on response rankings. The paper questions whether such ordering agreement implies agreement on the quantities judges are actually used to estimate: acceptance rates and occupational aggregates.

Method: The authors build O*NET-BENCH from an existing survey of 45,796 worker ratings, yielding 4,501 test ratings. They evaluate 33 pre-existing judge configurations spanning six model families, including a train-fitted response-only TF-IDF baseline. Evaluations cover tie-aware pair accuracy, estimated acceptance rates and worker means at task and occupation levels, various scoring protocols (pointwise vs. bundled few-shot/listwise), cross-validated calibration, individual-level variance explanation, and prediction-assisted estimation at studied label budgets, with replication on a task- and worker-disjoint validation split under prespecified criteria.

Result: Twenty-five configurations reach tie-aware pair accuracy of at least 0.60, but a response-only TF-IDF baseline nearly matches the strongest judge. Despite ordering agreement, estimated acceptance rates range from 3.0% to 97.9% versus 61.1% for occupation-matched workers. In one fine-tuned lineage, switching from pointwise scoring to a bundled few-shot/listwise protocol improves ordering while reducing agreement with worker means at task and occupation levels, a reversal that replicates on the disjoint validation split. Cross-validated calibration largely removes mean bias but explains at most 8.5% of individual worker-rating variance, and prediction-assisted estimation yields at most small precision gains.

Conclusion: Ranking agreement is insufficient for occupational measurement. LLM judges should be validated against the acceptance rates and aggregates their scores will be used to estimate, not merely against response rankings.

Abstract: LLM judges are increasingly used to assess whether AI outputs meet workplace requirements, but agreement on response rankings does not establish agreement on acceptance rates or occupational aggregates. We introduce O*NET-BENCH, an audit suite derived from an existing survey of 45,796 worker ratings, and evaluate 33 pre-existing judge configurations across six model families on 4,501 test ratings. Twenty-five configurations achieve tie-aware pair accuracy of at least 0.60, although a train-fitted response-only TF-IDF baseline nearly matches the strongest judge. Despite this ordering agreement, judges estimate that 3.0%-97.9% of responses are acceptable, compared with 61.1% for occupation-matched workers. In one fine-tuned lineage, changing from pointwise scoring to a bundled few-shot/listwise protocol improves response ordering while reducing agreement with worker means at the task and occupation levels; this reversal replicates on a task- and worker-disjoint validation split under prespecified criteria. Cross-validated calibration largely removes mean bias, but calibrated scores explain at most 8.5% of individual worker-rating variance. Prediction-assisted estimation yields at most small precision gains at the studied label budgets. These results show that ranking agreement alone is insufficient for occupational measurement. Judges should be validated against the acceptance rates and aggregates their scores will be used to estimate.

</details>


### [18] ["I just assumed that it would translate": examining MT risk awareness among healthcare staff with abbreviations as a use case](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02496)
*Eleanor Taylor-Stilgoe,Félix do Carmo,Constantin Orăsan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UK healthcare staff use Google Translate to overcome language barriers, but research on their awareness of risks in higher-stakes contexts (e.g., medical records) is scarce. Using medical abbreviations as a use case, French/Spanish clinical corpus examples translated by GT were shown to 21 healthcare staff in semi-structured interviews; qualitative and cross-analysis revealed the potential risks of uninformed MT reliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UK healthcare staff report relying on machine translation, predominantly Google Translate, to communicate with patients across language barriers. Although intended to support duty of care, uninformed reliance may seriously threaten patient safety. Existing literature focuses on interpersonal use or patient-oriented documentation, leaving a gap regarding staff awareness of risks in higher-stakes MT use, especially with patient medical records. Medical abbreviations are a well-documented risk even monolingually, with misuse or misinterpretation causing outcomes from temporary harm to patient death, making them a suitable use case for identifying MT-related risks.

Method: Medical abbreviations were selected as the use case. Contextualised French and Spanish data examples drawn from authoritative clinical corpora were translated via Google Translate and presented during semi-structured interviews to 21 healthcare staff participants in diverse roles and specialties. The results were then subjected to qualitative analysis and cross-analysis.

Result: The abstract does not report specific empirical findings; it states that the interview data were qualitatively and cross-analysed to identify the potential risks posed by translating medical abbreviations with MT. (No concrete outcomes are provided in the given text.)

Conclusion: The study addresses a research gap on staff awareness of risks from higher-stakes MT use, particularly with patient medical records, by investigating the translation of medical abbreviations via Google Translate. The design—contextualised clinical corpus examples, GT translation, and semi-structured interviews with 21 diverse healthcare staff—aims to reveal the potential risks of uninformed MT reliance in patient-facing contexts. (Detailed conclusions depend on the full paper.)

Abstract: In the UK, public healthcare staff report turning to machine translation (MT) - predominantly Google Translate (GT) - to communicate with patients across language barriers. Though intended to support their duty of care, potentially uninformed reliance on MT in such contexts could have serious consequences for patient safety. Research nonetheless remains limited on staff awareness of the possible risks posed by higher-stakes MT use in general and with patient medical records in particular, most existing literature instead examining its use in interpersonal situations or with patient-oriented documentation. Moreover, medical abbreviations are well-documented as increasing patient risk even monolingually, with outcomes from their misuse and/or misinterpretation ranging from temporary harm to the death of the patient. Abbreviations were therefore selected as a use case for identifying the potential risks posed by their translation with MT. Contextualised French and Spanish data examples drawn from authoritative clinical corpora and translated via GT were presented during semi-structured interviews to 21 healthcare staff participants in diverse roles and specialties. The results were then subject to qualitative analysis and cross-analysis.

</details>


### [19] [HXAI: Hierarchical Privacy-Preserving Explainable AI in Distributed Energy Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02504)
*Poushali Sengupta,Sabita Maharjan,Frank Eliassen,Yan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HXAI is a hierarchical framework that enables privacy-preserving explainable analysis for grid-level demand management by generating fine-grained explanations locally and aggregating them zonally under a flexible privacy budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grid operators need fine-grained, decision-relevant insights into household energy consumption to manage peak loads and design responsive tariffs, but this transparency raises privacy concerns. Traditional XAI methods can leak sensitive information, while standard privacy techniques degrade explanation usefulness.

Method: HXAI has two components: (1) a local model producing fine-grained explanations within a secure, private environment, and (2) a zonal model that aggregates these explanations for grid-level analysis while enforcing privacy via flexible privacy-budget management. The framework explicitly limits cumulative privacy exposure across repeated operator queries.

Result: Experiments on simulated and real-world energy datasets show HXAI yields useful insights for zonal load management while keeping appliance-level consumption local and never transmitted to grid operators. Preserving the semantic structure of explanations, rather than minimizing numerical error, is key to XAI under differential privacy.

Conclusion: HXAI provides a way to achieve both privacy and explainability in energy management, preserving decision-relevant information without compromising household privacy.

Abstract: Balancing electricity demand and supply is increasingly difficult due to the inherent intermittency of renewable power generation and the stochastic power consumption. Grid operators require fine-grained, decision-relevant insights into household energy consumption to manage peak loads and design responsive tariffs, but increased transparency at this level raises significant privacy concerns. Traditional methods for explainable AI (XAI) can reveal sensitive information, while standard privacy techniques often reduce the usefulness of explanations. To address this issue, we introduce HXAI, a hierarchical framework that preserves privacy while enabling reasonable explainable analysis for grid-level demand management. HXAI consists of two main components: (1) a local model that generates fine-grained explanations within a secure, private environment, and (2) a zonal model that aggregates these explanations to support grid-level analysis while enforcing privacy through flexible privacy-budget management. We explicitly limit cumulative privacy exposure under repeated operator queries and show that the proposed framework preserves decision-relevant information without compromising household privacy. Experiments on both simulated and real-world energy datasets demonstrate that HXAI provides useful insights for zonal load management while ensuring that appliance-level consumption remains local and is never transmitted to grid operators. Our results show that preserving the semantic structure of explanations, rather than minimizing numerical error, is the key to XAI under differential privacy. This framework provides a way to achieve both privacy and explainability in energy management.

</details>


### [20] [World Action Modeling with Progressive Visual Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02508)
*Fei Zhang,Zhaochong An,Duncan Frost,Yikai Wang,Pengfei Liu,Ya Zhang,Michal Drozdzal,Amir Bar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProWAM is a progressive world action model that jointly predicts actions and an ordered sequence of sparse visual sub-goals, providing visual guidance for long-horizon robotic control while avoiding dense video rollout inefficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world action models (WAMs) struggle with long-horizon prediction: generating dense video rollouts is highly inefficient, while predicting a single future frame neglects how to progress toward the goal. This limits their effectiveness in closed-loop robotic control.

Method: ProWAM jointly predicts actions and an ordered sequence of sparse visual sub-goals to anchor action generation throughout task execution. Sub-goal prediction is learned from large-scale action-free videos, offloading visual planning from the action policy. For efficiency, ProWAM executes a single video-backbone forward pass to cache sparse sub-goal features, eliminating iterative full-video generation and requiring only lightweight action denoising during replanning.

Result: ProWAM achieves superior out-of-distribution robustness. It sets new state-of-the-art on LIBERO-Plus (85.8%) and randomized RoboTwin (75.7%), with relative gains up to +35.9% over the strongest baseline. On RoboCasa365, it reaches 48.1% success and 18.2% on the Composite-Unseen split (4th overall). In zero-shot real-world experiments, it achieves 70.0% success, outperforming the strongest baseline by +15.0 points (+27.3% relative) in novel scenes.

Conclusion: Progress-indexed visual foresight is valuable for closed-loop control. ProWAM demonstrates that sparse, ordered sub-goal prediction enables efficient, scalable, and robust long-horizon robotic manipulation.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for robotic control by jointly predicting future visual dynamics and actions from an initial observation and instruction. However, existing WAMs struggle with long-horizon prediction, as generating dense video rollouts is highly inefficient. Some recent WAMs address this by predicting a single future frame without generating the full video, but this approach neglects how to progress toward the goal. We present ProWAM, a progressive world action model that jointly predicts actions and an ordered sequence of sparse visual sub-goals, providing explicit visual guidance to anchor action generation throughout task execution. This design scales naturally, as sub-goal prediction can be learned from large-scale action-free videos, allowing the video backbone to offload complex visual planning from the action policy. For efficient action generation, ProWAM executes a single video-backbone forward pass to cache sparse sub-goal features, eliminating iterative full-video generation and requiring only lightweight action denoising during replanning. Across extensive evaluations, ProWAM achieves superior out-of-distribution robustness. On simulation benchmarks, it sets new state-of-the-art results on LIBERO-Plus (85.8%) and randomized RoboTwin (75.7%), outperforming the strongest baseline with relative gains of up to +35.9%. On RoboCasa365, ProWAM achieves a 48.1% success rate and 18.2% on the challenging Composite-Unseen split, ranking 4th overall. Crucially, in zero-shot real-world experiments, ProWAM achieves 70.0% success, outperforming the strongest baseline by +15.0 (from 55.0% to 70.0%, a +27.3% relative gain) in novel scenes. These results demonstrate the value of progress-indexed visual foresight for closed-loop control. Our program is in https://sii-ferenas.github.io/ProWAM-page.

</details>


### [21] [On-Premises Multi-Course RAG Tutoring for Business Education: Hardware-Software Trade-offs in a Campus AI Tutor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02510)
*Sidney Shapiro,Joshua Lindemann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CourseChat is an on-premises, multi-course RAG tutor for undergraduate business education, deployed behind a campus gateway with six CRN-isolated courses on twin-edge AI hosts using FastAPI, a local vector DB, and a local LLM via Ollama; the paper reports model bake-offs, source-fidelity comparison, and audits, retaining an 8B production model while flagging that learning gains are not established.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Campus AI tutors must ground answers in assigned course materials while keeping textbooks and student dialogue on institutional infrastructure, motivating an on-premises, retrieval-augmented design rather than a cloud-based service.

Method: The authors deploy CourseChat across six isolated course offerings keyed by CRN, sharing twin-edge AI hosts that run a FastAPI service, a local vector database, and a local LLM served by Ollama; they conduct two generation-model bake-off rounds, a separate fixed-evidence source-fidelity comparison, and conversation and quiz audits, with 435 prebuilt questions across 65 modules decoupling practice from live generation.

Result: Several larger models failed the classroom speed gate, while a 12B model and a 7B alternative passed; a mixture-of-experts candidate improved some corrections but introduced new factual and continuity errors. Software changes improved follow-up topic resolution while preserving course scope.

Conclusion: The authors retain the 8B production model pending a demonstrated overall improvement, treating model choice, evidence selection, serving compatibility, and product design as a joint engineering decision; they do not establish learning gains, noting that faculty ratings, peak-load capacity, and complete public-gateway acceptance remain separate evaluation needs.

Abstract: Campus AI tutors based on retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) must ground answers in assigned course materials while keeping textbooks and student dialogue on institutional infrastructure. We present CourseChat, an on-premises, multi-course RAG tutor for undergraduate business education, deployed behind a campus web gateway and intended for use embedded in Moodle. Six isolated course offerings, each keyed by its own course reference number (CRN), share twin-edge AI hosts running a FastAPI service, a local vector database, and a local large language model (LLM) served by Ollama. We report two generation-model bake-off rounds, a separate fixed-evidence source-fidelity comparison, and conversation and quiz audits. Several larger models failed the classroom speed gate, but a 12B model and a 7B alternative passed. A separate mixture-of-experts candidate improved some corrections while introducing new factual and continuity errors. We therefore retain the 8B production model pending a demonstrated overall improvement, rather than claiming that 8B is universally optimal. Software changes improved follow-up topic resolution while preserving course scope; 435 prebuilt questions across 65 modules decouple practice from live generation. The results support treating model choice, evidence selection, serving compatibility, and product design as a joint engineering decision. They do not establish learning gains: faculty ratings, peak-load capacity, and complete public-gateway acceptance remain separate evaluation needs.

</details>


### [22] [Hypothesis-guided discovery of cognitive algorithms via program refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02523)
*Huiwen Alex Yang,Mark K. Ho,Bill D. Thompson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hybrid cognitive modeling system that treats cognitive algorithm discovery as program refinement: human-authored probabilistic programs are iteratively revised by LLM agents, with a probabilistic inference module handling latent variables and likelihoods. Evaluated on a problem-solving task, revised models outperform ancestral ones and expose a small set of recurring innovations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional cognitive modeling is interpretable and leverages human expertise but lacks flexibility and scalability. LLM-based de novo model generation is scalable and flexible but leaves little room for human expertise and has mostly been applied to simpler tasks than algorithm recovery. The paper aims to combine the strengths of both.

Method: A hybrid system framing cognitive algorithm discovery as program refinement. Human-created cognitive models are expressed as probabilistic programs and passed to a system of LLM agents tasked with (1) identifying mismatches between model and behavior, (2) proposing code-level modifications within researcher-specified constraints, and (3) verifying structural fidelity. Revisions propagate to a probabilistic inference module that performs inference for latent variables and computes data likelihood.

Result: On human behavior in a problem-solving paradigm that exposes multiple cognitive algorithms, revised models consistently improved model fit relative to ancestral models and revealed a small set of recurring innovations that capture meaningful behavioral variability in the task.

Conclusion: A hybrid, human-in-the-loop program-refinement pipeline using LLM agents can effectively and scalably refine cognitive models of algorithmic reasoning while preserving interpretability and researcher control.

Abstract: Developing cognitive models of algorithmic reasoning from behavioral data is a central problem in cognitive science that challenges current methods. Traditional approaches to cognitive modeling are interpretable and benefit from human expertise, but lack flexibility and scalability. Emerging techniques using large language models (LLMs) for de novo generation of cognitive models are scalable and flexible, but lack a role for human expertise and have mostly been applied to simpler tasks than algorithm recovery. We propose a hybrid system that treats discovery of cognitive algorithms as a program refinement problem. Human-created cognitive models are expressed as probabilistic programs and provided to a system of LLM agents with a mandate to: identify mismatches between model and behavior; propose code-level modifications within researcher-specified constraints; and verify structural fidelity. Revisions propagate to a probabilistic inference module that performs inference for latent variables and data likelihood computations. We evaluate the pipeline on human behavior in a problem-solving paradigm that exposes a variety of cognitive algorithms. Revised models consistently improve model fit relative to ancestral models and reveal a small set of recurring innovations that capture meaningful behavioral variability in this task.

</details>


### [23] [Learning What to Investigate Next: Meta-Reasoning for Long-Horizon Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02525)
*Ankur Samanta,Yonathan Efroni,Paul Sajda,Kaveh Hassani,Anirudh Goyal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MIRA is a hierarchical architecture that separates research allocation (meta-reasoning) from execution, enabling improved long-horizon research agents and a trainable generative actor-critic (MIRA-AC) for efficient reinforcement learning without policy training in the base version.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon research agents must decide both how to investigate and what to investigate next, but such decisions are sparse in long traces and their consequences emerge several investigations later, making them hard to learn.

Method: MIRA separates an outer-loop meta-reasoner, which curates context from a persistent research record and writes a work order or ends the episode, from a fresh inner-loop executor that carries out each work order, making execution part of the transition between meta-reasoning actions. Decision boundaries serve as credit assignment units; a generative critic forecasts expected remaining return from partial states at each boundary.
