Cross-environment pretraining yields a transferable prior for valuing partial progress, which initializes MIRA-AC, a generative actor-critic jointly trained to forecast remaining return and select the next investigation without a separate critic model.

Result: Without policy training, MIRA improves long-horizon inference and allocates additional compute more effectively in theorem proving and open-ended neural-architecture research. The generative critic outperforms token-level alternatives for forecasting remaining return. Training MIRA-AC on the model's own proxy hill-climbing signals improves gold performance across four autoresearch environments, and the actor transfers with cross-environment value initialization.

Conclusion: Meta-reasoning can be learned as an explicit policy for directing long-horizon autonomous research.

Abstract: Long-horizon research agents must decide both how to investigate and what to investigate next as evidence accumulates. This is hard to learn because such decisions are sparse in long execution traces, and their consequences may emerge several investigations later. We introduce Meta-reasoning for Iterative Research Agents (MIRA), a hierarchical architecture separating research allocation from execution. An outer-loop meta-reasoner curates context from a persistent research record, then writes a work order for the next investigation or ends the episode. A fresh inner-loop executor carries out each work order, making execution part of the transition between meta-reasoning actions. Without policy training, MIRA improves long-horizon inference and allocates additional compute more effectively in theorem proving and open-ended neural-architecture research. Its decision boundaries also provide natural units for credit assignment. At each boundary, we train a generative critic to forecast expected remaining return from partial states, outperforming token-level alternatives. Cross-environment pretraining improves forecasting and adaptation, yielding a transferable prior for valuing partial progress. We use this prior to initialize MIRA-AC, a generative actor-critic jointly trained to forecast remaining return and choose the next investigation, without a separate critic model. MIRA-AC concentrates policy optimization on meta-reasoning decisions, enabling efficient long-horizon reinforcement learning without directly optimizing the longer execution traces they initiate. Training MIRA-AC on the model's own proxy hill-climbing signals improves gold performance across four autoresearch environments; the actor transfers with cross-environment value initialization. Together, these results show that meta-reasoning can be learned as an explicit policy for directing long-horizon autonomous research.

</details>


### [24] [How To Train Your World Model: Fine-tuning vs RAG for LM-based World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02542)
*Dhananjay Ashok,Shantanu Agarwal,Vivek Datla,Jonathan May,Alfy Samuel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper systematically compares fine-tuning and RAG for LM-based world models in text environments, finds fine-tuning generally stronger but RAG more data-efficient, diagnoses retrieval failures via counterfactual intervention, improves retrieval with hierarchical query reformulation, and proposes a hybrid world model that outperforms both.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models let agents plan by simulating environment dynamics. In text-based settings, fine-tuning an LM as a world model is the dominant paradigm, but retrieval-based approaches (e.g., RAG), despite broad success elsewhere, remain underexplored for LM-based world modelling. The paper aims to close this gap by systematically comparing the two construction paradigms.

Method: Systematic evaluation across five diverse environments (embodied, web navigation, social) comparing fine-tuning versus RAG-based LM world models. Analysis of data efficiency and scaling of collected experience. A counterfactual-intervention procedure to estimate the retrieval-stage error rate. Study of query reformulation strategies, including a hierarchical approach versus the traditional retrieval pipeline. A hybrid world model combining parametric capture of core dynamics with retrieval from an actively maintained memory store.

Result: Fine-tuning often outperforms RAG, yielding higher rewards in 15/20 settings. Both paradigms benefit from additional diverse exploration; RAG is more data-efficient while fine-tuning benefits disproportionately from scaling experience. Counterfactual intervention shows retrievers consistently surface suboptimal transitions. The hierarchical query reformulation outperforms the traditional retrieval pipeline, and the hybrid system consistently outperforms other methods across environments and models.

Conclusion: Retrieval for LM-based world modelling is a viable and complementary alternative to fine-tuning. Addressing retrieval errors via better query reformulation and combining parametric and retrieval-based modelling in a hybrid system offers a robust, generally superior approach to LM-based world models.

Abstract: World models (WMs) simulate the transition dynamics of environments, enabling agents to plan over the consequences of their actions. In text-based environments, fine-tuning a Language Model (LM) to serve as a WM has emerged as a dominant paradigm. However, despite the widespread success of non-parametric approaches such as Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), retrieval for LM-based world modelling remains underexplored. We conduct a systematic evaluation across five diverse environments spanning embodied, web navigation and social settings, comparing fine-tuning and RAG-based approaches for LM-based world modelling. Our study reveals that fine-tuning often outperforms RAG, with fine-tuned WMs enabling agents to obtain higher rewards on 15/20 settings. While both construction paradigms benefit from additional and more diverse exploration, RAG-based approaches prove more data-efficient, and fine-tuning approaches disproportionately benefit from scaling the amount of experience collected. With a focus on RAG-based WMs, we devise a procedure that uses counterfactual intervention to estimate the error rate of the retrieval stage, and show that retrievers consistently surface suboptimal transitions from the experience buffer. Hoping to address this failing, we study a variety of query reformulation strategies, demonstrating that a hierarchical approach outperforms the traditional retrieval pipeline. Finally, we compose our findings into a hybrid world modelling system that parametrically captures core environment dynamics, while learning to rely on retrieval from an actively maintained memory store. Our hybrid system consistently outperforms other methods across multiple environments and models, showcasing the robustness of the approach and the applicability of our findings.

</details>


### [25] [How to Have a Sensitive Debate: An Instance-Optimal Protocol for AI Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02557)
*Jiawei Li,Zhiyang Xun,Lijie Chen,Jonah Brown-Cohen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new AI debate protocol for problems with stable decompositions, achieving worst-case correctness, a dominant-strategy equilibrium, and instance-wise optimality under black-box human queries, via a connection to fractional block sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems match or exceed human experts on cognitively demanding tasks, supervising them becomes urgent. AI debate is a promising oversight approach; prior theoretical work formalized it via computational complexity and identified a best protocol for problems with stable decompositions, but that protocol only guaranteed average-case correctness and relied on a weaker Stackelberg equilibrium.

Method: The authors design a new debate protocol for the same class of problems (those with sufficiently stable decompositions into subproblems). They establish their results by relating stable problem decompositions to the concept of fractional block sensitivity from query complexity, and they prove black-box lower bounds on protocols using only black-box queries to human judgments.

Result: The new protocol improves on prior work in three ways: (1) correctness holds in a worst-case rather than average-case sense; (2) honesty and correctness form a dominant-strategy equilibrium for both debaters, rather than only a Stackelberg equilibrium; and (3) the protocol is instance-wise optimal, as no black-box protocol for this problem class can outperform it.

Conclusion: The proposed protocol strengthens the theoretical guarantees for AI debate in the stable-decomposition setting, delivering worst-case correctness, dominant-strategy honesty, and black-box instance-wise optimality, with fractional block sensitivity serving as the key analytical bridge.

Abstract: As powerful AI systems reach and sometimes surpass the abilities of human experts across a range of cognitively demanding tasks, the problem of accurate oversight and supervision of these systems has become increasingly urgent. One promising approach is AI debate, which seeks to leverage a debate between two powerful AIs to break complex questions down into simpler claims that can be easily judged directly. Theoretical work on debate has formalized this intuition in the language of computational complexity theory, where the goal is to design protocols (i.e., rules of the debate game) that provide rigorous guarantees on correctness for judging solutions to complex problems with limited supervision. Specifically, the current best protocol has been shown to work for all problems that have sufficiently stable decompositions into subproblems. In this paper, we design a new protocol for this same class of problems that improves on the prior work in several ways. First, correctness holds in a worst-case rather than an average-case sense. Second, being honest and correct is a dominant-strategy equilibrium for both debaters, rather than a Stackelberg equilibrium. Finally, we prove black-box lower bounds, showing that our new protocol is instance-wise optimal. That is, no protocol for this class of problems can outperform ours while making only black-box queries to human judgments. We obtain these results by relating the notion of stable problem decompositions to the concept of fractional block sensitivity from query complexity.

</details>


### [26] [Mitigating Social Sycophancy via Pluralistic Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02568)
*Stephane Hatgis-Kessell,Myra Cheng,Xiaoxuan Hou,Qian Hu,Rahul Gupta,Natasha Jaques,Emma Brunskill*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Pluralistic Preference Optimization (PlurPO), a ground-truth-free method that reduces social sycophancy in language models by having them simulate multiple stakeholders' perspectives and train toward responses acceptable to all, cutting harm-endorsement rates by 89% and roughly halving the human-endorsement gap on general advice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personal and relationship advice is a top use case for generative AI, but LMs exhibit sycophancy—affirming users far more than humans would—which makes users overconfident and less willing to repair conflicts. Prior mitigation work targets factual settings with ground-truth answers; social sycophancy (e.g., personal advice) lacks ground truth, and existing prompt-based and post-training approaches are only marginally effective.

Method: The insight is that social sycophancy arises partly because LMs over-center on the user and ignore other stakeholders affected by the user's behavior. PlurPO operates on inputs describing interpersonal conflicts: the LM identifies and simulates relevant stakeholders, then is trained to prefer and generate responses acceptable to all stakeholders. It relies solely on signals the model produces about its own outputs, requiring no ground-truth labels.

Result: PlurPO substantially reduces social sycophancy across four datasets and four model families versus prior methods. On statements of intent to cause harm—where endorsement is inappropriate—it lowers the endorsement rate by 89% on average across four models. On general advice questions, where the target is to match human endorsement rates, it closes the gap by over half, from 17.8% to 8.0% on average. The preference dataset built by PlurPO for an 8B model also transfers effectively to mitigating sycophancy in a larger 32B model.

Conclusion: Social sycophancy can be reduced by leveraging a model's own capabilities to simulate a plurality of relevant perspectives, yielding a label-free, transferable mitigation strategy.

Abstract: Personal advice, including relationship advice, now ranks among the most common uses of generative AI. But language models (LMs) exhibit sycophancy: they affirm users much more often than humans do, which can make people overconfident and less willing to repair their relationships after a conflict. Prior work on mitigating sycophancy has focused on factual settings where a response can be checked against a ground truth answer, while mitigations for social sycophancy (e.g., personal advice, where there is no ground truth) have relied on simple prompting and post-training methods with limited effectiveness. Our insight is that social sycophancy occurs in part because LMs overly center on the user and fail to consider the perspectives of other stakeholders impacted by the user's behavior. To address this problem we propose Pluralistic Preference Optimization (PlurPO): given inputs describing interpersonal conflicts, the LM identifies and simulates the relevant stakeholders, and is then trained to prefer and generate responses acceptable to all stakeholders. PlurPO uses only signals the model produces about its own outputs, without ground-truth labels. PlurPO substantially reduces social sycophancy across four datasets and four model families compared to prior methods. For example, on statements of intent to cause harm, where the users' actions should not be endorsed, PlurPO reduces the endorsement rate by 89% on average across four models. On general advice questions, where the target is to match the endorsement rate of human responses, it closes the gap by more than half, from 17.8% to 8.0% on average. The preference dataset constructed by PlurPO for an 8B model also effectively transfers to mitigating sycophancy in a larger (32B) model. Our results indicate that social sycophancy can be reduced by leveraging a model's own capabilities to simulate a plurality of relevant perspectives.

</details>


### [27] [Answering clinicians' questions over trial evidence tables with verifiable, feedback-driven language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02576)
*Manan Roy Choudhury,Suparno Roy Chowdhury,Swastik Sahoo,Muhammad Ali Khan,Kaneez Zahra Rubab Khakwani,Mohamad Bassam Sonbol,Irbaz Bin Riaz,Vivek Gupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FD-SCoPE is a language-model framework that answers clinician questions over systematic-review evidence tables, handles both direct database queries and questions requiring derived attributes, and exposes the query, selected trials, and derivation rules behind every answer while learning from expert feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Systematic reviews condense clinical trials into evidence tables, but clinicians can only interrogate these tables through database queries. Many clinically relevant questions concern attributes not recorded in the table, such as a drug's target class or a harmonised endpoint, so existing query-based access is insufficient.

Method: The authors introduce FD-SCoPE, a language-model framework that answers both direct-record questions and questions requiring derived attributes. It exposes the query, the selected trials, and the derivation rule behind each answer, and learns from expert corrections. It was evaluated on an oncology evidence table of 159 immune checkpoint inhibitor trial records across 140 clinician-style tasks.

Result: FD-SCoPE completed all 140 clinician-style tasks (alternatives scored 90.7-97.9%). For questions needing derived attributes it retrieved 99.3% of relevant trial records at a positive predictive value of 89.8%, outperforming four alternative approaches (derived-value F1 77.7% versus 64.8-73.4%). Corrections on 299 simulated questions raised F1 on 1,201 unseen questions from 77.9% to 84.9%.

Conclusion: Language models coupled with executable queries, verified programs, and expert feedback can give clinicians auditable access to trial evidence, enabling both direct interrogation and derivation of attributes not recorded in evidence tables.

Abstract: Systematic reviews condense clinical trials into evidence tables, yet clinicians can interrogate these tables only through database queries, and many questions concern attributes that the table does not record, such as a drug's target class or a harmonised endpoint. Here we introduce FD-SCoPE, a language-model framework that answers both kinds of question, exposes the query, the selected trials and the derivation rule behind every answer, and learns from expert corrections. On an oncology evidence table of 159 immune checkpoint inhibitor trial records, FD-SCoPE completed all 140 clinician-style tasks (alternatives, 90.7-97.9%). For questions needing derived attributes it retrieved 99.3% of relevant trial records at a positive predictive value of 89.8% and outperformed four alternative approaches (derived-value F1 77.7% versus 64.8-73.4%). Corrections on 299 questions, simulated from reference answers, raised F1 on 1,201 unseen questions from 77.9% to 84.9%. Language models coupled with executable queries, verified programs and expert feedback can give clinicians auditable access to trial evidence.

</details>


### [28] [Labels Override Definitions in Jev-Style Typed Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02586)
*Seyedarmin Azizi,Erfan Baghaei Potraghloo,Massoud Pedram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Open-weight "typed decision models" largely answer by option *label* rather than its written *definition*, and the cause is a one-line prompt-rendering choice—not the constrained decision head previously blamed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typed decision models are meant to apply the rule a developer writes in each option's definition, but it was unclear whether the returned probability reflects those definitions or merely the labels. Prior work blamed the constrained decision head used in place of a text decoder; the authors suspect the real cause lies elsewhere and want to identify it precisely.

Method: Probe four open-weight typed decision models and three answer-reading variants of a Qwen2.5 backbone across eleven classification tasks plus PolicyBench, a new synthetic routing suite whose rule appears only in definitions. Ablate by deleting all definitions, by renaming options to "A"/"B", and by rewriting the rendering expression ("{label}: {definition}" vs. definition only) in both directions with weights frozen. Add a two-call diagnostic test and evaluate four mitigations.

Result: Accuracy is driven mostly by labels. Deleting every definition leaves accuracy essentially unchanged (laya-td 0.8559 vs. 0.8487) even though definitions alone score 0.7971; renaming options to A/B raises accuracy by +0.1511 [+0.1377, +0.1646]. One system, von, is unaffected. The two code bases differ only in the rendering: laya uses "{label}: {definition}", von uses the definition alone. Flipping that expression with frozen weights makes all three laya checkpoints exactly invariant (+0.0000 [+0.0000, +0.0000]) and induces the bias in von, whose accuracy drops from 0.8511 to 0.2281 when a label contradicts its definition.

Conclusion: Option-label bias resides in the prompt rendering, not the constrained decision head. A two-call test tells a practitioner which case applies to their model, and the paper quantifies the value of four mitigations.

Abstract: A typed decision model answers a fixed question about an input by returning a probability for each of several caller-defined options. Each option carries a short label and a written definition, which is where a developer states the rule the model should apply. Jev introduced this interface for routing, moderation and triage, open implementations followed, and the same operation occurs whenever a language model is used as a classifier by scoring label strings. We study the open implementations, whose weights we can inspect and patch, and ask whether the probability follows the definitions or the labels. A preference for the label we call option-label bias. Across four open-weight typed decision models, three ways of reading an answer from a Qwen2.5 backbone, eleven classification tasks and PolicyBench, a synthetic routing suite we introduce in which the rule appears only in the definitions, the answer is mostly the labels. Deleting every definition leaves accuracy unchanged (laya-td: 0.8559 against 0.8487), although those definitions support 0.7971 on their own, and renaming the options to A and B raises accuracy by +0.1511 [+0.1377, +0.1646]. One system, von, is unaffected, and the two code bases differ in one expression: laya writes each option as "{label}: {definition}", while von writes only the definition. Changing that expression in both directions, with no weight changed, makes all three laya checkpoints exactly invariant (+0.0000 [+0.0000, +0.0000]) and creates the effect in von, whose accuracy falls from 0.8511 to 0.2281 when a label contradicts its definition. Earlier work attributed this failure to the constrained decision head these models use in place of a text decoder; our results locate it in the prompt rendering. We give a two-call test that tells a practitioner which case applies to their model, and measure what four mitigations are worth.

</details>


### [29] [Open-Endedness Bench: Measuring Epistemic Process from Agent Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02588)
*Chengyang Shi,Xianglin Ji,Jintao Huang,Jicheng Wang,Yifeng He,Jiachen Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OEB is a benchmark-agnostic methodology that evaluates an AI research agent's epistemic process by reading only its execution record, compiling it into a verified event graph, and scoring four competence axes plus six persona traits, revealing that only 16-29% of claimed improvements are real.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agents increasingly perform open-ended research tasks, but their runs are judged only by outcome scores, which cannot establish whether claims follow from executed experiments, and a reference answer may be unavailable. This creates a need to assess the epistemic process itself.

Method: OEB (Open-Endedness Bench) reads only the agent's execution record, using no reference answer or outcome score. It compiles the record into a unified epistemic event graph linking stated propositions to the executed actions testing them, with each node carrying a code-verified exact excerpt. Its core principle: prose can state a proposition but only evidence from an executed action can support or refute it, so OEB checks what the agent writes against what it ran. From the graph it scores four competence axes (evidence, experiment, revision, no reward hacking), mostly as the share of sound-research opportunities taken, and profiles six subjective persona traits.

Result: Scoring 119 runs over 12 tasks from three benchmarks (LLM post-training, chip design, a training-speed record): only 16-29% of claimed improvements are real; on 9 of 10 tasks the best run tries more new ideas in its second half than the worst run; persona readings follow the model, with model explaining more variance per trait than task (median 43% vs 7%).

Conclusion: OEB provides a process-based, reference-free evaluation of research agents that verifies claims against executed evidence, exposes a large gap between claimed and real improvements, and attributes research habits primarily to the underlying model rather than the task.

Abstract: Agents are increasingly given open-ended research tasks: discovering an empirical law from self-designed experiments, improving a heuristic whose optimum nobody knows, or beating a standing record. Their execution logs record every step of this research, yet the runs are still judged by their outcome score. That score alone does not establish whether an agent's claims follow from executed experiments, and a reference answer may be unavailable. We evaluate the agent's epistemic process: how it forms hypotheses, tests them, and revises them in response to evidence. We introduce OEB (Open-Endedness Bench), a benchmark-agnostic methodology that reads only the agent's execution record and never a reference answer or an outcome score. OEB compiles the record into a unified epistemic event graph whose edges connect the propositions the agent states to the executed actions that test them; each node carries an exact excerpt that code verifies against the record. One principle governs scoring: prose can state a proposition, but only evidence returned by an executed action can support or refute it, so OEB checks what the agent writes against what it actually ran. From the graph, OEB scores four competence axes (evidence, experiment, revision, and no reward hacking), mostly as the share of opportunities for sound research that the agent took, and profiles six subjective persona traits that describe the agent's research habits. We score 119 existing runs over 12 tasks from three benchmarks: LLM post-training, chip design, and a training-speed record. Against logged results, only 16-29% of the improvements agents claim are real. On 9 of 10 tasks, the best run tries more new ideas in its second half than the worst run. The persona readings follow the model: for every trait, the model that ran explains more of its variance across runs than the task (a median of 43% against 7%).

</details>


### [30] [TasteBench: Multimodal Benchmark for Sensory Prediction, from Molecules to Sustainable Foods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02599)
*Anna T. Thomas,Sohum Patnaik,Caroline Cotto,Benjamin Sanchez-Lengeling*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TasteBench is a multimodal benchmark and privacy-preserving competition for sensory prediction of plant-based foods, comprising a food-level ranking task (21K+ human evaluations, 215 foods, 24 categories, 935 ranking pairs) and a molecular-level taste classification task (15K flavor molecules), with characterized ground truth and baselines across four input modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sustainable protein discovery lacks fast computational proxies analogous to molecular docking or DFT that accelerate drug and materials discovery. Assessing whether a novel food tastes like its animal-based target requires costly human sensory panels, which bottleneck the design-build-test loop.

Method: The authors introduce TasteBench, spanning two tasks: (1) a food-level ranking task built on 21K+ human evaluations across 215 plant-based foods in 24 product categories, yielding 935 within-category ranking pairs, and (2) a supporting molecular-level taste classification task over 15K flavor molecules. They characterize the ground truth via inter-rater agreement (Krippendorff's α = .077) and split-half reliability ceiling of panel-aggregated rankings (.825), then evaluate baselines across four input modalities.

Result: On the same pairs rated by panelists, the best model achieves .661 pairwise accuracy, competitive with the median individual panelist (.650), and .683 across all within-category pairs.

Conclusion: TasteBench provides the evaluation infrastructure and baselines for measuring progress on computational screening for sustainable protein discovery.

Abstract: Sustainable protein discovery lacks the fast computational proxies, analogous to molecular docking or density functional theory, that accelerate drug and materials discovery. Evaluating whether a novel food tastes like its animal-based target requires expensive human sensory panels, bottlenecking the design-build-test loop. We introduce TasteBench, a multimodal benchmark and privacy-preserving competition for sensory prediction, spanning two tasks: a food-level ranking task built on 21K+ human evaluations across 215 plant-based foods in 24 product categories, yielding 935 within-category ranking pairs, and a supporting molecular-level taste classification task over 15K flavor molecules. To enable rigorous interpretation of model performance, we characterize the ground truth: inter-rater agreement among panelists is low (Krippendorff's $α= .077$), and the split-half reliability ceiling of panel-aggregated rankings is .825, establishing the range within which ML systems on this benchmark should be assessed. We evaluate baselines across four input modalities; on the same pairs panelists rated, the best model achieves .661 pairwise accuracy, competitive with the median individual panelist (.650), and .683 across all within-category pairs. TasteBench provides the evaluation infrastructure and baselines for measuring progress on computational screening for sustainable protein discovery.

</details>


### [31] [Time Series Forecasting Benchmarks Need Scenario-Grounded Stress Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02608)
*Yuyang Zhao,Lian Xu,Hao Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current time series forecasting (TSF) evaluation is too narrow—benchmarks reward low held-out error and robustness studies reduce failure to Gaussian noise, random masking, or bounded adversarial perturbations—so the authors advocate scenario-grounded stress testing in which each test instance pairs historical inputs and future targets with a semantic scenario, an explicit failure operator, and a measurable difficulty level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TSF increasingly drives decisions in transportation, energy, finance, healthcare, and infrastructure, but existing evaluation obscures the real failure modes of deployed systems. Input-side anomalies are not merely noisier inputs: they often reflect structured events that alter temporal dynamics, break cross-variable dependencies, induce regime shifts, or propagate from faulty sensors to downstream decisions. These semantic, causal, and system-level failures cannot be faithfully captured by i.i.d. perturbations alone. The rise of TSF foundation models makes this gap more urgent, since unauditable pretraining corpora make held-out generalization increasingly unreliable.

Method: The authors propose scenario-grounded stress testing. Each test instance should include historical inputs and future targets, together with a semantic scenario, an explicit failure operator, and a measurable difficulty level.

Result: The proposed shift makes evaluation interpretable, attributable, and deployment-relevant (and friendly), enabling the community to ask not only which model is accurate, but under what conditions it fails and why.

Conclusion: The authors argue that TSF evaluation should move beyond held-out error and i.i.d. robustness perturbations toward scenario-grounded stress testing that exposes semantic, causal, and system-level failure modes.

Abstract: Time series forecasting (TSF) increasingly drives decisions in transportation, energy, finance, healthcare, and infrastructure, yet current evaluation remains overly narrow: standard benchmarks reward low held-out error, while robustness studies typically reduce failure to Gaussian noise, random masking, or bounded adversarial perturbations. This obscures the real failure modes of deployed forecasting systems. Input-side anomalies are not merely noisier inputs: they often reflect structured events that alter temporal dynamics, break cross-variable dependencies, induce regime shifts, or propagate from faulty sensors to downstream decisions. These semantic, causal, and system-level failures cannot be faithfully captured by i.i.d. perturbations alone. The rise of TSF foundation models makes this evaluation gap more urgent, as unauditable pretraining corpora make held-out generalization increasingly unreliable. We therefore advocate scenario-grounded stress testing. Each test instance should include historical inputs and future targets, together with a semantic scenario, an explicit failure operator, and a measurable difficulty level. This shift makes evaluation interpretable, attributable, and deployment-relevant and friendly, enabling the community to ask not only which model is accurate, but under what conditions it fails and why.

</details>


### [32] [VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02616)
*Zekai Wang,Yingqiang Ge,Zekun Wang,Hai Wang,Yuhui Xu,Joshua Frandsen,Shancong Fu,Ashia C. Wilson,Chandan K. Reddy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VERSE is a verified self-evolving optimizer that improves both an executor agent's harness and its own prompts, skills, tools, hooks, and notes, achieving higher accuracy on held-out and out-of-distribution coding tasks than fixed-optimizer baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing harness evolution improves an LLM agent's prompts, tools, and workflow, but the optimizer's own tools and procedures typically stay fixed. The authors ask whether an optimizer can improve another agent more effectively by also improving how it diagnoses failures, develops edits, and tests their effects.

Method: A controlled study shows optimizer self-evolution fails without execution-based verification but performs best when verification is available. Motivated by this, VERSE lets the optimizer test draft edits, replay failures, and perturb suspected steps before submission, tracking fixes and regressions across rounds. The optimizer then revises both the executor harness and its own prompts, skills, tools, hooks, and notes, while model weights stay fixed. Evaluation uses a shared protocol with disjoint training, validation, and test tasks across five executors.

Result: VERSE improves all four evaluated harness optimizers on held-out SWE-rebench tasks and newer out-of-distribution tasks in five languages. Its best validation-selected harness reaches 42.3% and 37.7% accuracy, versus 39.2% and 29.3% for the strongest baselines.

Conclusion: Execution-based verification is key to effective optimizer self-evolution, and allowing an optimizer to evolve its own diagnostic and verification procedures alongside the executor harness yields consistent gains on held-out and out-of-distribution coding tasks.

Abstract: Harness evolution improves an LLM agent's prompts, tools, and workflow, while the optimizer's own tools and procedures often remain fixed. We study whether an optimizer can improve another agent more effectively by also improving how it diagnoses failures, develops edits, and tests their effects. Two observations guide our design. In a controlled study, optimizer self-evolution fails to improve performance without execution-based verification, but achieves the best result of that study when verification is available. Across five executors, self-evolving optimizers build their own tools for failure analysis, verification, training audits, and workflow control. Motivated by these findings, we introduce VERSE, a Verified Self-Evolving optimizer for agent harnesses. VERSE lets the optimizer test draft edits, replay failures, and perturb suspected steps before submission, while tracking fixes and regressions across rounds. Using this feedback, the optimizer revises both the executor harness and its own prompts, skills, tools, hooks, and notes, while the weights of the optimizer and executor models stay fixed. Under a shared protocol with disjoint training, validation, and test tasks, VERSE improves all four evaluated harness optimizers on held-out SWE-rebench tasks and newer out-of-distribution tasks in five languages. Its best validation-selected harness reaches 42.3% and 37.7% accuracy, respectively, against 39.2% and 29.3% for the strongest baselines. Code is available at https://github.com/wzekai/VERSE.

</details>


### [33] [CuBEs: Culturally-Situated Behavioral Evaluations and the Limitations of Culture-Blind LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02622)
*Hoda Ayad,Tanu Mitra,Abhishek Mukherji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CuBEs, a culturally-situated behavior evaluation framework that injects cultural context into LLM behavioral test scenarios, revealing significant cross-cultural variations that standard evaluations miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing situated behavioral evaluations of LLMs typically ignore cultural context, limiting their generalizability across a global user base. This gap undermines the prediction of real-world deployment risks for diverse user cultures.

Method: The authors extend an automated testing pipeline to inject cultural context into behavioral test scenarios and subsequent evaluation. They build a human-labeled dataset capturing nuanced dimensions of behavior understanding across 12 distinct cultures to assess the pipeline's cultural adaptability, then evaluate 13 open- and closed-source LLMs.

Result: The dataset reveals significant cross-cultural variations that one-size-fits-all judgments fail to capture. Introducing cultural situatedness produces significant variation in the presence of a behavior. Baseline political bias experiments capture localized Western dimensions (e.g., the American conservative-progressive divide), whereas non-Western culturally situated evaluations surface entirely different axes of bias such as religious and colonial political issues.

Conclusion: Standard, culturally-agnostic evaluations fail to capture these behavioral shifts, highlighting the necessity of culturally situated behavioral testing for global LLM deployments.

Abstract: Evaluating the occurrence and triggers of large language model (LLM) behaviors - such as sycophancy, self-preference, or over-confidence - is critical for predicting real-world model deployment risks. However, existing situated behavioral evaluations typically ignore cultural context, limiting their generalizability across an increasingly global user base. To address this gap, we propose CuBEs - Culturally-situated Behavior Evaluations that probe for response patterns across diverse user cultures. We first extend an automated testing pipeline to inject cultural context into behavioral test scenarios and subsequent evaluation. We assess the cultural adaptability of this pipeline by building a human-labeled dataset that captures nuanced dimensions of behavior understanding across 12 distinct cultures. Our dataset reveals significant cross-cultural variations that one-size-fits all judgments fail to capture. Through evaluating 13 open- and closed-source LLMs, we find that introducing cultural situatedness in the evaluation scenario creates significant variation in the presence of a behavior. For example, while our baseline experiments testing for political bias capture localized Western political dimensions like the American conservative-progressive divide, non-Western culturally situated evaluations surface entirely different axes of bias such as religious and colonial political issues. Our findings demonstrate that standard, culturally-agnostic evaluations fail to capture these shifts, highlighting the necessity of culturally situated behavioral testing for global deployments.

</details>


### [34] [Lost in the Request: How Communication Variation Disrupts Retrieval and Action in Email Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02627)
*Feng Chen,Ritam Dutt,Atnaz Taheri,Alex Williams*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Email assistant robustness is largely untested because benchmarks use a single canonical phrasing per task. Holding the information need, evidence, and expected outcome fixed while varying communication style (5 axes) and English dialect (4 rule-based conditions), the authors find indirect requests hurt all three benchmarks (a RAG pipeline and two tool-using agents), and formal requests hurt both agentic benchmarks—via distinct mechanisms (retrieval failure vs. action omission).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: An assistant should not do less work just because the same request is phrased differently, yet most benchmarks test each task with only one canonical request, leaving phrasing/dialect robustness unmeasured.

Method: Construct validated request variants along five communication-style axes and four rule-based dialect conditions, keeping requested information, available evidence, and expected outcome fixed. Evaluate on three benchmarks: a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline and two tool-using agents, then diagnose failure causes per system.

Result: Indirect requests reduce performance on all three benchmarks; formal requests reduce performance on both agentic benchmarks. Causes differ: verbose requests mainly hurt a lexical retriever by making the relevant email harder to find, whereas indirect and dialect variants remain harmful even when the relevant email is retrieved. In the agentic setting, indirect and formal requests mainly cause agents to omit required actions rather than take more unsupported actions.

Conclusion: A successful response alone does not establish robustness. Evaluations should vary how requests are expressed and separately measure whether agents complete the requested work.

Abstract: An email assistant should not complete less work simply because a user phrases the same request differently. Yet most benchmarks test each task with only one canonical request, leaving this form of robustness largely unmeasured. We test whether email assistants remain reliable when the requested information, available evidence, and expected outcome stay fixed, but the communication style or English variety changes. We construct validated variants along five communication-style axes and four rule-based dialect conditions, and evaluate them on three benchmarks: a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline and two tool-using agents. Indirect requests reduce performance on all three benchmarks, while formal requests reduce performance on both agentic benchmarks. Examining the systems more closely shows that these failures have different causes. Verbose requests mainly hurt a lexical retriever by making the relevant email harder to find. By contrast, indirect and dialect variants remain harmful even when the relevant email is retrieved. In the agentic setting, indirect and formal requests mainly cause the agents to omit required actions, not to take more unsupported actions. These results show that a successful response is not enough to establish robustness: evaluations should vary how requests are expressed and separately measure whether agents complete the requested work.

</details>


### [35] [Designing the Future of User Feedback for Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02631)
*Alisa Frik,Julia Bernd,Amitis Karami,Mohammad Tahaei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how to design effective post-deployment user feedback mechanisms for generative AI systems. Through a benchmark evaluation of current industry approaches, a collaboration between academic researchers and eBay identified common usability issues and developed best-practice recommendations plus a tested prototype feedback-collection tool.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-deployment user feedback is a cost-effective, scalable, and representative way to monitor and improve generative AI systems, and it can increase user engagement and trust. Government regulations and industry guidelines call for such feedback, yet there is little guidance on designing mechanisms that are both usable for consumers and yield actionable input for product teams.

Method: A multi-phase study conducted as a collaboration between academic researchers and eBay. It included a benchmark evaluation of current industry feedback approaches, followed by the development of best-practice recommendations and the design and testing of a prototype feedback-collection tool.

Result: The benchmark evaluation identified common issues in current industry approaches: lack of discoverability, unclear terminology, and inattention to user value. The prototype feedback-collection tool was designed and tested to give users an efficient, flexible, and positive feedback-giving experience while providing product teams with rich data on performance and potential problems in a usable format.

Conclusion: Effective post-deployment feedback design should address discoverability, clear terminology, and user value. The paper offers best-practice recommendations and a tested prototype that balances a positive consumer feedback experience with actionable, usable data for product teams.

Abstract: Post-deployment feedback from users can be a cost-effective, scalable, and representative means to monitor and improve generative AI systems and features. When implemented effectively, giving such feedback can increase users' engagement with and trust in GenAI systems. Government regulations and industry guidelines call for post-deployment user engagement, but there is little guidance on designing mechanisms that are usable for consumers and provide actionable input for product teams. We conducted a multi-phase study as a collaboration between academic researchers and eBay. Our benchmark evaluation of current industry approaches identified common issues including lack of discoverability, unclear terminology, and inattention to user value. Based on these findings, we developed best-practice recommendations and designed and tested a prototype feedback-collection tool. The tool aimed to provide users with an efficient, flexible, and positive feedback-giving experience, and provide product teams with rich data on performance and potential problems in a usable format.

</details>


### [36] [Batched Speech Decisions Without Decoding: Single-Token Supervision Lets a Frozen LLM Hear Beyond the Transcript](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02638)
*Jie Jin,Ziyin Ma,Min Yin,Jinyu Chen,Haigang Song,Zhikun Pang,Xiaowen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DuplexJev maps ASR-encoder hidden states through a small connector into a frozen LLM, enabling full-duplex voice agents to resolve small closed decisions in a single forward pass (one-token distribution over options) rather than slow autoregressive decoding—achieving ~0.1 s for 80 decisions across eight utterances on an 8-GPU node.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-duplex voice agents make many small, closed decisions, but current systems answer these via slow autoregressive decoding, which is inefficient. The paper also notes that the usual transcript distillation teacher never hears the voice, limiting paralinguistic capability.

Method: Feed ASR-encoder hidden states through a small connector into a frozen LLM; read each question as a single-token distribution over its options (no decoding). Train decisions with cross-entropy on the read-out answer token instead of transcript distillation, while keeping distillation for content. Connectors include a last-layer type and a cross-attention type; encoders and LLMs are interchangeable.

Result: An 8-GPU node answers 80 decisions about eight utterances in ~0.1 s. With the last-layer connector, spoken QA is 90% vs. 91% for reading the transcript. With the cross-attention connector, gender and emotion accuracy both reach 90% (up from 55% and 28%), with spoken QA dropping only 1 point (83% to 82%).

Conclusion: Single-token read-out over frozen LLMs is an effective, fast approach for closed decisions in full-duplex voice agents, with cross-attention connectors enabling paralinguistic perception at minimal QA cost. Weights, training recipe, a batched-inference pipeline for full-duplex serving, and a bilingual spoken-QA set are released.

Abstract: Full-duplex voice agents make many small, closed decisions, which current systems answer by slow autoregressive decoding. We propose DuplexJev, which feeds ASR-encoder hidden states through a small connector into a frozen LLM and reads each question as a single-token distribution over its options. Nothing is decoded, and an 8-GPU node answers 80 decisions about eight utterances in about 0.1 s. With a last-layer connector, spoken QA stays close to reading the transcript (90% vs. 91%). DuplexJev also hears the speaker: gender and emotion accuracy both reach 90% (from 55% and 28%) with a cross-attention connector, whose spoken QA drops by only 1 point (83% to 82%). We train decisions with cross-entropy on the read-out answer token, instead of the usual transcript distillation, whose teacher never hears the voice, and keep distillation for content. Encoders and LLMs are interchangeable; we release weights, training recipe, a batched-inference pipeline for full-duplex serving and a bilingual spoken-QA set.

</details>


### [37] [Coherence-Driven Belief Formation and Population Dynamics of Contagion in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02654)
*Tathagata Banerjee,Nima Moghaddas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language model agents show a sigmoid belief-adoption kernel characteristic of complex contagion, with a threshold governed by a single effective dimension of coherence with prior beliefs; collectively, these agents reproduce clustered-network spread, cascade windows, and hysteretic consensus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional social contagion models assume how individuals adopt beliefs and then derive population-level behavior from that assumption, rather than measuring the adoption rule empirically. The paper aims to replace this assumption-driven approach by directly measuring belief adoption in language model agents, grounding contagion modeling in observed agent behavior.

Method: The authors empirically measure belief adoption in language model agents, quantifying the probability that an agent adopts a claim as a function of the number of peers endorsing it (the adoption kernel). They then examine how the adoption threshold varies with three sources—the claim's plausibility, the source's reliability, and the agent's disposition—and test whether these reduce to a single effective dimension. Finally, they study collective belief-adoption dynamics in a system of AI agents across clustered versus random networks.

Result: The measured adoption kernel is sigmoid, indicating complex contagion, with a threshold sensitive to claim plausibility, source reliability, and agent disposition. These three dimensions are well approximated by a single effective dimension, interpretable as the coherence of the incoming belief with the agent's prior beliefs. Collectively, the AI agent system shows further spread on clustered than random networks, a bifurcating cascade window, and self-sustaining hysteretic consensus.

Conclusion: Belief adoption in language model agents follows complex-contagion dynamics governed by a single effective coherence dimension, and systems of such agents reproduce canonical complex-contagion collective phenomena—clustered-network advantage, cascade windows, and hysteresis—implying consensus is far harder to remove than to establish.

Abstract: Models of social contagion usually assume how individuals adopt beliefs and derive population behavior from it. We instead empirically measure belief adoption in language model agents, quantifying the probability an agent adopts a claim given how many peers endorse it. We find this adoption kernel to be sigmoid, a characteristic of complex contagion, with a threshold that is sensitive to three sources: the claim's plausibility, the source's reliability, and the agent's disposition. These three dimensions are well approximated by a single effective dimension which we propose can be understood as the coherence of the incoming belief with the LLM agent's prior beliefs. Further, we observe a characteristic of complex contagion in the collective dynamics of belief adoption in a system of AI agents: further spread on clustered than random networks. These systems also exhibit a bifurcating cascade window, and self-sustaining hysteretic consensus which lead to consensus being far harder to remove than to establish.

</details>


### [38] [A GHOST in Long-Horizon Agents: Governance Hazard from Overlooked Safety Constraints across Turns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02664)
*XinPeng Shen,Lan Zhang,Yixiao Huang,Haoran Cheng,Jiewei Lai,Leilei Chen,Haoxiang Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies GHOST, a long-horizon agent safety failure where actions violate safety constraints specified many turns earlier under benign conditions (11.5% occurrence on GPT-5.5), provides a theoretical characterization via non-summable residual hazard, and proposes STAR-Guard, a two-layer defense eliminating observed GHOST events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agents increasingly assist with complex problem-solving, but their extended interaction history introduces an underexplored execution-safety concern: an agent may execute an action that violates a safety constraint specified many turns earlier even under benign interaction conditions, potentially causing irreversible damage.

Method: The paper formalizes the failure mode as GHOST (Governance Hazard from Overlooked Safety Constraints across Turns), empirically measures its occurrence rate, and theoretically analyzes it by bounding the residual conditional violation hazard along each safe prefix. It then proposes STAR-Guard, a two-layer defense coupling historical semantic safety constraint restoration (to reduce unsafe proposals) with a deterministic pre-execution audit layer (to prevent residual violations from reaching the environment).

Result: GHOST events are not isolated: they occur at a rate of 11.5% on GPT-5.5 under benign interaction conditions. Theoretically, if the residual conditional violation hazard along each safe prefix is bounded below by a non-summable sequence, execution enters the hazard region almost surely. With STAR-Guard, no GHOST events were observed in the GPT-5.5 setup.

Conclusion: Neglecting safety constraints across turns is a systematic, quantifiably frequent safety hazard in long-horizon agents, but a two-layer defense combining historical constraint restoration with deterministic pre-execution auditing effectively eliminates the observed GHOST failures.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents are now playing an increasingly significant role in assisting humans with complex problem-solving. However, it is exactly their extended interaction history that introduces an underexplored execution-safety concern. Under benign interaction conditions, an agent may execute an action that violates a safety constraint specified many turns earlier. We term this failure mode Governance Hazard from Overlooked Safety Constraints across Turns (GHOST), which may cause irreversible damage. Our experiments reveal that GHOST events are not isolated cases: this failure mode, occurring precisely under benign interaction conditions, yields an occurrence rate of 11.5% on GPT-5.5. Furthermore, we theoretically show that if the residual conditional violation hazard along each safe prefix is bounded below by a non-summable sequence, the execution enters the hazard region almost surely. Leveraging this theoretical insight, we further propose STAR-Guard, a two-layer defense coupling historical semantic safety constraint restoration with pre-execution audit. STAR-Guard restores applicable safety constraints to reduce unsafe proposals, while its deterministic audit layer prevents residual violations from reaching the environment. Consistent with this two-layer design, we observe no GHOST events in our experiments under the GPT-5.5 setup.

</details>


### [39] [Spend Teacher Tokens Where They Matter: Success-Referenced On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02678)
*Xiang Chen,Futao Su,Kong Wang,Jiayi Chen,TanLin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SR-OPD cuts the teacher-input cost of on-policy distillation to 3.46-5.02% of Vanilla OPD in the one-pass setting while maintaining comparable reasoning performance, by supervising only selected prompts and failed rollouts using a successful student rollout as reference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation (OPD) combines student rollouts with dense token-level teacher supervision, but supervising every rollout incurs substantial teacher computation. This cost motivates a more selective allocation of teacher supervision.

Method: SR-OPD focuses on prompts where the student produces both successful and failed rollouts. For such prompts, a successful rollout acts as a natural reference, and failed rollouts are prioritized when their hidden-state trajectories show sustained divergence from this successful reference; estimated teacher-input cost is also accounted for in the selection.

Result: Across three teacher-student pairs and six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, SR-OPD uses only 3.46-5.02% of the teacher-input tokens required by Vanilla OPD in the one-pass setting while keeping comparable reasoning performance. Under a controlled 5% teacher-input budget, further experiments support both key design choices.

Conclusion: A student's own successful behavior can serve as a useful reference for allocating teacher supervision under a fixed teacher-input budget.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) combines student-generated rollouts with dense token-level supervision from a teacher, but providing such supervision for every rollout requires substantial teacher computation. We introduce Success-Referenced On-Policy Distillation (SR-OPD), which reduces this cost by selecting which prompts and rollouts receive teacher supervision. When the student produces both successful and failed rollouts for the same prompt, a successful rollout can serve as a natural reference for selecting failed rollouts. SR-OPD therefore focuses on such prompts and prioritizes failed rollouts whose hidden-state trajectories show sustained divergence from a successful reference, while accounting for estimated teacher-input cost. Across three teacher-student pairs and six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, SR-OPD uses only 3.46-5.02% of the teacher-input tokens required by Vanilla OPD in the one-pass setting while maintaining comparable reasoning performance. Under a controlled setting matched to 5% of Vanilla OPD's teacher-input budget, further experiments support both key design choices: focusing supervision on prompts with both successful and failed rollouts, and using successful rollouts to guide failure selection. These results indicate that a student's own successful behavior can serve as a useful reference for allocating teacher supervision under a fixed teacher-input budget.

</details>


### [40] [DataWeave: Deploying Human-LLM Analytics for Exploratory Structured Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02679)
*Raquib Bin Yousuf,Harith Laxman,Vitaliy Shkremetko,Eunice Son,Shambhavi Verma,Brian O'Leary,Venketesh Subramony,Sylvain Nazef,Jacquelyn Elias,Ron Coddington,Chris Contakes,Michael Riley,Naren Ramakrishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DataWeave is an LLM-powered system for data journalism that combines conversational interaction, schema grounding, analytical planning, and executable query generation, treating LLMs as steerable interactive partners rather than autonomous answer engines, validated via a case study with professional journalists on the IPEDS dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data journalism relies on the ability of reporters and investigative journalists to uncover trends, disparities, and accountability narratives in large structured datasets. However, exploring such datasets remains slow and brittle: journalists must navigate hundreds of variables across many datasets spanning years, understand coding conventions, and write non-trivial analysis code while hypotheses evolve. Although LLMs are often framed as "ask in English, get SQL/answers," real newsroom workflows expose recurring failures such as schema mismatches and drift, misread domain semantics and units, and silent assumptions.

Method: The authors present DataWeave, a system combining conversational interaction, schema grounding, analytical planning, and executable query generation to support exploratory analysis over structured data. Rather than treating LLMs as autonomous answer engines, DataWeave frames them as interactive partners whose outputs can be inspected, corrected, and steered as hypotheses shift. The authors conduct a case study with professional journalists applying the system to the U.S. Department of Education's Integrated Postsecondary Education Data System (IPEDS), a high-stakes public dataset with substantial domain semantics and frequent schema updates, and report how deployment experience and iterative refinement shaped the current architecture and analytical workflow.

Result: The case study with professional journalists analyzing the IPEDS dataset demonstrates DataWeave's ability to support exploratory structured-data analysis while mitigating LLM failure modes such as schema drift, misread semantics, and silent assumptions. Deployment experience and iterative refinement informed the final DataWeave architecture and its analytical workflow.

Conclusion: The paper distills design principles and deployment lessons for trustworthy human-LLM collaboration in structured data analysis, advocating for interactive, steerable LLM partnerships over autonomous answer generation in high-stakes journalistic workflows.

Abstract: Data journalism, the practice of using data analysis to surface newsworthy stories, depends increasingly on the ability of reporters and investigative journalists to uncover trends, disparities, and accountability narratives. In practice, exploring large structured datasets remains slow and brittle: journalists must navigate hundreds of variables across many datasets over years, understand data coding conventions, and write non-trivial analysis code while hypotheses evolve. Although LLMs are often touted as "ask in English, get SQL/answers," real newsroom workflows expose recurring failures, e.g., schema mismatches and drift, misread domain semantics and units, and silent assumptions. We present DataWeave, a system that addresses these needs by combining conversational interaction, schema grounding, analytical planning, and executable query generation to support exploratory analysis over structured data. Rather than treating LLMs as autonomous answer engines, DataWeave frames them as interactive partners whose outputs can be inspected, corrected, and steered as hypotheses shift. We present a case study with professional journalists using our system to analyze the U.S. Department of Education's Integrated Postsecondary Education Data System (IPEDS), a high-stakes public dataset with substantial domain semantics and frequent schema updates. We also report how deployment experience and iterative refinement shaped the current DataWeave architecture and its analytical workflow. Our findings distill design principles and deployment lessons for trustworthy human-LLM collaboration in structured data analysis.

</details>


### [41] [Large language models exhibit unreliable updating of clinical judgment as patient evidence evolves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02684)
*Min Zeng,Rui Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs often update clinical judgments unreliably as patient evidence evolves—conditioning on prior judgments raises prediction error, with asymmetric responses to worsening versus improving evidence and heavy anchoring on prior beliefs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used for clinical reasoning, yet it is unclear whether they appropriately revise judgments as patient evidence accumulates over time; longitudinal belief updating is underexplored as a dimension of reliability.

Method: Evaluated longitudinal belief updating using matched intensive-care trajectories from electronic health records across diverse LLMs. Used controlled interventions: holding preceding assessment fixed while varying worsening versus matched improving respiratory evidence (with headroom normalization), and holding current evidence fixed while varying prior risk (10%–90%). Tested prompting and a method called Evidence-Validated Longitudinal Update (EVLU) for fewer, more reliable revisions.

Result: Conditioning on a preceding judgment more often increased than reduced prediction error when estimates changed, replicated for a second endpoint. Models responded more strongly to worsening than to matched improving evidence, with asymmetry persisting after headroom normalization at moderate and strong evidence levels. Increasing prior risk from 10% to 90% shifted estimates by 26.2 percentage points, showing causal influence of prior model beliefs. Prompting did not restore reliable updating. EVLU reduced revisions but exposed a reliability-coverage trade-off.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit maladaptive longitudinal belief updating—anchoring on prior beliefs and asymmetric sensitivity to worsening evidence—and prompting fails to fix it. EVLU trades coverage for reliability, establishing longitudinal belief updating as a distinct dimension of LLM reliability.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for clinical reasoning, but whether they appropriately revise judgments as patient evidence evolves remains unclear. We evaluated longitudinal belief updating using matched intensive-care trajectories from electronic health records. Across diverse LLMs, conditioning on a preceding judgment more often increased than reduced prediction error when estimates changed, replicated for a second endpoint. Controlled interventions revealed two failure modes. First, with preceding assessment fixed, models responded more strongly to worsening than matched improving respiratory evidence; this asymmetry persisted after headroom normalization at moderate and strong evidence levels. Second, with current evidence fixed, increasing prior risk from 10% to 90% shifted estimates by 26.2 percentage points, demonstrating causal influence of prior model beliefs. Prompting did not restore reliable updating. Evidence-Validated Longitudinal Update (EVLU) identified fewer, more reliable revisions, revealing a reliability-coverage trade-off. These findings establish longitudinal belief updating as a distinct dimension of LLM reliability.

</details>


### [42] [Decoupling Memory from Context: Structured Memory for Token-Efficient Test-Time Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02687)
*Yehya Farhat,Michael Desmond,Anastasios Kyrillidis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formulates agent memory updates as context-optimization steps and proposes GraphMemory, a lightweight graph-based memory that retrieves only relevant subgraphs, achieving competitive performance with ~81-85% fewer memory-construction tokens than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs deployed in enterprise, scientific, and medical domains must incorporate domain-specific knowledge and adapt from experience. Context engineering avoids weight updates but online context adaptation is costly (trial-and-error) and queries are processed independently, so experience is not reused. Memory systems mitigate this, yet continually appending to a shared context causes growing token costs, context-window limits, and performance degradation.

Method: The authors introduce a unified formulation of context optimization, interpreting an agent memory system update as an optimization update procedure over the model's context, providing a principled framework for studying memory design and efficiency. They then propose GraphMemory, a lightweight graph-based memory that accumulates, refines, organizes, and connects reusable strategies, retrieving only the relevant subgraph per query for online context adaptation without exposing the model to the entire memory.

Result: Under bounded retrieval, retrieved memory remains constant as the number of processed examples grows. GraphMemory achieves competitive downstream performance while using approximately 81-85% fewer memory-construction tokens than the baselines.

Conclusion: Framing memory updates as context optimization yields a principled lens on memory design and efficiency, and GraphMemory demonstrates that graph-based, subgraph-retrieval memory can deliver competitive performance at substantially lower construction-token cost, with retrieval cost that stays bounded as experience accumulates.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in enterprise, scientific, and medical applications, where agents must incorporate domain-specific knowledge and adapt from experience. Context engineering offers a practical alternative to weight updates by improving model behavior through instructions, strategies, and evidence supplied at inference time. However, adapting context online typically requires a costly trial-and-error process, while queries are often processed independently, preventing useful experience from carrying forward. Memory systems address this limitation by retaining information across interactions, but approaches that continually append information to a shared context face increasing token costs, context-window limits, and performance degradation as the context expands. We introduce a unified formulation of context optimization and show that an agent memory system update can be interpreted as an optimization update procedure over the model's context. This perspective attempts to provide a principled framework for studying memory design and its efficiency. We then propose GraphMemory, a lightweight graph-based memory that accumulates, refines, organizes, and connects reusable strategies. For each query, GraphMemory retrieves only the relevant subgraph, enabling online context adaptation without exposing the model to the entire memory. Under bounded retrieval, the amount of retrieved memory remains constant as the number of processed examples grows. Experiments show that GraphMemory achieves competitive downstream performance while using approximately 81-85% fewer memory-construction tokens than our baselines.

</details>


### [43] [Learning to Revise Reasoning with Segment-wise On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02703)
*Yuxiang Zhang,Ding Cao,Shuting Cui,Lei Wang,Weijieying Ren,Tianxiang Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Seg-OPD introduces segment-wise on-policy distillation that learns reasoning revision by training students to prefer teacher redrafts over uncertain student segments, improving mathematical and programming reasoning accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Token-wise on-policy distillation (OPD) provides only token-level supervision and fails to offer a coherent alternative reasoning step showing how a student's step could be revised. Moreover, when the student produces a degenerate reasoning prefix, subsequent teacher supervision conditioned on that prefix may reinforce poor reasoning patterns.

Method: The authors perform controlled reasoning interventions, showing that replacing student segments with teacher redrafts improves subsequent reasoning accuracy. They then propose Segment-wise On-Policy Distillation (Seg-OPD), which selects student segments via an uncertainty metric, obtains corresponding teacher redrafts, and trains the student to prefer teacher redrafts over paired student segments while retaining dense token-wise OPD supervision.

Result: Extensive experiments on mathematical reasoning and competitive programming tasks show that Seg-OPD-trained students achieve higher revision success rates and consistently outperform state-of-the-art baselines in reasoning accuracy, with an average relative improvement of 5.22% across diverse models and tasks.

Conclusion: Learning explicit reasoning revision at the segment level, via teacher redrafts as supervision, is more effective than token-wise OPD alone and yields consistent gains in reasoning accuracy.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) improves large language model reasoning by training students on their own rollouts with dense token-wise supervision from the teacher. However, token-wise OPD does not explicitly provide a coherent alternative reasoning step showing how the student's step could be revised to improve subsequent reasoning. Furthermore, this paradigm can become less effective when the student produces a degenerate reasoning prefix, as subsequent teacher supervision remains conditioned on that prefix and may reinforce poor reasoning patterns. In this work, we focus on learning reasoning revision with segment-wise OPD to rework intermediate reasoning steps and better support subsequent reasoning. Through controlled reasoning interventions, we find that replacing student segments with teacher redrafts improves subsequent reasoning accuracy. Therefore, we address the problem of turning teacher redrafts into explicit supervision for learning to revise reasoning. We propose Segment-wise On-Policy Distillation (Seg-OPD), which selects student segments based on an uncertainty metric and obtains corresponding teacher redrafts. Seg-OPD trains the student to prefer teacher redrafts over their paired student segments while retaining dense token-wise OPD supervision. Extensive experiments on mathematical reasoning and competitive programming tasks show that Seg-OPD-trained students achieve higher revision success rates than baselines. Seg-OPD consistently outperforms the compared state-of-the-art baselines in reasoning accuracy with an average relative improvement of 5.22% across diverse models and tasks. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Seg-OPD.

</details>


### [44] [Label-Efficient Time Series Classification at Scale: A Dual-Stream OSSE-LSTM with Counterfactual Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02704)
*Nguyen Ho,Bach Tung Tran,Trung Ky Nguyen,Zhenchang Xia,Bolong Zheng,Long Van Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dual-Stream OSSE-LSTM is an episodic metric-learning framework combining an Omni-Scale CNN with Squeeze-and-Excitation and a BiLSTM, fused into a prototype embedding, and explained via Counterfactual Integrated Gradients; it dominates 19 UCR datasets across all support sizes with accuracy within a 0.36-point band.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series are generated at massive scale, but annotation is manual, costly, and expert-dependent, making label volume—not data volume—the binding constraint. Practitioners need to know how many labeled examples per class are required before a classifier becomes usable, so the paper targets the few-shot regime with a fixed, known label space.

Method: An episodic metric-learning framework with two independently normalized streams: an Omni-Scale CNN with Squeeze-and-Excitation recalibration for multi-scale motif extraction without per-dataset kernel tuning, and a Bidirectional LSTM for global temporal context; the streams are fused into a prototype-oriented embedding. Decisions are explained by Counterfactual Integrated Gradients, which attributes the prototype margin between target and opposing classes rather than an isolated logit, and the resulting maps are reused as soft masks for test-time prototype refinement without encoder updates.

Result: On 19 univariate UCR datasets, OSSE-LSTM achieves the highest average accuracy and per-dataset win count at every support size, with accuracy staying within a 0.36-point band (96.36–96.72%). Its weakest configuration still exceeds the best result of any compared baseline at any K (93.99%).

Conclusion: The method makes few-label time series classification accurate and stable across support sizes while remaining explainable, indicating that prototype-oriented, multi-scale episodic learning with counterfactual attribution can relax the label bottleneck in large-scale time series analytics.

Abstract: Time series are produced continuously at enormous scale by industrial equipment, wearables, power grids, and clinical monitors, yet annotation remains manual, expensive, and expert-dependent. The binding constraint in large-scale time series analytics is therefore not data volume but label volume, and the question facing a practitioner is concrete: how many examples per class must be labeled before a classifier becomes usable? We study this question directly, in a regime where the label space is fixed and known in advance and the decision rule must be constructed from only K labeled examples per class. We propose Dual-Stream OSSE-LSTM, an episodic metric-learning framework that pairs an Omni-Scale CNN with Squeeze-and-Excitation recalibration, for multi-scale motif extraction without per-dataset kernel tuning, with a Bidirectional LSTM for global temporal context. The two streams are independently normalized and fused into a prototype-oriented embedding. Because decisions taken from a few labels must also be explainable, we introduce Counterfactual Integrated Gradients (C-IG), which attributes the prototype margin between target and opposing classes rather than an isolated classifier logit, and reuses the resulting maps as soft masks for test-time prototype refinement without updating the encoder. On 19 univariate UCR datasets, OSSE-LSTM attains the highest average accuracy and per-dataset win count at every support size, and its accuracy remains within a 0.36-point band (96.36-96.72%) across that range. Its weakest configuration still exceeding the best result any compared baseline achieves at any K (93.99%).

</details>


### [45] [Ego2World: Compiling Egocentric Cooking Videos into Executable Worlds for Belief-State Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02715)
*Qinchuan Cheng,Zhantao Gong,Pengzhan Sun,Angela Yao,Shijie Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ego2World is a benchmark that compiles annotated egocentric cooking activities into executable, partially observable planning environments, enabling controlled evaluation of planners' actions, world states, and beliefs across 105 tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Egocentric videos capture everyday activities, but testing an agent requires evaluating the consequences of actions it chooses itself. Existing setups lack executable environments that support outcome-checking under partial observation.

Method: A compiler links source steps and objects to symbolic action rules, persistent world states, and explicit task conditions, so proposed actions can be executed and outcomes checked. World state and agent belief are maintained separately to enable controlled studies of planning and information reuse. Six planners are evaluated on 105 tasks, plus a paired Qwen-Plus study.

Result: Accepted operations often leave task goals unmet. Execution traces and condition checks distinguish interrupted runs, partial attainment, and completed execution without goal attainment. In the paired Qwen-Plus study, persistent belief improves action validity by 4.15 percentage points and reduces visual-query attempts by 90.27%, with higher token use and no detected completion gain.
