Conclusion: Ego2World provides a reusable testbed for tracing how planning and memory choices affect execution, observation demand, and task attainment, connecting recorded human activity to the development and evaluation of interactive agents.

Abstract: Egocentric videos capture how people carry out everyday activities, yet testing an agent requires evaluating the consequences of actions it chooses itself. We introduce Ego2World, a benchmark that turns annotated cooking activities into executable planning environments under partial observation. Its compiler links source steps and objects to symbolic action rules, persistent world states, and explicit task conditions, so researchers can execute an agent's proposed actions and check their outcomes. World state and agent belief are maintained separately, enabling controlled studies of planning and information reuse across continuing tasks. Evaluating six planners on 105 tasks shows that accepted operations often leave task goals unmet. Execution traces and condition checks distinguish interrupted runs, partial attainment, and completed execution without goal attainment. In a separate paired Qwen-Plus study, persistent belief improves action validity by 4.15 percentage points and reduces visual-query attempts by 90.27%, with higher token use and no detected completion gain. Ego2World provides a reusable testbed for tracing how planning and memory choices affect execution, observation demand, and task attainment, connecting recorded human activity to the development and evaluation of interactive agents.

</details>


### [46] [On the Chain-of-Thought Monitorability of Looped Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02741)
*Han Wang,Ishwar B Balappanawar,Huan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper provides the first systematic evaluation of chain-of-thought (CoT) monitorability in looped language models (LoopLMs), finding task-dependent, stress-test-specific reductions in monitorability at deeper loop depths, but no evidence that looped architectures are systematically less monitorable than non-looped models matched on size or depth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CoT monitoring is a promising method for detecting undesirable model behavior, and LoopLMs add latent recurrent computation without growing model size, which could in principle alter how faithfully the CoT reflects internal computation. Whether looped architectures degrade CoT monitorability was previously unexplored, motivating a systematic investigation.

Method: The authors run two complementary evaluations on eight MonitorBench tasks under standard and stress-test settings: (1) varying loop depth within the same LoopLM family to isolate the effect of recurrent computation, and (2) comparing LoopLMs against non-looped LMs matched by parameter size, transformer-layer count, or effective depth. They further diagnose potential confounds (task difficulty, verification pass rate, generated token length) and analyze qualitative examples of cue usage and attribution.

Result: Stress tests reveal task-dependent reductions in CoT monitorability on specific Logic/Science/Engineering Cue Answer tasks, while other tasks show weaker or qualitatively different trends. These declines are not fully explained by task difficulty, verification pass rate, or output length; qualitative examples suggest deeper-loop models change how they explicitly use or attribute provided cues. The cross-model comparison finds no evidence that LoopLMs are systematically less monitorable than non-looped models matched on size or depth.

Conclusion: Deeper loop depth can reduce CoT monitorability on some tasks under stress tests, but the looped transformer architecture alone does not necessarily imply lower monitorability. The effect is task- and setting-dependent rather than an inherent property of looping.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring provides a promising approach for detecting undesirable model behavior. Looped language models (LoopLMs) repeatedly apply shared transformer layers, increasing effective computational depth and enabling additional latent computation without increasing model size. However, the effect of looped architectures on CoT monitorability remains largely unexplored. In this work, we provide the first systematic evaluation of CoT monitorability in LoopLMs. We study two complementary settings: (1) varying the loop depth within the same LoopLM family to isolate the effect of additional recurrent computation, and (2) comparing LoopLMs with non-looped language models matched by parameter size, transformer-layer count, or effective depth to study whether LoopLMs are less monitorable. Across eight tasks from MonitorBench and both standard and stress-test settings, we observe task-dependent reductions in CoT monitorability under stress tests on specific Logic/Science/Engineering \texttt{Cue Answer} tasks, while other tasks exhibit weaker or qualitatively different trends. Our diagnosis suggests that these declines are not fully explained by task difficulty, verification pass rate, or generated token length; qualitative examples further suggest changes in how deeper-loop models explicitly use or attribute provided cues. Our cross-model comparison finds no evidence that LoopLMs are systematically less monitorable than non-looped language models matched on size or depth. Overall, our results suggest that deeper loop depth can reduce CoT monitorability in some tasks under stress tests, but looped transformer architecture alone does not necessarily imply lower monitorability.

</details>


### [47] [Dynamic LLM Routers are Often Misguided](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02762)
*Sam Wang,Julia White,Sahibzada Allahyar,Dhruv Atreja,Urchade Zaratiana,Kelton Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Six commercial LLM routers are analyzed across 14 settings and eight task categories; none beats a random router that picks between two well-chosen models at matched cost, and the failures trace to four systemic patterns rewarded by the standard cost-accuracy Pareto objective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic LLM routers are promoted as a way to reduce inference cost by assigning each query to the cheapest model that can answer it correctly, so their real-world effectiveness needs rigorous, comparative scrutiny.

Method: Benchmark six commercial routers across 14 settings on a diverse benchmark spanning eight task categories, compare them against a matched-cost random router over two well-chosen models, and diagnose failure modes by linking them to properties of the standard cost-accuracy Pareto-efficiency objective; then test the model-granularity and model-specialization assumptions and propose an alternative evaluation methodology.

Result: None of the six routers outperforms the matched-cost random two-model router, with some trailing by over 10 percentage points. Four recurring patterns emerge: difficulty blindness, length reversal, semantic matching, and roster suboptimality. The first three are directly incentivized by the standard objective, while the two assumptions that would justify large rosters—model granularity and model specialization—do not hold empirically.

Conclusion: The standard cost-accuracy Pareto evaluation rewards degenerate routing behaviors and should be replaced by an alternative methodology that does not. A proof-of-concept two-model router avoiding all four patterns still yields only limited gains over random routing, since a well-chosen roster leaves little to route.

Abstract: Dynamic LLM routers promise to cut inference costs by sending each query to the cheapest model that can answer it correctly. We analyze six commercial routers across 14 settings on a diverse benchmark spanning eight task categories, finding that none of them outperforms a router that randomly selects between two well-chosen models at matched cost. Some underperform by more than 10 percentage points. We trace this gap to four patterns prevalent across routers: difficulty blindness, length reversal, semantic matching, and roster suboptimality. We show that the first three are what the standard objective rewards: cost-accuracy Pareto efficiency on realized costs favors escalating moderately hard queries over the hardest ones, shorter queries over longer ones, and routing by a query's source over its difficulty. We also argue that the two assumptions that would justify large rosters, model granularity and model specialization, do not hold empirically. We propose an alternative evaluation methodology that does not reward these patterns, and as a proof of concept, we design a simple two-model router that avoids all four. Nevertheless, its gain over random routing is limited, because a well-chosen roster leaves little to route.

</details>


### [48] [Law And Order: Tax Law Autoformalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02792)
*Sophia Simeng Han,Yoshiki Takashima,Anjiang Wei,Zhaoyu Li,Michael Genesereth*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Law&Order, a neuro-symbolic framework, formalizes tax forms and instructions into executable symbolic programs by combining LLM synthesis with cell-level verification and localized error repair, achieving 100% cell-level and form-level accuracy on 51 held-out returns versus 66% for the best pure LLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Legal systems are increasingly software-implemented, but scalable methods for translating legal texts into accurate symbolic representations remain underdeveloped. Tax law is a demanding testbed because forms and filing instructions encode large computational structures with arithmetic, branching, recursion, and tabular reasoning.

Method: The authors propose Law&Order, a neuro-symbolic framework that automatically formalizes tax forms and instructions into executable symbolic programs. It establishes two correspondences between law and logic: structural correspondence (aligning legal and symbolic components such as cells and schedules) and denotational correspondence (requiring symbolic components to implement the computations specified by their legal counterparts). The system combines LLM synthesis with cell-level verification and iterative localized error repair, using human-written OpenTaxSolver tax returns.

Result: Evaluation on independently authored, held-out TaxCalcBench returns, never exposed during generation or repair, shows that Law&Order achieves 100% cell-level and form-level accuracy on 51 held-out returns, while the most advanced LLM alone achieves only 66% accuracy.

Conclusion: Combining LLM-based synthesis with symbolic verification enables scalable, verifiable large-scale legal autoformalization, substantially outperforming LLM-only approaches.

Abstract: Legal systems are increasingly implemented through software, yet scalable methods for translating legal texts into accurate symbolic representations remain underdeveloped. We study this problem through tax law, where forms and filing instructions define large computational structures involving arithmetic, branching, recursion, and tabular reasoning. We propose Law&Order, a neuro-symbolic framework for automatically formalizing tax forms and instructions into executable symbolic programs. Our approach establishes two forms of correspondence between law and logic: structural correspondence, which aligns legal and symbolic components such as cells and schedules, and denotational correspondence, which requires symbolic components to implement the computations specified by their legal counterparts. We combine large language model synthesis with cell-level verification and iterative localized error repair using human-written OpenTaxSolver tax returns. We then evaluate the resulting formalizations on independently authored, held-out TaxCalcBench returns, that are never exposed during generation or repair. Although the most advanced LLM achieves only 66% accuracy, Law&Order achieves 100% cell-level and form-level accuracy on 51 held-out returns, demonstrating the effectiveness of combining LLM-based synthesis with symbolic verification for scalable and verifiable large-scale legal autoformalization compared with using an LLM alone.

</details>


### [49] [PAPER2LLM++: Continual Self-Evolution of LLMs from Research Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02793)
*Hongji Pu,Yilun Zhao,Wenpeng Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PAPER2LLM++ is a framework that enables LLMs to continually self-evolve by treating research papers as a stream of evidence and supervision rather than static retrievable knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research continually uncovers LLM limitations, causes, and solutions, but these human discoveries remain disconnected from model evolution. An LLM does not automatically learn from new research about its own failures, leaving a gap between human discovery and model improvement.

Method: For each incoming paper, PAPER2LLM++ extracts evidence-grounded findings, tests whether the reported limitation persists in the current model, and, when needed, converts the findings into candidate learning signals. A try-evaluate-commit procedure integrates an update only when it improves the targeted behavior without substantially forgetting prior improvements or degrading general capabilities.

Result: Across a sequential stream of research-discovered LLM failures, models progressively incorporate new findings while retaining earlier gains.

Conclusion: PAPER2LLM++ takes a step toward closing the loop between human discovery and model evolution, enabling models to continually learn from research about their own limitations and improvements.

Abstract: Research on LLMs continually uncovers model limitations, their causes, and potential solutions. Yet these human discoveries remain largely disconnected from model evolution: an LLM does not automatically learn from new research about its own failures. We introduce PAPER2LLM++, a framework for continual self-evolution of LLMs from research papers. Rather than treating papers merely as knowledge to retrieve, PAPER2LLM++ uses the growing literature as a stream of evidence and supervision for model improvement. For each incoming paper, it extracts evidence-grounded findings, tests whether the reported limitation persists in the current model, and, when needed, converts the findings into candidate learning signals. A try-evaluate-commit procedure integrates an update only when it improves the targeted behavior without substantially forgetting prior improvements or degrading general capabilities. Across a sequential stream of research-discovered LLM failures, we show that models can progressively incorporate new findings while retaining earlier gains. PAPER2LLM++ thus takes a step toward closing the loop between human discovery and model evolution, enabling models to continually learn from research about their own limitations and improvements.

</details>


### [50] [Modeling Shared and Individual Structure for Cross-Subject Continuous Affect Regression from EEG-fNIRS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02796)
*Xuan Wang,Bing Wang,Shuai Chang,Hao Yuan,Xinbo Qi,Xinyue Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses zero-shot cross-subject continuous valence–arousal estimation from synchronized EEG-fNIRS, decomposing affect trajectories into a stimulus-shared component and a per-subject individual component calibrated by a label-free alpha-band cross-channel synchrony marker, achieving MAE of 25.96/22.80 versus baseline scores of 60.6 and 55.0.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior continuous valence–arousal estimation from physiological signals is predominantly studied in a subject-dependent setting, where the model is trained and evaluated on labeled data from the same individual. This limits real-world applicability. The harder zero-shot cross-subject variant—predicting raw-scale valence and arousal trajectories for unseen subjects whose labels are never observed—remains underexplored.

Method: Using a synchronized EEG-fNIRS dataset, the authors predict raw-scale ([1, 255]) valence and arousal trajectories for unseen subjects while watching the same video stimuli as a disjoint training set. They decompose the affect trajectory into (1) a structure shared across subjects viewing the same stimuli, and (2) an individual structure estimated per test subject from a label-free EEG marker (alpha-band cross-channel synchrony), which rescales the shared trajectory around the scale midpoint. The calibration mechanism is validated on four axes: leave-one-subject-out correlation between the marker and each subject's true optimal gain, functional-form comparison against non-linear alternatives, repeated leave-4-out component ablation, and a ceiling analysis of remaining headroom for per-subject scaling.

Result: On held-out subjects, the model achieves an overall MAE of 25.96/22.80 across two evaluation batches (valence 21.94/19.6, arousal 29.98/26.0), substantially below EEGNet and ASAC-Net baselines for the same subject-independent split (raw scale scores 60.6 and 55.0). A systematic negative-result search across architectures, feature representations, and prediction targets found no signal improving on the single alpha-synchrony marker.

Conclusion: Zero-shot cross-subject valence–arousal estimation can be made tractable by separating a stimulus-shared trajectory from a per-subject scaling component driven by a single label-free alpha-synchrony EEG marker. The mechanism is validated across multiple independent axes, and the negative-result search suggests the alpha-synchrony marker is a robust, hard-to-improve calibration signal for this task.

Abstract: Continuous, second-by-second valence-arousal estimation from physiological signals is typically studied in a subject-dependent setting, where the model sees labeled data from the same person it is later evaluated on. We study the harder zero-shot cross-subject variant on a synchronized EEG-fNIRS dataset: predict raw-scale ([1, 255]) valence and arousal trajectories for subjects whose labels the model never observes, given only their unlabeled EEG/fNIRS recordings while watching the same video stimuli as a disjoint set of training subjects. We decompose the affect trajectory into a structure shared across subjects who watch the same stimuli and an individual structure estimated for each test subject from a label-free EEG marker (alpha-band cross-channel synchrony), which rescales the shared trajectory around the scale midpoint. We validate the per-subject calibration mechanism on four independent axes: leave-one-subject-out correlation between the marker and each subject's true optimal gain, a functional-form comparison against non-linear alternatives, a repeated leave-4-out component ablation isolating each part of the pipeline's contribution, and a ceiling analysis bounding the remaining headroom for per-subject scaling. On held-out subjects, the model reaches an overall MAE of 25.96 / 22.80 across two evaluation batches (valence 21.94 / 19.6, arousal 29.98 / 26.0), well below EEGNet and ASAC-Net baselines reported for the same subject-independent split (raw scale score 60.6 and 55.0 respectively). We further report a systematic negative-result search across model architectures, feature representations, and prediction targets that found no signal able to improve on the single alpha-synchrony marker.

</details>


### [51] [BitNest: Bit-Nested Speculative Decoding for Memory-Efficient LLM Inference Acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02800)
*Chence Yang,Ningxi Cheng,Arash Akbari,Qitao Tan,Qingchan Zhu,Ci Zhang,Changdi Yang,Yanzhi Wang,Wei Niu,Jinhui Wang,Jin Lu,Geng Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BitNest is a bit-nested speculative decoding framework that embeds a low-precision draft directly within a higher-precision target model by sharing a single physical weight representation, achieving high acceptance rates and speedups without extra memory overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing speculative decoding methods typically require an additional draft model or weight representation, causing non-negligible memory overhead on resource-constrained devices. Self-speculative approaches reduce this overhead but still face trade-offs among draft quality, target quality, and storage efficiency.

Method: BitNest embeds a low-precision draft into the higher-precision target representation. Instead of deriving the draft from a predefined target, it first constructs a strong low-precision base and then recovers the higher-precision target through residual refinement, letting both models share one physical weight representation. This progressive-precision design is also extended to the KV cache for long-context inference.

Result: Across multiple 7B--8B edge-friendly LLMs and diverse workloads, BitNest reaches an average speculative acceptance rate of 95.2%, closely preserves higher-precision model quality, and delivers 1.48--1.61x end-to-end speedup over FP16 autoregressive decoding. On LLaMA models supported by all representative self-speculative baselines, BitNest achieves consistently competitive or higher decoding speedup.

Conclusion: BitNest provides an efficient bit-nested speculative decoding framework that avoids additional draft-model memory overhead while maintaining target quality and improving decoding speed, making it well suited for resource-constrained edge deployment.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by using a lightweight draft to propose multiple tokens for parallel verification. However, existing methods often require an additional draft model or weight representation, introducing non-negligible memory overhead on resource-constrained devices. Self-speculative approaches reduce this overhead, yet still face trade-offs between draft quality, target quality, and storage efficiency. We propose BitNest, a bit-nested speculative decoding framework that embeds a low-precision draft directly into the higher-precision target representation. Instead of deriving a draft from a predefined target, BitNest first constructs a strong low-precision base and then recovers the higher-precision target through residual refinement, enabling both models to share a single physical weight representation. BitNest further extends this progressive-precision design to the KV cache for long-context inference. Across multiple 7B--8B edge-friendly LLMs and diverse workloads, BitNest achieves an average speculative acceptance rate of 95.2% while closely preserving higher-precision model quality, and delivers 1.48--1.61x end-to-end speedup over FP16 autoregressive decoding. On the LLaMA models supported by all representative self-speculative baselines, BitNest also achieves consistently competitive or higher decoding speedup.

</details>


### [52] [VIGOR: Zero-Shot Visual Generalization via Latent-Space Consistency in Model-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02801)
*Mingyu Park,Samyeul Noh,Hyun Myung,Donghwan Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VIGOR is a framework that achieves zero-shot generalization to unseen visual distractions in model-based RL by enforcing augmentation-invariant latent dynamics and stabilized encoders, outperforming baselines by 3.4% (DMC) and 43.6% (Robosuite).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MBRL is sample-efficient via latent planning, but degrades under unseen visual distractions. Unlike model-free RL where encoder perturbations affect only single-step predictions, MBRL has a two-level vulnerability: distractions push encoder outputs out of distribution, and these errors compound through recursive latent rollouts over the planning horizon.

Method: VIGOR integrates three interdependent components: (i) asymmetric weak-to-strong augmentation pairing weak-only and weak-to-strong latent views within a single batch; (ii) dynamics-level consistency enforcing augmentation-invariant transition predictions via direct latent regression; and (iii) encoder-level stabilization preventing encoder drift under the cross-augmentation supervision imposed by dynamics-level consistency.

Result: On DeepMind Control Suite (DMC) and Robosuite, VIGOR outperforms state-of-the-art model-free and model-based baselines, surpassing the second-best by 3.4% on DMC and 43.6% on Robosuite. Ablations show robustness is augmentation-agnostic: replacing the default augmentation with alternatives from distinct perturbation families preserves strong generalization, confirming latent-space consistency rather than augmentation choice drives robustness.

Conclusion: VIGOR enables zero-shot visual generalization in MBRL while retaining its backbone's sample efficiency. Its robustness stems from latent-space consistency rather than the specific augmentation choice, demonstrating an augmentation-agnostic solution to the compounding error problem in recursive latent planning.

Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) achieves strong sample efficiency by planning within learned latent dynamics, yet its performance degrades substantially under unseen visual distractions such as background variations, lighting changes, or camera shifts. Unlike model-free RL, where encoder perturbations affect only single-step predictions, MBRL suffers from a two-level vulnerability: visual distractions first push encoder outputs out of distribution, and these errors then compound through recursive latent rollouts over the planning horizon. We propose visual generalization via latent-space consistency in model-based RL (VIGOR), a framework that enables zero-shot generalization to unseen visual distractions while retaining the sample efficiency of its MBRL backbone. VIGOR integrates three interdependent components: (i) asymmetric weak-to-strong augmentation, which pairs weak-only and weak-to-strong latent views within a single batch; (ii) dynamics-level consistency, which enforces augmentation-invariant transition predictions through direct latent regression; and (iii) encoder-level stabilization, which prevents encoder drift under the cross-augmentation supervision imposed by dynamics-level consistency. Evaluations on the DeepMind Control Suite (DMC) and Robosuite show that VIGOR outperforms state-of-the-art model-free and model-based baselines, surpassing the second-best baseline by 3.4% on DMC and 43.6% on Robosuite. Ablations further show that VIGOR's robustness is augmentation-agnostic: replacing the default augmentation with alternatives from distinct perturbation families preserves strong generalization, confirming that latent-space consistency, not the augmentation choice, drives robustness.

</details>


### [53] [ROUTEAUDIT: Interaction-Aware Identification for Budgeted Multi-Verifier Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02808)
*Miaobo Hu,Shuhao Hu,Xiaobo Guo,Xin Wang,Bokun Wang,Tianshu Fu,Daren Zha,Jun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reformulates adaptive multi-verifier routing as a contract-conditioned identification problem and introduces ROUTEAUDIT, a protocol that yields matched route contrasts, path-sensitive attribution via a contract lattice, policy-independent response tapes for paired sequential contrasts, and sharp finite-population bounds under incomplete matching, returning an attribution certificate per comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Endpoint quality-cost gaps are commonly used to compare adaptive multi-verifier systems, but these comparisons are confounded when the verifier catalog, availability, accounting, information filtration, or scorer change together with the policy, so apparent gains cannot be cleanly credited to the policy.

Method: Verifier routing is cast as contract-conditioned identification, where the contract fixes request support, verifier catalog, realized availability, resource accounting, online filtration, and post-trace scoring so that a matched route contrast varies only the policy coordinate. ROUTEAUDIT adds a contract lattice that averages coordinate increments over admissible bridge orders and reports path sensitivity, a policy-independent response tape for paired sequential contrasts, and request-level bounds with a sharp finite-population interval for incomplete matching. The protocol commits paid observations and ledger events before the oracle join and issues an attribution certificate per comparison.

Result: On two held-out raw-tail caches, matched static SF+SA equals the cascade, attributing apparent gains of 0.1797 and 0.1250 over full static to the verifier-set edge. On 1,319 held-out task requests, learned and RLVR studies attain quality 0.9522 and 0.9553 versus 0.9484 for matched static; the RLVR-static paired difference is +0.0068 with a request-paired interval [0.0015, 0.0122] and a training-seed-by-request hierarchical interval [0.0006, 0.0131]. Controlled attribution recovery gives route mean absolute error 0.0011 and endpoint reconstruction error 0.0004.

Conclusion: Factorial, bridge-order, and stochastic-provider studies validate the certificate interface, while RLVR provides a learned-policy stress test under the same identification contract, showing that ROUTEAUDIT yields interpretable, path-sensitive attributions and sharp bounds for matched verifier-routing comparisons.

Abstract: Adaptive multi-verifier systems are commonly compared through endpoint quality-cost gaps, even when the verifier catalog, availability, accounting, information filtration, or scorer changes with the policy. We formulate verifier routing as a contract-conditioned identification problem. The contract records request support, verifier catalog, realized availability, resource accounting, online filtration, and post-trace scoring; a matched route contrast changes only the policy coordinate. ROUTEAUDIT adds three measurable objects to this contract. A contract lattice averages coordinate increments over every admissible bridge order and reports the resulting attribution together with its path sensitivity. A policy-independent response tape identifies paired sequential contrasts when adaptive policies reveal different observations. For incomplete matching, request-level bounds use whichever potential outcome remains observed and give a sharp finite-population interval. The protocol commits paid observations and ledger events before the oracle join and returns an attribution certificate for each comparison. On two held-out raw-tail caches, matched static SF+SA equals the cascade, assigning the apparent gains of 0.1797 and 0.1250 over full static to the verifier-set edge. On 1,319 held-out task requests, the learned and RLVR studies report quality 0.9522 and 0.9553 versus 0.9484 for matched static; the RLVR-static paired difference is +0.0068 with a request-paired interval $[0.0015,0.0122]$ and a training-seed-by-request hierarchical interval $[0.0006,0.0131]$. Controlled attribution recovery yields route mean absolute error 0.0011 and endpoint reconstruction error 0.0004. Factorial, bridge-order, and stochastic-provider studies evaluate the certificate interface; RLVR supplies a learned-policy stress test under the same identification contract.

</details>


### [54] [iS-KV: Online Low-Rank KV Cache Compression via Block-Incremental SVD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02815)
*Yiren Zhao,Guanghui Song,Tianrui Qin,Kejiang Ye,Cheng-zhong Xu,Xitong Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: iS-KV is an online low-rank KV-cache compression method for long-horizon reasoning that keeps a recent exact window and incrementally folds older states into bounded-rank representations, synchronizing historical coordinates as the basis evolves.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long chain-of-thought reasoning causes KV-cache memory to grow linearly with decoding length. Existing token-eviction methods irreversibly delete historical states that later reasoning may need to revisit, while SVD-based low-rank compression is hard to extend from a fixed prompt cache to online decoding because updating the basis for new tokens while old tokens keep old coordinates causes substantial drift in stored history.

Method: iS-KV performs online low-rank KV-cache compression by keeping a recent window exact and incrementally folding older states into bounded-rank representations. As the low-rank basis evolves for new tokens, it synchronizes historical coordinates with the updated basis to maintain representation consistency.

Result: On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, iS-KV attains 82.6% accuracy at 4.06-fold persistent-KV compression, close to the original 83.6%. On Qwen3-8B, it reaches 89.2% accuracy at 5.64-fold compression. Under matched memory budgets, iS-KV consistently outperforms token-eviction baselines.

Conclusion: iS-KV enables effective online low-rank KV-cache compression for long-horizon reasoning, preserving accuracy at substantial compression ratios and surpassing token-eviction baselines under equal memory budgets.

Abstract: Long chain-of-thought reasoning substantially increases KV-cache memory during autoregressive decoding, as every generated token introduces new key and value states and causes the cache to grow linearly with decoding length. Existing KV-cache compression methods typically control this growth through token eviction, but irreversible deletion can remove historical states that later reasoning may need to revisit. SVD-based low-rank compression provides an alternative by retaining all positions with a more compact representation. However, extending it from a fixed prompt cache to online decoding is non-trivial. Through our investigation, we find that if the basis is updated for new tokens while old tokens keep their coordinates in the old basis, the stored history drifts substantially. Based on this observation, we propose iS-KV, an online low-rank KV-cache compression method for long-horizon reasoning. iS-KV keeps a recent window exact while incrementally folding older states into bounded-rank representations. As the low-rank basis evolves, it synchronizes historical coordinates with the updated basis to maintain representation consistency. On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, iS-KV achieves 82.6% accuracy at 4.06-fold persistent-KV compression, close to the original model's 83.6%. On Qwen3-8B, it achieves 89.2% accuracy at 5.64-fold compression. Under matched memory budgets, iS-KV consistently outperforms token-eviction baselines.

</details>


### [55] [MetaRubric: Learning to Reward for Rubric-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02824)
*Yuxuan Fan,Jaehong Yoon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MetaRubric fixes "Vacuous Credit" in rubric-based RL by only awarding criterion credit when the response contains sufficient supporting evidence, alternating evidence-aware policy optimization with response-guided rubric adaptation, and improves medical benchmark accuracy substantially over static-judge GRPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rubric-based reinforcement learning applies reward-driven optimization to open-ended tasks by giving partial credit per requirement. However, rubric judges can assign high criterion scores even when the required information or action is absent, a failure mode called Vacuous Credit. These awards persist even after required information is removed and can reverse the sign of a response's GRPO advantage, undermining optimization.

Method: MetaRubric alternates evidence-aware policy optimization with response-guided rubric adaptation. It builds counterfactual counterparts by changing one task-relevant fact per prompt. During policy optimization, credit is assigned only if the response contains sufficient evidence for the required criterion. After each optimization stage, current policy responses guide revisions of original and counterfactual criteria while preserving the meaning of the original prompt's initial rubric under each prompt's facts. Criterion weights are also adapted at stage boundaries to better target observed policy errors.

Result: Across multiple backbones, MetaRubric improves PubMedQA accuracy by 6.00--20.40 percentage points over static-judge GRPO, with additional gains on HealthBench-Hard and two multimodal medical benchmarks.

Conclusion: Grounding rubric credit in explicit response evidence and iteratively adapting rubrics and weights mitigates Vacuous Credit, yielding consistent and sizable improvements in rubric-based RL for medical open-ended tasks across text and multimodal benchmarks.

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning extends reward-driven optimization to open-ended tasks by assigning partial credit to individual response requirements. However, rubric judges can assign a high criterion score even when the information or action it requires is absent from the response, a failure mode we term Vacuous Credit. Such awards persist after the required information is removed and can reverse the sign of a response's GRPO advantage. To address this problem, we introduce MetaRubric, which alternates evidence-aware policy optimization with response-guided rubric adaptation. We construct counterfactual counterparts by changing one task-relevant fact in each prompt. During policy optimization, credit is assigned only when the response contains sufficient evidence to satisfy the required rubric criterion. After each policy-optimization stage, current policy responses guide revisions to original and counterfactual criteria while preserving the meaning of the original prompt's initial rubric as interpreted under each prompt's facts. We also adapt criterion weights at stage boundaries to better address observed policy errors. Across multiple backbones, MetaRubric improves PubMedQA accuracy by 6.00--20.40 percentage points over static-judge GRPO, with further gains on HealthBench-Hard and two multimodal medical benchmarks.

</details>


### [56] [Scaling Trajectories for Complex Tasks through Recursive Self-Rewrite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02826)
*Zongxia Li,Yucheng Shi,Zhongzhi Li,Junyao Yang,Ruhan Wang,Chengsong Huang,Fuxiao Liu,Haitao Mi,Jordan Boyd-Graber,LeoweiLiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Recursive Self-Rewrite (RSR) reconstructs successful trajectories found under specialized harnesses into training data usable under a general harness, improving terminal-task performance of Qwen-3.8-27B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Specialized harnesses aid task success but involve interventions unavailable at deployment. Their successful trajectories are valuable supervision yet not directly reusable under a general harness, motivating a method to convert harness-assisted experiences into generalizable capabilities for one base model.

Method: One base model (Qwen-3.8-27B) discovers solutions under diverse harnesses. A planner extracts procedures into runbooks; a critic screens for verifier and solution leakage and guides recursive revision; an executor follows qualified runbooks in fresh sandboxes. Source trajectories from ~3K self-curated terminal tasks are expanded into rewritten SFT trajectories; training compares against base model and direct trajectory SFT.

Result: Three harnesses jointly solve 759 tasks (+34.3% vs strongest individual harness). 2,001 successful source trajectories become 11,094 rewritten trajectories. Pass@3 gains: Terminal-Bench 2 57.0%→74.2%; Terminal-Bench 4 1.5%→9.1%; Terminal-Bench Hard 39.0%→63.0%; Software Terminal-Bench 3.0%→6.0%. Process reward on Long-Horizon Terminal-Bench rises 0.21→0.29.

Conclusion: Diverse harness-assisted experiences can be reconstructed into reusable capabilities for a single model operating under a general harness, outperforming base model and direct trajectory SFT.

Abstract: Successful trajectories on difficult tasks provide valuable supervision for model improvement, but specialized harnesses introduce interventions that may be unavailable during deployment. We propose Recursive Self-Rewrite (RSR), a framework that uses one base model, Qwen-3.8-27B, to discover successful solutions under diverse harnesses and reconstruct them as training trajectories under a general harness. A planner extracts procedures into runbooks, a critic screens for verifier and solution leakage and guides recursive revision, and an executor follows qualified runbooks in fresh sandboxes. Across approximately 3K self-curated terminal tasks, three harnesses jointly solve 759 tasks, 34.3% more than the strongest individual harness in the recorded pool. RSR expands 2,001 successful source trajectories into 11,094 rewritten trajectories for supervised finetuning. Training on these trajectories outperforms both the base model and direct trajectory SFT. Compared with the base model, pass@3 increases from 57.0% to 74.2% on Terminal-Bench 2, from 1.5% to 9.1% on Terminal-Bench 4, from 39.0% to 63.0% on our self-curated Terminal-Bench Hard, and from 3.0% to 6.0% on our Software Terminal-Bench. Process reward on Long-Horizon Terminal-Bench rises from 0.21 to 0.29. These results show how diverse harness-assisted experiences can be reconstructed into reusable capabilities for a model operating under a general harness.

</details>


### [57] [MLCommons Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02827)
*Carsten Maple,Cagatay Yucel,Isaac Holeman,Chris Knotz,Peter Mattson,James Goel,Jonathan Petit,Sean McGregor,James Ezick,Abhishek Kumar,Alicia Parrish,Murali Emani,Kashyap Iyer,Faiza Khan Khattak,Washington Mbonu,Daniel Machlab,Eileen Long,Shaona Ghosh,Jibin Varghese,Roman Lutz,Andrew Gruen,Bennett Hillenbrand,Prabal Gupta,Mohammed Serrhini,Dhivya Nagasubramanian,Aakash Gupta,Jun,Lu,Kurt Bollacker,Chang Liu,Jonathan Petit,Cong Chen,Jean-Philippe Monteuuis,Brent Miller,Apurv Verma,Roman Eng,Armstrong Foundjem,Mohammed Serrhini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MLCommons Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0 is an end-to-end pipeline for evaluating LLM robustness to single-turn text-based jailbreak attacks, tested on eight open-weight systems across 264 prompts and eleven hazard categories, revealing a 7.57% average Resilience Gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern AI systems are designed to refuse hazardous requests, but jailbreak prompts can bypass these safeguards and elicit normally-refused outputs. There is a need for a reproducible, standardized methodology to evaluate and compare the robustness of large language models against such attacks.

Method: The benchmark integrates criteria-driven system and attack selection, paired baseline and adversarial evaluation, human annotation, automated evaluator calibration, scoring, grading, and risk-calibrated disclosure into a single pipeline. It evaluates eight open-weight systems using 264 seed prompts spanning eleven hazard categories and representative attacks from the MLCommons Jailbreak Taxonomy. Responses are assessed via the AILuminate Assessment Standard v1.4, and robustness is quantified through the Resilience Gap—the change in safety performance between baseline and adversarial conditions.

Result: Across all evaluated systems and attacks, the unsafe-response rate rose from 11.08% (baseline) to 18.65% (jailbreak), yielding an average Resilience Gap of 7.57%. Accessible systems exhibited a larger mean gap, and attack effectiveness varied substantially across attack categories and hazards. The study also examines evaluator reliability and sources of measurement error.

Conclusion: Beyond reporting results, Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0 establishes a reproducible methodological foundation for comparative jailbreak evaluation and future expansion across systems, attacks, hazards, and evaluation methods.

Abstract: Modern AI systems are designed to refuse hazardous requests. A jailbreak is a prompt crafted to bypass those safeguards and elicit outputs that the system would normally refuse to provide. The MLCommons Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0 provides an end-to-end methodology for evaluating the robustness of large language models to single-turn, text-based jailbreak attacks. It combines criteria-driven system and attack selection, paired baseline and adversarial evaluation, human annotation, automated evaluator calibration, scoring, grading, and risk-calibrated disclosure within a single benchmarking pipeline. The benchmark evaluates eight open-weight systems using 264 seed prompts spanning eleven hazard categories and representative attacks drawn from the MLCommons Jailbreak Taxonomy. Responses are assessed using the AILuminate Assessment Standard v1.4, and robustness is measured through the Resilience Gap: the change in safety performance between baseline and adversarial conditions. Across all evaluated systems and attacks, the unsafe-response rate increased from 11.08% under baseline conditions to 18.65% under jailbreak conditions, producing an average Resilience Gap of 7.57%. Accessible systems showed a larger mean gap, while attack effectiveness varied substantially across attack categories and hazards. The benchmark also examines evaluator reliability and sources of measurement error. Beyond reporting results, Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0 establishes a reproducible methodological foundation for comparative jailbreak evaluation and for future expansion across systems, attacks, hazards, and evaluation methods.

</details>


### [58] [FSPO: Policy-Consistent Risk and Pareto-Feasible Control for Budgeted LLM RL Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02828)
*Miaobo Hu,Shuhao Hu,Xiaobo Guo,Xin Wang,Bokun Wang,Daren Zha,Jun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FSPO is a feedback-state controller for budgeted LLM RL post-training that jointly solves risk estimation, calibration, and multi-resource feasibility, achieving higher held-out/OOD accuracy and lower miscalibration and failure rates than adaptive baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adaptive LLM RL post-training adjusts many online actuators (temperature, group size, clipping, KL, verifier allocation, update budget), but three coupled issues persist: behavior-trained risk models may not estimate risk under the deployed controller; scores calibrated on logged data can become miscalibrated after selective action choice; and independent per-resource minimum costs cannot certify feasible multi-resource continuation.

Method: FSPO learns a policy-consistent risk-to-go model whose Bellman target follows the same frozen controller used for future decisions, plus a long-horizon utility model. Decision-conditioned trajectory calibration (DCTC) calibrates risk on cross-fitted trajectories from provisional-controller actions. A Pareto resource continuation certificate (PRCC) admits actions only when a non-dominated cumulative reservation remains feasible over the residual horizon.

Result: Under a matched GRPO resource envelope, FSPO attains 66.11% held-out and 59.43% OOD accuracy vs. 64.47%/57.03% for PB2. Three paired seeds yield +2.42/+3.19 pp over the contextual bandit. Policy-consistent risk lowers selected-decision ECE from 0.108 to 0.053 under high mismatch; DCTC lowers it from 0.039 to 0.022 at matched acceptance; PRCC eliminates false-feasible admissions ($0.197→0.000$) on an 18-action catalog; all components reduce trajectory failure from 0.181 to 0.083 in a factorial ablation.

Conclusion: Jointly addressing policy-consistent risk modeling, decision-conditioned calibration, and Pareto multi-resource certification improves accuracy, calibration, and feasibility in budgeted adaptive LLM RL post-training.

Abstract: Adaptive LLM reinforcement-learning post-training changes multiple training actuators online, including rollout temperature, group size, clipping, KL regularization, verifier allocation, and update budget. Three coupled issues remain unresolved. A future-risk model trained from behavior trajectories need not estimate the risk induced by the controller that will be deployed; a score calibrated on logged state-action pairs can become miscalibrated after selective action choice; and independent per-resource minimum costs do not in general certify a feasible multi-resource continuation. We introduce FSPO, a feedback-state controller for budgeted LLM RL post-training that addresses these issues jointly. FSPO learns a policy-consistent risk-to-go model whose Bellman target follows the same frozen controller used for future decisions, together with a long-horizon utility model. Decision-conditioned trajectory calibration (DCTC) calibrates risk on cross-fitted trajectories generated by actions selected by provisional controllers. A Pareto resource continuation certificate (PRCC) admits an action only when a non-dominated cumulative reservation remains feasible over the residual horizon. Under a matched GRPO resource envelope, FSPO reaches 66.11% held-out and 59.43% OOD accuracy, compared with 64.47% and 57.03% for PB2, the strongest evaluated adaptive baseline. Three paired training seeds give gains of +2.42 and +3.19 percentage points over the contextual bandit on held-out and OOD evaluation. Under high behavior-deployment mismatch, policy-consistent risk lowers selected-decision ECE from 0.108 to 0.053; DCTC lowers it from 0.039 to 0.022 at matched acceptance; PRCC removes false-feasible admissions on an 18-action catalog ($0.197\rightarrow0.000$); and enabling all three components reduces trajectory failure from 0.181 to 0.083 in a factorial ablation.

</details>


### [59] [AMBER: Multi-View Adaptive Budget Allocation for Listwise Vision-Language Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02831)
*Wenteng Chen,Jiachen Zhu,Rong Shan,Tianyi Xu,Yuxiang Chen,Congmin Zheng,Teng Wang,Junjie Wu,Weiwen Liu,Changwang Zhang,Weinan Zhang,Jun Wang,Jianghao Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AMBER is an online, budgeted multi-view reranking framework that treats fragmented listwise VLM outputs as local tournaments and uses continuous Elo updates to maintain a global ranking, dynamically allocating VLM calls to ambiguous candidate views and high-information queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models are powerful listwise rerankers for multimodal retrieval, but their high inference cost limits them to evaluating only small local candidate views. Existing multi-call strategies use fixed schedules, wasting expensive VLM calls on uninformative candidate pairs and easy queries.

Method: AMBER treats fragmented listwise VLM outputs as local tournaments and applies continuous Elo updates to maintain a lightweight global ranking state, allocating computation at two levels: dynamically constructing candidate views with high score ambiguity, and scheduling queries to maximize expected information gain. The authors show each Elo update corresponds to a stochastic gradient ascent step on the Bradley-Terry log-likelihood, and provide a submodular information-theoretic motivation for the query-level allocation strategy.

Result: Experiments on CIRR, CIRCO, and PhotoBench show that AMBER achieves the strongest overall performance among compared multi-call VLM reranking methods under comparable VLM-call budgets, and remains effective in lower-budget settings.

Conclusion: AMBER provides an efficient, adaptive budgeted multi-view reranking approach that outperforms existing multi-call VLM reranking baselines under equal call budgets and is robust to budget reduction. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/wnlfc/AMBER.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are powerful listwise rerankers for multimodal retrieval, but high inference costs restrict them to evaluating small local candidate views. Existing multi-call strategies rely on fixed schedules, wasting expensive VLM calls on uninformative candidate pairs and easy queries. To address this, we propose Adaptive Multi-view Budgeted Elo Reranking (AMBER), an online, budgeted multi-view reranking framework that dynamically optimizes global resource allocation. AMBER treats fragmented listwise VLM outputs as local tournaments, using continuous Elo updates to maintain a lightweight global ranking state. Building on this, it allocates computation at two levels: dynamically constructing candidate views with high score ambiguity, and scheduling queries to maximize expected information gain. We show that each Elo update corresponds to a stochastic gradient ascent step on the Bradley-Terry log-likelihood, and provide a submodular information-theoretic motivation for the query-level allocation strategy. Experiments on CIRR, CIRCO, and PhotoBench demonstrate that AMBER achieves the strongest overall performance among the compared multi-call VLM reranking methods under comparable VLM-call budgets, while remaining effective in lower-budget settings. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/wnlfc/AMBER.

</details>


### [60] [DNAlign: Dynamic Null-Space Safe Alignment for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02844)
*Jisheng Dang,Yushuo Zhao,Dewei Liu,Junfeng Fang,Bimei Wang,Tiantian Rao,Hong Peng,Bin Hu,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DNAlign is a lightweight safety alignment framework combining control-theoretic optimization with null-space projection to reduce harmful LLM outputs while preserving fluency, factual utility, and diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safe and reliable LLM deployment is a fundamental challenge. Existing safety alignment methods either incur high computational cost or unintentionally disrupt core knowledge, degrading fluency and factual accuracy on benign tasks, revealing a persistent safety-utility trade-off.

Method: DNAlign integrates control-theoretic optimization with null-space projection, treating the LLM as a dynamic system and introducing controllable perturbations to steer generation toward safe behavior. A projection module restricts perturbations to a harmful-related subspace derived from neutral hidden states, preserving general knowledge and response quality. A value function trained on human preference data adaptively optimizes the control signals to align with human safety preferences.

Result: Extensive evaluations across multiple LLM backbones show DNAlign consistently reduces harmful outputs while maintaining fluency, coherence, and factual utility, achieving superior overall performance versus prior alignment baselines without sacrificing generation diversity.

Conclusion: DNAlign provides an effective and practically deployable solution for safe LLM alignment, mitigating the safety-utility trade-off.

Abstract: Ensuring the safe and reliable deployment of large language models (LLMs) remains a fundamental challenge. Existing safety alignment approaches either incur high computational cost or unintentionally disrupt the model's core knowledge, leading to degraded fluency and factual accuracy on benign tasks. This reveals a persistent trade-off between safety and utility. We propose DNAlign, a lightweight alignment framework that integrates control-theoretic optimization with null-space projection. By treating the LLM as a dynamic system, the proposed framework introduces controllable perturbations to steer generation toward safe behavior. A key component is the projection module, which restricts these perturbations to the harmful-related subspace derived from neutral hidden states, thereby preserving general knowledge and response quality. A value function trained on human preference data adaptively optimizes the control signals to align with human safety preferences. Extensive evaluations across multiple LLM backbones demonstrate that our framework consistently reduces harmful outputs while maintaining fluency, coherence, and factual utility. It achieves superior overall performance compared to prior alignment baselines without sacrificing generation diversity. These results indicate that the proposed framework provides an effective and practically deployable solution for safe LLM alignment. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DNAlign.

</details>


### [61] [Bounded Reachability & Jailbreak Detection via Contraction-Constrained State Space Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02853)
*Omanshu Thapliyal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proves that an SSM-based safety head can be certified robust against bounded embedding-space perturbations exactly when the state transition matrix satisfies ||A||∞<1, enabling exact interval bound propagation certification; enforcing this contraction via a hinge penalty improves certified fraction from 41% to 59% and yields strong zero-shot jailbreak detection transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety heads are lightweight classifiers attached to pretrained language models to flag harmful inputs before generation. While their empirical detection performance has been studied, their formal robustness properties remain largely unexplored. The authors ask when an SSM-based safety head can be certified to produce the same prediction for all inputs within a bounded embedding-space perturbation.

Method: The authors analyze the SSM safety head as a linear time-invariant classifier and derive a certification condition based on the l∞ norm of the state transition matrix. They prove that ||A||∞<1 (the contraction condition) enables exact interval bound propagation (IBP) certification. They enforce contraction with a hinge penalty and validate the theory on toxic comment data, then apply a contraction-regularized S4 head to jailbreak detection on JailbreakBench with zero-shot transfer to AdvBench and HarmBench. They also compare against logistic regression on mean-pooled Mamba-130M embeddings.

Result: When the contraction condition holds, the reachable output interval has bounded steady-state width and examples can be certified as robustly classified. When it fails, the interval grows exponentially with sequence length and certification is impossible at any practical perturbation radius. Enforcing contraction improves the certified fraction from 41% to 59%, with a sharp empirical phase transition at ||A||∞=1 matching the theory. The contraction-regularized S4 head achieves zero-shot transfer to AdvBench (DR=0.994) and HarmBench (DR=0.988). A logistic regression on mean-pooled Mamba-130M embeddings matches or exceeds the S4 head on every detection metric.

Conclusion: The contractivity of the SSM state transition matrix is the single condition governing whether exact robustness certification is possible for safety heads. The S4 safety head's contribution is not superior discrimination—since harmful intent is already linearly separable in the embedding space—but the formal certification that no probe-based approach provides.

Abstract: Safety heads are lightweight classifiers attached to pretrained language models for flagging harmful inputs before generation. Their empirical detection performance has been studied, but their formal robustness properties remain largely unexplored. We ask when a State Space Model (SSM)-based safety head can be certified to produce the same prediction for all inputs within a bounded embedding-space perturbation. We prove that the answer turns on a single condition: the $l_\infty$ norm of the state transition matrix must satisfy $\norm{A}_\infty<1$ (the \emph{contraction condition}), which enables exact interval bound propagation (IBP) certification for linear time-invariant classifiers. When the contraction condition holds, the reachable output interval has bounded steady-state width and examples can be certified as robustly classified. When it fails, the interval grows exponentially with sequence length and certification is impossible at any practical perturbation radius. We enforce contraction with a hinge penalty and show on toxic comment data that certified fraction improves from 41\% to 59\%, with a sharp empirical phase transition at $\norm{A}_\infty=1$ matching the theory. Applying a contraction-regularized S4 head to jailbreak detection on JailbreakBench, we achieve a zero-shot transfer to AdvBench (DR=0.994) and HarmBench (DR=0.988). A logistic regression on mean-pooled Mamba-130M embeddings matches or exceeds the S4 head on every detection metric, confirming that harmful intent is already linearly separable in the embedding space. The S4 safety head's contribution is not superior discrimination but the formal certification that no probe-based approach provides.

</details>


### [62] [Harness-Aware Distillation for Small Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02858)
*Moonseok Choi,Taehong Moon,Giung Nam,Juho Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Harness-Aware Distillation (HAD) distills only the teacher-specific abilities a fixed agent harness cannot supply, using an action preference that contrasts teacher actions with/without harness information and a validity check, beating on-policy distillation without task rewards or success labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language model agents run inside a harness (software managing context, tools, feedback). When distilling such an agent into a smaller student, the harness remains, so the student mainly needs teacher-specific abilities the harness cannot provide—e.g., acting correctly on harness information. Standard distillation instead imitates the teacher's full outputs and treats the harness as mere input, wasting capacity on information the harness already supplies.

Method: HAD builds on on-policy distillation with two components: (1) an action preference that contrasts the same teacher's actions with and without harness information, scored after the student's own reasoning; (2) a validity check that discards preference pairs whose preferred action contradicts the harness records.

Result: Across multiple long-horizon agent benchmarks and models, HAD outperforms on-policy distillation baselines under the same fixed harness. The contrast provides information that pure imitation cannot. HAD requires no task rewards, success labels, or future information.

Conclusion: HAD enters fewer unproductive loops and recovers from errors more often than baselines, suggesting the student adaptively keeps learnable feedback in its weights while reading state information from the harness.

Abstract: Language model agents are deployed with a harness, the software around the model that manages its context, tools, and feedback. When such an agent is distilled into a smaller one, the harness stays in place, so the student mainly needs the teacher-specific abilities that the harness cannot provide, such as acting correctly on harness information. Standard distillation, however, imitates the teacher's full outputs and treats the harness as part of the input. We propose Harness-Aware Distillation (HAD), which focuses distillation on what the teacher adds beyond the harness. HAD complements on-policy distillation with two components: an action preference that contrasts the same teacher's actions with and without the harness information, scored after the student's own reasoning, and a validity check that drops preference pairs whose preferred action contradicts the harness records. We show that the contrast gives the student information that imitating the teacher alone cannot provide, and HAD needs no task rewards, success labels, or future information. Across multiple long-horizon agent benchmarks and models, HAD outperforms on-policy distillation baselines with the same fixed harness. Our analysis shows that HAD enters fewer unproductive loops and recovers from errors more often than the baselines, and suggests that it adaptively keeps learnable feedback in its weights while reading state information from the harness.

</details>


### [63] [TACD: Distilling Efficient Text-to-Motion Models via Terminal Amplification Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02867)
*Wei-Jin Huang,Yuan-Ming Li,Kun-Yu Lin,Wang Luo,Yinlin Zhu,Yue Yu,Shenghao Ye,Junbin Yuan,Fa-Ting Hong,Qing Zhang,Wei-Shi Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TACD is an on-policy distillation method that stabilizes few-step text-to-motion generation by bounding loss weights at the denoising endpoint, yielding faster and lighter students with improved FID.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-motion models achieve good quality and instruction following but are slow and memory-intensive due to many-step denoising and large components, hindering deployment. Existing distillation approaches suffer a failure mode where velocity matching on a fixed supervision grid overweights errors near the denoising endpoint, degrading few-step generation.

Method: TACD is an on-policy approach that trains efficient motion generators from text prompts and pretrained teachers without real-motion data. Building on segmented on-policy flow distillation, it supervises clean-motion predictions along student-generated trajectories. TACD ties the latest teacher query to the student's step size, bounding effective loss weights in clean-motion space while leaving inference unchanged. An endpoint-matching form is provided for diffusion teachers.

Result: On HumanML3D and KIT-ML, TACD improves few-step generation, with a 58% reduction in eight-step HY-Motion student FID versus unbounded distillation. For diffusion teachers, four-step students on HumanML3D attain lower FID and matched or improved text-motion retrieval relative to 50-step teachers. On HY-Motion and Kimodo, eight-step students with compact components achieve 7.7-11.9x end-to-end speedups and 3.8-6.7x lower peak GPU memory.

Conclusion: TACD enables data-free, on-policy distillation of efficient text-to-motion generators, mitigating endpoint error amplification and delivering substantial speed and memory gains alongside improved few-step quality and retrieval performance.

Abstract: Recent text-to-motion models have improved motion quality and instruction following, yet many-step denoising and large model components make deployment slow and memory-intensive. We present Terminal-Amplification-Controlled Distillation (TACD), an on-policy approach for training efficient motion generators from text prompts and pretrained teachers, without real-motion training data. Building on segmented on-policy flow distillation, we supervise clean-motion predictions along student-generated trajectories. We identify a failure mode in which velocity matching on a fixed supervision grid repeatedly overweights errors near the denoising endpoint, degrading few-step generation. TACD ties the latest teacher query to the student's step size, bounding the effective loss weights in clean-motion space without changing inference. Experiments on HumanML3D and KIT-ML demonstrate improved few-step generation, including a 58% reduction in eight-step HY-Motion student FID relative to distillation without this bound. For diffusion teachers, the endpoint-matching form of TACD yields four-step students with lower FID and matched or improved text-motion retrieval relative to their 50-step teachers on HumanML3D. On HY-Motion and Kimodo, eight-step students with compact components achieve 7.7-11.9x end-to-end speedups and reduce peak GPU memory by 3.8-6.7x relative to their teachers. Project page: https://vkgo.github.io/TACD/

</details>


### [64] [PsyEvo: A Personalized Counseling Agent That Self-Evolves at Test Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02885)
*Yuting Yan,Shihao Xu,Junhao Yu,Mingcong Zuo,Lu Chen,Nan Xiang,Haiyang Geng,Dongjie Tao,Minghao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PsyEvo is an LLM-based counseling framework enabling client-specific personalization and response-policy improvement at test time via three components (HBSP, LiPO, SOCA), achieving 7.684 Overall on PsychEval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mental health disorders affect a large share of the global population, but a persistent shortage of trained practitioners leaves most people without adequate care. LLM-based counselors offer scalable conversational psychological support, yet offline training alone limits adaptation to individual clients and learning from ongoing therapeutic interaction at test time.

Method: PsyEvo comprises three components: (1) Hierarchical Bayesian Skill Policy (HBSP), which personalizes intervention selection by maintaining a per-client skill posterior updated from session feedback; (2) Inter-session Listwise Preference Optimization (LiPO), which improves how the selected skill is expressed by updating a shared response adapter from cross-client preference evidence; and (3) State-conditioned Ordinal Credit Assignment (SOCA), which supplies candidate preferences and trajectory credit via consistency-checked comparisons and ordinal projection.

Result: In simulated-client evaluation with shared online cohort adaptation, PsyEvo achieves 7.684 Overall on PsychEval and surpasses every component variant across three matched runs. Removing individual components reduces the mean overall score by 0.138–0.171 under the shared configuration.

Conclusion: PsyEvo supports both client-specific personalization and test-time response-policy improvement, with ablation results supporting conditional contributions of each component within the complete scaffold. Code is released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Mental health disorders affect a substantial proportion of the global population, yet a persistent shortage of trained practitioners leaves the majority without adequate care. Large language model (LLM)-based counselors present a promising direction for delivering scalable conversational psychological support. Offline model training alone leaves limited room to adapt to individual clients or to learn from ongoing therapeutic interaction at test time. We introduce PsyEvo, an LLM-based counseling framework that enables both client-specific personalization and response-policy improvement at test time through three components: Hierarchical Bayesian Skill Policy (HBSP) personalizes what intervention to apply by maintaining a per-client skill posterior updated from session feedback; Inter-session Listwise Preference Optimization (LiPO) improves how the selected skill is expressed by updating a shared response adapter from cross-client preference evidence; and State-conditioned Ordinal Credit Assignment (SOCA) supplies candidate preferences and trajectory credit to the two components through consistency-checked comparisons and ordinal projection. In simulated-client evaluation with shared online cohort adaptation, PsyEvo obtains 7.684 Overall on PsychEval and exceeds every component variant in each of three matched runs. Removing individual components lowers mean overall score by 0.138--0.171 under the shared configuration, supporting conditional contributions within the complete scaffold. Our code is available at https://github.com/Lingxi-mental-health/PsyEvo

</details>


### [65] [Interpreting at Write Time: A Policy Ablation for Multi-Goal Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02897)
*Albert Sadowski,Jarosław A. Chudziak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Long-running assistants must summarise an ever-growing history, and the choice of what to keep is made against some notion of the record's purpose before it is known which standing goals will later query it. Comparing three write policies—goal-neutral, single all-goal, and per-goal summaries—across models and event streams with a fixed read step, per-goal summaries win on relevance, completeness and accuracy; the all-goal summary performs worst, even below a neutral summary written at a fraction of its budget. Interpretation at write time pays off only for the goal that later asks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assistants with long histories cannot retain everything and must summarise, yet summaries are already lossy commitments: the write-time decision about what matters is made before the querying goal is known, and once the history exceeds re-readability the summary irreversibly replaces the stream. The paper asks what a memory should optimise for when it must serve several standing goals simultaneously.

Method: The authors formalise three write policies: (i) summarise with no goal in view (neutral), (ii) write a single summary intended to cover every goal, and (iii) write one summary per goal and read them together. They compare these policies across several models and event streams while holding the read step fixed, so that only the write policy varies.

Result: Goals are shown to pull summaries apart: summaries written for different goals overlap each other less than a summary overlaps a rewrite of itself. Per-goal summaries achieve the best relevance, completeness and accuracy. The single all-goal summary performs worst, losing even to the neutral summary produced at a fraction of its token budget. Benefits of interpreting at write time accrue only to the goal that subsequently queries the memory.

Conclusion: Summarisation is inherently goal-conditioned rather than neutral, and a monolithic all-goal summary is an ineffective compromise. Writing separate summaries per standing goal and reading them together is the superior policy, though its gains are local to the goal that asks; write-time interpretation does not transfer across goals.

Abstract: A long-running assistant cannot keep everything it has seen, so it summarises. Summarising is not neutral: what is kept is chosen against some notion of what the record is for, and that choice is made once, before anyone knows which of the user's standing goals will ask. Goals rarely disagree about what happened. They disagree about which parts of it were worth the space. Once the history is too long to re-read, the summary replaces the stream, and whatever it left out is gone. We ask what a memory should summarise for when it serves several standing goals at once. Three policies answer differently: summarise with no goal in view, write one summary covering every goal, or write one summary per goal and read them together. We compare them across several models and event streams, holding the read step fixed so that only the write differs. The goals do pull apart: summaries written for different goals overlap each other less than a summary overlaps a rewrite of itself. Per-goal summaries win on relevance, completeness and accuracy, and the all-goal summary loses even to the neutral one written at a fraction of its budget. Interpreting at write pays off, but only for the goal that later asks.

</details>


### [66] [LUMOS: Tracing Parametric Knowledge from Training Data to Behavioral Outputs in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02902)
*Seoyeon Ye,Gayoung Kim,Jiyoung Hong,Sookyung Kim,Hyunsoo Cho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LUMOS, a diagnostic framework leveraging OLMo 2's transparent training corpus, traces LLM knowledge from training-data exposure to behavioral output, revealing that models encode rare facts with high separability yet fail to express them, and that self-reflection collapses on unseen content even with chain-of-thought prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM knowledge analyses are output-centric, inferring what a model "knows" without verifying actual training exposure. This leaves unresolved whether correct responses reflect genuine generalization or rote memorization, making existing diagnoses speculative rather than evidence-based.

Method: The authors introduce LUMOS, a diagnostic framework that traces knowledge along the causal chain from training-data exposure to behavioral output, using OLMo 2 with its fully transparent training corpus to ground the analysis in verified exposure.

Result: Models internally encode rare facts with high separability (84%) but fail to express them behaviorally (54%), a retrieval gap that narrows with scale. On self-reflection, models perform reliably on trained content (83%) but drop to random-baseline levels (49%) on unseen content; this collapse persists under chain-of-thought prompting, which inflates confidence signals rather than improving calibration.

Conclusion: Incorporating the training-data axis into LLM evaluation transforms speculative diagnoses into verifiable claims, and this axis should become a standard component of knowledge assessment in LLMs.

Abstract: Current analyses of LLMs' parametric knowledge are largely output-centric, drawing conclusions about what a model knows without verifying what it was actually trained on. This leaves fundamental questions, such as whether a correct response reflects genuine generalization or rote memorization, grounded in speculation rather than evidence. To resolve these ambiguities, we introduce LUMOS, a diagnostic framework that traces knowledge along the causal chain from training-data exposure to behavioral output, leveraging OLMo 2 with its fully transparent training corpus. By grounding analysis in verified exposure, we reveal that models internally encode rare facts with high separability (84%) yet fail to express them behaviorally (54%), though this retrieval gap narrows with scale. Furthermore, when models are asked to self-reflect on their own answers, they perform reliably on trained content (83%) but drop to random-baseline levels (49%) on unseen content. This collapse persists even under chain-of-thought prompting, which inflates confidence signals rather than improving calibration. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that incorporating the training-data axis into LLM evaluation transforms speculative diagnoses into verifiable claims, and we advocate that this axis should be a standard component of knowledge assessment in LLMs.

</details>


### [67] [Frequency Is Not Sensitivity Identifying Safety-Sensitive Experts in Sparse MoE LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02910)
*Md Nurul Absar Siddiky,Liuwan Zhu,Yingfei Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Router-gradient sensitivity identifies experts whose suppression most weakens refusal in sparse MoE models, outperforming activation frequency across five architectures, two budgets, and layer-matched controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Suppressing a small set of routed experts can weaken the safety behavior of a sparse MoE model without retraining, making expert selection a security question. The conventional criterion, activation frequency, measures usage rather than influence on the loss, so a more causally relevant signal may better identify safety-critical experts.

Method: The paper proposes router-gradient sensitivity: the sensitivity of the sequence loss to the gate weights that select an expert. Experts are ranked by this signal versus activation frequency on 500 benign and 500 malicious prompts across five MoE architectures, then refusal is measured on 100 held-out malicious prompts under two budgets: equal expert counts and equal nominal malicious routing traffic (1%-5%). A ten-trial random-mean baseline and a per-layer expert-count matching control are also used, plus an exploratory cross-model analysis relating malicious-versus-benign concentration gaps to peak gradient effects.

Result: Under each budget, router-gradient selection reduces refusals more than activation in 24 of 25 conditions, and more than the ten-trial random mean in all 25. The largest effect occurs in OLMoE, where refusals fall from 34 to 9 of 100 prompts (73.53% relative) without degraded outputs, indicating substantive compliance rather than broken generation. With expert counts matched in every layer, gradient selection still yields greater refusal reduction than activation in 23 of 25 conditions, with two ties. The exploratory analysis links larger malicious-versus-benign concentration gaps to greater peak gradient effects (rho = 0.90; exact two-sided p = 0.083, n = 5).
