Conclusion: Router-gradient sensitivity is a more effective criterion than activation frequency for selecting experts whose suppression weakens MoE safety behavior, and the results support gradient selection under the tested budgets. The cross-model correlation is exploratory given n = 5 and only marginal significance.

Abstract: Suppressing a small set of routed experts can weaken the safety behavior of a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language model without retraining. Which experts to suppress is therefore a security question, and the usual answer is activation frequency, but frequency measures use, not influence. We test an alternative: router-gradient sensitivity, the sensitivity of the sequence loss to the gate weights that select an expert. Across five MoE architectures, we rank experts by each signal on 500 benign and 500 malicious prompts and measure refusal on 100 held-out malicious prompts under two budgets: equal expert counts and equal nominal malicious routing traffic (1%-5%). Under each of the two budgets, router-gradient selection reduces refusals more than activation in 24 of 25 conditions, and more than a ten-trial random mean in all 25. The largest effect is in OLMoE, where refusals fall from 34 to 9 of 100 prompts (73.53% relative) with no degraded outputs, indicating substantive compliance rather than broken generation. After matching expert counts in every layer, gradient selection still produces greater refusal reduction than activation in 23 of 25 conditions, with two ties. An exploratory cross-model analysis links larger malicious-versus-benign concentration gaps to greater peak gradient effects (rho = 0.90; exact two-sided p = 0.083, n = 5). Together, the results support gradient selection under the tested budgets.

</details>


### [68] [HASTE: Evolving Agent Harnesses Against Emerging Attacks Using Sparse Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02920)
*Xiqiao Xiong,Moxin Li,Zhixin Ma,Ouxiang Li,Wenjie Wang,Fuli Feng,Xiangnan He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HASTE is a multi-agent framework that evolves agent harnesses from sparse threat evidence via adversarial interplay between safety-specification generation and attack-case generation, reducing attack success rates while preserving benign utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent harnesses enforce safety constraints, but rapidly emerging attacks outpace manual harness adaptation. Automated harness evolution is needed, yet available signals are often sparse (brief descriptions or few attack examples in threat reports/preprints).

Method: HASTE evolves harnesses through an adversarial interplay between safety-specification generation and attack-case generation. Specifications guide harness updates toward identified vulnerabilities; attack cases probe for remaining vulnerabilities after each update. Evaluation outcomes are fed back into both processes, enabling evolution against attacks beyond the initially observed evidence.

Result: Across multiple backbone models, attack types, and evidence forms, HASTE consistently reduces attack success rates while preserving benign-task utility.

Conclusion: HASTE effectively automates agent harness evolution from sparse threat evidence through adversarial specification and attack generation, generalizing to emerging attacks beyond observed evidence.

Abstract: Agent harnesses play a critical role in defenses by enforcing safety constraints to prevent unsafe actions. However, rapidly emerging attacks outpace manual harness adaptation, motivating automated harness evolution. Yet the signals available for harness evolution are often sparse, such as brief descriptions or a few attack examples in threat reports and preprints. To address this limitation, we introduce HASTE, a multi-agent framework that evolves agent harnesses from sparse threat evidence through an adversarial interplay between safety-specification generation and attack-case generation. Safety specifications guide harness updates toward addressing identified safety vulnerabilities, while attack cases probe for remaining safety vulnerabilities after each update. By feeding evaluation outcomes back into both processes, HASTE enables harness evolution against emerging attacks beyond the initially observed evidence. Experimental results across multiple backbone models, attack types, and evidence forms show that HASTE consistently reduces attack success rates while preserving benign-task utility. The code is available at https://github.com/xxiqiao/HASTE.

</details>


### [69] [Positive-Unlabeled Learning for Agent Safety False Alarm Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02925)
*Xichen Yan,Chongyang Gao,Kezhen Chen,Guangyi Zhang,Jiaqi Wu,Lixu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper frames false-alarm auditing for language-model safety monitors as a positive-unlabeled (PU) ranking problem and proposes a two-stage framework that improves false-alarm ranking without alarm safety labels or modifying the monitor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conservative safety monitors produce many false alarms, consuming review resources and reducing trust. Since false and genuine alarms are interleaved in native monitor scores, reliable cutoff selection still needs heavy manual verification. Often only a small set of verified-safe non-alarmed trajectories exists while alarms remain unlabeled, making false-alarm auditing a PU ranking problem. The core difficulty is monitor-induced selection: observed safe references are monitor-accepted, whereas the hidden safe alarms of interest are exactly those incorrectly flagged, so observed positives poorly represent the positives to recover.

Method: A two-stage framework: (1) Trust-aware PU Supervision adapts safe references toward the alarm domain and protects plausible false alarms from excessive negative pressure; (2) Reliability-gated Rank Distillation consolidates consistent ordering preferences from multiple PU reference models into a single student. Consensus-guided Structural Refinement then refines the student ranking using hierarchical safe-reference support, alarm relations, and predicted reference consensus. The framework requires no alarm safety labels for fitting and leaves the underlying monitor unchanged.

Result: Across mainstream safety monitors, the method achieves macro AUPRC of 0.6444, outperforming eight evaluated PU baselines by 5.27–16.98 absolute percentage points. Compared with PULDA, the strongest PU baseline, it recovers 33.3% more false alarms at a 5% review budget.

Conclusion: The proposed PU ranking framework effectively audits safety-monitor false alarms under monitor-induced selection, delivering consistent and substantial gains over strong PU baselines without requiring alarm safety labels or altering the monitor.

Abstract: Safety monitors help safeguard language-model agents interacting with external tools and environments, but conservative monitoring can generate many false alarms, consuming extensive review resources and weakening trust in alerts. Because false and genuine alarms often remain interleaved in native monitor scores, obtaining a reliable cutoff still requires substantial manual verification. In practice, a small set of verified-safe non-alarmed trajectories may be available while alarms remain unlabeled, naturally casting false-alarm auditing as a positive-unlabeled (PU) ranking problem. The key challenge is monitor-induced selection, since observed safe references are accepted by the monitor, while the hidden safe alarms of interest are precisely those it incorrectly flags, making the observed positives poorly representative of the positives to be recovered. To address this challenge, we propose a two-stage framework in which Trust-aware PU Supervision adapts safe references toward the alarm domain and protects plausible false alarms from excessive negative pressure, while Reliability-gated Rank Distillation consolidates consistent ordering preferences from multiple PU reference models into a single student. Consensus-guided Structural Refinement then improves the student ranking using hierarchical safe-reference support, alarm relations, and predicted reference consensus. The framework requires no alarm safety labels for fitting and leaves the underlying monitor unchanged. Across mainstream safety monitors, our method achieves a macro AUPRC of $0.6444$, outperforming eight evaluated PU baselines by 5.27--16.98 absolute percentage points; compared with PULDA, the strongest evaluated PU baseline, it recovers 33.3% more false alarms at a 5% review budget.

</details>


### [70] [When to Compile a Computer-Use Agent? Measuring Payback and Making Compilation Decisions for Token Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02932)
*Yulong Ming,Jie Xu,Zihan Wu,Xiaohua Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PACE is a payback-aware system that measures compilation costs and reuse to decide online when to compile repeatedly executed GUI procedures into programs, achieving near-optimal token cost and 17-25% savings over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compiling repeatedly executed GUI agent procedures into programs can reduce token costs, but deciding when to compile is hard: compilation costs are uncertain (attempts may need repair and can still fail), and future reuse is unknown (tasks may stop arriving or GUI drift may break the program).

Method: PACE combines a measurement protocol with an online compilation algorithm. The protocol records successful and failed compilation costs and compares agent vs. program execution costs on matched task inputs to estimate per-use savings and payback counts. The online algorithm then compares estimated future savings against compilation costs (including failed attempts) using past task arrivals and compilation outcomes, and gates execution/compilation charges against a cumulative budget derived from observed arrivals.

Result: Under stated action-cost assumptions, total cost after each arrival is at most (1+ε) times the cost of running every task with the agent. For successful compilations, estimated payback counts excluding source agent runs are 2-16 uses. In simulations with recorded task arrivals, PACE reduces token costs by 17.3% vs. ReAct, 24.9% vs. the AutoRPA adaptation, and 17.3% vs. the ToolPro adaptation on average (ε=0.25).

Conclusion: Payback-aware, budget-constrained online compilation of GUI procedures yields provably near-optimal total cost and substantial token savings over agent-only and adaptation baselines.

Abstract: Compiling GUI procedures that agents execute repeatedly into programs can reduce their token costs. However, measuring payback and deciding when to compile have two challenges. First, compilation costs are uncertain because attempts can require repair and still fail to produce a usable program. Second, future reuse is unknown because tasks may stop arriving or GUI drift may stop the program from working. To address these challenges, we propose PACE (Payback-Aware Compilation from Experience), a system with a measurement protocol and an online compilation algorithm. The measurement protocol records successful and failed compilation costs, and compares agent and program execution costs on matched task inputs to estimate per-use savings and payback counts. Using these measurements, the online algorithm compares estimated future savings with compilation costs, including failed attempts, based on past task arrivals and compilation outcomes. It checks execution and compilation charges against a cumulative budget determined by observed task arrivals before allowing either action. Under stated action-cost assumptions, total cost after each arrival is at most $1+ε$ times the cost of running every task with the agent. For successful compilation attempts, estimated payback counts excluding source agent runs are 2-16 uses. In simulations using recorded task arrivals, PACE reduces token costs by 17.3% compared with ReAct, 24.9% with the AutoRPA adaptation, and 17.3% with the ToolPro adaptation on average ($ε=0.25$).

</details>


### [71] [Continual Graph Memory for Mathematical Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02945)
*Junyi Zhang,Jinxi Yu,Eric Hanchen Jiang,Jiachen Lu,Zhi Zhang,Xinjie He,Hyunsik Chae,Ethan Ji,Alexander K Taylor,Vigyan Sahai,Yiwen Kou,Kai-Wei Chang,Raghu Meka,Nanyun Peng,Amit Sahai,Terence Tao,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ansatz is a mathematical research agent built around Continual Graph Memory, a graph-based, evolvable, cross-problem memory system that organizes long-horizon proof-search processes and reuses knowledge from prior explorations, achieving closure on all ten First Proof Second Batch problems and autonomously solving several open problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving frontier mathematics problems may require massive numbers of agents working in parallel for extended periods to construct proofs, generating enormous volumes of intermediate proof results. Organizing these intermediate results throughout a long-horizon proof-search process and reusing knowledge gained from prior explorations remain major challenges.

Method: Ansatz is built around Continual Graph Memory, a graph-based, evolvable, cross-problem memory system. It comprises: (1) a unified graph memory representing all intermediate exploration results (facts, plans, counterexamples) with edges explicitly encoding their relationships; (2) dependency-aware retrieval supplying precisely targeted local context; (3) an evidence-sensitive curator that updates the research frontier and distills lessons from prior attempts; and (4) scoped recall that surfaces earlier statements and negative findings for local re-proving rather than uncritical reuse.

Result: Experiments cover runs across all ten First Proof Second Batch problems plus four component studies. Ansatz reports closure on all ten research tasks. It also produces solutions to the Jamison caterpillar conjecture and Erdős Problems 289, 348, and 488 without human intervention, and makes partial progress on several open problems.

Conclusion: Ansatz demonstrates the ability to sustain and resume long-horizon mathematical search, showing strong capability in solving open mathematical research problems.

Abstract: Using frontier agent harnesses to tackle mathematical research problems has emerged as an effective means of advancing mathematics. However, solving frontier problems in mathematics may require a massive number of agents working in parallel for extended periods to construct proofs, thereby generating an enormous volume of intermediate proof results. Organizing these intermediate results throughout a long-horizon proof-search process and reusing knowledge gained from prior explorations remain major challenges. We present Ansatz, a mathematical research agent built around Continual Graph Memory, a graph-based, evolvable, cross-problem mathematical research memory system that explicitly organizes the entire proof search process and reuses information from exploration trajectories of previous problems. Specifically, we develop a unified graph memory that represents all intermediate exploration results, including facts, plans, and counterexamples, together with edges that explicitly represent the relationships among them; dependency-aware retrieval supplies precisely targeted local context; an evidence-sensitive curator updates the research frontier and distills lessons from prior attempts; and scoped recall surfaces earlier statements and negative findings for local re-proving rather than uncritical reuse. Experiments cover runs across all ten First Proof Second Batch problems, together with four component studies. Ansatz reports closure on all ten research tasks, demonstrating its ability to sustain and resume long-horizon mathematical search. Beyond these problems, Ansatz also produces solutions to the Jamison caterpillar conjecture and Erdős Problems 289, 348, and 488 without human intervention, and makes partial progress on several open problems, illustrating its strong ability to solve open mathematical research problems.

</details>


### [72] [Reasoning with Evidence, Not Merely Rationales: Verifiable Preference Proofs for LLM-Based Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02968)
*Yu Hou,Nathaniel Kang,Pengkai Wang,Hua Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PROVE-REC is a two-pass framework for verifiable preference reasoning in LLM-based recommendation that closes the "grounding-influence gap" by generating a compact preference proof (Pass A) used alone to predict the next item (Pass B), verified via masking evidence and removing claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs infer user preferences from interaction histories and reviews, but the rationales they generate may not reflect the information actually used for recommendation. Two failures arise: a preference claim may be weakly supported by its selected evidence (grounding), or may have little effect on the final ranking (influence). These constitute the "grounding-influence gap."

Method: PROVE-REC, a general framework for verifiable preference reasoning. Pass A converts the complete pre-target history into a compact preference proof of positive and avoidance claims linked to selected evidence entries. Pass B predicts the next item using only the proof and its selected evidence, preventing bypassing of the reasoning path. Evidence-to-proof grounding is verified by comparing the effect of masking selected evidence against masking a comparable control entry; proof-to-recommendation influence is verified by removing a preference claim and measuring the decrease in the target item's ranking margin. A ranking-preservation objective retains useful information from the complete history.

Result: Comprehensive experiments on wide-ranging real-world datasets show PROVE-REC consistently outperforms strong sequential, generative, and LLM-enhanced baselines, with improvements up to 7.45%. Controlled ablations confirm the effectiveness of the two-pass architecture and verification objectives.

Conclusion: PROVE-REC produces claims more strongly grounded in historical evidence and more influential to recommendation while preserving ranking quality, offering a verifiable approach to LLM-based preference reasoning.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can infer user preferences from interaction histories and reviews, yet the rationales they generate may not reflect the information actually used for recommendation. A preference claim may be weakly supported by its selected evidence, or may have little effect on the final ranking. We refer to these two failures as the grounding-influence gap. We introduce PROVE-REC, a general framework for verifiable preference reasoning in LLM-based recommendation. Pass A converts the complete pre-target history into a compact preference proof consisting of positive and avoidance claims linked to selected evidence entries. Pass B predicts the next item using only the proof and its selected evidence, preventing the recommender from bypassing the reasoning path. To verify evidence-to-proof grounding, we compare the effect of masking selected evidence with masking a comparable control entry. To verify proof-to-recommendation influence, we remove a preference claim and measure the resulting decrease in the target item's ranking margin. A ranking-preservation objective further retains useful information from the complete history. Comprehensive experiments on wide-ranging real-world datasets demonstrate that PROVE-REC consistently outperforms strong sequential, generative, and LLM-enhanced baselines, with improvements of up to 7.45%. Controlled ablations confirm the effectiveness of the two-pass architecture and verification objectives. Moreover, PROVE-REC produces claims that are more strongly grounded in historical evidence and more influential to recommendation while preserving ranking quality.

</details>


### [73] [CreateScore: Domain-Theory-Informed Bayesian Routing for LLM-Based CV Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02972)
*Rupsa Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CreateScore is a Bayesian-network router that escalates low-certainty CV criterion decisions from an 8B to a 120B LLM, cutting tokens by 65.2% but failing to selectively catch the 8B model's errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Applying a high-capability LLM to every candidate and criterion in rubric-based CV screening is costly, motivating a cheaper routing mechanism that keeps expensive adjudication only where needed.

Method: A hand-specified DAG with Dirichlet-multinomial conditional probability tables converts CV evidence into posterior uncertainty (standard Bayesian conditioning, not causal inference). Low-uncertainty decisions are resolved by a local 8B model; uncertain ones escalate to a 120B reference model at a threshold calibrated on a training fold (target 70% local resolution) and then frozen. Evaluated on 200 synthetic Data Science CVs (139 train, 61 test, five criteria).

Result: 77.7% of criterion decisions (237/305) were resolved locally. Versus 120B adjudicating everything, routed escalation cut token use by 65.2% and raised exact score agreement from 32.8% (8B alone) to 42.6% (95% CI 31.0-55.1%); at n=61 the gain was not statistically distinguishable. Uncertainty did not identify 8B errors: disagreement with the reference was 16.2% among escalated vs 19.4% among locally resolved decisions (AUROC 0.47, 95% CI 0.39-0.56), equivalent to random. An earlier evaluation was invalidated when truncated reasoning-model outputs were silently replaced by local labels.

Conclusion: CreateScore is supported as an auditable cost-reduction mechanism, not as a targeted error detector, and is not an autonomous hiring system; safeguards for cascade evaluation are recommended.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can support rubric-based screening of CVs, but applying a high-capability model to every candidate and criterion is costly. We present CreateScore, a domain-theory-informed Bayesian network for criterion-level LLM routing. A hand-specified directed acyclic graph with Dirichlet-multinomial conditional probability tables converts CV evidence into posterior uncertainty; low-uncertainty decisions are resolved by a local 8B model and uncertain ones are escalated to a 120B reference model. The graph is causally motivated, but the system performs standard Bayesian conditioning, not causal inference. The escalation threshold is calibrated on a training fold (target: 70% resolved locally) and then frozen. On 200 synthetic Data Science CVs (139 training and 61 test candidates, five criteria), 77.7% of criterion decisions were resolved locally (237 of 305). Relative to a reference condition in which the 120B model adjudicated every criterion, routed escalation reduced token use by 65.2% and raised exact score agreement from 32.8% (8B alone) to 42.6% (95% CI 31.0-55.1%); at n = 61 the gain was not statistically distinguishable. The uncertainty signal did not, however, identify the decisions on which the 8B model erred: disagreement with the reference was 16.2% among escalated and 19.4% among locally resolved decisions (AUROC 0.47, 95% CI 0.39-0.56), no better than random selection. We also document how an earlier evaluation was invalidated when truncated reasoning-model outputs were silently replaced by local labels, and we recommend safeguards for cascade evaluation. CreateScore is supported as an auditable cost-reduction mechanism, not yet as a targeted error detector, and is not an autonomous hiring system.

</details>


### [74] [Reliable Self-Evolution with Imperfect Proxy Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02975)
*Kangjun Noh,Soyu Kim,Kyungwoo Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CISE introduces conformal interval-based rewards for self-evolving scientific search, constructing candidate-specific reward intervals via conditional conformal inference and online density-ratio estimation to avoid false-positive contamination from imperfect proxy rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-fidelity evaluation of every candidate is prohibitively expensive in some scientific discovery domains, so self-evolving search systems rely on low-cost but imperfect proxy rewards that may assign high scores to infeasible candidates. These false positives contaminate both the final output and the feedback guiding subsequent generations, motivating statistically calibrated reward intervals for more reliable search.

Method: CISE constructs candidate-specific reward intervals using conditional conformal inference and iteration-wise online density-ratio estimation. It uses conservative interval-based rewards for evolutionary feedback and returns candidates only when all required property intervals lie entirely within their respective feasible regions. Fixed-iteration coverage results are derived under explicit assumptions of independence and covariate shift.

Result: Evaluation on three self-evolving search tasks in materials science shows all candidates returned by CISE are true positives under high-fidelity evaluation, whereas baselines return more candidates but include false positives.

Conclusion: Statistically calibrated reward intervals yield a smaller but more precise shortlist, which is valuable when downstream validation budgets are limited.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based self-evolving search is a promising approach to scientific discovery. However, high-fidelity evaluation of every candidate is prohibitively expensive in some domains. Self-evolving systems in such settings therefore rely on low-cost but imperfect proxy rewards, which may assign high scores to infeasible candidates. These false positives may contaminate both the final output and the feedback used to guide subsequent generations. This motivates statistically calibrated reward intervals for more reliable self-evolving search. We propose Conformal Interval-Driven Self-Evolution (CISE), which constructs candidate-specific reward intervals using conditional conformal inference and iteration-wise online density-ratio estimation. CISE uses conservative interval-based rewards for evolutionary feedback and returns candidates only when all required property intervals lie entirely within their respective feasible regions. We derive fixed-iteration coverage results under explicit assumptions of independence and covariate shift. We evaluate CISE on three self-evolving search tasks in materials science. In our experiments, all candidates returned by CISE are true positives under high-fidelity evaluation, whereas the baselines return more candidates but include false positives. These results highlight the value of a smaller, more precise shortlist when downstream validation budgets are limited. Our repository is available at https://github.com/MLAI-Yonsei/CISE.git.

</details>


### [75] [Relevant Evidence Decoding for Audio-Visual Hallucination Mitigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02976)
*Hyunjae Ra,Aecheon Jung,Jungin Park,Sungeun Hong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RED is a training-free decoding method that identifies question-relevant perceptual evidence (audio, video, or their interaction) via pointwise mutual information and selectively strengthens it, reducing cross-modal hallucinations in AV-LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AV-LLMs are prone to cross-modal hallucinations, where one modality incorrectly influences predictions about another. Existing contrastive decoding methods, when directly applied to AV-LLMs, fail to account for the fact that different questions require different perceptual evidence (audio, video, or their interaction). The authors observe that joint audio-visual inference can even weaken predictions when the correct answer is recoverable from a single informative modality (e.g., confidence in "violin" drops when a guitar video is added to correct audio).

Method: RED is a training-free method. It uses pointwise mutual information (PMI) to quantify predictive support from audio and video beyond the question alone, decomposing the joint contribution into audio, video, and residual interaction components. A question-only inference pass determines the required evidence type, and the model then augments the original audio-visual prediction with the corresponding PMI contribution.

Result: Across three audio-visual hallucination benchmarks and three AV-LLMs, RED improves accuracy over standard decoding by up to 7% on CMM, 6.3% on AVHBench, and 3.8% on SVHalluc, with an average relative time to first token of 1.5x standard decoding.

Conclusion: RED effectively mitigates cross-modal hallucinations in AV-LLMs by adaptively selecting and strengthening question-relevant evidence, achieving consistent accuracy gains across benchmarks and models at a modest inference cost (1.5x TTFT).

Abstract: Audio-Visual Large Language Models (AV-LLMs) remain prone to cross-modal hallucinations, where one modality incorrectly affects predictions about another. Although contrastive decoding reduces hallucinations in vision-language models, its direct extension to AV-LLMs overlooks a key challenge: different questions require different perceptual evidence, including audio, video, or their interaction. Notably, we observe that joint audio-visual inference can weaken the prediction even when a model can recover the correct answer from a single informative modality. For example, when asked which instrument is heard, a model may correctly predict violin from the audio alone. Once a video showing a guitar is added, its confidence in violin may drop. In this paper, we introduce Relevant Evidence Decoding (RED), a training-free method that identifies question-relevant evidence and selectively strengthens its contribution. RED uses pointwise mutual information to quantify the predictive support provided by audio and video beyond the question alone. It decomposes their joint contribution into audio, video, and residual interaction components. A question-only inference pass determines the required evidence type, after which the model augments the original audio-visual prediction with the corresponding PMI contribution. Across three audio-visual hallucination benchmarks and three AV-LLMs, RED improves accuracy over standard decoding by up to 7% on CMM, 6.3% on AVHBench, and 3.8% on SVHalluc, with an average relative time to first token of 1.5x standard decoding.

</details>


### [76] [RASPER: Reward-Aligned Summarization of Clinical Notes for EHR Outcome Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02979)
*Arya Hadizadeh Moghaddam,Mohsen Nayebi Kerdabadi,Chen Chen,Dongjie Wang,Zijun Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RASPER is a reward-aligned summarizer that optimizes EHR discharge-note summarization directly against downstream clinical tasks, using RL from prediction feedback plus structured-code soft prompts, and it beats strong baselines on readmission prediction and medication recommendation across MIMIC-III/IV.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unstructured EHR discharge notes carry patient-specific evidence complementary to structured codes, but such evidence is buried in long, noisy text not written for any prediction task. Generic summaries tuned for fluency omit decisive evidence and keep plausible but uninformative detail, so a summarization approach aligned with the actual clinical prediction objective is needed.

Method: RASPER uses a tunable LLM-based summarizer to extract task-relevant evidence from discharge notes. It is trained with reinforcement learning from prediction feedback, where the reward is derived from the downstream predictor's loss. A longitudinal encoder converts structured codes into soft prompts to ground the summarizer in each patient's clinical context. Rewarding the quality of the resulting multimodal prediction pushes the summarizer to retain patient-specific evidence that complements rather than duplicates structured-code information.

Result: RASPER consistently outperforms strong baselines on both readmission prediction and medication recommendation across MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV.

Conclusion: Aligning note summarization directly with downstream prediction via prediction-feedback RL and structured-code grounding yields more useful, complementary summaries and improves clinical prediction performance.

Abstract: Unstructured discharge notes in Electronic Health Records (EHRs) often carry signal complementary to structured medical codes, holding patient-specific evidence that standardized cohort-level codes alone cannot capture. However, this evidence in notes is frequently buried in lengthy, noisy text that is not intentionally written with any specific clinical prediction in mind. Summarization is an obvious mitigation, but generic summaries, tuned for fluency rather than the outcome, routinely omit decisive evidence while retaining plausible but uninformative detail. To this end, we propose RASPER, a Reward-Aligned Summarizer for Prediction in EHR, that optimizes note summarization directly against the downstream clinical task. RASPER employs a tunable LLM-based summarizer to extract task-relevant evidence from discharge notes and trains it via reinforcement learning from prediction feedback, using a reward derived from the downstream predictor's loss. To ground the summarizer, a longitudinal encoder converts structured codes into soft prompts that incorporate each patient's clinical context into note summarization. By rewarding the quality of the resulting multimodal prediction, RASPER encourages the summarizer to retain patient-specific evidence that complements, rather than duplicates, information captured by structured codes. RASPER consistently outperforms strong baselines on both readmission prediction and medication recommendation across MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV.

</details>


### [77] [Safeguarding Mutual Correction in Source-Free Domain Adaptation via Cut Statistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02981)
*Seongjun Lee,Changhee Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SafeCut introduces a reliability-gated mutual correction framework for Source-Free Domain Adaptation, using the cut statistic as a label-free reliability measure to control cross-model supervision between a source-pretrained model and a Vision-Language model, achieving state-of-the-art results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early single-model SFDA methods rely on self-refinement and suffer from confirmation bias, struggling to correct systematic errors. Recent ViL-based methods add external knowledge but operate unidirectionally, using the ViL model only to supervise the source model. This misses a key structural property: the two models have distinct failure modes, so one may be correct where the other errs, offering a natural chance for mutual correction. However, without ground-truth labels, determining which model is correct is non-trivial, and naive prediction exchange risks propagating errors.

Method: SafeCut leverages the cut statistic as a label-free measure of prediction reliability to gate cross-model supervision. It dynamically controls both the direction and strength of supervision based on relative reliability, selectively amplifying true corrections while suppressing miscorrections on a per-sample basis. Theoretical justification is provided showing that this reliability-gated mechanism guarantees a net-positive correction signal.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse SFDA benchmarks demonstrate that SafeCut achieves state-of-the-art performance.

Conclusion: The paper highlights the effectiveness of safeguarding mutual correction in SFDA via cut statistics, showing that reliability-gated bidirectional supervision between source-pretrained and ViL models yields superior adaptation outcomes.

Abstract: Source-Free Domain Adaptation (SFDA) aims to adapt a source-pretrained model to an unlabeled target domain without access to the original source domain. While early single-model approaches rely on self-refinement, they are inherently susceptible to confirmation bias and struggle to correct their own systematic errors. To overcome this limitation, recent methods introduce Vision-Language (ViL) models as external knowledge sources. However, these approaches operate in a largely unidirectional paradigm, using the ViL model primarily to supervise the source-pretrained model. This overlooks a key structural property: the two models exhibit distinct failure modes -- where one produces an incorrect prediction, the other may produce a correct one, creating a natural opportunity for mutual correction within the target domain. Yet, without ground-truth labels, identifying which model is correct on any given sample is non-trivial, and naively exchanging predictions risks propagating errors across models. To address this challenge, we propose SafeCut, a novel approach that leverages the cut statistic as a label-free measure of prediction reliability to gate cross-model supervision. Our approach dynamically controls both the direction and strength of supervision based on relative reliability, selectively amplifying true corrections while suppressing miscorrections on a per-sample basis. We further provide theoretical justification showing that this reliability-gated mechanism guarantees a net-positive correction signal. Extensive experiments across diverse SFDA benchmarks demonstrate that SafeCut achieves state-of-the-art performance, highlighting the effectiveness of safeguarding mutual correction in SFDA via cut statistics.

</details>


### [78] [PLCWorld: Benchmarking LLM-Generated PLC Programs in Closed-Loop Plant Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02982)
*Yunji Kim,Yunseok Lee,Hyunwoo Seo,Jaerim Choi,Woojin Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PLCWorld is a closed-loop execution environment and benchmark that couples PLC Structured Text execution with simulated plant responses and sensor feedback, comprising 100 synthetic tasks (473 task-condition pairs) across Motion Control and Material Handling to evaluate LLM-generated PLC programs on Task Success and Safety Violation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating whether LLM-generated PLC programs meet task requirements and safety constraints requires observing how issued commands affect device and workpiece states. Existing evaluation settings lack a common closed-loop environment and benchmark for this purpose.

Method: PLCWorld couples ST execution with simulated plant responses and sensor feedback, grounded in control relations from industrial PLC programs and engineering documentation. It contains 100 synthetic tasks and 473 registered task-condition pairs, with difficulty defined by control-dependency scope. A common protocol reports Task Success and Safety Violation separately. Validation uses practitioner review, reference and alternative programs, targeted counterexamples, specification-evaluator alignment checks, and comparisons with independent ST runtimes. Execution Gap relates submission-profile acceptance to subsequent task failure or observed Safety Violation.

Result: Reference and alternative programs satisfy their applicable cases; all 542 targeted counterexamples activate their designated evaluator rules under at least one registered condition. Across constructed task groups, direct GPT-5.5 achieves 82.70% Task Success on Easy cases but 25.10% on Hard cases. Evaluations of six LLMs and four adapted generation-and-verification workflows expose differences between completion, safety, and generation cost.

Conclusion: PLCWorld provides a common closed-loop benchmark for LLM-generated PLC programs, showing a large Easy-to-Hard performance drop and differentiating LLMs and generation-and-verification workflows on task completion, safety, and cost. Code, simulation environment, benchmark tasks, and baseline implementations are publicly available.

Abstract: Programmable logic controllers (PLCs) coordinate industrial equipment by reading sensor inputs and issuing control commands. Evaluating whether large language model (LLM)-generated PLC programs satisfy task requirements and safety constraints requires observing how their commands affect device and workpiece states. We introduce PLCWorld, a common closed-loop execution environment and benchmark that couples Structured Text (ST) execution with simulated plant responses and sensor feedback. Grounded in control relations identified in industrial PLC programs and engineering documentation, PLCWorld contains 100 synthetic tasks and 473 registered task-condition pairs across Motion Control and Material Handling, with difficulty defined by control-dependency scope. A common protocol reports Task Success and Safety Violation separately. Validation combines practitioner review, reference and alternative programs, targeted counterexamples, specification-evaluator alignment checks, and comparisons with independent ST runtimes. Reference and alternative programs satisfy their applicable cases, while all 542 targeted counterexamples activate their designated evaluator rules under at least one registered condition. Execution Gap relates submission-profile acceptance to subsequent task failure or observed Safety Violation. Across the constructed task groups, direct GPT-5.5 achieves 82.70% Task Success on Easy cases but 25.10% on Hard cases. Evaluations of six LLMs and four adapted generation-and-verification workflows further expose differences between completion, safety, and generation cost. Our code, simulation environment, benchmark tasks, and baseline implementations are publicly available at https://yunji0516.github.io/PLCWorld/.

</details>


### [79] [Personalized Automatic Speech Recognition for a Dysarthric and Tracheostomic Speaker using Artificial Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03017)
*David Nadrchal,Monorama Swain,Florian Schmid,Gerhard Widmer,Paul Primus*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This work develops a personalized ASR system for a Czech speaker with a permanent tracheal stoma and severe dysarthria, releasing a 33-hour annotated dataset and a multi-stage Whisper Base fine-tuning pipeline that halves relative Character Error Rate and outperforms the speaker's human assistants on isolated utterances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speech from individuals with a permanent tracheal stoma and severe dysarthria is unintelligible to untrained listeners, making standard ASR systems ineffective. The authors aim to show that a helpful, personalized ASR system is achievable even for severely impeded speech, thereby restoring communication for such speakers.

Method: The authors collect 33 annotated hours of the target speaker's speech using a novel "artificial conversation" protocol designed for high engagement and dialogue realism, and release it publicly. They build on Whisper Base and apply a multi-stage training pipeline: fine-tuning on standard Czech speech, then on acoustically simulated tracheostomic speech, then on the speaker's own data. Evaluation covers three near real-time scenarios: scripted conversations, question answering, and spontaneous dialogue.

Result: The personalized system achieves a 50% relative reduction in Character Error Rate compared to the Whisper Base baseline, and surpasses the average recognition accuracy of the speaker's human assistants on isolated utterances. Positive quantitative results are corroborated by feedback from the speaker.

Conclusion: Even for severely impeded speech, a helpful and usable ASR system is achievable. The combination of a dedicated dataset, acoustic simulation of tracheostomic speech, and staged fine-tuning enables effective personalization, as evidenced by both quantitative metrics and the speaker's qualitative feedback.

Abstract: This work presents an automatic speech recognition (ASR) system personalized for a Czech speaker with a permanent tracheal stoma and severe dysarthria rendering their speech unintelligible to untrained listeners. We release a public dataset containing 33 annotated hours of the speaker's speech, collected using a novel "artificial conversation" protocol designed for high engagement and dialogue realism. We propose a multi-stage training pipeline based on Whisper Base: fine-tuning on standard Czech speech, acoustically simulated tracheostomic speech, and the speaker's data. We evaluate the system across three near real-time scenarios: scripted conversations, question answering, and spontaneous dialogue, achieving a 50\% relative reduction in Character Error Rate compared to Whisper Base baseline and surpassing the average recognition accuracy of their assistants in acoustic recognition of isolated utterances. We demonstrate that even for severely impeded speech, a helpful ASR is achievable, as evidenced by the quantitative results and the feedback from the speaker.

</details>


### [80] [DyadMem: A Long-Term Memory Benchmark of How Agents Work with Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03020)
*Yifei Tao,Xinyu Zhong,Henry Hengyuan Zhao,Fanyi Wang,Tengda Guo,Wentao Qiu,Ying Wang,Liujian Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces DyadMem, a dual-domain benchmark operationalizing User-conditioned Relational Agent Memory (URAM) alongside user-side memory, with fine-grained session-level annotations and a full-pipeline evaluation revealing that frontier LLMs still struggle with memory capture, recall, and safe deletion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term agents need to remember not only user facts/preferences but also how a specific agent should work with a given user as their shared history evolves (relationship-specific memory). Existing benchmarks only supervise user facts/preferences or cross-user reusable experience, leaving relationship-specific agent memory implicit. Prior evaluations also rely solely on final-answer QA over long histories, which is incomplete and unreliable.

Method: The authors propose DyadMem with a new definition, User-conditioned Relational Agent Memory (URAM). DyadMem jointly annotates user-side memory and URAM along the same multi-session trajectories, yielding 6 memory categories. It contains 3,065 episodes, 50,961 sessions, and 61,210 QA instances, with extensive session-level Capture and Update gold annotations, query-level Recall support, and two QA settings (Gold-Memory and Full-Pipeline). They evaluate 16 open-weight and 4 proprietary models, and run a rigorous experiment to validate URAM's effectiveness.

Result: Across 20 models, Gold-Memory QA is consistently strong, but Full-Pipeline QA drops sharply, supporting the fine-grained evaluation design. Quantitative results reveal low Capture recall, incomplete Recall, and unsafe-deletion issues even in frontier LLMs. The URAM validation experiment shows positive effects for all 20 models.

Conclusion: DyadMem is a dual-domain, full-pipeline memory benchmark with extensive annotation effort, advancing development of relationship-specific agent memory evaluation and exposing key failure modes in current LLMs.

Abstract: Long-term agents must remember not only what is true about a user, but also how a particular agent should work with that user as their shared history evolves. Existing benchmarks primarily supervise user facts and preferences or experience reusable across users, leaving this relationship-specific agent memory implicit. Additionally, most prior works measure the model solely with final-answer QA over long interaction histories, making the assessment still incomplete and unreliable. To this end, we introduce DyadMem with the proposed new definition User-conditioned Relational Agent Memory (URAM). DyadMem jointly annotates user-side memory and URAM along the same multi-session trajectories, resulting in 6 memory categories. To summarize, it includes 3,065 episodes, 50,961 sessions, and 61,210 QA instances, with extensive session-level Capture and Update gold annotations, query-level Recall support, and two QA settings: Gold-Memory and Full-Pipeline. Across 16 open-weight and 4 proprietary models, Gold-Memory QA is consistently strong, yet Full-Pipeline QA drops sharply. Such a gap explicitly supports our fine-grained evaluation design. Additionally, several quantitative results further reveal low Capture recall, incomplete Recall, and unsafe-deletion issues arising from even the frontier LLMs. We further conduct a rigorous experiment to validate the effectiveness of our URAM and observe the positive effects for all 20 models. In summary, DyadMem is a dual-domain, full-pipeline memory benchmark with extensive annotation efforts for advancing the domain's development.

</details>


### [81] [Verifiable, Articulable, and Tacit Components of Preference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03025)
*Alexander Spangher,Sheldon Huang,Andreas Haupt,Noah D. Goodman,Diyi Yang,Daniel E. Ho,Sanmi Koyejo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI models are typically improved via articulated constitutions, rubrics and verifiers, but the tacit components of preference are understudied. Using CreativePreferences, a 2.8M-text dataset labeled by 317M human judgments across 7 creative domains and 42 benchmark tasks, the authors quantify robust "articulability" and "verifiability" gaps between densely trained value models and executable programs or rubric banks, showing these gaps hold even in supposedly fully verifiable domains and widen as more people participate in judgment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Preference constructs like gripping stories, newsworthy articles and elegant proofs resist articulation or verification because their meaning is at least partially tacit. Yet modern AI improvement pipelines (RLAIF, RLVR) rely primarily on articulated constitutions, rubrics and verifiers, leaving the tacit component of preferences largely unstudied. The paper aims to measure how much of human preference is not captured by articulable or verifiable criteria.

Method: The authors introduce CreativePreferences, a large labeled preference dataset of 2.8M texts with 317M human preference judgments spanning 7 creative domains and 42 benchmark tasks. They model these labels using three approaches: executable programs, rubric banks, and densely trained models (V, A and VAT). They measure two gaps: an articulability gap (VAT-VA) and a verifiability gap (VAT-V). They estimate upper and lower bounds for each gap with a novel measurement approach that discovers articulable and verifiable metrics, identifies spurious variables, and estimates the value of undiscovered metrics using capture-recapture methods. They then analyze gap size across domains and as a function of the number of judges, and examine consequences for human-generation alignment and for preference shifts under articulation (analogous to Goodhart's law).

Result: The authors observe robust articulability gaps (VAT-VA) and verifiability gaps (VAT-V) across all domains, including correctness-centered domains (mathematics and software engineering) and claim- and novelty-centric domains (news, patents, peer review), which are traditionally treated as fully verifiable. Gap size varies by domain, with peer review and creative writing showing the largest articulability gaps, and widens as more people take part in the judgment, consistent with Collins' collective tacit knowledge. Two consequences are shown: (1) on human generations, the full model more closely matches human preferences, often disagreeing with articulated criteria, and (2) analogous to Goodhart's law, articulating preference shifts it away from the tacit dimension.

Conclusion: Articulability and verifiability gaps are consequential: a substantial portion of human preference is neither articulable nor verifiable, even in domains assumed verifiable, and it grows with collective participation. The authors give recommendations on when tasks can be prompted, how learning mechanisms might improve, and when judgments should be left to humans.

Abstract: What makes a short story gripping; a news article newsworthy; or a math proof elegant? These constructs resist articulation or verification; their meaning is at least partially tacit. However, modern AI models are improved primarily via articulated constitutions, rubrics and verifiers (i.e. in RLAIF and RLVR); tacit components of preferences are typically understudied. We introduce a large, labeled preference dataset CreativePreferences, containing 2.8M texts labeled by 317M human preference judgments across 7 creative domains, with 42 benchmark tasks. We model these labels with executable programs, rubric banks and densely trained models (V, A and VAT, respectively). We observe robust articulability gaps, VAT-VA; and verifiability gaps, VAT-V; we estimate upper and lower bounds for each gap with a novel measurement approach that discovers articulable and verifiable metrics, identifies spurious variables and estimates the value of undiscovered metrics using capture-recapture. These gaps occur across all domains, even in domains traditionally treated as fully verifiable: correctness-centered domains (i.e. mathematics and software engineering) and claim- and novelty-centric domains (i.e. news, patents, peer review). The size of the gap varies based on domain (e.g. peer review and creative writing have the largest articulability gaps) and widens as more people take part in the judgment, consistent with Collins' collective tacit knowledge. We show two consequences: (1) on human generations, the full model more closely matches human preferences, often in disagreement with articulated criteria, and (2) in an analogy to Goodhart's law, articulating preference shifts it away from the tacit dimension. Articulability and verifiability gaps are consequential; we give recommendations on when tasks can be prompted; how learning mechanisms might improve; and when to leave judgments with humans.

</details>


### [82] [SoftGene: Protein Language Model-Enhanced Soft Prompting for Interpretable Gene Set Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03029)
*Drew Ross,Arya Hadizadeh Moghaddam,Dongjie Wang,Xiaoyu Zhang,Zijun Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SoftGene is an LLM-based gene set annotation framework that combines protein-sequence embeddings with textual prompts to improve functional annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gene set analysis is labor-intensive and relies on manual curation and expert interpretation. Existing LLM approaches depend on symbolic gene names and overlook domain-specific biological structure, especially protein sequence information that governs molecular activity, interactions, and downstream gene function.

Method: The framework first uses a hierarchical attention-based encoder built on ESM (a protein language model) to represent each gene set from protein-level amino acid sequence information. It then constructs a hybrid prompting scheme combining soft prompts derived from gene set embeddings with hard prompts containing auxiliary context generated by an LLM, feeding the result into a local LLM for annotation.

Result: Evaluated on Gene Ontology (GO) and the Molecular Signatures Database (MSigDB), integrating protein-sequence representations with textual context improves gene set annotation overall, while per-domain analyses show that the contribution of protein embeddings varies across biological domains.

Conclusion: Combining protein-sequence representations with textual context via hybrid prompting enhances LLM-based gene set annotation, though the benefit of protein embeddings is domain-dependent.

Abstract: Gene set analysis is a cornerstone of functional genomics, yet it remains labor-intensive and heavily dependent on manual curation and expert biological interpretation. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for genomic reasoning and annotation, most existing approaches rely on symbolic gene names and fail to capture domain-specific biological structure, particularly protein sequence information that governs molecular activity, interactions, and downstream gene function. In this work, we propose SoftGene, a novel framework for LLM-based gene set annotation that leverages the hierarchical structure of gene sets. First, we use a hierarchical attention-based encoder built on ESM, a protein language model, to represent each gene set using protein-level amino acid sequence information. Second, we construct a hybrid prompting scheme that combines soft prompts derived from gene set embeddings with hard prompts containing auxiliary context generated by an LLM, and feed the resulting prompt into a local LLM for annotation. We evaluate our framework on two benchmark datasets: Gene Ontology (GO) and the Molecular Signatures Database (MSigDB). Our results show that integrating protein-sequence representations with textual context improves gene set annotation overall, while per-domain analyses reveal that the contribution of protein embeddings varies across biological domains.

</details>


### [83] [When Numbers Start Talking: Numerical Signalling and Strategic Behaviour Among LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03033)
*Alessio Buscemi,Daniele Proverbio,Alessandro Di Stefano,The Anh Han,German Castignani,Pietro Liò*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Message type (natural language, numerical signals, random sequences) alters cooperation payoffs in LLM-based multi-agent strategic games unpredictably, and agent-generated numerical messages deviate from randomness—especially under explicit communication instructions—making message-level fingerprints a better coordination-monitoring signal than behavioral decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents increasingly populate multi-agent systems characterized by strategic interaction, yet it remains unclear whether and how different message types affect strategic game outcomes. The paper asks whether message kind, interacting with assigned agent personalities, significantly shifts cooperation levels across games with distinct cooperation equilibria.

Method: Four popular LLMs as agents, each assigned personalities, play four strategic games with differing cooperation equilibria. The experimental manipulation varies the message modality: natural language, numerical signals, or random sequences. The authors measure resulting cooperation levels and final payoffs, and analyze the symbol/payoff distributions of agent-generated numerical messages for randomness, concentration under repetition, and human interpretability.

Result: Structured messages change final payoffs in most game–LLM combinations, but without a predictable pattern, undercutting the assumption that AI agents converge to stable equilibria irrespective of added capabilities. Agent-generated numerical messages depart from randomness, most strongly and consistently when agents are explicitly told to communicate. Their symbol distributions track the payoff structure and concentrate with repetition, yet remain hard for humans to interpret, adding an interpretability challenge.

Conclusion: Monitoring restricted-channel coordination among AI agents should prioritize message-level fingerprints—which generalize across models—over behavioral decisions, which do not. Message content itself, rather than observed actions, is the more robust and transferable detection surface.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agents increasingly operate in multi-agent systems (MAS) characterised by strategic interaction. However, little is known about whether, and to what extent, different types of messages affect the outcomes of strategic games. By investigating AI agents based on four popular LLMs, playing four games with different cooperation equilibria, we study whether messages of different kinds (natural language, numerical signals, or random sequences) significantly modify the levels of cooperation in each game, also depending on the agents' assigned personalities. We observe that structured messages alter the final payoffs for most games and LLMs, but without a predictable pattern; this challenges the assumption that AI agents can converge to stable equilibria regardless of additional capabilities. Moreover, we observe that agent-generated numerical messages depart from randomness, most strongly and consistently when agents are explicitly instructed to communicate; however, they introduce an additional interpretability challenge, as their symbol distributions are mostly associated with the payoff structure and typically become more concentrated with repetition, but are overall difficult for humans to interpret. Monitoring for coordination of AI agents through restricted channels should thus prioritise message-level fingerprints, which generalise across models, over behavioural decisions, which do not.

</details>


### [84] [hacktrace: behavior-supervised detection of reward hacking during code generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03055)
*Hao Jiang,Xin Li,Annan Wang,Yichi Zhang,Weisi Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper releases 173,561 annotated multi-turn coding trajectories and introduces HACKTRACE, a behavior-supervised monitor that detects reward hacking (including failed shortcuts) by reading the agent's internal states during code generation, achieving AUC 0.997 with 8 ms overhead and reducing cheating in passing solutions from 82–91% to 1–5% under GRPO penalties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A coding agent can earn a passing grade either by legitimately fixing its code or by deleting the test that exposes the bug—a form of reward hacking. Detecting such shortcuts, including attempted but unsuccessful ones, is essential for building trustworthy coding agents. The authors argue that both the supervision target (whether to supervise shortcut behavior independently of exploit success) and the source of monitoring evidence matter for turning accurate detection into a useful training signal.

Method: The authors release 173,561 annotated multi-turn coding trajectories from Qwen3-8B and show that supervising shortcut behavior independently of exploit success substantially improves detection. They introduce HACKTRACE, a behavior-supervised monitor that reads the internal states the agent already computes while generating code. Reusing these states enables monitoring before a turn is complete, without additional language-model tokens or passes. The monitor combines this internal-state evidence with static features of the final files. The same generation states also serve as an inexpensive monitoring signal for reinforcement learning.

Result: HACKTRACE achieves a mean per-problem AUC of 0.997 with 8 ms of monitoring overhead, improving both accuracy and latency over monitors that re-run the model on an honesty question-and-answer. Under strong GRPO penalties, HACKTRACE reduces the cheating share of passing solutions from 82–91% to 1–5%, while retaining honest, correct solutions and maintaining high detection accuracy as the policy evolves.

Conclusion: Supervising shortcut behavior independently of exploit success, combined with reusing the agent's own generation states as monitoring evidence, yields a monitor that is both accurate and efficient. HACKTRACE turns detection into an effective training signal, suppressing reward hacking in coding agents without sacrificing honest, correct solutions, and maintaining high detection accuracy as the policy evolves.

Abstract: A coding agent can earn a passing grade by fixing its code, or by deleting the test that exposes the bug. Detecting such reward hacking requires recognizing attempted shortcuts, including those that fail. We release 173,561 annotated multi-turn coding trajectories from Qwen3-8B and show that supervising shortcut behavior independently of exploit success substantially improves detection. We introduce HACKTRACE, a behavior-supervised monitor that reads the internal states the agent already computes while generating code. Reusing these states enables monitoring before a turn is complete, without additional language-model tokens or passes. Combining this evidence with static features of the final files achieves a mean per-problem AUC of 0.997 with 8 ms of monitoring overhead, improving both accuracy and latency over monitors that run the model again on an honesty question and answer. The same generation states also provide an inexpensive monitoring signal for reinforcement learning. With strong GRPO penalties, HACKTRACE reduces the cheating share of passing solutions from 82-91% to 1-5%, while retaining honest, correct solutions and maintaining high detection accuracy as the policy evolves. Our results show that both the supervision target and the source of monitoring evidence matter for turning accurate detection into a useful training signal.

</details>


### [85] [MOF-VERIFY: A Failure-Aware Agentic Harness for MOF Hypothesis Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03056)
*Donghyun Lee,Taehoon Lee,Geonhee Ahn,Jieun Kim,Jihyun Park,Suyeon Cho,Yoona Kim,Chaerim Shin,Hoi Ri Moon,Jonggeol Na,Sukho Hong,Jihwan Oh,Soo Kyung Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a diagnostic benchmark and an agentic harness (MOF-Verify) to improve the reliability of LLM-based hypothesis verification in MOF materials science, diagnosing failure modes across structural grounding, synthesis-condition checking, evidence sufficiency, and MLIP-based computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as reasoning components in AI-driven materials Co-Scientists, but the reliability of the resulting verification pipelines is unclear. MOFs pose a particularly challenging setting: structures may appear under different identifiers, synthesis outcomes depend strongly on experimental conditions, evidence is distributed across heterogeneous sources, and some hypotheses require computation rather than literature alone.

Method: A diagnostic benchmark with four task families: structural grounding, synthesis-condition verification, evidence-sufficiency verification, and MLIP-based computational verification. Tasks T-MOF-1-3 are evaluated under closed-book, retrieval-enabled, and oracle-evidence settings to localize failures in knowledge access, evidence acquisition, and reasoning; T-MOF-4 separately evaluates computational verification. Guided by the diagnosed failure modes, MOF-Verify is developed as a failure-aware agentic harness targeting structural, literature, evidence-sufficiency, and computational bottlenecks before producing a final verdict.

Result: Across multiple backbone LLMs, MOF-Verify substantially improves hypothesis-verification performance over direct inference and retrieval-based baselines. Benchmark datasets are released at https://github.com/IMMS-Ewha/MOF-Verify-Benchmark.

Conclusion: Diagnosing failure modes via a staged benchmark enables a failure-aware agentic harness that materially improves LLM-based verification of MOF hypotheses, with the benchmark publicly released to support further work.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used as reasoning components in AI-driven materials Co-Scientists, yet the reliability of the resulting verification pipeline remains unclear. Metal-organic frameworks (MOFs) provide a particularly challenging setting because structures may appear under different identifiers, synthesis outcomes depend strongly on experimental conditions, evidence is distributed across heterogeneous sources, and some hypotheses require computation rather than literature alone. We introduce a diagnostic benchmark with four task families covering structural grounding, synthesis-condition verification, evidence-sufficiency verification, and MLIP-based computational verification. T-MOF-1-3 are evaluated under closed-book, retrieval-enabled, and oracle-evidence settings to localize failures in knowledge access, evidence acquisition, and reasoning, while T-MOF-4 separately evaluates computational verification. Guided by these diagnosed failure modes, we develop MOF-Verify, a failure-aware agentic harness that targets structural, literature, evidence-sufficiency, and computational bottlenecks before producing a final verdict. Across multiple backbone LLMs, MOF-Verify substantially improves hypothesis-verification performance over direct inference and retrieval-based baselines. Benchmark datasets are released at https://github.com/IMMS-Ewha/MOF-Verify-Benchmark.

</details>


### [86] [RIFAR: Reliability and Forgetting-Aware Replay for Continual Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03079)
*Zirong Song,Zheng Lu,Haoran Liao,Wanqi Zhong,Yunhe Ni,Lijie Wang,Xiuying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RIFAR enables continual robot learning by generating past experience rather than storing it, using reliability screening and drift-aware replay selection to combat forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied robots need continual learning to acquire new skills without forgetting old ones. Experience replay reduces forgetting but storing full demonstrations becomes costly as tasks accumulate. World-action models (WAMs) can regenerate past experience generatively, but their visually plausible rollouts may include actions that fail to produce the predicted transitions, and adapting to new tasks can disrupt previously learned behavior.

Method: RIFAR combines reliability screening with drift-aware replay selection. It reconstructs trajectories from compact demonstration prefixes and uses a frozen inverse-dynamics model to check action-visual consistency. Training first mixes current demonstrations with the highest-quality screened trajectories. RIFAR then compares action predictions before and after adaptation on identical historical inputs, and reselects trajectories showing larger normalized drift from the same screened pool for continued training.

Result: Across three LIBERO suites and real-world experiments, RIFAR outperforms the prior state of the art in WAM-based generative replay. On LIBERO-Goal it reaches 90.97 AUC while retaining only 320 historical time steps per task, about 4.9% of the steps kept by 50-demonstration replay.

Conclusion: RIFAR shows that generative reconstruction with reliability screening and drift-aware selection can deliver strong continual learning at much lower storage cost than conventional replay.

Abstract: Genuine embodied agency requires robots to turn continuous real-world experience into lasting, transferable skills. This demands continual learning that integrates new capabilities without eroding prior knowledge as tasks and environments evolve. Experience replay mitigates forgetting, but storing complete demonstrations becomes costly as tasks accumulate. World-action models offer a generative alternative, reconstructing past experience through joint predictions of actions and future observations. However, visually coherent rollouts may contain actions that cannot realize the predicted transitions, while new-task adaptation can disrupt previously learned behavior. RIFAR therefore combines reliability screening with drift-aware replay selection. It reconstructs trajectories from compact demonstration prefixes and uses a frozen inverse-dynamics model to assess action-visual consistency. Training first combines current demonstrations with the highest-quality screened trajectories. RIFAR then compares action predictions before and after this adaptation on identical historical inputs, reselecting trajectories with larger normalized drift from the same screened pool for continued training. Across three LIBERO suites and real-world experiments, RIFAR surpasses the previous state of the art in WAM-based generative replay. On LIBERO-Goal, it achieves 90.97 AUC while retaining only 320 historical time steps per task, approximately 4.9% of the steps retained using 50-demonstration replay.

</details>


### [87] [Peer Influence across Heterogeneous AI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03095)
*Frida Nøhr Laustsen,Marie Haahr Petersen,Victoria Popa,Ariel Flint,Romualdo Pastor-Satorras,Andrea Baronchelli,Luca Maria Aiello*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Across seven open-weight LLMs and three language understanding tasks, persuasion after a single exchange with a dissenting peer is strong and is driven more by the listener's susceptibility than the speaker's persuasiveness or model scale, making outcomes specific to each model pairing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent systems increasingly combine language models of different families and sizes, so when two agents disagree it matters which judgment survives interaction. The paper asks who persuades whom and whether persuasion can be predicted from individual model properties.

Method: The authors measure persuasion as the probabilistic shift in an agent's decision after a single exchange with a dissenting peer, testing seven open-weight models across three language understanding tasks.

Result: Persuasion is strong: receivers often abandon their initial judgment after seeing a peer's answer and explanation. Neither standalone certainty nor model scale reliably predicts persuasion dynamics; models with near-perfect decision consistency in isolation can be highly susceptible, and small models can match larger ones as persuaders and resist influence equally well. Shift size depends more on listener susceptibility than speaker persuasiveness, and patterns are pairing-specific, with heterogeneity amplifying persuasion in some combinations and suppressing it in others.

Conclusion: A dissenting agent running a small model can overturn the judgments of a much larger one. Interacting models' behavior cannot be inferred from their individual properties and must be evaluated in the specific combinations in which they will operate.

Abstract: When two AI agents disagree, who persuades whom? As multi-agent systems increasingly combine language models of different families and sizes, the answer can determine which judgments survive interaction. Measuring persuasion as the probabilistic shift in an agent's decision after a single exchange with a dissenting peer, we test seven open-weight models across three language understanding tasks. We find that persuasion is strong: when models disagree, receivers often abandon their initial judgment after seeing a peer's answer and explanation. Surprisingly, however, neither standalone certainty nor model scale reliably predicts persuasion dynamics. Models producing almost perfectly consistent decisions in isolation can be among the most susceptible to persuasion, and small models can match larger ones as persuaders and resist their influence just as effectively. Furthermore, we show that the size of the shift depends more on the susceptibility of the listener than on the persuasiveness of the speaker. Persuasion patterns are therefore specific to each model pairing, with heterogeneity amplifying persuasion in some combinations and suppressing it in others, allowing a dissenting agent running a small model to overturn the judgments of a much larger one. These findings show that the behavior of interacting models cannot be inferred from their individual properties but must be evaluated in the combinations in which they will operate.

</details>


### [88] [Predictor-Guided Latent Space Codon Optimization for Maximizing Protein Expression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03098)
*Alberto Caron,Tianyu Cui,Dmytro S. Lituiev,Mangal Prakash,Artem Moskalev,Amina Mollaysa,Bo Zhai,Hirsh Nanda,Daniel M. Poole,Zhongyin Liu,Iman Farasat,Robert Davidson,Nikolay V. Manyakov,Tommaso Mansi,Scott Oloff,Rui Liao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LSCO reframes discrete codon optimization as continuous optimization in the latent space of a pretrained mRNA language model, enabling gradient-based search and outperforming frequency-based and deep generative baselines on a wet-lab antibody expression dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Codon optimization is critical for therapeutic protein production and mRNA vaccines, but the design space is discrete and combinatorially large, preventing gradient-based methods. Existing tools depend on heuristic proxies such as the Codon Adaptation Index or GC-content that poorly reflect true expression.

Method: LSCO maps sequences into the latent space of a pretrained mRNA language model to convert the discrete problem into a continuous one, enabling efficient gradient-based search. It integrates four components: a data-driven expression objective from an uncertainty-aware predictor, a Minimum-Free-Energy regularizer for structural stability, a naturalness prior from a protein-to-codon back-translation model, and constrained decoding for protein fidelity.

Result: On a real-world wet-lab antibody expression dataset, LSCO outperforms simple frequency-based and modern deep generative baselines in predicted expression while retaining suitable biophysical properties.

Conclusion: LSCO offers an effective continuous latent-space framework for codon optimization, surpassing heuristic and deep generative baselines and preserving favorable biophysical characteristics.

Abstract: Codon optimization, the process of selecting synonymous codons to improve mRNA translation efficiency and protein expression, is central to therapeutic protein production and mRNA vaccines, yet it remains a hard problem. The design space is discrete and combinatorially large, precluding gradient-based methods, and existing tools rely on heuristic proxies (e.g., Codon Adaptation Index or GC-content) that poorly capture true expression. We introduce Latent-Space Codon Optimization (LSCO), which recasts this discrete problem as a continuous one by mapping sequences into the latent space of a pretrained mRNA language model, enabling efficient gradient-based search. LSCO combines four components: a data-driven expression objective from an uncertainty-aware predictor, a Minimum-Free-Energy regularizer for structural stability, a naturalness prior from a protein-to-codon back-translation model, and constrained decoding for protein fidelity. On a real-world, wet-lab antibody expression dataset, LSCO outperforms simple frequency-based, as well as modern deep generative baselines in predicted expression, while retaining suitable biophysical properties.

</details>


### [89] [Trading Strategy Optimization via Textual Gradient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03128)
*Chaoqun Yang,Qian Wang,Fengbin Zhu,Xinyu Lin,Bingsheng He,Roger Zimmermann,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TradeGrad is an experience-guided textual-gradient framework for robust quantitative trading strategy optimization, introducing multi-scale revisions and a Cross-Period Robust Objective (CPRO). It achieves state-of-the-art in-sample and out-of-sample performance across Chinese A-share and U.S. equity markets in both cross-sectional and time-series settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based textual gradients provide explicit optimization directions for iterative trading strategy refinement, but direct application suffers from two issues: (1) myopic optimization that underutilizes accumulated experience from previous evaluations, and (2) aggregate backtest feedback that ignores temporal robustness, favoring strategies that work only in specific market periods.

Method: TradeGrad is an experience-guided textual-gradient framework that estimates textual gradients using accumulated optimization experience and applies multi-scale revisions for strategy exploration and refinement.
