It introduces the Cross-Period Robust Objective (CPRO), which emphasizes performance in unfavorable historical periods to promote temporal robustness.

Result: On cross-sectional and time-series strategy design in Chinese A-share and U.S. equity markets, TradeGrad achieves the best in-sample and out-of-sample performance across all four settings. Its Chinese cross-sectional strategy reaches 27.99% annualized return, 12.19% maximum drawdown, and a Sharpe ratio of 1.63—about 68% higher than the CSI 300 benchmark. Ablations validate the components and show consistent improvements throughout optimization.

Conclusion: TradeGrad effectively addresses myopic optimization and temporal robustness in LLM-driven trading strategy design, delivering superior and robust performance across markets and strategy types. Code is available at https://github.com/transcend-0/TradeGrad.

Abstract: Quantitative trading strategy design aims to discover trading programs from historical data that remain effective in future markets, which can be viewed as a black-box program optimization problem. LLM-based textual gradients offer a promising approach by providing explicit optimization directions for iterative strategy refinement. However, directly applying textual gradients faces two challenges: (1) optimization is myopic, underutilizing experience from previous evaluations; and (2) aggregate backtest feedback overlooks temporal robustness, potentially favoring strategies that perform well only in specific market periods. To address these challenges, we propose TradeGrad, an experience-guided textual-gradient framework for robust trading strategy optimization. TradeGrad leverages accumulated optimization experience to estimate textual gradients and employs multi-scale revisions for both strategy exploration and refinement. It further introduces the Cross-Period Robust Objective (CPRO), which emphasizes performance in unfavorable historical periods to promote temporal robustness. Experiments on cross-sectional and time-series strategy design in Chinese A-share and U.S. equity markets show that TradeGrad achieves the best in-sample and out-of-sample performance across all four settings. Notably, its Chinese cross-sectional strategy achieves 27.99% annualized return, 12.19% maximum drawdown, and a Sharpe ratio of 1.63, approximately 68% higher than the CSI 300 benchmark. Further analyses validate the proposed components and show consistent improvements in both in-sample and out-of-sample performance throughout optimization. The code is available at https://github.com/transcend-0/TradeGrad.

</details>


### [90] [Keeping JEPA World Models Plannable When Little of the Frame Moves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03137)
*Florian Strohm,Patrick Wagner,Jannik Schwab,Marco Huber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces SLIM, a language-conditioned pushing benchmark, and shows that a Latent World Model (LeWM) succeeding on PushT fails catastrophically on SLIM (<1% success). The failure is traced to an action-insensitive encoder latent; adding an inverse-dynamics auxiliary loss restores action sensitivity, fixes all diagnostic probes, and boosts success from 0.003 to 0.35, enabling a small language-goal head to plan from sentences without retraining the world model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Specifying goals in natural language (rather than as explicit goal frames) is a natural interface for latent-world-model planning. However, evaluating whether language can discriminate among multiple objects requires benchmarks with several objects and paired visual/language goals on identical scenes—something existing benchmarks like PushT lack. The authors aim to expose and diagnose this gap.

Method: They build SLIM, a pushing benchmark with several small objects and paired visual and language goals on identical scenes. They evaluate a LeWM latent world model, then use probes to localize failures (action sensitivity, position decodability, rollout quality). They add a single inverse-dynamics auxiliary loss applied to encoder latents and to predicted latents via a shared head discarded at test time. Controls isolate the gradient into the encoder, and an action-sensitivity probe (environment-free) is proposed as a necessary condition. A small language-goal head is trained on the repaired latent to plan from sentences.

Result: A scripted controller with simulator state solves every tier, but LeWM solves under 1% of SLIM trials. Probes locate the failure in the encoder: the latent is nearly action-insensitive, neither pusher nor object positions decode, and rollouts equal copying the current latent. The inverse-dynamics loss restores every probe and raises success from 0.003 to 0.35 (0.16 on the hard tier, where a goal-agnostic policy scores zero), and improves PushT at twice the trained horizon. All configurations below the action-sensitivity probe threshold failed to plan. The language-goal head reaches 0.84 on navigation (visual-goal oracle 1.00), follows swapped named zones over decoys, and degrades gracefully to unseen nouns. Stage-sentence sequences raise medium/hard pushing success from 0.04 to 0.25, on par with the goal-frame oracle and robust to reading stage switches from the latent alone.

Conclusion: An action-insensitive encoder is the root cause of latent-world-model planning failure under language goals. A single inverse-dynamics auxiliary loss repairs the encoder, enabling planning from language and stage-sequence instructions without retraining the world model. A cheap environment-free action-sensitivity probe serves as an empirical necessary condition for planability.

Abstract: Specifying a goal in language rather than as a goal frame is a natural interface for planning with a latent world model, but testing it needs scenes in which language must discriminate between several objects. We build SLIM, a pushing benchmark with several small objects and paired visual and language goals on identical scenes. On SLIM a LeWM world model that solves PushT succeeds on under 1% of trials, although a scripted controller with simulator state solves every tier. Probes locate the failure in the encoder: its latent is nearly action-insensitive, neither pusher nor object positions can be decoded from it, and rollouts are no better than copying the current latent forward. One inverse-dynamics auxiliary loss, applied to encoder latents and to predicted latents through a shared head discarded at test time, restores every probe and raises success from 0.003 to 0.35 (0.16 on the hard pushing tier, where a goal-agnostic policy scores zero), and improves PushT at twice the trained horizon. Controls attribute the repair to the gradient into the encoder, and a response sweep shows that the vanilla model plans once enough of the frame responds to actions. A cheap action-sensitivity probe, computable without environment access, acts as an empirical necessary condition: all configurations below its threshold failed to plan. On the repaired latent, a small language-goal head plans from sentences without retraining the world model: it reaches 0.84 on navigation (visual-goal oracle 1.00), follows the named zone when it is swapped with a decoy, and degrades gracefully to unseen nouns. A single goal sentence rarely completes a push, but given the push as a sequence of stage sentences the head raises success on the medium and hard pushing tiers from 0.04 to 0.25, on par with the goal-frame oracle, also when the switch between stages is read from the latent alone.

</details>


### [91] [Gains and Collapse in On-Policy Distillation:A Reinforcement Learning Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03185)
*Han Cui,Jianhao Yan,Yun Luo,Hongbo Zhang,Zhizhang Fu,Yue Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: On-policy distillation can collapse into verbose, repetitive text because the teacher acts as an implicit reward model; when this implicit reward misaligns with quality, reward hacking occurs, and masking unhealthy responses or SFT initialization mitigates the collapse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy distillation (OPD) yields post-training gains for language models but can also collapse into excessively long, repetitive generation. The mechanism behind these divergent outcomes is poorly understood, motivating a principled explanation.

Method: The authors adopt a reinforcement learning perspective in which the teacher implicitly rewards student behaviors, including those the teacher rarely exhibits itself. They conduct experiments to test whether OPD expands student capabilities or merely reshapes the sampling distribution, and they diagnose reward hacking when the implicit reward misaligns with response quality. They then evaluate mitigations: masking unhealthy responses during training and SFT initialization.

Result: OPD improves performance without expanding the student's capabilities. When the implicit reward model is reliable, OPD makes correct responses easier to sample. When preference misaligns with quality, reward hacking emerges: the implicit reward amplifies overlong, repetitive student rollouts even though the teacher rarely generates such text. Masking unhealthy responses during training and SFT initialization each effectively mitigate the collapse.

Conclusion: OPD amplifies student behaviors favored by the teacher's implicit feedback, shifting the focus from how well the teacher generates to how reliably it evaluates student rollouts. Code is available at https://github.com/HancCui/opd_hacking.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has become an important approach to language model post-training. However, despite its performance gains, OPD can also collapse into excessively long and repetitive generation, and the mechanism underlying these divergent outcomes remains poorly understood. We explain these outcomes through a reinforcement learning perspective: the teacher implicitly rewards student behaviors, even those it rarely exhibits itself. From this perspective, our experiments show that OPD improves performance without expanding the student's capabilities. When the implicit reward model is reliable, OPD makes correct responses easier to sample. In contrast, when the preference misaligns with quality, reward hacking happens: the implicit reward model amplifies overlong, repetitive student rollouts, even though it rarely generates such text itself. Guided by this diagnosis, we find that masking unhealthy responses during training and using SFT initialization can each effectively mitigate the collapse. Together, these findings show that OPD amplifies student behaviors favored by the teacher's implicit feedback, shifting the focus from how well the teacher generates to how reliably it evaluates student rollouts. Our code is available at https://github.com/HancCui/opd_hacking.

</details>


### [92] [KV$^2$: A Self-Refining KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03198)
*Johannes Wesch,Danni Liu,Jan Niehues*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KV² is a query-agnostic KV-cache compression method using selective reconstruction: a lightweight proxy scorer selects informative tokens, which are then selectively reprocessed to compute final eviction scores, achieving strong quality at tight budgets with lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The KV-cache memory footprint limits practical long-context model use and dominates cost when a single prefilled context serves many queries. In this reusable setting, query-agnostic compression faces a trade-off: lightweight estimators are cheap but inaccurate, while full-context reconstruction scoring is accurate but reprocesses the whole prompt.

Method: KV² performs selective reconstruction. It first applies a lightweight proxy scorer to identify informative in-context tokens, then reprocesses only this selected subset to compute final eviction scores, avoiding full-context reprocessing.

Result: On RULER, Needle-in-a-Haystack, and LongBench, KV²'s margin over baselines widens as the budget tightens. On RULER 16K at a 2% KV-cache budget it improves the average score over the next-best baseline by more than 40 percentage points; on LongBench it attains the highest average across 2%-10% budgets with lower compression-stage runtime and peak memory than full-context reconstruction.

Conclusion: Reusable KV-cache compression does not require reprocessing the full context. Selective reconstruction delivers superior quality at tight budgets with reduced compression cost.

Abstract: The memory footprint of the key-value (KV) cache constrains the practical use of long-context models, and it dominates cost when one prefilled context must later serve many different queries. In this reusable setting, query-agnostic compression trades cost against quality: lightweight estimators are cheap but less accurate, whereas full-context reconstruction scoring is more accurate yet reprocesses the entire prompt. We introduce KV$^2$, a query-agnostic KV-cache compression method based on selective reconstruction. KV$^2$ first uses a lightweight proxy scorer to identify informative in-context tokens, then reprocesses only this subset to compute final eviction scores. On RULER, Needle-in-a-Haystack, and LongBench, KV$^2$'s margin over baselines widens as the budget tightens: on RULER 16K at a 2% KV-cache budget it improves the average score over the next-best baseline by more than 40 percentage points, and on LongBench it attains the highest average across 2%-10% budgets at lower compression-stage runtime and peak memory than full-context reconstruction. Reusable KV-cache compression thus does not require reprocessing the full context. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/KVsquared-0B97.

</details>


### [93] [Toward SLM-based agentic task-tool intent matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03213)
*Chiara Troiani,Arash Salarian,Majed El Helou,Benjamin Ryder,Jean Diaconu,Hervé Muyal,Marcelo Yannuzzi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates using Small Language Models (SLMs) as per-call task-tool relevance classifiers to verify whether each tool call by an AI agent is a logical, relevant step toward the task intent, going beyond conventional authorization. A novel dataset of multi-tool tasks spanning distinct MCP servers is introduced, and SLMs are optimized via prompt optimization, supervised fine-tuning, and GRPO reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agentic systems grow horizontally, the number of tool interactions increases, motivating automated, low-latency, on-prem per-call oversight. Conventional authorization can decide whether an agent may invoke a tool, but cannot assess the agent's underlying cognition—whether the tool selection is a logical, relevant step toward the task intent. Thus an allowed call may still deviate from intent: a rogue agent could deviate calls or nudge other agents into intent-misaligned call combinations. Every call therefore needs to be verified.

Method: An SLM serves as a task-tool relevance classifier that independently evaluates each selected tool against the assigned task and returns a relevance signal for downstream enforcement. The authors introduce a novel dataset of multi-tool tasks whose required tools span distinct Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, and optimize/specialize SLMs using prompt optimization, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning via GRPO.

Result: The abstract reports the dataset construction and the application of three optimization approaches (prompt optimization, SFT, GRPO) to specialize SLMs for task-tool relevance classification. Specific quantitative results are not stated in the provided abstract.

Conclusion: SLMs are explored as a viable mechanism for automated per-call oversight of AI agent tool calls, evaluating relevance to task intent independently of authorization, with specialization achieved through prompt optimization, supervised fine-tuning, and GRPO-based reinforcement learning.

Abstract: Tool-equipped AI agents use tool calls to access data and act on external systems. Horizontal growth of agentic systems increases the number of these interactions, and further motivates the need for automated, per-call oversight that can operate at low latency and/or on-prem. Conventional authorization schemes can determine whether an agent is allowed to invoke a tool, but cannot assess the agent's underlying cognition, specifically, whether the tool selection represents a logical, relevant step toward satisfying the intent of the task or not. Consequently, an allowed call may still deviate from the task's intent: a rogue agent might deviate the calls or nudge other agents to make a combination of calls that would not align with the intent of the task. Therefore, every call needs to be verified. In this study we investigate the applicability of Small Language Models (SLMs) to this purpose: an SLM functions as a task-tool relevance classifier that evaluates every selected tool independently against the assigned task and returns a relevance signal for downstream enforcement. Equipped with a novel dataset with multi-tool tasks whose required tools span distinct Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers, we used prompt-optimization, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning through GRPO to optimize and specialize SLMs.

</details>


### [94] [Learning a Fact Is Not Learning How to Retrieve It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03251)
*Chaemin Jang,Jihee Kim,Dongman Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Retrieval of a fact depends on prior experience with the request form and on a pre-answer hidden state ("context state"); shaping either during learning changes later retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Models can recall a fact under one phrasing ("The capital of X is") yet fail under an equivalent one ("The capital of X:"). The paper aims to disentangle learning a fact from learning how to retrieve it, and to identify the mechanism that governs this form-dependent retrieval.

Method: Two-stage training. Stage 1 (request-form training): one model sees each fact in five surface forms, the other only as statements. Stage 2 (target-fact training): both models receive identical training on new facts, all as statements. The authors then compare retrieval across request forms and analyze the hidden state immediately before the answer (the "context state"), including interventions that modify this state at retrieval time and only during target-fact training.

Result: After stage 2, both models retrieve new facts almost equally well from statements but diverge sharply on other request forms. The model exposed to five forms in stage 1 yields more similar context states across different request forms for the same fact. Manipulating the context state at test time can enable or prevent retrieval of an already learned fact, and the effect transfers across facts and factual relations (e.g., capitals and currencies). Altering the context state only during target-fact training changes later retrieval without any test-time intervention.

Conclusion: Later retrieval is jointly determined by earlier request-form experience and the context state during fact learning. A model can acquire the retrieval procedure for a request form before it learns the facts themselves, so fact learning and retrieval learning are separable processes mediated by the context state.

Abstract: A model trained on "The capital of X is Y" may produce "Y" after "The capital of X is" but fail after "The capital of X:". We call these different ways of eliciting the same fact request forms. To separate learning a fact from retrieving it, we train two models in two stages. In the first stage (request-form training), one model sees each fact in five forms and the other sees the same facts only as statements. In the second stage (target-fact training), both receive identical training on new facts, all as statements. Both then retrieve the new facts almost equally well from statements, but differ sharply on other request forms. Thus, a model can learn how to retrieve through a request form before it learns the facts. To understand this difference, we examine the hidden state immediately before the answer, which we call the context state. When given two different request forms for the same fact, the model trained on five forms in stage one produces more similar context states than the model trained on statements alone in that stage. Changing this state at retrieval time can enable or prevent retrieval of an already learned fact, and the same effect transfers across facts and factual relations, such as capitals and currencies. To test its role during learning, we change the context state only during target-fact training. This intervention changes later retrieval without intervention at test time. Together, these results show that later retrieval depends on earlier request-form experience and the context state during fact learning.

</details>


### [95] [EVOL: Simulator-Guided Evolutionary Expert Synthesis for Deployment-Free Learning Path Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03273)
*Geonwoo Bang,Dongho Kim,Moohong Min*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EVOL imports simulator-based demonstration learning from robotics into learning path recommendation: a knowledge tracing simulator synthesizes per-learner expert paths via evolutionary search, which are distilled into a feed-forward deployment-free policy using an asymmetric actor-critic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL for learning path recommendation faces two coupled obstacles: (1) the policy must commit to a sequence of L concepts without intermediate feedback, yielding a combinatorial space growing super-exponentially in L with reward only at the final step; (2) expert learning paths, the natural remedy for sparse-reward RL, are unavailable because student logs record observed behavior rather than optimal paths.

Method: The framework uses a knowledge tracing simulator to both synthesize per-learner expert demonstrations via evolutionary search and train a deployment-free policy that distills them into a feed-forward learner. EVOL implements this with an asymmetric actor-critic: the actor performs deployment-realistic blind planning, while the critic uses privileged simulator state during training. Three imitation strategies (BC, AWR, DAPG) are compared.

Result: On ASSIST15, Junyi, and EdNet (39-189 concepts) with path lengths L = 5, 10, 20, EVOL outperforms 8 baselines covering heuristic, sequential, RL, graph-enhanced RL, and LLM-enhanced methods. Final performance is governed by evolutionary expert quality rather than the choice of imitation objective.

Conclusion: Importing simulator-based demonstration learning to LPR resolves both the combinatorial blind-planning search and the absence of expert paths; quality of synthesized demonstrations dominates the imitation objective in determining performance.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) for learning path recommendation (LPR) faces two coupled obstacles. First, the policy must commit to a sequence of L concepts without intermediate feedback, producing a combinatorial search space that grows super-exponentially with L and provides reward only at the final step. Second, expert learning paths would be the natural cure for sparse-reward RL, but they do not exist in educational data, because student logs record what learners did, not what they should have done. We address both obstacles by importing a recipe from simulator-based demonstration learning in robotics: the knowledge tracing simulator is used both to synthesize per-learner expert demonstrations through evolutionary search and to train a deployment-free policy that distills these demonstrations into a feed-forward learner. Our framework, EVOL, instantiates this pipeline with an asymmetric actor-critic where the actor commits to deployment-realistic blind planning while the critic exploits the privileged simulator state during training. Across three datasets (ASSIST15, Junyi, and EdNet; 39-189 concepts) and path lengths L = 5, 10, and 20, EVOL surpasses 8 baselines spanning heuristic, sequential, RL, graph-enhanced RL, and LLM-enhanced methods. We further compare three imitation strategies (BC, AWR, and DAPG) and show that final performance is governed by the quality of evolutionary experts rather than by the particular imitation objective.

</details>


### [96] [JOVE: Joint Execution and Verification for Resource-Aware LLM Task Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03296)
*Haoran Zhang,Dongjun Kim,Seohyeon Cha,Kevin S Chan,Ananthram Swami,Gustavo De Veciana,Haris Vikalo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JOVE is an online framework that jointly assigns executor LLMs to subtasks of a decomposed reasoning DAG and selects intermediate outputs for paid, asynchronous verification, balancing execution spending against learning under a long-term budget and per-query latency constraint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex reasoning queries can be decomposed into directed acyclic task graphs and distributed across heterogeneous LLMs, cutting latency via parallelism and letting smaller models tackle complex tasks. In practice, an LLM's suitability for a given subtask is a priori unknown, and execution alone does not reveal output correctness, so principled allocation and verification are needed.

Method: JOVE solves a sequence of per-query mixed-integer linear programs to make execution and verification decisions. Online learning updates task-dependent estimates of LLM quality from verification feedback, and an information-gain bonus embeds the value of learning into allocation decisions. Verification runs asynchronously and feeds future allocations.

Result: Under a natural set of assumptions, JOVE attains sublinear quality-learning regret. Across four reasoning benchmarks, it achieves competitive accuracy versus standard inference baselines while reducing average cost and latency by at least 3.17 times.

Conclusion: JOVE shows that jointly optimizing executor assignment and paid verification via online learning and per-query MILPs can match baseline accuracy at substantially lower cost and latency, with theoretical regret guarantees.

Abstract: Complex reasoning queries can be decomposed into directed acyclic task graphs and distributed across heterogeneous LLMs, reducing latency through parallelism and enabling smaller models to solve complex tasks. In practice, however, the suitability of an LLM for a given subtask may be a priori unknown, and execution alone does not reveal output correctness. We propose JOVE, an online framework that jointly assigns executor LLMs and selects intermediate outputs for paid verification. Verification runs asynchronously and is used to improve future allocations, so the system must balance spending on execution now against learning for later. We study how to optimize this trade-off under a long-term budget and a per-query latency constraint, with stochastic, initially unknown LLM service quality, invocation costs, and execution times. JOVE makes execution and verification decisions by solving a sequence of per-query mixed-integer linear programs. Online learning updates task-dependent estimates of LLM quality based on verification feedback, while an information-gain bonus incorporates the value of learning into allocation decisions. Under a natural set of assumptions, we establish sublinear quality-learning regret for JOVE. Across four reasoning benchmarks, JOVE achieves competitive accuracy against standard inference baselines while reducing average cost and latency by at least 3.17 times.

</details>


### [97] [Optimal Planning in a Dynamic World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03312)
*Devin Wild Thomas,Solomon Eyal Shimony,Wheeler Ruml,Erez Karpas,Shahaf S. Shperberg,Andrew Coles*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "any-start-time planning" where the execution start time is unknown, and proposes a compact data structure (compound arrival time function, cATF) plus heuristic graph-search algorithms that encode the optimal plan as a function of start time, proving linear-size bounds and showing strong empirical gains over replanning on SIPP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many domains (e.g., moving-obstacle path planning / SIPP, boarding a train only while stopped), the set of feasible states or actions changes over time, so the optimal plan and its duration depend on when execution begins. In practice, the start time is often unknown until planning finishes or another agent signals go-ahead, yet most prior work either ignores dynamism or assumes a known start time—simplifying feasibility checks but impractical for these applications.

Method: The authors relax the known-start-time assumption, formally define the any-start-time planning setting, and present a data structure called a compound arrival time function (cATF) that compactly encodes the optimal plan as a function of start time. They develop general-purpose planning algorithms based on heuristic graph search that assemble cATFs by propagating functions along edges rather than scalar costs.

Result: They prove that the size of a cATF is at most linear in the problem size. An experimental evaluation of an implementation for SIPP shows that, on difficult problems, agents relying on replanning often fail, whereas any-start-time algorithms using cATFs can quickly look up the optimal plan once the execution start time becomes known.

Conclusion: By enabling efficient representation and reasoning for time-dependent plans, this work lays a foundation for planning in dynamic worlds.

Abstract: Background: We address the problem of planning when the set of feasible states or actions changes over time. For example, in the problem of path planning among moving obstacles (sometimes known as SIPP), the feasibility of being at a particular location can change as the obstacles move. Or, the action of boarding a particular train is feasible only while it is stopped at the station. This dynamism means that the optimal plan and its duration can change depending on when execution begins. In practice, execution start time is often unknown until planning has completed or another agent gives the go-ahead. However, most prior planning work either ignores dynamism or assumes a known start time. This makes it straightforward to assess state and action feasibility but is impractical for some applications. Objectives: In this paper, we relax the assumption of a known start time. We define the setting of {\em any-start-time planning} and provide algorithms for it. Methods: We present a data structure called a compound arrival time function (cATF) that compactly encodes the optimal plan as a function of start time. We provide general-purpose planning algorithms, based on heuristic graph search, that assemble cATFs by propagating functions along edges instead of scalar costs. Results: We prove that the size of a cATF is at most linear in the problem size. An experimental evaluation of an implementation for the specific problem of SIPP shows that, on difficult problems, agents that rely on replanning often fail, while any-start-time algorithms using cATFs can quickly look up the optimal plan once the execution start time is known. Conclusions: By enabling efficient representations and reasoning for time-dependent plans, this work provides a foundation for planning in dynamic worlds.

</details>


### [98] [Lightweight, Rubric-Guided Trajectory Evaluation for Production AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03315)
*Linh-An Phan,MingXue Wang,Guangyu Wu,Feng Pan,Zhaoyu Pang,Yanbin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LiteTrajEval is a lightweight, budget-bounded architecture for evaluating LLM agent trajectories offline-derived rule profiles and an online pipeline that marks heuristic failure signals, serializes traces under a fixed token budget, and uses a single rubric-guided LLM judge. It outperforms AgentRx on failure-localization alignment while cutting cost and latency substantially.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trajectory evaluation is key to improving LLM-based agent reliability, but repeated production evaluation is expensive. Modern agents emit long traces with tool calls, observations, retries, and external outputs, and not all raw tokens are equally useful for diagnosis, creating a need for cheaper, effective evaluation.

Method: LiteTrajEval first derives compact domain-specific rule profiles offline. Online, it preprocesses each trajectory, marks heuristic failure signals, serializes the trajectory under a fixed global budget, and invokes a single rubric-guided LLM judge to produce structured diagnostic reports.

Result: On public Magentic-One-style and τ-bench-style trajectory datasets, LiteTrajEval improves failure-localization alignment with human annotations by roughly 20–35 percentage points on Magentic-One and up to 23 percentage points on τ-retail versus AgentRx, while reducing cost by about 6× and evaluation time by more than 8×. It has been deployed in an enterprise agentic platform.

Conclusion: LiteTrajEval provides a cost- and time-efficient, budget-bounded approach to LLM agent trajectory evaluation with substantially better failure-localization alignment than AgentRx, and is practical enough for enterprise deployment.

Abstract: Trajectory evaluation is essential for improving the reliability of LLM-based agents, but production use makes it expensive to run repeatedly. Modern agents generate long traces containing tool calls, observations, retries, and external outputs, while not all raw tokens are equally useful for diagnosis. We present \textit{LiteTrajEval}, a lightweight architecture for budget-bounded trajectory evaluation. LiteTrajEval derives compact domain-specific rule profiles offline, then preprocesses each trajectory online, marks heuristic failure signals, serializes it under a fixed global budget, and invokes a single rubric-guided LLM judge to produce structured diagnostic reports. Evaluated on public Magentic-One-style and $τ$-bench-style trajectory datasets, LiteTrajEval improves failure-localization alignment with human annotations by roughly 20--35 percentage points on Magentic-One and up to 23 percentage points on $τ$-retail compared with AgentRx, while reducing cost by about 6$\times$ and evaluation time by more than 8$\times$. This solution has also been deployed in our enterprise agentic platform.

</details>


### [99] [Multi-Task Evolution for Zero-Shot Cross-Problem Generalization using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03316)
*Zhouliang Xie,Changliang Zhou,Genghui Li,Zhenkun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MECo is an LLM-driven multi-task evolutionary framework that achieves zero-shot cross-problem heuristic generalization by maintaining task-conditioned populations, using a transfer gap to guide heuristic transfer/recombination, and selecting a compact heuristic set via a coverage-based criterion; it outperforms eight AHD baselines on 32 VRP/FJSP variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automating heuristic design for diverse combinatorial optimization problems via LLMs still relies on evaluation feedback from the problem being optimized, making generalization to new problem definitions using only source-task feedback a central open challenge.

Method: MECo maintains task-conditioned heuristic populations and computes a transfer gap based on cross-task population performance to guide interactions, enabling transfer and recombination of heuristics across tasks. A complementary selection criterion then builds a compact heuristic set by rewarding each member's additional coverage of source combinations. The resulting set is applied to target problems without further search or adaptation.

Result: On 32 problem variants spanning VRP and FJSP, MECo attains the lowest mean costs versus eight AHD baselines under equal budgets; on out-of-domain problems it surpasses the strongest baseline in each family. Integrating MECo's framework with different AHD methods improves their ID and OOD performance across both families.

Conclusion: MECo enables effective zero-shot cross-problem heuristic generalization and is method-agnostic, improving diverse AHD approaches, thereby supporting its broad applicability.

Abstract: Designing effective heuristics for diverse combinatorial optimization problems requires substantial expertise and repeated search. Large language models (LLMs) automate heuristic generation and refinement, but heuristic search typically depends on evaluation feedback from the problem being optimized. Generalizing to new problem definitions using only source-task feedback therefore remains a central challenge. We introduce MECo, an LLM-driven multi-task evolutionary framework for zero-shot cross-problem generalization. MECo maintains task-conditioned heuristic populations and uses a transfer gap based on cross-task population performance to guide their interactions. These interactions enable the transfer and recombination of heuristics. A complementary selection criterion then constructs a compact heuristic set by rewarding each member's additional coverage of source combinations. The selected set is applied to target problems without further search or adaptation. Experiments on 32 problem variants across vehicle routing (VRP) and flexible job-shop scheduling (FJSP) show that MECo achieves the lowest mean costs compared with eight automated heuristic design (AHD) baselines under the same budgets. On out-of-domain problems, it outperforms the strongest baseline in each family. Moreover, integrating the framework of MECo with different AHD methods improves their ID and OOD performance in both families, supporting its effectiveness across different methods.

</details>


### [100] [Refinement Buys Intelligibility, Search Buys Identity: What Test-Time Compute Buys in Masked-Diffusion TTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03320)
*Nityanand Mathur,Hamees Sayed,Ayush Pratap Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In masked-diffusion codec TTS, refinement steps and model depth scale asymmetrically across capabilities: steps close most of the intelligibility gap but less than half of the speaker-identity gap, so depth and steps should be optimized separately.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion TTS models combine two computation axes—model depth (parameters) and refinement steps (inference budget). The paper asks whether these axes scale equally across different capabilities, i.e., whether intelligibility and speaker identity benefit comparably from added depth versus added refinement.

Method: They train 15 masked-diffusion codec TTS models spanning 19–133M parameters (3 seeds) on 2,000 hours of speech, then sweep refinement steps T in [1,16] at inference. Zero-shot synthesis is evaluated via ASR word error rate for intelligibility and speaker verification for identity on 174 held-out speakers. They compare against measured floors, test schedule retraining at 3x and 6x, apply Best-of-K search, and fit separable versus substitution scaling models via AICc.

Result: Refinement closes 86.2% of the intelligibility range but only 46.4% of the identity range—a 1.86x asymmetry robust across error metrics. Longer schedules attenuate but do not reverse the gap (1.84 to 1.36 to 1.23x), as intelligibility saturates with steps while identity keeps improving. Best-of-K search recovers speaker identity where refinement fails (64.6–79.0% win rates across four encoders). A separable B(d)B(T) fit significantly outperforms substitution models (Delta AICc=+69.3), and 62% of the residual identity deficit is attributed to the codec rather than the generator.

Conclusion: Depth and refinement steps are not interchangeable; they target different bottlenecks. Refinement mainly addresses intelligibility, while speaker identity is limited more by depth/codec capacity. Practitioners should optimize the two axes separately, and identity-oriented gains may require codec improvements or search-based methods rather than more denoising steps.

Abstract: Diffusion language models for text-to-speech combine two forms of computation: model depth (parameters) and refinement steps (inference budget). We ask whether they scale equally across capabilities. We train 15 masked-diffusion codec TTS models varying depth (19-133M parameters, 3 seeds) on 2,000 hours of speech and sweep refinement steps T in [1,16] at inference, measuring zero-shot synthesis via ASR word error rate (intelligibility) and speaker verification (identity) on 174 held-out speakers. Against measured floors, refinement closes 86.2% of the intelligibility range but only 46.4% of the identity range - a 1.86x asymmetry robust across multiple error metrics. Retraining at 3x and 6x schedule attenuates but does not reverse this gap (1.84 to 1.36 to 1.23x), because intelligibility saturates with steps while identity continues improving. Best-of-K search recovers speaker identity where refinement fails, with 64.6-79.0% win rates across four independent encoders. Depth and steps are not interchangeable: separable B(d)B(T) fits significantly better (Delta AICc=+69.3) than substitution models. Analysis shows 62% of remaining identity deficit lies in the codec, not the generator. We conclude that refinement and depth target different bottlenecks and should be optimized separately.

</details>


### [101] [Preserving Mathematical Reasoning in Compressed Diffusion Language Models via Trajectory-Aware Low-Rank Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03326)
*Tian Liang,Zishan Shao,Yiran Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces trajectory-aware calibration for compressing diffusion language models (dLLMs), formulating a low-rank objective over masked intermediate states and estimating it via Monte Carlo sampling (Traj-MC), which better preserves mathematical reasoning under compression than clean calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: dLLM compression suffers from a calibration-inference mismatch: calibration uses clean, fully visible activations while inference passes through partially masked intermediate states. This raises two open questions—whether low-rank optimality can be characterized over trajectory-distributed states, and whether the choice of calibration states affects retention of mathematical reasoning under compression.

Method: The authors formulate a trajectory-aware low-rank objective spanning corruption levels and masking realizations. To estimate this objective efficiently, they propose Traj-MC, which estimates the trajectory second moment through Monte Carlo sampling, providing exact sampled-state optimality and population consistency.

Result: Under matched compression budgets, trajectory-aware calibration improves reconstruction over the generation trajectory and preserves substantially more mathematical reasoning than clean calibration on mathematical reasoning benchmarks.

Conclusion: The work links trajectory-aware low-rank optimality to the reasoning capability retained after dLLM compression, and code is released at the provided GitHub repository.

Abstract: Diffusion language model (dLLM) compression faces a known challenge because calibration is typically performed on clean, fully visible activations, whereas inference traverses partially masked intermediate states. For low-rank compression, this raises two questions. First, can low-rank optimality still be characterized when approximation quality is measured over trajectory-distributed states, and second, does the choice of calibration states affect mathematical reasoning preservation under compression? We address these questions by formulating a trajectory-aware low-rank objective over corruption levels and masking realizations. To estimate this objective efficiently, we propose Traj-MC, which estimates the trajectory second moment through Monte Carlo sampling and yields exact sampled-state optimality and population consistency. Under matched compression budgets, trajectory-aware calibration improves reconstruction over the generation trajectory and preserves substantially more mathematical reasoning than clean calibration on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. Our results connect trajectory-aware low-rank optimality to the reasoning capability retained after dLLM compression. Our code is available at: https://github.com/Zishan-Shao/traj-mc.git.

</details>


### [102] [ReFract: Benchmarking Perspective Awareness in Language Model Agents with Text World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03356)
*Hainiu Xu,Vítor N. Lourenço,Mohnish Dubey,Yunfei Bai,Yulan He,Caroline Catmur,Aline Paes,Marco Caserta,Akash Chandrayan,Luca D'Angelo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ReFract, a benchmark of 150 expert-validated entries for evaluating "Perspective Awareness" in LLM agents in high-stakes industrial settings, where agents must adapt actions to a user's role. State-of-the-art LLMs solve at most 69% of tasks, with over 50% of trajectories containing perspective-violating actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are increasingly deployed in high-stakes settings like industrial maintenance and equipment fault troubleshooting, where users hold varied roles. A capable agent must calibrate its actions and information to the user's role, respecting knowledge and capability boundaries. Unlike coding, where errors are usually recoverable, agent responses here are enacted on physical equipment, risking irreversible damage, production loss, or personnel harm. Existing benchmarks overlook the need for agents to infer role intent and act only through tools that role may legitimately use.

Method: The authors propose Perspective Awareness as the capability for agents to act appropriately given a user's role. They build ReFract, a benchmark of 150 expert-validated entries where an agent must respond differently to the same query depending on the user's role. Entries are grounded in anonymized queries from domain support conversations. The authors construct Text World Models to simulate the agent's operating environments and assemble perspective-aware action trajectories.

Result: State-of-the-art LLMs solve at most 69% of the tasks, and more than 50% of their trajectories contain attempts at perspective-violating actions.

Conclusion: ReFract exposes perspective awareness as a distinct, largely unsolved axis of agent evaluation, motivating the development of agents that calibrate not just how to act, but for whom.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in high-stakes settings such as industrial maintenance and equipment fault troubleshooting, where workers occupy a variety of roles. A capable agent must therefore act in a way that is calibrated to user's role: taking actions and providing information that respect the role's knowledge and capability boundaries. Unlike coding, where mistakes are usually recoverable, agent responses in these settings are enacted on physical equipment, and can therefore cause irreversible equipment damage, production loss, or personnel harm. Existing benchmarks, however, largely overlook the need for agents to infer what a role intends and acting only through tools that role may legitimately use, a capability which we term Perspective Awareness. To this end, we introduce ReFract, a benchmark of 150 expert-validated entries in which an agent must act differently in response to the same query depending on user's role. Entries of ReFract are grounded in anonymized queries from domain support conversations, against which we construct Text World Models that simulate the agent's operating environments and assemble perspective-aware action trajectories. State-of-the-art LLMs solve at most 69% of the tasks with more than 50% of their trajectories contain attempts of taking perspective-violating actions. ReFract exposes perspective awareness as a distinct, largely unsolved axis of agent evaluation and motivates agents that calibrate not just how to act, but for whom.

</details>


### [103] [Geometry Meets Physics: Data-Efficient Pre-Training for Unstructured Neural PDE Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03363)
*Luis Medrano-Navarro,Giacomo Baldan,Qiang Liu,Benjamin Holzschuh,Jan Hagnberger,Mathias Niepert,Nils Thuerey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This work proposes a data-free pre-training framework for neural PDE surrogates on unstructured 3D geometries, using geometry-driven shape descriptors for steady-state problems and online-generated synthetic PDE data for transient problems, achieving faster convergence and higher accuracy under low-data fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural surrogate models for PDEs on unstructured 3D geometries suffer from poor generalization and the prohibitive cost of generating large-scale training datasets. Existing pre-training on massive pre-computed PDE datasets is neither compute- nor data-efficient because such data is very expensive to generate.

Method: A data-free pre-training framework covering both steady-state and transient regimes. For steady-state problems, a geometry-driven strategy leverages intrinsic shape descriptors to learn representations of complex 3D domains. For transient problems, a physics-driven approach uses online generation of synthetic PDE data, enabling scalable pre-training without expensive pre-computed datasets.

Result: Across multiple experiments, the approach achieves faster convergence, greater data efficiency, and higher accuracy during fine-tuning, especially in realistic low-data regimes.

Conclusion: The methodology offers a practical pathway toward data-efficient neural emulators for large-scale simulations, removing reliance on costly large-scale pre-computed datasets.

Abstract: Neural surrogate models for Partial Differential Equations (PDEs) on unstructured 3D geometries are often limited by poor generalization and the high cost of generating large-scale training datasets. Consequently, pre-training on massive datasets of related PDE dynamics has emerged as a critical alternative to enhance the robustness and scalability of these models. However, this strategy is neither compute- nor data-efficient, as it relies on massive pre-computed data that is very costly to generate. In this work, we introduce a disk-data-free pre-training framework tailored to both steady-state and transient regimes. For steady-state problems, we propose a geometry-driven strategy that leverages intrinsic shape descriptors to learn representations of complex 3D domains. For transient problems, we introduce a physics-driven approach based on online generation of synthetic PDE data, enabling scalable pre-training without reliance on expensive datasets. Across multiple experiments, our approach achieves faster convergence, greater data efficiency, and higher accuracy during fine-tuning, particularly under realistic low-data regimes. This methodology provides a practical pathway toward data-efficient neural emulators for large-scale simulations.

</details>


### [104] [Multilingual GSM-Symbolic: What determines capability transfer across languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03367)
*Kenneth Enevoldsen,Riley Herchert,Sofie Mosegaard,Dan Saattrup Smart,Simon Enni,Isaac Chung,Sofie Bruun,Ayush Sunil Munot,Max Müller-Eberstein,Adnan El-Assadi,Elisa Bassignana,Gianluca Barmina,Hafsteinn Einarsson,Iben Nyholm Debess,Linda Freienthal,Lukas Galke Poech,Mike Zhang,Nicolas Legrand,Vladimir Salnikov,Yevhen Kostiuk,Zafar Hussain,Sagandeep Kaur,Agnes Toftgård,Marie Mattson,Kristoffer Nielbo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Multilingual GSM-Symbolic, a 15-language, 30,000-pair item-matched benchmark built from symbolic templates, to jointly quantify determinants of cross-lingual capability transfer and enable low-cost performance prediction in unseen languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work understands little about how capabilities acquired in one language transfer to another. Existing evaluations rely on incomparable, saturation-prone datasets and rarely examine transfer determinants jointly, making it unclear what predicts transfer and forcing exhaustive evaluation across all language pairs.

Method: The authors construct Multilingual GSM-Symbolic, an extensible multilingual mathematical dataset of 30,000 item-matched question-answer pairs across 15 languages, with symbolic templates enabling generation of millions of high-quality variations from a single sample to prevent overfitting. They jointly estimate determinants of performance—model size, language resource level, reasoning, and typological distance—via regression analyses.

Result: The largest determinants are model size (β = 1.77), language resource level (β = 0.77), reasoning (β = 0.67), and typological distance (β = -0.25). Model size and reasoning narrow the low- vs. high-resource gap (β = -0.27 and β = -0.20), but have little effect on typologically distant languages. The framework explains 92% of between-language variation but only 23% of model-by-language variation, predicting unseen-language performance within 6.0pp (r = .96); using just 10 target-language templates reduces error to 4.19pp.

Conclusion: A joint, item-matched multilingual benchmark enables comparable quantification and mutual expression of transfer determinants (e.g., a 32B model in Marathi behaves like a 10B model in English). Developers can target levers that limit low-resource performance, and performance on unseen languages can be estimated with little or no downstream dataset, though model-by-language variation remains largely unexplained.

Abstract: We understand little about how capabilities acquired in one language carry over to another, or what governs this transfer: evaluations rely on incomparable, saturation-prone datasets and rarely examine its determinants jointly. Identifying what predicts transfer would let us avoid exhaustive evaluation across all language pairs and let developers target the factors that limit performance in low-resource languages. To evaluate cross-lingual capability transfer, we introduce Multilingual GSM-Symbolic, an extensible multilingual mathematical dataset covering 30,000 item-matched question-answer pairs and spanning 15 languages. It utilises symbolic templates to prevent overfitting and ensure generalisation by allowing generation of millions of high-quality variations from a single sample. Using Multilingual GSM-Symbolic, we quantify the largest determinants of capability as model size ($β= 1.77$), language resource level ($β= 0.77$), reasoning ($β= 0.67$) and typological distance ($β= -0.25$). This joint estimation allows these determinants to be expressed in terms of one another: a 32B model evaluated in Marathi performs like a 10B model in English. Our findings have important implications for model developers, showing that model size and reasoning narrow the performance gap between low- and high-resource languages ($β= -0.27$ and $β= -0.20$, respectively), while similar levers have little or no effect on typologically distant languages.
  Overall, our analysis framework explains 92% of between-language variation, but only 23% of the model-by-language variation, and predicts a model's performance on an unseen language within 6.0pp (r=.96). Incorporating measurements from just 10 templates in the target language reduces this to 4.19pp, enabling reasonable estimates of performance with little or no downstream dataset.

</details>


### [105] [CVE2AP: Automated Generation of PDDL-Encoded Attack Paths via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03383)
*Lin Cui,Vincenzo Scotti,Raffaela Mirandola*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CVE2AP uses LLMs with structured prompting and planner-feedback refinement to automatically generate PDDL-encoded attack paths from natural-language CVE descriptions, achieving up to 86.9% syntax correctness, 78.6% solvability, and 93.1% semantic correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Attack Path modeling underpins cybersecurity analysis, and PDDL is widely used to encode APs into formal, machine-verifiable representations for reasoning about vulnerability exploitation and attack progression. However, existing AP modeling relies on expert-driven manual construction, which is not scalable and cannot keep pace with rapidly evolving cyber threats. LLMs are promising for automating this translation given their pre-trained knowledge and reasoning abilities, but no systematic approach exploits them for this task.

Method: CVE2AP is an LLM-based approach that automatically generates PDDL-encoded attack paths from natural-language CVE descriptions. It employs structured prompting and an error-feedback mechanism that iteratively refines generated paths using planner-reported syntactic and solvability errors. A systematic empirical evaluation is conducted across multiple LLMs and generation configurations, assessing quality along syntactic, solvability, and semantic dimensions, alongside token consumption and generation time.

Result: CVE2AP generates high-quality PDDL-encoded attack paths, reaching up to 86.9% syntax correctness, 78.6% solvability, and 93.1% semantic correctness under LLM-as-expert evaluation. GPT-5.5 provides the best quality-cost trade-off, and the error-feedback mechanism yields the most consistent quality improvement.

Conclusion: LLM-based automatic generation of formal attack-path representations from CVE text is feasible and effective, with planner-driven error feedback being the most reliable quality lever. CVE2AP offers a scalable alternative to expert-driven manual AP modeling, though remaining gaps in solvability indicate room for further refinement.

Abstract: Attack Path (AP) modeling is fundamental to cybersecurity analysis, where the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) has been widely adopted to encode APs into formal and machine-verifiable representations for automated reasoning about vulnerability exploitation, attack progression, and their potential impacts. However, existing AP modeling approaches largely rely on expert-driven manual construction, limiting their scalability and ability to keep pace with rapidly evolving cyber threats. Large language models (LLMs) are promising candidates, as their extensive pre-trained knowledge and reasoning capabilities enable them to interpret and transform threat intelligence into formal representations. In this paper, we propose \textbf{CVE2AP}, an LLM-based approach for automatically generating PDDL-encoded attack paths from natural language CVE (Common Vulnerability Exposure) descriptions. CVE2AP leverages structured prompting and incorporates an error-feedback mechanism that iteratively refines the generated paths using planner-reported syntactic and solvability errors. We conduct a systematic empirical evaluation across multiple LLMs and generation configurations, assessing generation quality across syntactic, solvability and semantic dimensions, together with token consumption and generation time. The results demonstrate that CVE2AP effectively generates high-quality PDDL-encoded attack paths, achieving up to 86.9\% syntax correctness, 78.6\% solvability, and 93.1\% semantic correctness under LLM-as-expert evaluation, while \texttt{GPT-5.5} offers the best quality-cost trade-off and error feedback yields the most consistent quality improvement.

</details>


### [106] [Benchmarking Candidate Coverage in Typed Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03387)
*Jiawen Lu,Tongtong Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Typed decision models are evaluated with a paired candidate-coverage benchmark (300 calibration, 589 test texts; 23,932 predictions per model) on AG News, DBpedia, Emotion, and TREC, showing that missing-answer detection and valid-candidate acceptance are distinct, model-specific behaviors requiring separate measurement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy measured with complete answer options does not reveal whether a model recognizes that a reference answer is missing, nor whether it avoids rejecting valid candidates. Classification, score ranking, and rejection policies are conflated in standard evaluation, so a protocol isolating omission detection versus false rejection is needed.

Method: A paired candidate-coverage benchmark protocol with initial evaluation of Laya and Jev across AG News, DBpedia, Emotion, and TREC. Identical frozen texts and requests are used; 300 calibration and 589 test texts produce 23,932 predictions per model. Present/absent pairs are matched on ordinary candidate count, and name variants preserve descriptions, members, and order. Calibration-only none-score thresholds are applied as an intervention; competence-conditioned analysis, probability-precision sensitivity, and interface audits are also conducted.

Result: Native rejection behavior diverges sharply. At five TREC candidates with natural names, Laya detects 97.2% of missing-answer cases but falsely rejects 69.7% of present controls; Jev shows 24.8% and 0.0%. Calibration-only none-score thresholds shift these to 33.9%/3.7% (Laya) and 45.0%/1.8% (Jev). On DBpedia, Jev's high coverage-score AUROC supports a stronger operating point, while both models show weak complete-set accuracy on Emotion.

Conclusion: Classification, score ranking, and rejection policies require separate measurement. The benchmark is descriptive and limited to reference-label omission; it does not establish natural out-of-scope generalization, causal mechanisms, or a new rejection method.

Abstract: Typed decision models return choices or distributions over answer options supplied at request time. Accuracy with complete options does not establish whether a model recognizes that a reference answer is missing or avoids rejecting valid candidates. We present a paired candidate-coverage benchmark protocol and an initial evaluation of Laya and Jev across AG News, DBpedia, Emotion, and TREC. The models receive identical frozen texts and requests: 300 calibration and 589 test texts yield 23,932 predictions per model. Present/absent pairs match ordinary candidate count, and name variants preserve descriptions, members, and order. Native rejection behavior differs sharply: at five TREC candidates with natural names, Laya detects 97.2% of missing-answer cases but falsely rejects 69.7% of present controls; Jev's rates are 24.8% and 0.0%. Calibration-only none-score thresholds change these rates to 33.9%/3.7% and 45.0%/1.8%, respectively. On DBpedia, Jev's high coverage-score AUROC supports a stronger operating point, whereas both models have weak complete-set accuracy on Emotion. Competence-conditioned analysis, probability-precision sensitivity, and interface audits show why classification, score ranking, and rejection policies need separate measurement. This initial benchmark is descriptive and limited to reference-label omission; it does not establish natural out-of-scope generalization, causal mechanisms, or a new rejection method.

</details>


### [107] [Becoming Suspicious Across Borders: Algorithmic Extraterritoriality and AI-Driven Financial Surveillance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03425)
*Georgios Pavlidis,Savvas Chatzichristofis,Eleni Gavriil*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper reconceptualizes "suspicion" in AML/CFT as increasingly produced through AI-driven data processes, introducing "algorithmic extraterritoriality" as data-infrastructure-mediated regulatory power that complicates accountability and contestability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Suspicion is central to AML/CFT because it enables intervention below the threshold of proof, but the traditional understanding of suspicion as a situated legal judgement by human actors in identifiable jurisdictions is becoming inadequate as AI becomes integral to financial surveillance.

Method: Conceptual/legal-theoretical analysis. The article develops the concept of "algorithmic extraterritoriality" to capture regulatory power mediated by transnational data infrastructures rather than formal jurisdictional assertions, analyzing the epistemic and spatial transformation of suspicion.

Result: Suspicion is increasingly produced through data-driven processes, and regulatory reach depends less on where conduct occurs than on whether conduct becomes visible within data systems. Individuals are constituted as datafied subjects of suspicion, governable through dispersed and opaque evaluation processes.

Conclusion: The transformation of suspicion challenges accountability and contestability because suspicion becomes harder to locate, explain, or contest, undermining traditional legal safeguards in AML/CFT.

Abstract: Suspicion is an important, yet elusive concept in anti-money laundering and counter-terrorist financing (AML/CFT), which allows for intervention below the threshold of proof. In its traditional form, suspicion can be understood as a situated legal judgement by human actors within identifiable jurisdictions. It is argued that this understanding is no longer adequate. As artificial intelligence (AI) becomes an integral part of financial surveillance, suspicion is increasingly produced through data-driven processes. This transformation is epistemic, but also spatial. Since AI-driven financial surveillance operates through transnational data infrastructures, regulatory reach is less a matter of where conduct occurs than a question of whether such conduct becomes visible within data systems. This article develops the concept of algorithmic extraterritoriality, understood as a form of regulatory power mediated by data infrastructures rather than formal assertions of jurisdiction. Moreover, since individuals are increasingly constituted as datafied subjects of suspicion, they are rendered governable through dispersed and opaque processes of evaluation. This constitutes a challenge for accountability and contestability because suspicion becomes more difficult to locate, explain or contest.

</details>


### [108] [Jumping the Line: Exploiting Length Predictions in LLM Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03430)
*Yuyang Dai,Rana Shahout,Mahmood Sharif*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JIL is an attack on prediction-based LLM schedulers: it optimizes an adversarial suffix so a length probe underestimates a request's output length, granting higher scheduling priority and faster completion (up to 83.4% lower predicted length; up to 1.53x faster).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Size-based scheduling (e.g., SJF) reduces completion time in LLM serving but requires output-length prediction since true lengths are unknown pre-generation. Practically deployed schedulers therefore depend on predicted lengths, creating an exploitable scheduling signal.

Method: Using TRAIL as a case study, JIL optimizes an adversarial suffix that induces a lightweight output-length probe to underestimate a request's length, thereby obtaining higher priority. Evaluation spans two datasets, four LLMs, varied request profiles, and deployment configurations, including end-to-end serving experiments and scheduler-side defenses.

Result: JIL reduces predicted output lengths by up to 83.4% and makes adversarial requests complete up to 1.53x faster on average end-to-end. The predicted-length reduction far exceeds the change in actual output length, exposing a mismatch between scheduler estimate and realized request size. Response utility varies across models and tasks, showing a trade-off between scheduling advantage and response quality. Coarse-interval grouping of length predictions reduces JIL's scheduling advantage and mitigates delays to benign requests.

Conclusion: Prediction-based LLM scheduling is vulnerable to adversarial manipulation of the length-estimation signal, enabling priority gains at the cost of response quality. The estimate-vs-reality mismatch is the key mechanism. Defense: coarsening prediction granularity (grouping into intervals) curbs the advantage and protects benign requests.

Abstract: Efficient request scheduling is increasingly important for reducing completion time in large language model (LLM) serving. Size-based policies such as Shortest Job First prioritize shorter requests, but output lengths are unknown before generation, so practical schedulers rely on predicted lengths. We introduce JIL, an attack on prediction-based LLM schedulers that manipulates the scheduling signal to obtain higher priority and reduce completion time. Using TRAIL as a case study, JIL optimizes an adversarial suffix that causes a lightweight output-length probe to underestimate a request's length. We evaluate JIL on two datasets and four LLMs across varied request profiles and deployment configurations. JIL reduces predicted output lengths by up to 83.4 percent, and adversarial requests complete up to 1.53 times faster on average in end-to-end serving experiments. The reduction in predicted length is substantially larger than the change in actual output length, revealing a mismatch between the scheduler's estimate and the request's realized size. Response utility varies across models and tasks, exposing a trade-off between scheduling advantage and response quality. We also evaluate scheduler-side defenses and find that grouping length predictions into coarse intervals reduces JIL's scheduling advantage and mitigates delays to benign requests.

</details>


### [109] [A Near-Zero Monitor Readout Is Not Evidence of Behavioral Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03458)
*Zhe Zhou,Tianhua Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Low monitor readouts during reward-hacking post-training do not prove behavioral control; the same zero-median score spans regimes from mixed to near-pure hacking by seed alone, so an out-of-band behavioral check is required.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training with verifiable rewards can induce reward hacking, which motivates embedding monitors in the training objective rather than relying on them solely for offline auditing. The implicit assumption is that a low monitor readout indicates the intervention controls behavior; the paper challenges this assumption and shows the readout is insufficient evidence of control.

Method: A code-generation environment in which the dominant exploit is available at the start of the reasoning trace. Policies are trained against three monitors passing the same offline gate: an in-domain activation probe and two penalties conditioned on how early the policy commits to its own final answer. Monitor scales are not compared, but behavior is compared within each monitor family and within a fixed configuration across seeds. Analysis is on endpoint readouts plus text-level analysis of the reasoning traces.

Result: The probe score sits at its numerical floor from the first recorded training step, and the trained-score median is zero for every prefix-trained run at the endpoint. Within one fixed configuration, prefix-trained runs sharing the same zero-median trained score range, by seed alone, from a mixed regime with a low hacking share to near-pure reward hacking. All probe runs reach the hacking regime, but their floor-level readout reflects a mismatch between probe validation position and read position during training, not a second instance of the ambiguity. Text-level analysis exposes a prefix failure mode: generic planning and filler shells postpone the exploit past the cut without removing it from the final output, so low measured commitment cannot distinguish low hacking share from delayed commitment to the exploit.

Conclusion: Offline discrimination and low monitor-aligned readouts are insufficient evidence of behavioral control; an out-of-band behavioral check is required. The characterization is of the endpoint readout, not its evolution. Code is available at https://github.com/zhezhou1106/spoof-cost.

Abstract: Post-training with verifiable rewards can induce reward hacking, motivating the use of monitors within the training objective rather than solely for offline auditing. We show that a low monitor readout does not identify whether such an intervention controls behavior. In a code-generation environment whose dominant exploit is available at the start of the reasoning trace, we train policies against three monitors that pass the same offline gate: an in-domain activation probe and two penalties conditioned on how early the policy commits to its own final answer. The probe score is at its numerical floor from the first recorded training step, and the trained-score median is zero for every prefix-trained run at the endpoint. These readouts estimate different quantities, and we do not compare their scales; within each monitor family, however, low values do not establish behavioral control. Within one fixed configuration, prefix-trained runs with the same zero-median trained score range, by seed alone, from a mixed regime with a low hacking share to near-pure reward hacking. All probe runs reach the hacking regime, but their floor-level readout reflects a mismatch between the position where the probe was validated and the position where it was read during training, not a second instance of this ambiguity. Text-level analysis identifies a prefix failure mode: generic planning and filler shells postpone the exploit past the cut without eliminating it from the final output. Low measured commitment therefore does not distinguish a low hacking share from delayed commitment to the exploit. Offline discrimination and low monitor-aligned readouts are insufficient evidence of behavioral control; an out-of-band behavioral check is required. We characterize the endpoint readout, not its evolution. Code is available at https://github.com/zhezhou1106/spoof-cost.

</details>


### [110] [Efficient Reasoning Training Does Not Always Harm CoT Faithfulness and Monitorability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03509)
*Samuel Lewis-Lim,Xingwei Tan,Mario Sanger,Zhixue Zhao,Nikolaos Aletras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how training LLMs to reason with fewer tokens affects the faithfulness and monitorability of their chain-of-thought (CoT). Using three length-pressure fine-tuning methods, the authors find that efficient reasoning degrades CoT faithfulness in most settings (mainly due to reduced model consistency), while monitorability remains comparatively robust even under much shorter CoTs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CoT reasoning enables humans to inspect how LLMs reach answers and oversee model behavior, but it increases inference cost, motivating efficient methods that train models to use fewer tokens. A key concern is that such training may cause models to skip important reasoning steps, making the CoT no longer faithfully reflect the model's decision. The authors note it is unclear whether or when this happens in practice, since efficiency methods apply length pressure differently and the token cost of faithful explanation varies by task. This motivates a systematic study of these dynamics.

Method: The authors fine-tune a variety of models using three methods that apply length pressure differently: (1) a fixed generation budget, (2) a per-example length target, and (3) a group-relative length reward. They then evaluate how efficient reasoning affects CoT faithfulness (how well the CoT reflects model decisions on related inputs) and monitorability (whether the CoT reveals when input interventions alter the output).

Result: Efficient reasoning affects faithfulness and monitorability differently. Faithfulness falls in most settings, primarily because trained models become less consistent. Monitorability is more robust: models keep acknowledging the influence on their answer even when the CoT is much shorter.

Conclusion: Length-pressure training for efficient reasoning degrades CoT faithfulness in most cases, largely driven by reduced model consistency, whereas monitorability is comparatively preserved even with substantially shorter CoTs. This suggests faithfulness and monitorability should be assessed separately when evaluating efficient reasoning methods.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning allows humans to inspect how large language models reach their answers, and oversee model behaviour. This reasoning comes at an increased inference cost, motivating efficient methods that train models to solve tasks using fewer tokens. However, a common concern is that such training may cause models to skip important reasoning steps, so the CoT no longer faithfully reflects the model's decision. It is unclear whether or when this occurs in practice, since different efficiency methods apply length pressure to models' CoT in distinct ways, and faithfully explaining a model's decision takes more tokens on some tasks than others. To understand these dynamics, we fine-tune a variety of models with three methods that apply length pressure differently, namely a fixed generation budget, a per-example length target, and a group-relative length reward. We evaluate how efficient reasoning affects CoT faithfulness (i.e., how well the CoT reflects model decisions on related inputs) and monitorability (i.e., whether the CoT reveals when input interventions alter the output). We find that it affects faithfulness and monitorability differently. Faithfulness falls in most settings, primarily because the trained models are less consistent. Monitorability is more robust, as models keep acknowledging the influence on their answer even when the CoT is much shorter.

</details>


### [111] [Reasoning Models Are Accurate but Unsound on Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03519)
*Arman Behnam,Binghui Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CERTID is a formal causal-identification pipeline that certifies non-identifiable queries via the sound and complete ID algorithm and verifies models' returned formulas against structural causal models with known interventional distributions, revealing that accuracy is a poor proxy for soundness across frontier reasoning models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reasoning models asked whether a causal effect is recoverable from observational data can either wrongly refuse an identifiable query or, more consequentially, answer a non-identifiable one; the latter cannot be validated by any observational data. Prior evaluations lack provably non-identifiable queries and rely on string matching, which cannot accept equivalent correct formulas.

Method: CERTID builds a formal identification pipeline using the sound and complete ID algorithm to certify whether an effect is identifiable from a given graph and query, and verifies returned formulas against structural causal models whose interventional distributions are known exactly. It adds theoretical results to mitigate structural leakage, repair non-identifiable queries, and establish grading guarantees.

Result: Three frontier reasoning models (Gemini Flash, Gemini Pro, GPT5.5) were evaluated on 1,200 certified instances spanning 4 to 50 vertices. Accuracy was a poor proxy for soundness: on identical instances, the false-claim rate on non-identifiable queries varied seventeen-fold across models. Models decided identifiability with 97-100% accuracy on graphs generated after the strongest model's training snapshot.
