Conclusion: CERTID provides certified non-identifiable instances and equivalence-aware grading, exposing a large soundness gap that accuracy hides. Instances, the certification procedure, the verifier, and per-instance records are released.

Abstract: A reasoning model asked whether a causal effect is recoverable from observational data can fail in two ways: it refuses an identifiable query or answers a nonidentifiable one. The latter is more consequential, as no observational data can validate the claimed formula. Measuring this failure requires queries that are provably non-identifiable, which prior evaluations lack, and grading that accepts correct formulas in any equivalent form, which string matching cannot provide. We build CERTID, a formal identification pipeline that addresses both limitations. CERTID uses the sound and complete causal identification algorithm ID to certify whether an effect is identifiable from a given graph and query, and verifies returned formulas against structural causal models whose interventional distributions are known exactly. CERTID further develops theoretical results to mitigate structural leakage, repair non-identifiable queries, and establish grading guarantees. We evaluate three frontier reasoning models (Gemini Flash, Gemini Pro, and GPT5.5) on 1,200 certified instances spanning 4 to 50 vertices. Accuracy proves a poor proxy for soundness: on identical instances, the false-claim rate on non-identifiable queries varies by seventeen-fold across models. We also find that models decide identifiability with 97-100% accuracy on graphs generated after the strongest model's training snapshot. Instances, the certification procedure, the verifier, and per-instance records are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/certid-D718.

</details>


### [112] [From Benchmarks to Production: A Text-to-SQL System for Complex Financial Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03524)
*Arijit Sehanobish,Bruno Gomes Coelho,Guillaume Michel,Sophia Zhi,Valerie Faucon-Morin,Kristen Howell*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FLINT is a domain-specialized Text-to-SQL system for production financial databases that resolves opaque integer keys and multi-join requirements via a lookup agent, template retrieval, and foreign-key-based schema linking, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines and being deployed in production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose Text-to-SQL systems perform well on benchmarks like Spider and BIRD with shallow, human-readable schemas, but fall below 50% on production financial databases where concepts are stored as opaque integer keys. In such schemas, even simple queries require multiple joins and filter predicates referencing opaque IDs, creating a performance gap.

Method: FLINT comprises three components: (1) a lookup agent that dynamically resolves natural-language concepts to question-specific reference table constraints; (2) embedding-based retrieval of structurally similar query templates from a compact, expert-authored bank; and (3) schema linking that prunes a large table schema to the relevant subset by traversing foreign-key chains rather than relying on name similarity alone.

Result: Evaluated on two datasets totaling 359 questions over production financial schemas, FLINT outperforms various state-of-the-art baselines using the same LLM.

Conclusion: FLINT effectively closes the performance gap in production financial Text-to-SQL, and the system is deployed in production as part of a financial data retrieval service.

Abstract: General-purpose Text-to-SQL systems achieve strong performance on academic benchmarks like Spider and BIRD, where schemas are relatively shallow and column values are often human readable. In production financial databases, where concepts are stored as opaque integer keys rather than human-readable strings, these methods fall below 50%, as even simple queries require multiple joins and filter predicates reference opaque IDs. We present Financial LINking Text-to-SQL (FLINT), a domain-specialized Text-to-SQL system that closes this gap through three key components: (1) a lookup agent that dynamically resolves natural-language concepts to question-specific reference table constraints, (2) embedding-based retrieval of structurally similar query templates from a compact, expert-authored bank, and (3) schema linking that prunes a large table schema to the relevant subset by traversing foreign-key chains, rather than relying on name similarity alone. We evaluate on two datasets totaling 359 questions over production financial schemas. FLINT outperforms various state-of-the-art baselines using the same LLM. The system is deployed in production as part of a financial data retrieval service.

</details>


### [113] [Recursive Harness Self-Improvement for Frontier Reasoning Data Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03548)
*Wenlong Zhang,Zhengbo Jiao,Chenxu Zhang,Lekang Jiang,SiYuan Ma,Qituan Zhang,Guo Chen,Linfeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces task-harness co-evolution, a recursive self-improvement framework that adapts the reasoning-data synthesis harness alongside generated tasks, using online and post-task updates to produce progressively harder valid tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing task-level recursion reuses generated problems as seeds but leaves the construction harness unchanged, limiting the ability to synthesize progressively harder reasoning problems as the task distribution evolves.

Method: Online self-improvement converts intermediate solver failures into reusable skills during generation; post-task self-improvement revises skills, prompts, and workflows after each batch, adopting candidates only when they yield harder valid tasks within a bounded cost increase, while model weights and verification criteria remain fixed.

Result: Across mathematics, coding, and science, mean solver accuracy drops from 100.0% to 54.8% over fourteen evolution rounds. Ablations show that combining both update schedules produces harder tasks than fixed-harness recursion or either schedule alone. Synthesized data improves downstream SFT and GRPO, with a 27B student fine-tuned on 10K synthesized math examples reaching 62.5% mean-16 accuracy on APEX, competitive with selected frontier-model references.

Conclusion: Adapting the synthesis harness alongside the tasks enables generation of increasingly challenging reasoning data with downstream training value.

Abstract: Generating progressively harder reasoning problems requires synthesis procedures that adapt as the task distribution evolves. Existing task-level recursion reuses generated problems as seeds but leaves the construction harness unchanged. We present task-harness co-evolution, a framework for recursive harness self-improvement (RSI) in reasoning-data synthesis. Online self-improvement converts intermediate solver failures into reusable skills during generation. Post-task self-improvement revises skills, prompts, and workflows after each batch, adopting candidates only when they generate harder valid tasks within a bounded cost increase. Model weights and verification criteria remain fixed. Across mathematics, coding, and science, mean solver accuracy decreases from 100.0% to 54.8% over fourteen evolution rounds. Ablations show that combining both update schedules produces harder tasks than fixed-harness recursion or either schedule alone. The resulting data improves downstream SFT and GRPO performance. In particular, a 27B student fine-tuned on 10K synthesized mathematics examples achieves 62.5% mean-16 accuracy on APEX, competitive with selected frontier-model references. These results support adapting the synthesis harness alongside the tasks to generate increasingly challenging data with downstream training value.

</details>


### [114] [Knowledge or Calculator? Decomposing the Skill Premium in Verifiable Financial Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03564)
*Jermyn Zhen Yong Bek,Zhuang Qiang Bok,Zhongtian Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FinSkillBench evaluates financial AI agents on 2,603 point-in-time episodes across 12 subtasks, showing that curated skill packages raise scores by +16.2 points while self-generated skills add only +0.5 points, with gains driven primarily by executable domain tools (+19.5) over documents (+5.6).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial AI agents must go beyond fact retrieval to perform correct quantitative execution, use procedural resources reliably, and produce auditable structured outputs. Existing evaluations do not isolate how much of an agent's performance gain stems from curated procedural resources versus the model itself, nor how that gain depends on workflow type and harness design.

Method: The authors introduce FinSkillBench, a suite of 2,603 point-in-time episodes spanning 12 subtasks in portfolio construction, risk management, and fundamental analysis, with hidden regenerable ground truth and task-specific deterministic verifiers. They execute 17,820 episodes across 9 models and 3 resource conditions, run paired analyses across 8 models, decompose the curated premium by granting human-authored procedural documents and executable domain tools separately, and test robustness with 10 scoring variants, cluster bootstrap, and an independently implemented second harness.

Result: Curated skill packages raise mean scores by +16.2 points (0.366 to 0.528), whereas skills generated within a single episode add only +0.5 points while consuming more tokens and turns. Decomposing the premium: documents alone add +5.6 points, tools alone add +19.5 points, and their combination is subadditive. Effects are strongly workflow dependent—executable tools dominate numerically intensive workflows, documentation matters more when procedural or output-schema guidance is the bottleneck, and interpretive tasks benefit from both. Sign is stable across 10 scoring variants and cluster bootstrap; a second independent harness reproduces the directional pattern, though effect magnitudes depend on how tools and data are exposed.

Conclusion: The measured "skill premium" is a property of the full model–resource–harness system rather than of the underlying model alone. Gains from curated skills are real and robust in direction but contingent on workflow type and on how resources are externalized, implying that evaluations and deployments must report the complete configuration rather than model identity alone.

Abstract: Financial AI agents must do more than retrieve facts: investment workflows require correct quantitative execution, reliable use of procedural resources, and auditable structured outputs. We introduce FinSkillBench, an evaluation suite of 2,603 point in time episodes across 12 subtasks in portfolio construction, risk management, and fundamental analysis, with hidden regenerable ground truth and task specific deterministic verifiers. Executing 17,820 episodes across 9 models and 3 resource conditions, the paired analysis across 8 models shows that curated skill packages raise mean scores by +16.2 points (0.366 to 0.528), whereas skills generated within a single episode add only +0.5 points while consuming more tokens and turns. We then decompose the curated premium by granting human authored procedural documents and executable domain tools separately: documents alone add +5.6 points, tools alone add +19.5 points, and their combination is subadditive. The premium is strongly workflow dependent: executable tools dominate numerically intensive workflows, documentation matters more when procedural or output schema guidance is the bottleneck, and interpretive tasks benefit from both. The effects are sign stable across 10 scoring variants and cluster bootstrap analyses, and an independently implemented second harness reproduces the directional pattern while showing that effect magnitudes depend on how tools and data are exposed. Overall, a measured "skill premium" is a property of the full model, resource, and harness system rather than of the underlying model alone.

</details>


### [115] [Learning to Assess Heartbeat Observability for mmWave Heart-Rate Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03570)
*Yuxuan Hu,Shilin Shan,Jianfei Yang,Feng Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes HEAR, a dual-task Transformer that assesses heartbeat observability from mmWave radar measurements and jointly estimates heart rate, trained on simulated data and transferring zero-shot to real-world 60/120 GHz datasets for reliable selective heart-rate estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contactless heart-rate sensing with mmWave radar needs to determine whether an individual measurement is reliable. Coherent superposition of scatterer returns can suppress the heartbeat component even under similar macroscopic observation geometry, so reliability must be assessed directly from acquired measurements rather than from observation conditions.

Method: The authors define heartbeat observability as the readability of the heartbeat component in the phase spectrum. They build a controllable multi-scatterer FMCW simulator to generate training supervision across observability conditions, using agreement between the dominant heartbeat-band peak and the known heart rate as an automatic observability label. They then propose HEAR, a compact dual-task Transformer that jointly predicts an observability score and heart rate, with input combining spectral magnitudes and frequencies relative to the respiration fundamental.

Result: Trained solely on simulated observations, HEAR transfers zero-shot to two public real-world datasets at 60 and 120 GHz from 134 subjects. The learned score supports selective prediction with HEAR's own heart-rate head and with existing estimators. On the 120 GHz dataset, score-based selection reduces the HR head's MAE from 17.9 BPM at full coverage to 1.6 BPM at 50% coverage. The full pipeline runs at 50.8 ms end-to-end latency on an edge device.

Conclusion: Learning to assess heartbeat observability directly from measurements enables reliable selective heart-rate estimation that generalizes from simulation to real-world mmWave radar data and operates efficiently on edge hardware.

Abstract: Contactless heart-rate sensing with millimeter-wave (mmWave) radar requires assessing whether individual measurements support reliable estimation. We study learning to assess heartbeat observability, defined as the readability of the heartbeat component in an acquired phase spectrum, for selective heart-rate estimation. Coherent superposition of scatterer returns can suppress this component even under similar macroscopic observation geometry, motivating assessment directly from acquired measurements. To obtain training supervision across different observability conditions, we develop a controllable multi-scatterer frequency-modulated continuous-wave (FMCW) simulator. Agreement between the dominant heartbeat-band peak and the known heart rate provides an automatic observability label for each simulated measurement. We propose HEAR (Heartbeat Estimation with Assessed Reliability), a compact dual-task Transformer that jointly predicts an observability score and heart rate. Its input combines spectral magnitudes with frequencies relative to the respiration fundamental, providing context for respiratory harmonics. Trained solely on simulated observations, HEAR transfers zero-shot to two public real-world datasets collected at 60 and 120 GHz from 134 subjects. The same learned score supports selective prediction with both HEAR's own heart-rate head and multiple existing estimators. On the 120 GHz dataset, score-based selection reduces the HR head's mean absolute error from 17.9 BPM at full coverage to 1.6 BPM at 50% coverage. The complete pipeline achieves an end-to-end processing latency of 50.8 ms on an edge device. Project page: https://yuxuanhu9.github.io/HEAR/.

</details>


### [116] [HyperBrowseComp: A Multilingual and Multimodal Stress Test for Web-Browsing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03574)
*Alham Fikri Aji,Faiz Rizki Ramadhan,Zayd M. K. Zuhri,Seung Hun Eddie Han,Ryandito Diandaru,Qinrong Cui,Jan Christian Blaise Cruz,Badrinath Chandana,Peerawat Chomphooyod,Ahmed Attia,Jonibek Mansurov,Emilio Villa-Cueva,Canh Duong Nguyen,Imran Turganov,Minghao Wu,Peerat Limkonchotiwat,Irina Nikishina*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperBrowseComp is a new multilingual, multimodal browsing benchmark of 423 human-authored, human-validated questions in 13 languages, designed to be extremely hard and requiring open-web evidence discovery across videos, scans, images, and maps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing browsing benchmarks may not sufficiently stress persistent, multi-step information seeking across languages and evidence modalities. The authors aim to build a testbed where difficulty stems from discovering and connecting obscure, publicly verifiable evidence on the open web rather than from parametric knowledge.

Method: The benchmark contains 423 manually authored and human-validated questions across 13 languages, written by native or highly proficient speakers. Questions target concise, publicly verifiable answers requiring obscure evidence, multi-step clue chains, or heterogeneous sources (videos, scanned documents, images, maps). Easier questions are filtered out by evaluating them with models lacking internet access to reduce solvability via parametric knowledge. Models are evaluated using provider-native search and a shared external retrieval harness under a common agent protocol, alongside a human evaluation on a question sample.

Result: A challenging browsing benchmark spanning 13 languages and multiple evidence modalities is constructed, with models evaluated under a unified agent protocol using both provider-native search and an external retrieval harness; human performance on a sample is reported to contextualize model performance and effort.

Conclusion: HyperBrowseComp serves as a demanding testbed for persistent information seeking across languages and evidence modalities, with difficulty arising from discovering and connecting evidence on the open web.

Abstract: We introduce HyperBrowseComp, a multilingual and multimodal browsing benchmark comprising 423 manually authored and human-validated questions across 13 languages, written by native or highly proficient speakers. Questions are designed to be extremely challenging. Each question targets a concise, publicly verifiable answer whose discovery requires locating obscure evidence, following multi-step clue chains, or inspecting heterogeneous sources such as videos, scanned documents, images, or maps. Easier questions are filtered out by evaluating them with models without internet access to reduce the likelihood that they can be answered with parametric knowledge alone. We evaluate several models using provider-native search and a shared external retrieval harness under a common agent protocol. To contextualize model performance and effort, we also conduct a human evaluation on a sample of the questions. HyperBrowseComp provides a challenging testbed for persistent information seeking across languages and evidence modalities, with difficulty arising from discovering and connecting evidence on the open web.

</details>


### [117] [HazardWeaver: Scientific Route Selection for Hazard Analysis Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03591)
*Wangshu Zhu,Xueqi Cheng,Liang Wu,Yushun Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces HazardWeaver, an LLM agent framework for hazard analysis that formulates the task as state-dependent scientific route selection, using a Hazard Knowledge Compiler and Hazard Capability Graph, alongside a new benchmark for evaluating outputs and decision quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automating hazard analysis with AI agents is promising, but effective automation requires agents to determine which scientific methods are appropriate for an event and executable with available data and tools. As new evidence and execution results emerge, these conditions change, forcing agents to reconsider their choices.

Method: The problem is formulated as state-dependent scientific route selection. HazardWeaver uses a Hazard Knowledge Compiler to extract evidence-linked conditions governing scientific applicability, and a Hazard Capability Graph to represent executable scientific capabilities and check input-output compatibility. A Hazard Weaver Agent selects applicable and executable routes, runs their workflows, and revises decisions as the analysis state changes.

Result: A Hazard Weaver Benchmark of 141 instances spans seven single-hazard domains and four multi-hazard interaction classes, accommodating multiple valid scientific routes and evaluating output correctness, route validity, and justified abstention. Experiments show HazardWeaver outperforms existing agent systems, with the largest gains on tasks with multiple eligible scientific routes.

Conclusion: HazardWeaver advances AI-agent-based hazard analysis by coupling applicability conditions with executable capability representations and dynamic re-planning, and provides a benchmark that jointly evaluates scientific outputs and the decisions behind them. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Understanding and assessing natural hazards is essential for disaster preparedness and risk reduction. Recent advances in large language models have spurred growing interest in AI agents for hazard analysis, particularly their ability to integrate scientific data, models, and tools into automated workflows. However, effective automation requires agents to determine which scientific methods are appropriate for a given event and executable with the available data and tools. As new evidence and execution results become available, these conditions can change, requiring agents to reconsider their choices. We formulate this problem as state-dependent scientific route selection and introduce HazardWeaver. Specifically, HazardWeaver first leverages the Hazard Knowledge Compiler to extract evidence-linked conditions governing scientific applicability, then its Hazard Capability Graph represents executable scientific capabilities and checks compatibility between their inputs and outputs. Using these complementary representations, the Hazard Weaver Agent component selects applicable and executable routes, carries out their workflows, and revises its decisions as the analysis state changes. To evaluate both the scientific outputs and the decisions that produce them, we introduce the Hazard Weaver Benchmark, comprising 141 instances across seven single-hazard domains and four multi-hazard interaction classes. The benchmark accommodates multiple valid scientific routes and evaluates output correctness, route validity, and justified abstention. Extensive experiments on this benchmark show that HazardWeaver outperforms existing agent systems, with the largest gains on tasks with multiple eligible scientific routes. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/LabRAI/HazardWeaver.

</details>


### [118] [Low-Cost Video--Time Priors as a Strong Baseline for EEG--fNIRS Emotion Regression on Familiar Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03618)
*Minghao Kong,Jiurun Chen,Ying Gao,Xiangbin Meng,Rongjie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A video-time prior is a strong, low-cost baseline for continuous emotion regression on familiar videos, with EEG-fNIRS providing only small, variable residual correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous emotion regression (moment-to-moment valence and arousal) is typically assumed to benefit from physiological signals, but it is unclear whether physiology adds residual information beyond a strong participant- and video-specific response prior in familiar-video deployment.

Method: Five-fold subject-held-out evaluation with a prior-dominating fixed fusion design, testing whether EEG-fNIRS adds residual correction to training participant-specific responses. Source-explicit ablations quantified contributions of video identity, within-video time, and EEG-fNIRS.

Result: The video-time prior was within 0.05 MAE (internal) and 0.32 MAE (external) of fusion. Video identity and within-video time accounted for most of the reduction, while EEG-fNIRS gains were smaller and varied across participants and videos.

Conclusion: The video-time prior is a strong, low-cost baseline, positioning EEG-fNIRS as an optional residual signal for familiar-video emotion regression.

Abstract: Continuous emotion regression estimates moment-to-moment valence and arousal while a viewer watches a video. In familiar-video deployment, responses fron training participant-specific estimate, and prior-dominating fixed fusion tests whether physiology adds residual correction. In five-fold subject-held-out evaluation on 24was within 0.05 and 0.32 MAE of fusion in the internal and external evaluations, respectively. Source-explicit ablations showed that video identity and within-video tine accounted for most of the reduction, while EG-FNIRS gains were smaller and varied across participants and videos. These results identify the video-time prior as a strong, low-cost baseline and position EEG-fNIRS as an optional residual signal for familiar-video emotion regression.

</details>


### [119] [Depth as Time in One-Step Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03626)
*Arnold Caleb Asiimwe,William Yang,Sanghyuk Chun,Esin Tureci,Olga Russakovsky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: One-step generative models do not eliminate the temporal denoising computation of multi-step diffusion but reorganize it across network depth ("depth as time"), and this depthwise denoising enables dramatic parameter compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates what happens to the denoising trajectory of multi-step diffusion when generation is compressed into a single forward pass via distillation or learned flow maps, asking whether the temporal computation is lost or merely reorganized.

Method: The authors empirically probe intermediate layers of one-step generative models using the model's own output head, comparing flow-map models (e.g., MeanFlow) trained on time-indexed transport tasks against generators without such tasks (e.g., drifting models), and then exploit the layerwise computation as an explicit flow to train a single time-conditioned block.

Result: They observe "depth as time": denoising computation unfolds across depth, with MeanFlow showing denoising and renoising within a single evaluation when probing shorter transport intervals. Models exhibiting this phenomenon are more compressible across layers, allowing a MeanFlow SiT-L/2 model to be compressed 16.6x in parameters.

Conclusion: The temporal computation of diffusion is not eliminated by one-step generation but reorganized across network depth, and this structure can be leveraged for substantial parameter compression.

Abstract: The recent wave of one-step generative models, which compress the multi-step trajectory of diffusion via either distillation or learned flow maps, has reached an inflection point where they can generate high-quality images. Here, we ask a natural question that follows from these advances: what happens to the denoising trajectory of multi-step diffusion when generation is compressed into a single forward pass? We offer an empirical observation we call \textit{depth as time}: the denoising computation that multi-step diffusion performs across sampling steps appears to unfold across the depth of a single forward pass, and can be recovered by decoding intermediate layers with the model's own output head. Most interestingly, we show that this depthwise computation depends on the transport task a flow map is trained to solve. The most surprising case is MeanFlow, where probing shorter transport intervals reveals both denoising and renoising within a single network evaluation. In contrast, generators trained without a time-indexed transport task, such as drifting models, do not exhibit the same depthwise denoising. Consequently, we show that models that exhibit the depthwise denoising phenomenon are more compressible across the layerwise computation: a MeanFlow \texttt{SiT-L/2} model can be compressed by $16.6\times$ in parameters into a single time-conditioned block. We offer an explanation for this denoise-then-renoise behavior and show that, when we treat the layerwise computation explicitly as a flow, a single time-conditioned block can be trained to denoise across layers, compressing a MeanFlow \texttt{SiT-L/2} model by $16.6\times$ in parameters. Together, these results suggest that the temporal computation of diffusion is not eliminated by one-step generation, but reorganized across network depth.

</details>


### [120] [NeutronGym: Physics-Graded Neutron Instrument Design for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03631)
*Lijie Ding,Changwoo Do*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NeutronGym is introduced as the first executable environment for neutron instrument design, where LLM agents build instruments via validating tools, McStas ray-traces them, and a level-resolved ladder grades syntax, runtime, structure, and science without an LLM judge. Frontier models fail most curated tasks, but RL on the environment's reward lifts Qwen3-8B from 11% to 77% on held-out instances, revealing that partial credit and physics-informed rewards drive the gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to test whether language-model agents can genuinely do physics rather than merely recall it, and argues this requires a verifiable, non-arguable grading mechanism. It motivates an executable design environment with objective, multi-level grading to probe memorization and measure real design capability.

Method: The authors introduce NeutronGym, an executable environment for neutron instrument design. Agents construct instruments through validating tools, McStas performs ray-traces, and a level-resolved ladder grades syntax, runtime, structure, and science with no LLM judge. Procedural families generate unlimited instances of a fixed layout with held-out parameter regimes, while McStasBench adds 16 curated tasks from published instruments with memorization probes and a sandbox. The environment also supports RL training on its reward signal.

Result: Seven models reproduce at most 7 of 16 curated tasks, none retrieves a reference, and none meets the improvement target. RL on the environment reward raises Qwen3-8B from 11% to 77% on held-out instances of a family with hidden design targets (69% at a second seed), surpassing an untrained Qwen3-32B, and the recipe generalizes at one seed each to three further gated families. Removing partial credit causes a 60-point collapse. The trained model matches a classical optimizer with closed-form physics at the agent's simulation budget (77% vs 81%, a gap that does not separate at this size), while frontier models still solve 98-99%.

Conclusion: The environment provides a trustworthy, judge-free benchmark for physics-grounded agent evaluation and training. The findings show that partial credit is essential, that reward-driven RL can approach classical optimizer performance when given closed-form physics, and that frontier models retain a large advantage. Releasing the probes that exposed four solvable task designs underscores the commitment to honest evaluation.

Abstract: Designing a scientific instrument tests whether language-model agents can do physics rather than recall it, provided the grading cannot be argued with. We introduce NeutronGym, to our knowledge the first executable environment for neutron instrument design: agents build instruments through validating tools, McStas ray-traces what they build, and a level-resolved ladder grades syntax, runtime, structure and science with no LLM judge. Procedural families supply unlimited instances of a fixed layout whose design parameters the agent must set, with held-out parameter regimes; a curated slice, McStasBench, adds 16 tasks from published instruments behind memorization probes and a sandbox. Seven models reproduce at most 7 of the 16, none retrieves a reference, and none meets an improvement target. The environment also trains. Reinforcement learning on its reward takes Qwen3-8B from 11% to 77% of held-out instances of a family whose targets come from a hidden design (69% at a second seed), past an untrained Qwen3-32B, and the recipe holds, at one seed each, on three further gated families. The analysis says what that gain is. Without the ladder's partial credit it collapses by 60 points. From reward alone the trained model reaches what a classical optimizer reaches, at the agent's simulation budget, only when handed the closed-form physics (77% against 81%, a gap that does not separate at this size), while frontier models still solve 98-99%. Getting a trustworthy result meant failing four task designs that no-model baselines could solve, and we release the probes that found them.

</details>


### [121] [Credit Where It Matters: Dependency-Aware Policy Optimization for Terminal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03634)
*Yu Li,Guangfeng Cai,Long-Fei Li,Shuo Han,Shengtian Yang,Han Luo,Kaibing Yang,Lei Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DepGPO uses execution dependencies between commands to guide credit assignment for terminal-using RL agents, tracing backward from verifier-inspected resources to allocate credit to relevant writes and supporting reads along a command dependency graph.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Terminal-using agents trained with RL face credit assignment challenges because later commands depend on information or intermediate results from earlier commands. Existing trajectory-level and step-level credit assignment methods do not explicitly trace read-write dependencies, so training signals may be assigned to irrelevant operations, weakening learning from relevant steps.

Method: DepGPO constructs a command dependency graph from execution traces, traces backward from resources inspected by the task verifier, and assigns credit to relevant writes and their supporting reads along these dependency paths. This credit is then used to redistribute trajectory advantages across steps.

Result: Extensive comparative experiments and ablation studies demonstrate that DepGPO improves task performance and training stability on complex terminal tasks.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling read-write execution dependencies for credit assignment benefits RL training of terminal agents, yielding better performance and stability compared to existing trajectory- and step-level methods.

Abstract: Terminal-using agents benefit from reinforcement learning (RL) in coding, debugging, and other multi-step terminal tasks. In these tasks, later commands often depend on information or intermediate results produced by earlier commands. However, existing trajectory-level and step-level credit assignment methods do not explicitly trace the read-write dependencies through which commands affect the final outcome. Consequently, training signals could still be assigned to irrelevant operations, weakening learning from relevant steps. In this paper, we propose Dependency-Aware Group Policy Optimization (DepGPO), which uses execution dependencies between commands to guide credit assignment for terminal agents. Specifically, we construct a command dependency graph from execution traces and trace backward from the resources inspected by the task verifier. We then assign credit to relevant writes and their supporting reads along these paths, and use it to redistribute trajectory advantages across steps. Extensive comparative experiments and ablation studies demonstrate that DepGPO improves task performance and training stability on complex terminal tasks.

</details>


### [122] [Do Large Language Models Know Colombian Law? A Reliability Benchmark for the Colombian Legal System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03639)
*Rubén Manrique,Michelle Castellanos,Jorge Morales,Juan David Gutiérrez,Antonio Barreto Rozo,Joaquín Vélez Navarro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces an expert-validated benchmark of 1,042 items across ten legal areas and three formats to evaluate LLM reliability on the Colombian legal system, finding that free-text factual correctness never exceeds 0.45 despite high closed-question accuracy (up to 0.905).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed in legal practice, education, and research, but their reliability in national legal systems outside the United States remains largely undocumented, leaving a critical evaluation gap for non-US jurisdictions.

Method: The authors build a human-in-the-loop benchmark construction pipeline with multi-stage expert review, comprising 1,042 items spanning ten areas of law and three question formats (closed multiple-choice, semi-open, and open-ended IRAC). They evaluate 15 contemporary proprietary and open-weight models using format-appropriate metrics, plus an independent rubric-based LLM judge and blind human expert scoring.

Result: Closed-question accuracy ranges from 0.905 (Gemini 3.1 Pro) to 0.577, but free-text factual correctness never exceeds 0.45 (0-1 scale). A dissociation between answer relevancy and correctness emerges (Spearman rho = -0.46): models sound responsive while being frequently wrong. Closed-question accuracy and free-text correctness are strongly rank-correlated (rho = 0.94). The LLM judge and blind human expert scoring both reproduce the free-text ranking (rho >= 0.88). Only about half of cited norms are correct; reliability varies by legal area and follows an inverted-U across question complexity.

Conclusion: Current LLMs require expert supervision for Colombian legal tasks, and grounding answers in authoritative sources is a promising path to higher reliability. The benchmark construction pipeline is released to support reproducible evaluation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to support legal practice, education, and research, yet their reliability in national legal systems outside the United States remains largely undocumented. We introduce an expert-validated benchmark for evaluating LLM reliability on the Colombian legal system. The benchmark comprises 1,042 items spanning ten areas of law and three question formats (closed multiple-choice, semi-open, and open-ended IRAC), built through a human-in-the-loop pipeline with multi-stage expert review. We evaluate 15 contemporary proprietary and open-weight models with format-appropriate metrics. Accuracy on closed questions ranges widely, from 0.905 (Gemini 3.1 Pro) to 0.577, but on free-text legal answers factual correctness never exceeds 0.45 (on a 0-1 scale) for any model. We find a dissociation between answer relevancy and correctness (Spearman rho = -0.46): models reliably sound responsive while frequently being wrong, a pattern of particular concern for non-expert users. Closed-question accuracy and free-text correctness are strongly rank-correlated (rho = 0.94), so cheap multiple-choice screening predicts model ranking but overstates absolute reliability. An independent rubric-based LLM judge and blind human expert scoring both reproduce the free-text ranking (rho >= 0.88). The judge further reveals that only about half of the norms models cite are correct; the rest are wrong or non-existent. Reliability varies systematically by legal area and follows an inverted-U across question complexity. Our results indicate that current LLMs require expert supervision for Colombian legal tasks, and that grounding answers in authoritative sources is a promising path to higher reliability. We release the benchmark construction pipeline to support reproducible evaluation.

</details>


### [123] [MRVQ: One Resident Index for Dimension- and Rate-Elastic Vector Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03651)
*Sean Culatana,Shang-En Huang,Kang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MRVQ is a post-hoc residual vector quantizer for frozen embeddings whose maximum-rate code can be truncated by dropping residual stages (lowering rate) or embedding coordinates (lowering dimension), so a single resident artifact serves every (dimension, rate) pair evaluated. It is the lowest-RAM design tested, using 17.8-22.0x less memory than three separately trained QINCo2 indices and 1.89-2.02x less than a lean shared-model steelman, at a small nDCG@10 cost versus per-rate QINCo2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense-retrieval services must switch among embedding-prefix dimensions and index bit rates as latency, quality, and memory budgets change. Tuning a quantizer separately for each rate yields the best quality, but the retrieval tier then must hold several code streams and quantizer states at once, which is memory-expensive.

Method: Matryoshka Residual Vector Quantization (MRVQ), a post-hoc residual quantizer for frozen embeddings. Its maximum-rate code admits two truncation modes: dropping residual stages lowers the rate, and dropping embedding coordinates lowers the dimension. The paper also evaluates a low-build-cost PCA-scalar design, and reports two negative results (QINCo2 collapse at high training rates and a ranking-bound hypothesis that missed pre-specified acceptance criteria).

Result: Across FiQA and NFCorpus, four embedding families, and {4, 8, 16}-byte codes, MRVQ is the lowest-RAM design evaluated: 17.8-22.0x less memory than three separately trained QINCo2 indices and 1.89-2.02x less than a lean shared-model steelman. Per-rate QINCo2 is 0.026-0.107 nDCG@10 better on FiQA, but MRVQ beats PQ, OPQ, and AdANNS-OPQ at matched code size. The PCA-scalar design attains quality comparable to RaBitQ and its extension while fitting 420x faster at the median.

Conclusion: MRVQ is a low-memory operating point for elastic retrieval rather than a universal quality winner: it trades a modest nDCG@10 deficit against per-rate QINCo2 for large memory savings and single-artifact serving across dimension/rate pairs.

Abstract: Dense-retrieval services must switch among embedding-prefix dimensions and index bit rates as latency, quality, and memory budgets change. Tuning a quantizer separately for each rate gives the best quality, but the retrieval tier then holds several code streams and quantizer states at once. We introduce Matryoshka Residual Vector Quantization (MRVQ), a post-hoc residual quantizer for frozen embeddings. Its maximum-rate code can be truncated two ways: dropping residual stages lowers the rate, and dropping embedding coordinates lowers the dimension. One resident artifact therefore serves every (dimension, rate) pair we evaluate. Across FiQA and NFCorpus, four embedding families, and {4, 8, 16}-byte codes, MRVQ is the lowest-RAM design we evaluate. It uses 17.8-22.0x less memory than three separately trained QINCo2 indices, and 1.89-2.02x less than a lean shared-model steelman. The saving is not free: per-rate QINCo2 is 0.026-0.107 nDCG@10 better on FiQA. But MRVQ beats PQ, OPQ, and AdANNS-OPQ at matched code size. We also evaluate a low-build-cost PCA-scalar design that attains quality comparable to RaBitQ and its extension while fitting 420x faster at the median. Finally, we report two negative results: QINCo2 collapses when trained at high rates, and a ranking-bound hypothesis misses its pre-specified acceptance criteria. MRVQ is therefore a low-memory operating point for elastic retrieval, not a universal quality winner.

</details>


### [124] [Transcriptome-informed multi-modal AI for predicting neoadjuvant therapy response from breast cancer biopsies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03693)
*Jungkyu Park,Dhruva Biswas,Joseph Cappadona,Cerise Tang,Ken G. Zeng,Bartosz Machura,Chuwen Liu,Paolo Tarantino,Coral Omene,Francisco J. Esteva,Rohit Bhargava,Marcin Braun,Kamila Paździerz,Jakub Czerwiński,Hanna Romańska-Knight,Albert Grinshpun,Bareket Daniel,Michele Buchinger,Frederick Howard,Piotr Wysocki,Brie Chun,Freya Schnabel,Rich Caruana,Jan Witowski,Krzysztof J. Geras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A two-stage AI model first infers transcriptome from histopathology (trained on 8,742 patients, 32 cancer types), then predicts pCR to neoadjuvant therapy in breast cancer from inferred expression plus clinical variables, achieving pooled AUROC 0.79 across nine evaluation cohorts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Labeled data scarcity constrains the development of deep learning biomarkers in oncology, motivating approaches that leverage abundant unlabeled or indirectly supervised data to enable prediction in data-sparse clinical tasks.

Method: Two-stage AI model: Stage 1 learns to infer the transcriptome from histopathology using 8,742 patients across 32 cancer types, validated by pathologist review and spatial agreement with measured expression. Stage 2 predicts pCR from inferred expression and clinical variables, trained on 1,080 patients (five cohorts) and evaluated on 1,412 patients (nine cohorts), with ablations against clinical-variable-only and one-stage pathology models.

Result: Pooled AUROC 0.79 (95% CI, 0.73-0.85), with discrimination of responders within molecular subtypes. Outperforms histopathological biomarkers, remains stable across intratumoral sampling and with minimal biopsy tissue. Ablations show transcriptome-wide inference improves discrimination and avoids gene-selection constraints of genomic assays.

Conclusion: Biologically informed compression may generalize to data-sparse applications in precision oncology.

Abstract: Scarcity of labeled data limits development of deep learning biomarkers in oncology. We develop a two-stage AI model predicting pathological complete response (pCR) to neoadjuvant therapy in breast cancer. The first stage learns the transcriptome from histopathology using 8,742 patients across 32 cancer types, corroborated by pathologist review and spatial agreement with measured expression. This simplifies the second stage to predicting pCR from inferred expression and clinical variables. Developed using 1,080 patients (five cohorts) and evaluated in 1,412 patients (nine cohorts), the model achieves a pooled AUROC of 0.79 (95% CI, 0.73-0.85), discriminating responders within molecular subtypes. It outperforms histopathological biomarkers, remaining stable across intratumoral sampling and with minimal biopsy tissue. Ablations show transcriptome-wide inference improves discrimination over clinical variables alone or one-stage pathology models, and robustness by avoiding genomic assays' gene selection constraints. These results indicate that biologically informed compression may generalize to data-sparse applications in precision oncology.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [125] [Awomo-SimDataEngine: Agentic Simulation-ReadyWorld Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02274)
*Awomo-PhysicalRSI Team,Danjiao Ma,Enhui Ma,Haohan Liu,Heng Jia,Hui Shan,Jianhua Xu,Jiahuan Zhang,Jiangdi Xu,Kaiwen Guo,Kaicheng Yu,Linwei Zhang,Liyang Jin,Maochun Luo,Pengyao Niu,Shiwen Li,Shuangyu Feng,Tong Zhang,Tianheng Wang,Xin Wang,Xiangru Huang,Yongqiang Huang,Zhaozhi Wang,Zijian Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Awomo-SimDataEngine is an agentic system connecting asset/scene generation to robot demonstration synthesis, using shared asset services, dual-route scene generation, a graph-native validation/repair harness, and PolicyForge; co-training with its Isaac Sim demos raises a WAM's LIBERO-Plus success from 77.17% to 89.43%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Useful robot-training data demands more than visually plausible scenes: objects must support interaction, placements must remain physically valid, and tasks must be repeatably executable. Existing pipelines typically decouple asset, scene, and task generation, leaving gaps in interactivity, physical validity, and reproducibility.

Method: An agentic pipeline with four components: (1) shared asset services generating rigid and articulated objects, including structure-grounded part and joint generation via ISArt; (2) two complementary scene-generation routes—Unravel reconstructs editable scenes from images, SimForge builds single- and multi-room environments from text; (3) a graph-native harness coordinating construction, validation, and bounded repair that routes failures to the responsible module while retaining unaffected scene state; (4) PolicyForge, which binds validated worlds to tasks and robot embodiments to produce replayable demonstrations.

Result: Evaluations span asset geometry, scene quality, and downstream policy learning. On MuJoCo-based LIBERO-Plus, co-training with Isaac Sim demonstrations improves World-Action Model (WAM) overall success from 77.17% to 89.43%. Goal and spatial success rise by 31.66 and 6.25 percentage points, respectively. Gains are more limited on long-horizon tasks.

Conclusion: The generated data are useful for cross-simulator policy training, validating the integrated agentic generation-to-demonstration pipeline. Effectiveness on long-horizon tasks remains limited, indicating a direction for future improvement.

Abstract: Generating useful robot-training data requires more than visually plausiblescenes: objects must support interaction, placements must remain physicallyvalid, and tasks must admit repeatable execution. We present\textbf{Awomo-SimDataEngine}, an agentic system that connects asset and scenegeneration to robot demonstration synthesis. Shared asset services providerigid and articulated objects, including structure-grounded part and jointgeneration with ISArt. Scene generation supports two complementary routes:Unravel reconstructs editable scenes from images, while SimForge buildssingle-room and multi-room environments from text. A graph-native harnesscoordinates construction, validation, andbounded repair, routing failures to the responsible module while retainingunaffected scene state. PolicyForge binds validated worlds to tasks and robotembodiments to produce replayable demonstrations. Evaluations cover assetgeometry, scene quality, and downstream policy learning. On MuJoCo-basedLIBERO-Plus, co-training with Isaac Sim demonstrations improves the overallsuccess rate of a World-Action Model (WAM) from $77.17\%$ to $89.43\%$. Goal and spatialsuccess improve by $31.66$ and $6.25$ percentage points, respectively.These results support the utility of the generated data for cross-simulatorpolicy training, with more limited gains on long-horizon tasks.

</details>


### [126] [World-Calibrated Proposal-to-Action Flow for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02323)
*Jie He,Wei Li,Junwen Tong,Rui Shao,Wei-Shi Zheng,Liqiang Nie*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ProAct is a world-calibrated proposal-to-action framework that makes the generative source of flow-based VLA policies predictable, preserving motion continuity and improving efficiency over π_0.5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow-based VLA policies transport samples from a task-agnostic isotropic Gaussian source that is conditioned on neither recent execution nor predicted future evolution. This discards the local continuity established by recently executed motion. Moreover, even when predictive world representations are introduced, they typically only condition the transport dynamics rather than determine where generation starts, how far it may deviate, or along which action directions it may expand.

Method: ProAct comprises three components: (i) a lightweight Proposal Expert that converts recent actions into a scene-aware hypothesis via one motion-anchored endpoint flow-matching step, initializing generation near the demonstrated action manifold; (ii) a prospective World Expert that treats the hypothesis as a soft motion prior while predicting the task-consistent latent future; and (iii) a proposal-centered anisotropic source, where a bounded per-step extent controls allowed deviation and a trace-normalized low-rank geometry under a condition-number budget allocates refinement over coupled translation, rotation, and gripper directions.

Result: Compared with π_0.5, ProAct improves performance across simulation and real-world tasks while reducing denoising steps by 50%, inference latency by up to 25.8%, and increasing throughput by up to 34.8%.

Conclusion: Making the generative source itself predictable via a world-calibrated proposal-to-action framework yields both performance gains and substantial efficiency improvements in flow-based VLA policies.

Abstract: Flow-based Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies generate action chunks by transporting samples from a task-agnostic isotropic Gaussian source. As this source is conditioned on neither recent execution nor predicted future evolution, (i) it discards the local continuity established by recently executed motion. (ii) Even when predictive world representations are introduced, they often only condition the transport dynamics rather than determine where generation starts, how far it may deviate, or along which action directions it may expand. Building on this observation, we introduce ProAct, a world-calibrated proposal-to-action framework that makes the generative source itself predictable. (i) To preserve motion continuity, a lightweight Proposal Expert converts recent actions into a scene-aware hypothesis via one motion-anchored endpoint flow-matching step, initializing generation near the demonstrated action manifold. (ii) To jointly capture intended scene evolution and proposal-future compatibility, a prospective World Expert treats the hypothesis as a soft motion prior while predicting the task-consistent latent future. (iii) From this compatibility, the model calibrates a proposal-centered anisotropic source, where a bounded per-step extent controls the allowed deviation and a trace-normalized low-rank geometry under a condition-number budget allocates refinement over coupled translation, rotation, and gripper directions. Compared with $π_{0.5}$, ProAct improves performance across simulation and real-world tasks while reducing denoising steps by 50%, inference latency by up to 25.8%, and increasing throughput by up to 34.8%.

</details>


### [127] [SoTa: Soft Tactile Skins for Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02338)
*Jingyun Yang,Baiyu Shi,Timothy Yu,Haitian Liu,Alberta Longhini,Weichen Wang,Rika Antonova,Zhenan Bao,Jeannette Bohg*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SoTa is a low-cost capacitive tactile skin with full-hand coverage and a shared 202-taxel layout across human and robot hands, enabling scalable human-robot co-training for dexterous manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visuo-tactile robot data is scarce because dexterous demonstrations rely on robot teleoperation, which limits dataset scale. Human demonstrations are cheaper but require matching tactile signals across human and robot hands, demanding sensors that conform to different hand geometries, cover the full hand, and share a common layout across embodiments.

Method: A low-cost capacitive tactile skin (SoTa) with a multilayer design using fabric electrodes, fabricable in-house for under $10 per skin in materials, providing full-hand coverage and a shared layout of 202 taxels across corresponding finger and palm regions. The shared taxel layout supports human-robot co-training with a common tactile encoder and no learned cross-sensor mapping.

Result: The sensor retains over 97% of its initial response span after 10,000 loading-unloading cycles, with traces remaining continuous through 1,280 tight-fist folding cycles. Tactile observations improve in-distribution success over vision-only policies across three contact-rich manipulation tasks. With a fixed robot demonstration budget, adding human demonstrations more than doubles mean success across eight evaluation conditions (22.8% to 45.9%), improving success in all five out-of-distribution conditions.

Conclusion: SoTa enables scalable, embodiment-agnostic visuo-tactile data collection via a shared sensor layout and common tactile encoder, demonstrating significant gains from human-robot co-training. The authors plan to open-source fabrication and operation resources.

Abstract: A growing body of work suggests that tactile sensing gives robot policies contact information that complements vision in dexterous manipulation. However, visuo-tactile robot data remains scarce: dexterous demonstrations require teleoperating robots, which limits dataset scale. Human demonstrations are far cheaper to collect and offer a path to scale this data, but only if human and robot hands carry tactile sensors with corresponding signals. This requires sensors that conform to different hand geometries, cover the full hand, and share a common layout across embodiments. We present SoTa, a low-cost capacitive tactile skin that provides full-hand coverage on humans and robots while preserving a shared layout of 202 taxels across corresponding finger and palm regions. Our multilayer design with fabric electrodes enables in-house fabrication of thin, soft skins with customizable geometry for under $10 in materials per skin. The sensor retains over 97% of its initial response span after 10,000 loading-unloading cycles with traces retaining continuity through 1,280 tight-fist folding cycles. The shared taxel layout supports human-robot co-training with a common tactile encoder and no learned cross-sensor mapping. Across three contact-rich manipulation tasks, tactile observations improve in-distribution success over vision-only policies. With a fixed robot demonstration budget, adding human demonstrations more than doubles mean success across eight evaluation conditions, from 22.8% to 45.9%, improving success in all five out-of-distribution conditions. We plan to open-source the resources needed to fabricate and operate these skins.

</details>


### [128] [NEEDLEWORK: Offline Rewriting of Robot Data with Verified Local Stitches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02339)
*Juntao Ren,Yifan Hou,Shuran Song*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NEEDLE is an offline dataset-augmentation algorithm that adds short, verified action bridges between recorded observations in high-dimensional robot demonstrations, improving policy success rate by an average of 21 percentage points over the strongest baseline on real-robot tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot demonstrations often contain useful behavior even when individual episodes are inefficient or unsuccessful. Trajectory stitching can compose these behaviors into improved training data, but identifying useful connections and verifying their feasibility is difficult in high-dimensional robot data, where many prior methods rely on low-dimensional state representations.

Method: NEEDLE identifies and creates connections that bypass suboptimal detours, broaden action coverage, and augment the original dataset with failed trajectories, using only RGB images, proprioception, and episode-level outcomes, without new environment interaction or privileged object state. It then presents a sampling technique that incorporates accepted bridges into policy training without synthesizing intermediate images or discarding the original demonstrations.

Result: On real-robot tasks, NEEDLE improves success rate over the strongest baseline on each task by an average of 21 percentage points.

Conclusion: NEEDLE enables effective stitching of high-dimensional robot demonstrations via verified action bridges, allowing policies to learn alternative actions while retaining the original dataset's coverage.

Abstract: Robot demonstrations may contain useful behavior even when individual episodes are inefficient or unsuccessful. Trajectory stitching offers a way to compose these behaviors into improved training data, but identifying useful connections and verifying their feasibility is difficult in high-dimensional robot data, where many prior methods rely on low-dimensional state representations. We introduce NEEDLE, an offline dataset-augmentation algorithm that addresses these challenges by adding short, verified action bridges between recorded observations in high-dimensional robot demonstrations. First, NEEDLE identifies and creates connections that bypass suboptimal detours, broaden action coverage, and augment the original dataset with failed trajectories, using only RGB images, proprioception, and episode-level outcomes, without new environment interaction or privileged object state. Next, we present a sampling technique that incorporates accepted bridges into policy training without synthesizing intermediate images or discarding the original demonstrations, allowing policies to learn alternative actions while retaining the original dataset's coverage. On real-robot tasks, NEEDLE improves success rate over the strongest baseline on each task by an average of 21 percentage points. Videos and supplementary materials are on https://needle-work.github.io/.

</details>


### [129] [Filter-Aware Fine-Tuning for Safe Humanoid Whole-Body Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02341)
*Pranit Mohnot,Christian Helten,Daniele Gammelli,Marco Pavone*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper identifies fundamental mismatches between pretrained whole-body tracking policies and runtime safety filters (CBFs), and proposes CoFiT, a filter-aware fine-tuning method that substantially reduces constraint violations on humanoid robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safe whole-body motion is critical for deploying humanoid robots in unstructured environments. Modern pipelines separate reference specification (planner/teleoperator/motion generator) from execution (RL tracker), with runtime safety filters like CBFs enforcing new constraints by intervening on the tracker's outputs. The authors argue that treating the tracking policy and safety filter independently is flawed, since filtering alters both executed actions and the induced state distribution, creating dynamics, objective, and information mismatches.

Method: The authors study the policy-filter interface through case studies that isolate dynamics, objective, and information mismatches, identify their root causes, and use these insights to develop CoFiT (Constrained Filter-aware Tuning), a filter-aware fine-tuning method for pretrained trackers.

Result: Across diverse constraint scenes, CoFiT reduces violation time relative to filter-only training by 91% on TWIST2 and 21% on SONIC, while requiring smaller safety filter corrections. On Unitree G1 hardware, CoFiT reduces violation time by 83% for TWIST2 and completes every trial without operator intervention, whereas 50% of baseline trials require an operator stop.

Conclusion: The results provide actionable insights into policy-filter interactions and establish design principles for integrating learned trackers with runtime safety filters.

Abstract: Safe whole-body motion is essential for deploying humanoid robots in unstructured environments. Modern humanoid control commonly separates reference specification from execution, with a planner, teleoperator, or motion generator providing a reference that a reinforcement-learning policy tracks through dynamically feasible whole-body control. Runtime safety filters, such as control barrier functions (CBFs), offer a promising approach for enforcing newly introduced constraints via interventions on the tracker's outputs. We show, however, that treating the tracking policy and safety filter independently induces fundamental mismatches, as filtering alters both the executed actions and the induced state distribution. We study this policy-filter interface through case studies that isolate dynamics, objective, and information mismatches, highlight their root causes, and use these insights to develop CoFiT (Constrained Filter-aware Tuning), a filter-aware fine-tuning method for pretrained trackers. Across diverse constraint scenes, CoFiT reduces violation time relative to filter-only training by 91% on TWIST2 and 21% on SONIC, while requiring smaller safety filter corrections. On Unitree G1 hardware, CoFiT reduces violation time by 83% for TWIST2 and completes every trial without operator intervention, whereas 50% of baseline trials require an operator stop. Together, these results provide actionable insights into policy-filter interactions and establish design principles for integrating learned trackers with runtime safety filters.

</details>


### [130] [SocialVLA: A Social Perception Gateway for Human-Reaction-Based Failure Detection and Recovery in VLA Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02360)
*Sofya Konstantinova,Miguel Altamirano Cabrera,Artem Lykov,Dzmitry Tsetserukou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SocialVLA is a local, policy-agnostic perception gateway that converts spontaneous human social reactions (paralinguistic audio, visual, and explicit stop phrases) into real-time intervention signals to hold a VLA manipulation policy during execution failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA policies enable diverse robotic manipulation but can fail during execution without recognizing their own errors. Human observers naturally provide complementary error signals through rapid vocal, facial, or verbal reactions that occur before failure completes, which can be exploited for runtime intervention.

Method: SocialVLA combines causal paralinguistic audio detection, visual reaction recognition, explicit stop phrases, and robot-relevance estimation. An asynchronous first-event fusion mechanism triggers a VLA hold from the earliest sufficiently confident signal, while a separate speech channel captures verbal corrections enabling participant-directed continuation, restart, or instruction revision.

Result: Evaluated on physical Unitree G1 manipulation with 15 participants (238 annotated intervention-worthy episodes, 1.038 h of non-intervention behavior): frozen offline replay reaches 54.6% recall / 69.5% precision; unfiltered audio-video fusion reaches 64.3% recall; relevance estimation reduces false-stops from 100 to 57 and raises precision from 60.5% to 69.8%. On an unseen 16th participant, the frozen system achieves 59.5% recall and 91.7% precision. Median detector-to-fusion latency is 47.9 ms, VLA-gate-to-physical-hold latency is 336 ms, and reaction-onset-to-hold latency is 1.021 s.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate a complete local pathway from spontaneous social reaction to physical VLA interruption and participant-directed recovery.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies enable diverse robotic manipulation but can fail during execution without recognizing their own errors. Human observers provide complementary signals, as unexpected robot behavior can trigger rapid vocal, facial, or verbal reactions before failure is completed. We introduce SocialVLA, a local, policy-agnostic social perception gateway that converts spontaneous human reactions into runtime intervention signals for VLA manipulation. SocialVLA combines causal paralinguistic audio detection, visual reaction recognition, explicit stop phrases, and robot-relevance estimation. An asynchronous first-event fusion mechanism triggers a VLA hold from the earliest sufficiently confident signal, while a separate speech channel captures verbal corrections for participant-directed continuation, restart, or instruction revision. We evaluate SocialVLA on physical Unitree G1 manipulation using 15 participants, with 238 annotated intervention-worthy episodes and 1.038 h of non-intervention behavior. Frozen offline replay achieves 54.6% recall and 69.5% precision, while unfiltered audio-video fusion reaches 64.3% recall. Relevance estimation reduces false-stop episodes from 100 to 57 and increases precision from 60.5% to 69.8%. In prospective deployment on an unseen 16th participant, the frozen system achieves 59.5% recall and 91.7% precision. Median detector-to-fusion latency is 47.9 ms, VLA-gate-to-physical-hold latency is 336 ms, and reaction-onset-to-hold latency is 1.021 s. These results demonstrate a complete local pathway from spontaneous social reaction to physical VLA interruption and participant-directed recovery.

</details>


### [131] [Rethinking World-Action Model for Compositional and In-Context Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02368)
*Shukai Gong,Xuanran Zhai,Yintianrun Zhang,Ruopeng Cui,Ye Huang,Yiyang Fu,Dexuan Lyu,Chaojie Li,Xinyi Song,Peiwen Lin,Chuang Wang,Mingyuan Jia,Yufan Deng,Jiaxin Fang,Bo Liang,Jiaxin Li,Yuxiang Gao,Hao Liu,Daquan Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ViGAR is a hierarchical world-action framework that decomposes long-horizon manipulation into a visual subgoal planner and a subgoal executor sharing a pretrained world-model representation, achieving state-of-the-art results on RoboTwin Clean2Random and real-world tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon compositional manipulation requires coordinating multiple subtasks, yet existing world-action models (WAMs) predict only short-horizon visual futures and actions without explicit subtask-level reasoning, limiting their real-world deployment.

Method: ViGAR factorizes manipulation hierarchically: a subgoal planner predicts a visual subgoal for the next subtask given the current observation and global instruction, and a subgoal executor jointly generates future visual trajectories and actions conditioned on that subgoal. Both components share a pretrained world-model representation, and the framework supports in-context learning via a global goal image without parameter updates.

Result: On RoboTwin Clean2Random, ViGAR reaches 82.00% (Clean) and 67.02% (Random) success rates, surpassing the strongest baseline by 12.86 percentage points in average success rate. Real-world experiments on five compositional and two in-context learning tasks further confirm its effectiveness.

Conclusion: Explicit subtask-level reasoning through a shared world-model representation enables effective long-horizon compositional manipulation and flexible in-context task decomposition without retraining.

Abstract: Long-horizon compositional manipulation has become increasingly important for real-world robot deployment, where a single task involves multiple coordinated subtasks. Existing world-action models (WAMs) jointly predict short-horizon visual futures and actions, but typically lack explicit subtask-level reasoning. We propose Visual Goal-conditioned Action Reasoning (ViGAR), a hierarchical framework that factorizes manipulation into a visual subgoal planner and a subgoal executor. Given the current observation and global instruction, the subgoal planner predicts a visual subgoal for the next subtask. The subgoal executor then jointly generates future visual trajectories and actions conditioned on the predicted subgoal. Both components share a pretrained world-model representation, enabling task-level planning and action generation to benefit from common physical knowledge. Moreover, our framework naturally supports in-context learning: using a global goal image as context can induce different subtask decompositions and behaviors without parameter updates. On the RoboTwin Clean2Random benchmark, ViGAR achieves 82.00% and 67.02% success rates under the Clean and Random settings, respectively, surpassing the strongest baseline by 12.86 percentage points in average success rate. Real-world robot experiments on five compositional and two in-context learning tasks further confirm the effectiveness of ViGAR.

</details>


### [132] [Keep the Effect, Drop the Actor: Programmable Effect-to-Execution World-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02398)
*Junyi Hu,Zhewen He,Zhenhua Li,Yi Fang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A robot demonstration is compiled into an "effect program" of object-centric 3D keypoint trajectories, hold points, and terminal scene configuration with the demonstrator removed; PEWAM, a 71.5M-parameter world-action model, generates effect, robot execution, action and terminal state as four independently time-flow-matched streams, so clamping a program and sampling execution turns inference into closed-loop robot programming with a one-shot demonstration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic demonstrations entangle two signals in the same frames: the task-relevant effect on objects and the specific embodiment-dependent motions of the demonstrator's arm. Conditioning learning on the full demonstration conflates "what should happen" with "how this particular arm did it," limiting generalization across tasks, arms and perturbations, and preventing humans from editing the task specification.

Method: A demonstration is compiled into an effect program: 3D keypoint trajectories of moved objects, two points marking where each object was held, and the scene's terminal configuration with the demonstrator removed. PEWAM (71.5M parameters) is a world-action model that generates four streams—effect, robot execution, action, and terminal state—with independent flow-matching times. Clamping the program and sampling the execution stream converts inference into programming, re-solved closed loop from the live scene.

Result: On held-out LIBERO-Goal tasks, one demonstration's program completes 40/90 episodes, versus at most 19 for the same backbone conditioned on a goal image or language and for published demonstration-conditioned methods. It exceeds best published results on three of Meta-World's held-out classes, though not on the five-class average. Programs are editable as coordinates: shifted terminal state moves placement, rotated contact points rotate the grasp. The same program runs on four robot arms without retraining; after a push, re-solving completes 23/60 episodes where replaying the demonstration completes 6. On a Franka fine-tuned on real demonstrations of other tasks, programs compiled from single human videos complete 36/40 trials versus 22 for the same backbone conditioned on the video's last frame as a goal image.

Conclusion: Separating object-centric effect from embodiment-specific execution yields a compact, editable, transferable task representation: a single compiled program generalizes across arms, survives perturbations via closed-loop re-solving, transfers from human video to robot, and outperforms goal-image, language and prior demonstration-conditioned baselines, at the cost of a slight gap on Meta-World's five-class mean.

Abstract: A robot demonstration records two things in the same frames: what happened to the objects, and how one particular arm made it happen. We condition on the first. A demonstration is compiled into an effect program: the 3D keypoint trajectories of the objects that moved, two points marking where each was held, and the configuration the scene ends in, with the demonstrator removed. PEWAM, a 71.5M-parameter world-action model, generates effect, robot execution, action and terminal state as four streams with independent flow-matching times, so clamping a program and sampling the execution turns inference into programming, re-solved closed loop from the live scene. On held-out LIBERO-Goal tasks, one demonstration's program completes 40 of 90 episodes, where the same backbone given a goal image or language, and published demonstration-conditioned methods, complete at most 19; on three of Meta-World's held-out classes it exceeds the best published results, though not on the five-class mean. Because a program is a set of coordinates, a person can edit it: the placement follows a shifted terminal state and the grasp turns with rotated contact points. The same program runs on four robot arms without retraining, and after a push, re-solving completes 23 of 60 episodes where replaying the demonstration completes 6. On a Franka arm, fine-tuned on real demonstrations of other tasks, programs compiled from single human videos complete 36 of 40 trials, against 22 for the same backbone conditioned on the video's last frame as a goal image.

</details>


### [133] [Autonomous mobile robot operations logistics: a dataset of jobs, dispatch events and robot states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02428)
*Jan-Felix Klein,Yongkuk Jeong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MoRoOp is a dataset of autonomous mobile robot (AMR) operations collected over nine eight-hour shifts in a laboratory kit-preparation and supply scenario, providing linked job, operation, dispatch, and robot-state data for reuse in AI and logistics research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AMRs increasingly perform material transport in production logistics, where their operation depends on job generation, dispatching, and robot control. However, the paper does not explicitly spell out a research gap in this abstract beyond motivating the need to capture realistic AMR operational data.

Method: AMR operations were recorded in a laboratory kit-preparation and supply scenario across nine eight-hour shifts. During each shift, an AMR executed stochastically generated kit supply, empty-box refill, and charging jobs. The dataset links job specifications, constituent operations, dispatch events documenting operation state transitions and outcomes, and robot-state observations including position, orientation, velocity, per-wheel state of charge, and diagnostics. Technically valid observations of delays, obstructed navigation, and unsuccessful operations were retained, and both raw and cleaned robot-state tables are provided.

Result: The dataset contains 1,382 jobs, 4,815 operations, 19,352 dispatch events, and 140,386 robot-state observations, along with the kit specifications used during job generation.

Conclusion: MoRoOp supports reuse directions such as evaluating AI agents on operational decision records, disturbance detection, operation prediction, data-driven simulation, and event-log analysis.

Abstract: Autonomous mobile robots (AMRs) increasingly perform material transport in production logistics, where their operation is governed by job generation, dispatching and robot control. We present MoRoOp, a dataset of AMR operations recorded in a laboratory kit preparation and supply scenario over nine eight-hour shifts. During each shift, an AMR executed stochastically generated kit supply, empty-box refill and charging jobs. The dataset links job specifications, the operations constituting each job, dispatch events documenting operation state transitions and outcomes, and robot-state observations comprising position, orientation, velocity, per-wheel state of charge and diagnostics. It contains 1,382 jobs, 4,815 operations, 19,352 dispatch events and 140,386 robot-state observations together with the kit specifications used during job generation. The dataset was recorded in an operating laboratory environment, and technically valid observations of delays, obstructed navigation and unsuccessful operations were retained. Both raw and cleaned robot-state tables are provided. Documented reuse directions include the evaluation of AI agents on operational decision records, disturbance detection, operation prediction, data-driven simulation and event-log analysis.

</details>


### [134] [OpenRUA: Robot-Use Agents Are Zero-Shot Visuomotor Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02459)
*Zhaoyang Chu,Earl T. Barr,Claire Le Goues,Peter O'Hearn,Mark Harman,Federica Sarro,He Ye*

Main category: cs.RO
