TL;DR: OpenRUA is a zero-abstraction harness that gives off-the-shelf coding agents only terminal access to ROS 2, achieving 99.0% on CaP-Bench and 87.0% on LIBERO-PRO and revealing emergent perception and control behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior robot-agent work relies on complex custom harnesses with prescribed workflows and bespoke interfaces, raising the question of whether such additional harness engineering is actually necessary.

Method: A minimalist workspace-as-harness design that bypasses bespoke abstraction layers, providing coding agents only terminal access to the robot's native ROS 2 software interface, along with ROS 2 documentation and basic tools, while leaving the agent to organize its own work. Perception is recast as file I/O and manipulation as coding, with Claude Code powered by Claude Opus 5.

Result: Success rates of 99.0% on CaP-Bench and 87.0% on LIBERO-PRO, showing a zero-shot visuomotor policy without bespoke primitives or task-specific training. Emergent behaviors: metric measurement from raw sensory inputs in 96.80% of episodes, motion-control clients in 95.87%, and closed-loop control programs in 50.13%.

Conclusion: An off-the-shelf coding agent can serve as a zero-shot visuomotor policy through the robot's native interface, and elaborate custom harness engineering may be unnecessary. Code is available at https://github.com/terminalworld/OpenRUA.

Abstract: Coding agents are extending their reach into the physical world by writing and executing robot control programs. One might expect the agents to use the existing mature software stack that engineers have developed over decades to access sensors and control motion. Yet prior work primarily engineers complex custom harnesses to orchestrate agents for robot use, particularly by prescribing specialized workflows and providing bespoke interfaces. This raises the question: "Is such additional harness engineering necessary?" We introduce OpenRUA, a zero-abstraction harness that bypasses bespoke abstraction layers by providing off-the-shelf coding agents with only terminal access to the robot's native software interface ROS 2. OpenRUA employs a minimalist workspace-as-harness design, only offering ROS 2 documentation and basic tools while leaving the coding agent to organize its own work without orchestrating any agentic workflow. Within this workspace, OpenRUA recasts perception as file I/O and manipulation as coding. With Claude Code powered by Claude Opus 5, OpenRUA achieves success rates of 99.0% on CaP-Bench and 87.0% on LIBERO-PRO, demonstrating that an off-the-shelf coding agent can serve as a zero-shot visuomotor policy through the robot's native interface, without bespoke primitives or task-specific training. Under this minimalist design, further analysis reveals striking emergent behaviors of coding agents: (1) For perception, the agent spontaneously writes programs that process raw sensory inputs and derive metric measurements in 96.80% of episodes. (2) For manipulation, the agent spontaneously builds motion-control clients (e.g., gripper control) in 95.87% of episodes and closed-loop control programs (e.g., adjusting motion based on sensor feedback) in 50.13% of episodes. Our code is available at https://github.com/terminalworld/OpenRUA.

</details>


### [135] [RUL-Aware RRT*: Degradation-Balanced Motion Planning for Robotic Manipulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02469)
*Haibo Li,Zhiguo Zeng,Xu Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes RUL-aware RRT*, a motion-planning method that integrates joint remaining useful life into planning to mitigate uneven joint degradation in long-duration robotic manipulator operation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-duration manipulator operation causes uneven joint degradation, where the weakest actuator fails prematurely, leading to unplanned downtime and operational losses. Traditional motion planners ignore joint health, exacerbating imbalance over extended operation.

Method: RUL-aware RRT* incorporates joint remaining useful life (RUL) information into the planning process and adaptively adjusts joint usage according to evolving health conditions.

Result: Evaluated in three scenarios—Full Health (FHS), Heterogeneous Degradation (HDS), and Local Degradation (LDS)—the method suppresses degradation imbalance, delays bottleneck failures, and improves long-term reliability.

Conclusion: Integrating health feedback into motion planning offers a practical, robust pathway to enhance manipulator durability and operational resilience under continuous wear.

Abstract: Robotic manipulators operating over long durations often experience uneven joint degradation, which causes the weakest actuator to fail prematurely, leads to unplanned downtime, and results in significant operational losses. Traditional motion-planning algorithms do not account for joint health conditions and therefore tend to exacerbate this imbalance during extended operation. To address this challenge, this study introduces the RUL-aware RRT*, a motion-planning method that incorporates joint remaining useful life information into the planning process and adaptively adjusts joint usage in response to evolving health conditions. The method is evaluated across three representative scenarios, namely the Full Health Scenario (FHS), the Heterogeneous Degradation Scenario (HDS), and the Local Degradation Scenario (LDS). The results show that the RUL-aware RRT* effectively suppresses degradation imbalance, delays the emergence of bottleneck failures, and improves the long-term reliability of the robotic system during extended operation. These findings demonstrate that integrating health feedback into motion planning provides a practical and robust pathway for enhancing the durability and operational resilience of manipulators subject to continuous wear.

</details>


### [136] [CriticHack: Evaluating Visual Rewards Under Robot Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02527)
*Jiaxuan Luo,Xingguo Xu,Shanshan Wang,Yuhan Zhou,Zhen Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Optimizing learned visual reward models (e.g., Robometer) can amplify wrong-object failures even while task success and standard reward metrics both rise, because the reward model scores wrong-object executions slightly above successes. A frozen outcome verifier mitigates this.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners typically monitor reward and task success, both of which can look healthy while the policy increasingly acts on the wrong object. The paper aims to expose and characterize this failure mode of learned reward optimization.

Method: Fine-tune all denoiser parameters of a diffusion policy against Robometer on a drawer task, comparing against a simulator task-completion signal across five runs and 512 seeds. Test recurrence under a native diffusion sampler, matched distance to the initial policy, two critics, two optimizers, and 26 constrained settings. A tilt model under KL-regularized optimization explains outcome-frequency shifts.

Result: Reward optimization raises task success by 10.2 points and wrong-object failures by 10.9 points, whereas the task-completion signal raises success without amplification (difference 9.2 points, 95% CI 5.6 to 13.0). Robometer attains AUROC .81 overall but .37 on wrong-object failures versus successes. The tilt model predicts outcome shifts across 26 settings (Spearman .89), including cases where task success falls. Robometer's own success-termination recipe inherits the error.

Conclusion: Learned reward optimization can covertly amplify wrong-object failures, and the phenomenon is predictable via the tilt model. A frozen outcome verifier redirects the same optimization toward the requested task, offering a corrective.

Abstract: Learned visual reward models are increasingly used to optimize robot policies, yet a reward model can score an execution that acts on the wrong object as highly as one that completes the task. We show that optimizing such a reward can amplify these wrong-object failures while reward and task success both rise, so the signals a practitioner would normally monitor look healthy. We fine-tune every denoiser parameter of a diffusion policy against Robometer on a drawer task. Starting from a supervised policy with no prior reward exposure, five training runs raise task success by 10.2 percentage points and wrong-object failures by 10.9 points on 512 evaluation seeds, whereas five runs trained on the simulator's task-completion signal raise success without amplifying wrong-object failures (difference 9.2 points, 95% CI 5.6 to 13.0). The amplification recurs from a policy previously optimized against learned rewards, under the policy's native diffusion sampler, at matched distance from the initial policy, and across constrained-policy experiments with two critics and two optimizers. A tilt model explains when it occurs: under KL-regularized optimization, an outcome becomes more frequent whenever its expected reward under the initial policy exceeds the population average. Robometer separates successes from failures well overall (AUROC .81) but scores wrong-object failures slightly above successes (AUROC .37), so optimization raises both. The same model predicts the outcome shifts across 26 constrained settings (Spearman .89), including those in which task success falls, and Robometer's own published success-termination recipe inherits the error. A frozen outcome verifier redirects the same optimization toward the requested task.

</details>


### [137] [Real-time Event-camera Stereo Visual Odometry via Keytime Gaussian Process Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02601)
*Nikan Nobari,Jonathan D. Gammell*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a real-time continuous-time event-camera stereo visual odometry (VO) pipeline that preserves the native timestamps of asynchronous events. By reducing the estimation state to keytimes and interpolating measurements to their exact timestamps via a physically grounded white-noise-on-acceleration (WNOA) prior, it decouples state size from the dense measurement count without discarding asynchronicity. Evaluated on MVSEC and DSEC, it outperforms the discrete state-of-the-art ES-PTAM in nearly all sequences while running in real time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Event cameras offer microsecond-level temporal resolution and high dynamic range, making them more robust to motion blur and poor illumination than frame-based cameras. However, these benefits are only fully realized when the asynchronous event stream is processed at its native temporal resolution. Continuous-time Gaussian process (GP) regression with a WNOA prior can handle asynchronous measurements, but applying it naively yields a prohibitively large estimation state.

Method: A continuous-time event-camera stereo VO pipeline that maintains native measurement times of asynchronous events while running in real time. It reduces estimation states to keytimes and preserves full temporal resolution by interpolating measurements to their exact timestamps using a physically founded WNOA prior. This decouples state size from the dense number of measurements without discarding their asynchronous nature.

Result: Evaluated on the MVSEC and DSEC datasets. The pipeline provides real-time estimates that are more accurate than ES-PTAM (a state-of-the-art discrete estimator) in all but one tested sequence. It achieves 22 Hz and 6 Hz on MVSEC and DSEC, respectively, and RMS relative errors of 0.46 cm and 0.038 degrees across all valid sequences—11 and 15 times better than ES-PTAM, respectively.

Conclusion: A keytime-based, continuous-time formulation with a WNOA prior enables real-time event-camera stereo VO that retains the native temporal resolution of asynchronous events. It decouples state size from measurement density and delivers superior accuracy over a discrete state-of-the-art baseline, demonstrating the viability of continuous-time estimation for asynchronous event-based odometry.

Abstract: Event cameras have microsecond-level temporal resolution and high dynamic range which make them more resilient to motion blur and poor illumination than standard frame-based cameras. Event-camera visual odometry (VO) pipelines maximize these benefits when they process the asynchronous event stream at the native temporal resolution. Continuous-time Gaussian process (GP) regression and a white-noise-on-acceleration (WNOA) prior can handle asynchronous measurements but result in a prohibitively large estimation state when applied naively.
  This paper presents a continuous-time event-camera stereo VO pipeline that maintains the native measurement times of asynchronous events while also running in real time. It reduces the estimation states to keytimes while maintaining full temporal resolution by interpolating measurements to their exact timestamps with a physically founded WNOA prior. This decouples the state size from the dense number of measurements without discarding their asynchronous nature.
  The real-time continuous-time VO pipeline is evaluated on the MVSEC and DSEC datasets. It provides estimates in real time that are more accurate than ES-PTAM, a state-of-the-art discrete estimator, in all but one of the tested sequences. The pipeline respectively provides estimates at 22 Hz and 6 Hz on MVSEC and DSEC and RMS relative errors of 0.46 cm and 0.038 degrees across all valid sequences, which were 11 and 15 times better than ES-PTAM, respectively.

</details>


### [138] [A Passive AI System for Verifying Physical State on Automated Liquid Handlers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02668)
*Junqiong Joanne Qiu,Zeckria Kamrany,Ananya Anand,Emma Vidal,Yunjia Johanna Bai,Nathan S Chen,Sean Son,Varada Abhyankar,Taryn Jakub,Eleazar Eskin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A passive, external-camera vision system verifies Opentrons Flex deck setup against protocol references, achieving 100% slot assignment accuracy and ~95-98% labware classification accuracy in seconds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated liquid handlers run digital protocols, but operators must still manually assemble the deck and confirm the physical setup matches the intended experiment. An independent, non-invasive verification method is needed that does not modify robot hardware or firmware and does not interrupt runs.

Method: The Labware Setup Checker uses a consumer webcam mounted outside the robot feeding images to a browser application. It segments the deck into slots, corrects slot assignments using known deck geometry, classifies labware, and compares observed setup with a reference protocol library to flag missing, misplaced, or unexpected labware. It runs independently of the robot controller.

Result: During SwabSeq RVP pre-PCR deck preparation, the checker identified the completed plate-and-base assembly in all 29 frames before robot motion. 95.1% of 348 labware classifications for exact-match slots agreed with the protocol reference. In 480 frames with partially occluded reservoirs, all 6,720 slot assignments were correct. Across three camera positions and two lighting conditions, slot assignment was 100% correct and 97.73% of labware classifications matched the reference. Median verification time was 2.10 s on a laptop CPU and 4.24 s end-to-end during AWS deployment.

Conclusion: An external camera and independent software can verify deck setup against protocol requirements within seconds, enabling operators to review the physical setup before starting a run without modifying or gating the robot.

Abstract: Automated liquid handlers execute digital protocols, but operators must assemble the deck and confirm that the physical setup matches the intended experiment. We present the Labware Setup Checker, a passive vision system that verifies deck preparation on an Opentrons Flex without modifying its hardware or firmware. A consumer webcam mounted outside the robot supplies images to a browser application. The checker segments the deck into slots, corrects slot assignments using the known deck geometry, classifies the labware, and compares the observed setup with a reference protocol library to report missing, misplaced, or unexpected labware. It operates independently of the robot controller and does not interrupt or gate a run. During SwabSeq Respiratory Viral Panel (RVP) pre-PCR deck preparation, the checker tracked a slot from empty to a base and then to the completed plate-and-base assembly, identifying the completed assembly in all 29 frames before robot motion. Across these frames, 95.1% of the 348 labware classifications for slots requiring exact matches agreed with the protocol reference. In 480 frames with partially occluded reservoirs, all 6,720 slot assignments were correct. Across three camera positions and two lighting conditions, slot assignment was 100% correct, and 97.73% of labware classifications matched the protocol reference. Median verification time was 2.10 s on a laptop using only its CPU and 4.24 s from frame capture to receipt of results during AWS deployment in the laboratory. These results show that an external camera and independent software can provide verification of deck setup against protocol requirements within seconds, allowing operators to review the physical setup before starting a run.

</details>


### [139] [DeltaWorld: Physically Consistent Interactive World Simulators via Action-Conditioned Latent Increment Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02691)
*Boyuan Hou,Xiaoge Cao,Chaofan Zhang,Shuo Wang,Shaowei Cui*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DeltaWorld is a physically consistent interactive world simulator for robotic manipulation that predicts action-induced latent changes (deltas) instead of full next states, combined with interaction-aware supervision to reduce implausible artifacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interactive world simulators can enable scalable robot planning, policy training, and evaluation by predicting action consequences without repeated physical rollouts. However, existing world models typically predict the entire next latent state and fail to capture subtle action-induced changes, leading to physically implausible outcomes such as object interpenetration and excessive deformation.

Method: The authors propose DeltaWorld, comprising the Delta Latent Transition Model (Delta-LTM), which predicts action-induced latent changes and adds them to the current latent state to obtain the next state rather than predicting the next state directly. Additionally, an Interaction-aware Latent Alignment mechanism constructs counterfactual interaction regions and supervises interaction-related latent changes to mitigate interpenetration and deformation.

Result: Evaluated on the IWS manipulation benchmark and a self-collected cross-robot dataset spanning multiple embodiments and tasks, DeltaWorld reduces FVD by 46.6% and LPIPS by 31.1% relative to the IWS baseline on the cross-robot dataset.

Conclusion: DeltaWorld demonstrates strong potential for long-horizon, action-conditioned video prediction in robotic manipulation by improving physical consistency and predictive fidelity.

Abstract: Interactive world simulators can provide scalable environments for robot planning, policy training, and evaluation by predicting action consequences while reducing reliance on repeated physical rollouts. To serve these applications, they must generate future image sequences that respond faithfully to robot actions and preserve the dynamics of robot-object interactions over long horizons. However, existing world models typically predict the entire next latent state and often fail to capture subtle changes induced by robot actions. Such omissions can produce physically implausible outcomes, including object interpenetration and excessive deformation. To address this limitation, we propose DeltaWorld, a physically consistent interactive world simulator for robotic manipulation. Our method introduces the Delta Latent Transition Model (Delta-LTM), which predicts action-induced latent feature changes and adds them to the current latent state to obtain the next state, rather than predicting the next latent state directly. To mitigate object interpenetration and excessive deformation in predicted future frames, Interaction-aware Latent Alignment is introduced to construct counterfactual interaction regions and supervise interaction-related latent changes. DeltaWorld is evaluated on the IWS manipulation benchmark and a self-collected cross-robot dataset covering multiple robot embodiments and manipulation tasks. On the cross-robot dataset, DeltaWorld reduces FVD by 46.6% and LPIPS by 31.1% relative to the IWS baseline. These results highlight the potential of DeltaWorld for long-horizon action-conditioned video prediction in robotic manipulation.

</details>


### [140] [Who Went Where When on the Lunar Surface: Forensic Trajectory Analysis to Identify Byzantine Rovers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02694)
*Lachlan Holden,Feras Dayoub,Melissa de Zwart,David Harvey,Tat-Jun Chin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces forensic trajectory analysis for non-cooperative planetary rovers with Byzantine agents, and proposes an attribution-aware estimation method that reasons over rover credibility rather than individual measurement validity to identify falsifiers and improve trajectory accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Future planetary surface missions may involve multiple independently operated rovers sharing the same deployment region, requiring verification of compliance with operational constraints such as Lunar Safety Zones. Since continuous in-situ observability is rarely available, verification needs post-hoc trajectory reconstruction from sparse telemetry (odometry, pose priors, relative inter-rover detections). A key challenge is Byzantine rovers that supply miscalibrated or deliberately falsified measurements to support an incorrect trajectory. Standard outlier-robust pose graph optimisation is vulnerable because Byzantine rovers can produce internally consistent measurements numerous enough to make truthful incriminating measurements appear as outliers.

Method: The authors formulate the problem of forensic trajectory analysis for non-cooperative planetary rovers in the presence of Byzantine agents. They propose an attribution-aware trajectory estimation method that reasons over rover credibility rather than individual measurement validity. The method evaluates candidate credible rover subsets by comparing the statistical consistency of their internal and boundary relative detections against the provided priors, then estimates trajectories using only measurements attributed to credible agents.

Result: Across synthetic simulations and real planetary-analogue trajectory data, the proposed method identifies Byzantine rovers and produces significantly more accurate trajectory estimates than existing robust pose graph optimisation baselines.

Conclusion: Reasoning over rover credibility, rather than individual measurement validity, enables reliable identification of Byzantine rovers and more accurate post-hoc trajectory reconstruction, outperforming standard robust pose graph optimisation baselines.

Abstract: Future planetary surface missions are likely to involve multiple independently operated rovers sharing the same deployment region, raising the need to verify compliance with operational constraints such as Lunar Safety Zones. Because continuous in-situ observability is rarely available, such verification requires post-hoc reconstruction of rover trajectories from sparse telemetry, including odometry, pose priors, and relative inter-rover detections. We introduce the problem of forensic trajectory analysis for non-cooperative planetary rovers in the presence of Byzantine agents: rovers that provide miscalibrated or deliberately falsified measurements to support an incorrect trajectory. We show that standard outlier-robust pose graph optimisation methods are vulnerable in this setting, because Byzantine rovers can generate measurements that are internally consistent and numerous enough to make truthful incriminating measurements appear as outliers. To address this, we propose an attribution-aware trajectory estimation method that reasons over rover credibility rather than individual measurement validity. The method evaluates candidate credible rover subsets by comparing the statistical consistency of their internal and boundary relative detections against provided priors, and then estimates trajectories using only measurements attributed to credible agents. Across synthetic simulations and real planetary-analogue trajectory data, the proposed method identifies Byzantine rovers and produces significantly more accurate trajectory estimates than existing robust pose graph optimisation baselines.

</details>


### [141] [GeoScaffold: Learning Compact Geometric Latents via Reconstruction for Efficient Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02697)
*Yixuan Jiang,Wentong Li,An Liu,Zihao Xin,Fulin Tang,Cong Leng,Yang Gao,Jian Cheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GeoScaffold is a training-time geometric supervision framework that internalizes 3D geometry into streaming Video-LLM navigation policies without adding any inference-time cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streaming Video-LLM policies for VLN inherit weak 3D geometric priors from 2D pretraining, while existing geometry-aware extensions incur persistent inference-time costs (depth sensors, 3D encoders, or per-step perception tool calls).

Method: GeoScaffold learns a compact depth tokenizer on training-trajectory depth maps and freezes it. It then fine-tunes the policy with learnable geometry query tokens whose hidden states are trained to reconstruct navigation-critical geometry (depth, connectivity, traversability) via target generators and reconstruction heads. This yields compact geometric latents for action decoding and internalizes geometry into the backbone through shared weights. The tokenizer, target generators, and reconstruction heads are discarded after training.

Result: GeoScaffold consistently outperforms leading vision-only navigators on continuous VLN benchmarks.

Conclusion: GeoScaffold offers a practical paradigm for lightweight edge deployment of spatially aware embodied navigation models by paying the geometry cost once at training time while leaving the backbone and action interface unchanged.

Abstract: Recent vision-and-language navigation (VLN) systems increasingly adopt streaming Video-LLM policies that map egocentric RGB observations and instructions directly to low-level actions. Yet these policies inherit weak 3D geometric priors from 2D pretraining. Existing geometry-aware extensions charge a persistent inference-time price: depth sensors, 3D encoders, or per-step perception tool calls. We propose GeoScaffold, a geometric supervision framework that pays this price once, at training time, by internalizing geometry into the policy itself. It first learns a compact depth tokenizer on depth maps from the training trajectories and freezes it. It then fine-tunes the policy with a handful of learnable geometry query tokens, training their hidden states to reconstruct navigation-critical geometry such as depth, connectivity, and traversability. This supervision turns the query states into compact geometric latents for action decoding, and through the shared weights also internalizes geometry into the backbone's own representations. Like a scaffold, the tokenizer, target generators, and reconstruction heads are discarded after training, leaving the backbone and action interface unchanged. Extensive experiments show that GeoScaffold consistently outperforms leading vision-only navigators on continuous VLN benchmarks, offering a practical paradigm for lightweight edge deployment of spatially aware embodied navigation models.

</details>


### [142] [AdaTempo: Learning Shared Relative Tempo from Demonstrations for Faster Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02706)
*Jiale Cao,Yike Niu,Zhengrong Xue,Huazhe Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AdaTempo is a self-supervised method that accelerates visuomotor policies by extracting shared relative-tempo structure from demonstrations and resampling trajectories for accelerated training, achieving up to 3.57x speedup with a better success-speed trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visuomotor policies trained via imitation learning inherit the unnecessarily slow timing of teleoperated demonstrations, but uniform speedup is unreliable because different manipulation phases tolerate acceleration differently.

Method: AdaTempo establishes phase correspondence across demonstrations, aggregates aligned relative tempo into a consensus, and maps it to a continuous speedup profile. This profile resamples demonstrations into accelerated training trajectories, so standard policies (ACT, Diffusion Policy) learn the desired tempo directly without runtime tempo selection or online retiming.

Result: Evaluations show up to a 3.57x speedup and a stronger success-speed trade-off than original policies and representative acceleration baselines.

Conclusion: AdaTempo is a self-supervised approach that embeds speedup into learned visuomotor behavior via tempo-aware trajectory resampling, eliminating the need for runtime tempo selection while improving the success-speed trade-off.

Abstract: Visuomotor policies trained via imitation learning often inherit the unnecessarily slow timing of teleoperated demonstrations. Yet uniform speedup is unreliable because different phases of a manipulation task tolerate acceleration differently. In this work, we introduce AdaTempo, a self-supervised method that accelerates visuomotor policies by exploiting shared relative-tempo structure in demonstrations. AdaTempo establishes phase correspondence, aggregates the aligned relative tempo into a consensus, and maps it to a continuous speedup profile used to resample demonstrations into accelerated training trajectories. Training standard policies such as ACT or Diffusion Policy on these resampled trajectories directly embeds the desired tempo in the learned behavior, without runtime tempo selection or online retiming. Extensive evaluations show that AdaTempo achieves up to a $3.57\times$ speedup and yields a stronger success--speed trade-off than the original policies and representative acceleration baselines.

</details>


### [143] [RoboChemGym: A Protocol-Driven Generative Simulation Framework for Long-Horizon Chemical Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02708)
*Chenxi Li,Haiyuan Wan,Rui Li,Jingyuan Li,Sha Zhang,Bohan Feng,Jianbao Cao,Zhangrui Zhao,Di Hu,Wangmeng Zuo,Shixiang Tang,Minting Pan,Dongzhan Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces RoboChemGym, a framework that autonomously generates high-fidelity manipulation demonstrations for chemical experiments via self-improving task synthesis, enabling expert trajectories for multi-step protocols, plus a hierarchical benchmark spanning atomic operations to full-cycle workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wet-lab experimentation is the gold standard for hypothesis verification but is labor-intensive, costly, and safety-critical. Embodied agents could automate these workflows, yet their development is hampered by scarce real-world training data. Existing simulation-based demonstration methods target short-horizon, loosely structured tasks and fail to satisfy the strict procedural constraints and fine-grained manipulation required in chemical experiments.

Method: The authors propose RoboChemGym, a framework that autonomously generates high-fidelity manipulation demonstrations aligned with real-world experiment protocols. It features a self-improving task synthesis mechanism that iteratively refines task execution and scene configurations to reliably produce expert trajectories for complex, multi-object protocols exceeding 10 interaction steps. They also introduce a hierarchical benchmark evaluating performance from atomic operations to full-cycle experimental workflows.

Result: RoboChemGym enables reliable generation of expert trajectories for complex multi-object protocols exceeding 10 interaction steps, and its hierarchical benchmark systematically assesses capability across granularities from atomic operations to full experimental cycles.

Conclusion: RoboChemGym establishes a scalable paradigm for automated data synthesis and capability evaluation of embodied agents in intricate chemical tasks, serving as a critical stepping stone toward fully intelligent laboratories.

Abstract: Wet-lab experimentation serves as the gold standard for hypothesis verification in scientific discovery; yet it is inherently labor-intensive, costly, and safety-critical. Embodied agents hold the promise of automating these tedious workflows, but their development is hindered by the scarcity of real-world training data. While simulation offers a scalable alternative for producing demonstrations, current methods primarily target relatively short-horizon tasks with loosely structured interactions, failing to meet the strict procedural constraints and fine-grained manipulation demands of chemical experiments. To bridge this gap, we introduce \textbf{RoboChemGym}, a framework that autonomously generates high-fidelity manipulation demonstrations aligned with real-world experiment protocols, featuring a \textit{self-improving task synthesis} mechanism to iteratively refine task execution and scene configurations, enabling the reliable generation of expert trajectories for complex, multi-object protocols exceeding 10 interaction steps. Furthermore, we introduce a hierarchical benchmark that systematically assesses performance across varying granularities, spanning from atomic operations to full-cycle experimental workflows. RoboChemGym sets a scalable paradigm for the automated data synthesis and capability evaluation of embodied agents in intricate chemical tasks, serving as a critical stepping stone toward fully intelligent laboratories.

</details>


### [144] [RoboBridge: A Self-Evolving Embodied Agent Framework for Sim-to-Real Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02717)
*Chenxi Li,Zhangrui Zhao,Rui Li,Yuan Gao,Kehui Liu,Jiarui Li,Dong Wang,Tong Si,Minting Pan,Wanli Ouyang,Dongzhan Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoboBridge frames sim-to-real transfer as continued adaptation of executable task skills. It represents task knowledge as procedures (intent, observations, tool operations, verification), revises candidate skills using real-world feedback, and wraps a pretrained VLA policy as a reusable action tool with inference-time guidance—avoiding policy retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end vision-language-action policies transfer poorly to physical environments, typically requiring calibrated visual/dynamical conditions, extra target-domain demonstrations, and retraining. Existing tool-using embodied agents emphasize in-environment execution and experience reuse, offering limited support for transferring procedural knowledge and continually adapting it across simulation and reality.

Method: RoboBridge treats sim-to-real transfer as continuous adaptation of executable task skills. Task knowledge is represented as procedures linking task intent, observations, tool operations, and outcome verification. Interaction feedback generates candidate skill revisions that are evaluated before being persisted or rejected. A pretrained VLA policy is exposed as a reusable action tool and augmented with inference-time guidance for fine-grained execution without retraining. Transferable skills are grounded in task semantics and interaction interfaces shared across sim and reality, preserving reusable structure while allowing environment-dependent operations to be selectively revised.

Result: Evaluated on LIBERO-PRO and corresponding physical tasks, examining both skill evolution and post-transfer adaptation.

Conclusion: RoboBridge offers a route from one-shot policy deployment to continual procedural learning across environments, enabling transfer and adaptation without retraining the underlying policy.

Abstract: A key challenge in bringing embodied intelligence into the real world is transferring capabilities from simulation to reality and enabling agents to continually adapt after deployment. End-to-end vision-language-action policies provide strong manipulation capabilities, but their transfer to physical environments typically relies on calibrating simulated visual and dynamical conditions, collecting additional target-domain demonstrations, and optimizing the policy through further training. Tool-using embodied agents offer flexible task orchestration, yet existing systems primarily emphasize task execution and experience reuse within a given environment, with limited support for transferring procedural knowledge and continuously adapting it across simulation and reality. We propose RoboBridge, a framework that treats sim-to-real transfer as the continued adaptation of executable task skills. The agent represents task knowledge as procedures connecting task intent, observations, tool operations, and outcome verification. Interaction feedback is used to generate candidate skill revisions, which are evaluated before being persisted or rejected. A pretrained vision-language-action policy is exposed as a reusable action tool and enhanced with inference-time guidance, enabling fine-grained execution without retraining the underlying policy. RoboBridge grounds transferable skills in task semantics and interaction interfaces shared across simulation and reality. This representation preserves reusable task structure while allowing environment-dependent operations to be selectively revised through real-world execution feedback. We evaluate the framework on LIBERO-PRO and corresponding physical tasks, studying both skill evolution and post-transfer adaptation. Our framework provides a route from one-shot policy deployment to continual procedural learning across environments.

</details>


### [145] [Around the World: Unified Learned Locomotion on a 270 g Continuous-Rotation Quadruped](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02728)
*Arturo Flores Alvarez,Nathan Lintu,Dennis Hong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MiNI-Q, a 270 g quadruped with continuous-rotation legs, is controlled by a single posture-conditioned RL policy running onboard. A torus T^8 joint-space reference and gravity-conditioned transformation unify upright walking, inverted walking, and landing recovery without state machines, trained via coordinated posture and release curricula.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Closed-loop learned locomotion is well established on commercial quadrupeds but remains uncommon at the sub-kilogram scale. The platform's continuous-rotation legs provide supporting configurations on either side of the body, a motion range that conventional controllers and state-machine-based policies do not exploit.

Method: A single posture-conditioned reinforcement-learning policy executes fully onboard a 270 g quadruped. Locomotion is represented by a continuous joint-space reference on the torus T^8 with a gravity-conditioned transformation, enabling upright walking, inverted walking, and landing recovery without state machines or phase switching. Training uses coordinated posture and release curricula; deployment is supported by identified actuation, cross-engine validation, and embedded execution. The network has fewer than 100k parameters.

Result: The same sub-100k-parameter network tracks forward velocity with RMSE 0.037 m/s upright and 0.050 m/s inverted, resumes walking in 24 of 30 release trials, and operates with four interchangeable foot geometries across four indoor surfaces. Hardware experiments and simulation ablations link these capabilities to the representation, conditioning, and training choices that exploit the platform's motion range.

Conclusion: A single compact posture-conditioned policy can exploit continuous-rotation legs to unify upright and inverted locomotion plus recovery on a sub-kilogram quadruped, with hardware transfer supported by identified actuation, cross-engine validation, and embedded execution. Videos and supplementary material are available at the project website: https://submissionreview.github.io/around-the-world/.

Abstract: Closed-loop learned locomotion is established on commercial quadrupeds but remains uncommon at the sub-kilogram scale. Continuous-rotation legs give MiNI-Q, a 270 g quadruped, access to supporting configurations on either side of the body. We exploit this range with a single posture-conditioned reinforcement-learning policy that runs entirely onboard. A continuous joint-space reference on the torus $T^8$ and its gravity-conditioned transformation connect upright walking, inverted walking, and landing recovery without state machines or phase switching. Coordinated posture and release curricula train this behavior family; identified actuation, cross-engine validation, and embedded execution support hardware transfer. The same sub-100k-parameter network tracks forward velocity with RMSE of 0.037 m/s upright and 0.050 m/s inverted, resumes walking in 24 of 30 release trials, and operates with four interchangeable foot geometries across four indoor surfaces. Hardware experiments and simulation ablations connect these capabilities to the representation, conditioning, and training choices that exploit the platform's motion range. Demonstration videos and supplementary material are available on the project website: https://submissionreview.github.io/around-the-world/.

</details>


### [146] [CSIR: Contextually and Socially Informed Robots for Efficient Person Goal Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02750)
*Tyler Chung,Nahl Farhan,Hao Zhang,Mingfeng Yuan,Jinjun Shan,Amy Wu,Yue Hu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces Person Goal Navigation (PersonNav), a planning framework that lets robots search for people using distance and semantic information about the recipient weighted by the trust of user-provided information, validated via a synthetic benchmark and hardware tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current person-finding solutions require exact knowledge of the individual and rely on rigid systems for item-delivery in indoor settings. Learned methods depend on public or sparse data that is often unavailable due to human privacy. There is a need for robots to search for people under realistic constraints on information accessibility and language uncertainty.

Method: The authors propose a planning framework combining distance and semantic information about the recipient (habits and intent), weighted by the trust of user-provided information. They develop a synthetic benchmark with actors, items, and requests to simulate natural language human-robot interactions, and test an LLM-based variant alongside an explicit belief representation.

Result: The informed search outperforms classical distance-based graph baselines, while semantics alone lead to ungrounded, sporadic search. The method achieves strong gains over baselines, with an LLM-based variant performing comparably. Hardware tests show the method supports real-world embodiment, leveraging between semantic and distance information in real settings.

Conclusion: The work advances intelligent mobile service agents capable of human-like, informed search in realistic environments for day-to-day use. The explicit belief representation naturally supports future Bayesian filtering.

Abstract: We address the Person Goal Navigation (PersonNav) problem, enabling robots to search for people under realistic constraints on information accessibility and language uncertainty. This tackles the current solutions revolving around rigid person finding systems requiring exact knowledge of the individual for item-delivery in indoor settings. While the focus in literature is on learned methods using unavailable public or sparse data due to human privacy. We propose a planning framework that combines distance and semantic information about the recipient (habits and intent) weighted by the trust of the user-provided information. A synthetic benchmark of scenarios is also developed including actors, items, and requests to evaluate performance before real-world deployment aiming to simulate natural language human-robot interactions. Results show our informed search outperforms classical distance-based graph baselines, while semantics alone lead to ungrounded, sporadic search. Our method achieves strong gains over baselines, with an LLM-based variant performing comparably, and its explicit belief representation naturally supports future Bayesian filtering. Hardware tests demonstrate our method supports real-world embodiment able to leverage between semantic and distance information in a real setting. This work moves toward more intelligent mobile service agents capable of human-like, informed search in realistic environments to be leveraged in day-to-day use. https://anonymous.4open.science/r/personnavsite-4ED2/index.html.

</details>


### [147] [Proprioceptive Sketches as Long-Horizon Intent for Generative Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02759)
*Fangyuan Wang,Songhao Huang,Haoxiang Sun,Shipeng Lyu,Chengyang He,Anqing Duan,Peng Zhou,David Navarro-Alarcon*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PAM jointly generates a timing-free, arc-length-parameterized sketch of the remaining joint-space path and a dense executable action chunk within a single transformer denoiser, improving over action-only baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative robot policies predict short action chunks but lack explicit long-horizon intent. Existing methods expose longer-horizon structure via language plans, subgoal images, or video forecasts, but these are costly to generate and still require translation into robot motion. Predicting future robot motions avoids translation, yet dense time-indexed trajectories need many parameters and largely repeat the action chunk over short horizons, adding little guidance.

Method: PAM uses a single transformer denoiser to jointly generate a compact, timing-free sketch of the remaining joint-space path (parameterized by arc length rather than time, capturing geometric intent invariant to execution timing) and a dense executable action chunk. Block-causal attention and a staggered denoising schedule maintain directed sketch-to-action dependence, so action tokens condition on a progressively cleaner sketch throughout sampling.

Result: In simulation, PAM improves over action-only counterparts on Push-T and LIBERO-Long. On four real-world bimanual tasks, it raises success from 47.5% to 75.0%.

Conclusion: PAM provides explicit long-horizon intent via a compact timing-free path sketch co-generated with dense actions, avoiding costly translation while effectively guiding action generation, demonstrated across simulation and real-world bimanual tasks.

Abstract: Generative robot policies predict short action chunks but lack explicit long-horizon intent. Recent methods expose longer-horizon structure through language plans, subgoal images, or video forecasts, which are costly to generate and still need to be translated into robot motion. Predicting future robot motions avoids this translation, but a dense, time-indexed trajectory requires numerous parameters to cover the full remaining task, and over a short horizon it largely repeats the action chunk and adds little guidance for action generation. We propose Proprioceptive Action Models (PAM), which jointly generate a compact, timing-free sketch of the robot's remaining joint-space path and a dense executable action chunk within a single transformer denoiser. The sketch parameterizes the path by arc length rather than time, capturing geometric intent invariant to execution timing. Block-causal attention and a staggered denoising schedule maintain directed sketch-to-action dependence, ensuring the action tokens condition on a progressively cleaner sketch throughout sampling. In simulation, PAM improves over its action-only counterparts on Push-T and LIBERO-Long; on four real-world bimanual tasks, it raises success from 47.5% to 75.0%. Project page: https://nicehiro.github.io/pam_dp/

</details>


### [148] [Localized Conformal Safety Monitoring with Vision-Language Models for Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02765)
*Luís Marques,Rong Fang,Disha Kamale,Dmitry Berenson*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SLLCP is a post-hoc conformal prediction calibration layer over frozen VLMs that converts unreliable VLM predictions into probabilistically calibrated safety prediction sets, improving collision-flagging of driving trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monitoring planned driving trajectories requires estimating collision likelihood with actors whose motion depends on ego motion. Classical approaches are limited by forecasting model quality, and while VLMs can reason about high-level action consequences, their approximate predictions are unsuitable for safety-critical autonomous driving. There is a need to quantify uncertainty of black-box VLM predictions.

Method: The authors propose Split Label-Localized Conformal Prediction (SLLCP), a post-hoc calibration layer over frozen VLMs that transforms unreliable predictions into probabilistically calibrated safety prediction sets. It introduces a localized procedure that upweights relevant past experience when computing uncertainty thresholds, conditioned on the observed driving scene. The method provides label-conditional finite-sample distribution-free coverage under exchangeability.

Result: Evaluated over 15k CARLA trajectories from unseen scenarios, SLLCP correctly flags 89.6% of collision-causing trajectories with a Qwen backbone and 88.4% with a Cosmos backbone, whereas base VLMs only flagged 4.6% and 39.1% respectively.

Conclusion: Local, label-conditional calibration can substantially reduce missed unsafe trajectories, indicating that SLLCP effectively makes frozen VLM predictions usable for safety-critical collision prediction.

Abstract: Monitoring planned driving trajectories requires accurately estimating the collision likelihood with actors whose motion is itself impacted by the ego motion. Existing classical approaches are often limited by the quality of their forecasting model. Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown promise in reasoning about the consequences of high-level actions, yet their approximate predictions are unsuitable for safety-critical applications such as autonomous driving. Conformal prediction (CP) has emerged as a data-driven framework for quantifying the uncertainty of black-box model predictions. We propose Split Label-Localized Conformal Prediction (SLLCP), a post-hoc calibration layer over frozen VLMs that transforms their unreliable predictions into probabilistically calibrated safety prediction sets. We consider how the ability to estimate safety can depend on the observed driving scene and introduce a localized procedure that upweights relevant past experience when calculating uncertainty thresholds. We provide label-conditional finite-sample distribution-free coverage under exchangeability. Evaluated over 15k CARLA trajectories from unseen scenarios, SLLCP correctly flags 89.6% of collision-causing trajectories with a Qwen backbone and 88.4% with a Cosmos backbone, while the base VLMs only flagged 4.6% and 39.1% of the collision-causing trajectories, respectively. These results indicate that local, label-conditional calibration can reduce missed unsafe trajectories.

</details>


### [149] [SimpleTouch: Can Vision-Language-Action Models Master Contact-Rich Manipulation Without Tactile Policy Pretraining?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02784)
*Chen Yang,Linzhe Shi,Changjie Wu,Hang Zhang,Ronghan Chen,Lingjun Zhang,Xu Hu,Mu Xu,Jiansheng Fan,Chen Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SimpleTouch is a single-stage extension of π₀.₅ that adds a tactile expert and achieves strong contact-rich manipulation performance without tactile policy pretraining or separate visuotactile alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrating tactile sensing into pretrained VLA models is challenging; naive touch introduction during fine-tuning may fail to bridge the cross-modal gap, so existing methods rely on large-scale tactile policy pretraining or separate visuotactile alignment, adding data and training stages. The paper questions whether these extra stages are necessary.

Method: SimpleTouch augments π₀.₅ with a tactile expert that uses all tokens from a frozen pretrained tactile encoder. The expert is trained in a single stage via action supervision and multi-horizon prediction of future tactile latents, using only task demonstrations—no tactile policy pretraining or separate alignment.

Result: With 50 demonstrations per task, SimpleTouch achieves the highest success rate on all six UniVTAC tasks, averaging 77.5% vs. 45.2% for FTP-π₀.₅ and 66.7% for FTP-1 (+32.3 and +10.8 points). Across four real-world tasks it averages 71.3%, exceeding FTP-1 by 8.8 points.

Conclusion: Given pretrained VLA and tactile representations, additional tactile policy pretraining is not a prerequisite for strong performance on these tasks, offering a simpler route to contact-rich manipulation.

Abstract: Tactile sensing provides essential contact information for robotic manipulation, yet incorporating it into pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) models remains challenging. A common concern is that simply introducing touch during task-specific fine-tuning may fail to bridge the cross-modal gap, yielding limited gains or even reduced success. Consequently, existing methods often rely on large-scale tactile policy pretraining or separate visuotactile alignment, adding data requirements and training stages. We introduce SimpleTouch, a simple VLA extension that augments $π_{0.5}$ with a tactile expert, to test whether these additional stages are necessary. Leveraging all tokens from a frozen pretrained tactile encoder, the expert learns from action supervision and multi-horizon prediction of future tactile latents. This single-stage training uses only task demonstrations, without additional tactile policy pretraining or separate alignment. With 50 demonstrations per task, SimpleTouch achieves the highest success rate among evaluated methods on all six UniVTAC tasks. Its average success rate reaches 77.5%, compared with 45.2% for FTP-$π_{0.5}$ and 66.7% for FTP-1, corresponding to gains of 32.3 and 10.8 percentage points, respectively. Across four real-world tasks, it averages 71.3%, exceeding FTP-1 by 8.8 percentage points. These results demonstrate that, given pretrained VLA and tactile representations, additional tactile policy pretraining is not a prerequisite for strong performance on these tasks, offering a simpler route to contact-rich manipulation. Project page: https://simpletouch-robot.github.io/

</details>


### [150] [Skill2Real: Agentic Skill Learning for Zero-Shot Sim-to-Real Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02788)
*Xincheng He,Siyu Ma,Chang Yu,Yunuo Chen,Yanjia Huang,Ying Nian Wu,Yin Yang,Chenfanfu Jiang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Skill2Real is an agentic framework that learns executable robot skills via a shared API and a Proposer-Verifier-Governor loop, enabling sim-to-real transfer of hierarchical skill memories without task-policy fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transferring robotic skills from simulation to reality demands task knowledge that stays usable across differences in perception, dynamics, and embodiment. Existing skill learning tends to entangle task knowledge with specific observations, dynamics, or hardware, limiting transfer.

Method: A shared application programming interface (API) defines the skill interface. A Proposer-Verifier-Governor (PVG) loop uses privileged simulation evidence to diagnose outcomes and validate updates while grounding learned skills in public observations and API semantics. The Cerebellum first acquires local manipulation skills, then the Brain learns task-level composition with the Cerebellum frozen. Both memories transfer to the real robot without task-policy fine-tuning or skill-memory updates.

Result: On LIBERO-90 with GPT-5.6 Sol learning skills and each frozen checkpoint evaluated by GPT-6 Astra, LIBERO-Pro Long success rises from 2.0% to 56.3% without Pro Long training. Independent Robosuite training reaches 85.1% and 89.4% mean success with Sol and Opus 5 across seven tasks. Frozen Sol-trained LIBERO-90 skills achieve 78.75% mean completion across four real-world manipulation tasks with Astra. Removing the Verifier or Governor during LIBERO-90 training lowers final Pro Long success by 17.3 and 13.3 percentage points, respectively.

Conclusion: The results support learning and transferring a hierarchy of executable skills through a common robot interface, with the PVG loop and its Verifier/Governor components contributing materially to generalization and transfer.

Abstract: Transferring robotic skills from simulation to reality requires task knowledge that remains usable across differences in perception, dynamics, and embodiment. We introduce Skill2Real, an agentic policy framework that learns executable skills through a shared application programming interface (API). A Proposer-Verifier-Governor (PVG) loop uses privileged simulation evidence to diagnose outcomes and validate updates, while keeping learned skills grounded in public observations and API semantics. The Cerebellum first acquires local manipulation skills; the Brain then learns task-level composition with the Cerebellum frozen. Both memories transfer to the real robot without task-policy fine-tuning or skill-memory updates. As GPT-5.6 Sol learns skills on LIBERO-90, evaluating each frozen checkpoint with GPT-6 Astra raises LIBERO-Pro Long success from 2.0% to 56.3%, without training on Pro Long. Independent Robosuite training reaches 85.1% and 89.4% mean success with Sol and Opus 5 across seven tasks, respectively. Frozen Sol-trained LIBERO-90 skills achieve 78.75% mean completion across four real-world manipulation tasks with Astra. Removing the Verifier or Governor during LIBERO-90 training lowers final Pro Long success by 17.3 and 13.3 percentage points, respectively. These results support learning and transferring a hierarchy of executable skills through a common robot interface.

</details>


### [151] [ManiPhysicsBench: Physics-Based Assessment of Object Preservation in VLA Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02802)
*Sangwu Park,Yeonjun In,Wonjoong Kim,Sungwon Kim,Sein Kim,Chanyoung Park*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ManiPhysicsZoo provides physics-based object assets and a solver-based damage assessment, enabling ManiPhysicsBench to measure object preservation in LIBERO and SimplerEnv. Public VLA checkpoints show a large gap between task success and safe success, and object-specific continuous gripper labels improve safe success but reduce task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Task success in existing rigid-body benchmarks does not indicate whether VLA models preserve manipulated objects, so there is a need to assess physical realism and object preservation.

Method: ManiPhysicsZoo consolidates literature-supported material properties, 3D meshes, and references into reusable object assets. A solver-based assessment computes grasp-specific damage thresholds from geometry, material properties, and grasp conditions, then compares them with recorded contact forces to assess deformation and fracture. ManiPhysicsBench evaluates LIBERO and SimplerEnv across three physics axes and three difficulty levels. A VLA model is retrained with object-specific continuous gripper labels.

Result: Public VLA checkpoints show a substantial gap between task success and safe success (task completion while preserving the object). Gripper commands concentrate near full opening and closure with similar aggregate distributions across objects, consistent with binary gripper supervision. The retrained model shows more object-dependent gripping and higher safe success, but lower task success and limited generalization of object-preserving behavior.

Conclusion: Current VLA benchmarks and models under-evaluate object preservation, and binary gripper supervision encourages non-object-specific grasping. Continuous gripper labels can improve safe success, but at the cost of task success and generalization, highlighting the need for better object-preserving VLA learning.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models aim to perform diverse manipulation tasks, but task success in existing rigid-body benchmarks does not indicate whether they preserve objects. We introduce ManiPhysicsZoo, which consolidates literature-supported material properties, 3D meshes, and supporting references into reusable object assets. Using these assets, a solver-based assessment computes grasp-specific damage thresholds from object geometry, material properties, and recorded grasp conditions and compares them with recorded contact forces to assess potential deformation and fracture. Building on these components, ManiPhysicsBench evaluates object preservation in LIBERO and SimplerEnv across three physics axes and three difficulty levels. Public VLA checkpoints show a substantial gap between task success and safe success, defined as task completion while preserving the object. Their gripper commands concentrate near full opening and closure, with largely similar aggregate distributions across objects, consistent with binary gripper supervision. We examine how object-specific continuous gripper labels change model behavior by retraining a VLA model. The retrained model shows more object-dependent gripping and higher safe success, but lower task success and limited generalization of object-preserving behavior.

</details>


### [152] [LOCUS: Landmark-Oriented Container Discrimination Using Spatial Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02803)
*Taylor Bergeron,Shibani Senthilbabu,Kevin Leahy*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LOCUS trains a GNN to refine CLIP embeddings over a scene graph for spatial‑semantic reasoning, improving target-container search over CLIP, Tidybot, LLM planners, and random baselines in simulation, and is validated on a real mobile manipulator with Detic labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots in unstructured environments must decide where to search for out-of-sight objects using both physical plausibility and semantic likelihood. Cosine similarity with CLIP cannot disambiguate containers that are spatially close or semantically similar.

Method: LOCUS trains a GNN to update CLIP embeddings on a full environment scene graph, propagating embedding information between nodes by proximity. Ontology fusion from household knowledge sources augments the CLIP signal, yielding joint spatial-semantic reasoning. Evaluation uses a noiseless scene graph from simulator metadata to remain detection-agnostic, plus a physical mobile manipulator exploration pipeline with Detic-generated scene graph labels.

Result: LOCUS outperforms random, CLIP, Tidybot, and an LLM planner in the majority of room classes and configurations in simulation, and runs robustly on a physical mobile manipulator despite scene graph node placement and label noise.

Conclusion: Graph-based refinement of CLIP embeddings with ontology-augmented semantics provides a robust, generalizable approach to landmark-oriented container discrimination, transferring from simulation to real-world robotic search with noisy perceptual labels.

Abstract: As robots are increasingly deployed in unstructured, real-world environments, the ability to reason about complex spatial and semantic relationships among objects remains a fundamental challenge in enabling robust and generalizable manipulation and navigation. For example, deciding where to search for an object that is not in plain sight depends on where it is physically plausible as well as semantically likely. Popular methods such as cosine similarity with CLIP struggle to disambiguate between spatially and semantically similar objects that could contain a target object. We propose Landmark-Oriented Container Discrimination Using Spatial Graphs (LOCUS). To jointly reason about spatial information and semantics, we train a GNN to update CLIP embeddings on a full environment scene graph by passing embedding information between nodes based on proximity. CLIP embeddings, augmented by semantic knowledge from a fusion of household ontologies provide a robust semantic signal. We evaluate in simulation on a noiseless scene graph from simulator metadata, making the approach detection-agnostic. Our approach outperforms random, CLIP, Tidybot, and an LLM planner in the majority of room classes and configurations in simulation. To show our approach is robust to scene graph node placement and label noise, we demonstrate a physical mobile manipulator running in an exploration pipeline start-to-finish including scene graph labels from Detic.

</details>


### [153] [SARI: Phase-Split Sim-Real Co-Training for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02804)
*Xingxin He,Yuxuan Jiang,Haonan Zhang,Chuhan Cui,Kaile Li,Zhongxing Zheng,Caihao Xu,Ziqi Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SARI is a phase-split sim-and-real co-training framework for VLA models that learns free-space approaches from simulation and contact interactions from a few real demonstrations, achieving strong generalization to unseen placements with 34.3% less real-data collection time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models need costly real-world demonstrations to adapt to contact-rich manipulation, especially when generalizing across object placements. The paper observes that spatial coverage and contact physics are best acquired from different domains: free-space approaches need spatial diversity but tolerate simulation gaps, while contact interactions need accurate physics but vary little across placements.

Method: SARI generates diverse simulated approaches in a photorealistic digital twin and collects real contact interactions at only a few placements. A single policy is post-trained on these phase-segmented demonstrations, stitching simulated approaches with real contact interactions via visual appearance alignment and a shared camera-relative action representation, without explicit phase labels or hand-coded switches.

Result: Across five real-world contact-rich manipulation tasks, SARI reduces real-data collection time by 34.3% and achieves 27.5% success at unseen placements, whereas all full-task sim-real baselines fail completely (0%).

Conclusion: Splitting approach and contact phases across simulation and real domains, combined with appearance alignment and a shared action representation, enables a single seamless policy that generalizes to unseen placements far more efficiently than full-task sim-real co-training baselines.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models often require costly real-world demonstrations to adapt to contact-rich manipulation tasks, particularly when generalization across object placements is needed. We propose SARI (Simulated Approach, Real Interaction), a phase-split sim-and-real co-training framework built on a simple insight: spatial coverage and contact physics should be acquired from the domains best suited to them. Specifically, free-space approaches require spatial diversity but tolerate modest simulation gaps, making them ideal for synthetic generation; conversely, contact interactions demand accurate physics but vary little across object placements, allowing a few real demonstrations to generalize across the workspace. SARI generates diverse simulated approaches in a photorealistic digital twin while collecting real contact interactions at only a few placements. Post-trained on these phase-segmented demonstrations, a single policy seamlessly stitches simulated approaches with real contact interactions using visual appearance alignment and a shared camera-relative action representation--without explicit phase labels or hand-coded switches. Across five real-world contact-rich manipulation tasks, SARI reduces real-data collection time by 34.3% and achieves 27.5% success at unseen placements, where all full-task sim-real baselines fail completely (0%).

</details>


### [154] [Learning Reflexive Behavior for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02811)
*Quan Nguyen,Yunho Kim,Joonho Lee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper learns a proprioceptive reflex policy from three simple interaction primitives (spring, plane, rail) and freezes it as a low-level execution layer beneath higher-level controllers, enabling robust contact-rich manipulation without force or geometric sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contact-rich manipulation involves robot-environment interactions that encode local geometry, but common controllers (position, hybrid force-position, Cartesian impedance) rely on prescribed tracking objectives, stiffness, or force-control directions that may not match local constraints, degrading performance. The aim is to exploit these interactions to comply with environmental constraints while preserving task intent.

Method: A proprioceptive reflex policy is learned in simulation on three interaction primitives: a spring, a plane, and a rail. The policy maps task-space commands to joint-position targets using state history, without direct force or geometric measurements. Once trained, it is frozen and deployed as an execution layer beneath higher-level controllers (motion planners, learned policies, teleoperators).

Result: Evaluated on dual-arm box lifting, peg insertion, and surface following. In box lifting, the reflex kept force below the threshold while the baseline failed. In rough-surface following, it stayed below the 10 N reference, on par with tuned hybrid force-position control. In 0.02 mm peg insertion, it cut mean estimated contact force to less than half the baseline while raising hardware success rates from at most 22% to 36-58%.

Conclusion: Separating contact response from command generation lets the reflex policy provide motion planners, learned policies, and teleoperators with robust contact-rich execution, improving compliance and success without explicit force or geometry sensing.

Abstract: During contact-rich manipulation, interactions between a robot and its environment carry information about local geometry: a surface prevents penetration, a bore guides a peg. A controller that exploits these interactions can comply with environmental constraints while preserving task intent. Robot-earning systems commonly use position, hybrid force-position, or Cartesian impedance control. Their prescribed tracking objectives, stiffness, or force-control directions may not match local constraints and may degrade performance. We learn a proprioceptive reflex policy in simulation on three simple interaction primitives: a spring, a plane, and a rail. The policy maps task-space commands to joint-position targets using state history, without direct force or geometrical measurements. Once trained, it serves as a frozen execution layer beneath higher-level controllers. We evaluate it in dual-arm box lifting, peg insertion, and surface following. In box lifting, the reflex kept the force below the threshold while the baseline failed. In rough-surface following it stayed below the 10 N reference, on par with tuned hybrid force-position control. In 0.02 mm peg insertion It reduced mean estimated contact force to less than half that of the baseline while increasing hardware success rates from at most 22 % to 36-58 %. By separating contact response from command generation, the reflex policy provides motion planners, learned policies, and teleoperators with robust contact-rich execution.

</details>


### [155] [Register-Routed Delayed Fusion: Rewiring Shortcut-Prone Observation Fusion in Visuomotor Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02813)
*Jieting Long,Weidong Cai,Weiming Zhi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces Register-Routed Delayed Fusion (RRDF), a fusion topology for visuomotor imitation policies that blocks direct compact-to-visual attention early and routes cross-modal interaction through a learned register workspace, improving robustness over dense ACT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In visuomotor imitation policies, fusion topology determines when and how high-dimensional visual tokens and compact proprioceptive tokens interact. In dense token fusion, visual tokens may attend directly to compact tokens from the first encoder layer, letting action-predictive compact cues shape spatial visual representations prematurely. The authors investigate whether controlling this route improves visual responsiveness and policy behavior.

Method: RRDF masks direct compact-visual attention and stages cross-modal interaction through a learned register workspace. Its isolate-collect-route schedule protects an early stream-separated prefix, then permits only register-mediated exchange, while compact conditioning remains available to the native action generator.

Result: Across five simulation tasks and three real-robot tasks, RRDF matches or improves dense ACT under nominal conditions. Appearance-shift evaluations on four simulation tasks and held-out-position evaluations on three real-robot tasks also favor RRDF. Phase-matched input probes show lower measured state-to-image sensitivity, and ablations indicate registers alone do not reproduce the full gain.

Conclusion: Controlling cross-modal propagation while retaining compact action conditioning improves visual responsiveness and robustness, supporting the proposed register-routed delayed fusion design.

Abstract: Visuomotor imitation policies combine high-dimensional visual observations with compact signals such as proprioception, and their fusion topology determines when and through which tokens these streams interact. In dense token fusion, visual tokens may attend directly to compact tokens from the first encoder layer, allowing action-predictive compact cues to influence spatial visual representations early in their formation. We ask whether controlling this route improves visual responsiveness and policy behavior. We introduce Register-Routed Delayed Fusion (RRDF), which masks direct compact-visual attention and stages cross-modal interaction through a learned register workspace. Its isolate-collect-route schedule protects an early stream-separated prefix and later permits only register-mediated exchange, while compact conditioning remains available to the native action generator. Across five simulation tasks and three real-robot tasks, RRDF matches or improves dense ACT under nominal conditions. Appearance-shift evaluations on four simulation tasks and held-out-position evaluations on three real-robot tasks also favor RRDF. Phase-matched input probes show lower measured state-to-image sensitivity, while ablations indicate that adding registers alone does not reproduce the full performance gain. These results support controlling cross-modal propagation while retaining compact action conditioning.

</details>


### [156] [FastOPD: On-Policy Distillation for Lightweight VLA Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02832)
*Yoojin Oh,Jeongsol Kim,Yeonwoo Seo,Jangho Park,Seonghyun Jin,Sunwoo Park,Youngmin Kim,Youngjun Jun,Kyumin Choi,Jong Chul Ye*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FastOPD is a foundation-to-lightweight Vision-Language-Action framework that uses on-policy distillation with a flow map for single-state teacher supervision plus a self-consistency objective to produce compact, few-step students, retaining 84% of π₀.₅ performance with two inference steps and cutting latency 78.1%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA foundation models scale rapidly for better manipulation and generalizability, but the computational cost makes real-world deployment harder. Prior fixes rely on smaller architectures or fewer iterative denoising steps in flow-based policies, which limit performance or efficiency gains.

Method: FastOPD performs efficient on-policy distillation from a large VLA teacher to a lightweight student. It adapts a flow map for single-state teacher supervision and combines it with a self-consistency objective so the compact student learns the teacher dynamics. The authors theoretically show that minimizing this objective lets the distilled student recover a distribution matching that induced by an ideal few-step teacher.

Result: On LIBERO, FastOPD retains 84% of π₀.₅'s performance with only two inference steps, reduces inference latency by 78.1%, and beats existing few-step distillation baselines in average success rate. With LingBot-VLA as teacher, it improves the single-step success rate over the base student by 15.9 percentage points on RoboTwin 2.0. It also applies to a World Action Model and deploys a compact student distilled from MolmoAct2 on a real robot.

Conclusion: FastOPD enables practical deployment of large-scale VLAs via on-policy distillation, achieving strong performance retention with drastically fewer inference steps across simulation, diverse foundation policies, and real-robot deployment.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) foundation models have scaled rapidly to enhance manipulation performance and generalizability, but this scaling incurs high computational costs that render real-world deployment increasingly challenging. Existing approaches typically mitigate this issue by designing smaller architectures or reducing the iterative denoising steps in flow-based policies. In this work, we propose FastOPD, a foundation-to-lightweight VLA framework that enables the practical deployment of large-scale VLAs through efficient on-policy distillation. Specifically, FastOPD adapts a flow map for single-state teacher supervision and combines it with a self-consistency objective to construct a compact student that learns the teacher dynamics. Furthermore, we theoretically demonstrate that minimizing this objective allows the distilled student to recover a distribution on par with that induced by an ideal few-step teacher model. We evaluate FastOPD across diverse foundation policies in simulation and real-world experiments. On LIBERO, FastOPD retains 84% of the performance of $π_{0.5}$ with only two inference steps, reducing inference latency by 78.1% while outperforming existing few-step distillation baselines in average success rate. With LingBot-VLA as the teacher, FastOPD improves the single-step success rate over the base student by 15.9 percentage points on RoboTwin 2.0. We further demonstrate its applicability to a World Action Model (WAM) and deploy a compact student distilled from MolmoAct2 on a real robot.

</details>


### [157] [PointWAM: 3D World Action Modeling for Dexterous Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02840)
*Chunghyun Park,Beomjun Kim,Seungcheol Park,Heeseung Kwon,Yashu Shukla,Seunghoon Sim,Jinwoo Shin,Minsu Cho*

Main category: cs.RO
