TL;DR: PointWAM is a 3D world action model that represents the world as disentangled scene and hand point trajectories in a shared space-time frame, forecasts their joint evolution, and retargets hand motion to robot actions, achieving state-of-the-art dexterous manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world action models represent the world as RGB frames or latent features and predict actions as end-effector poses or joint angles, which struggle to capture the 3D spatial structure and contact geometry essential for dexterous manipulation.

Method: PointWAM decomposes the world into scene (environment) and hands (actor) and jointly forecasts both as 3D point trajectories in a shared space-time coordinate frame. Given a colored point cloud and a language instruction, it predicts how scene and hands co-evolve in 3D over time, then retargets the forecast hand motion to robot actions. The explicit, disentangled representation enables pre-training on large-scale human demonstration videos without task-specific object or keypoint selection.

Result: Pre-training on human videos improves average DexJoCo success by 56.9 percentage points; scene-trajectory supervision adds 10.9 points over forecasting hands alone. With both, PointWAM surpasses prior state of the art on ten DexJoCo tasks by 11.7 points and outperforms strong VLAs on a real robot.

Conclusion: An explicit, disentangled 3D point-trajectory representation of scene and hands, combined with human-video pre-training and scene-trajectory supervision, yields substantial gains for dexterous manipulation, surpassing prior state of the art in simulation and strong VLAs on a real robot.

Abstract: World action models jointly learn to forecast world dynamics and predict robot actions, such that the learned internal world dynamics guide accurate actions. Existing approaches typically represent the world as RGB frames or latent counterparts while predicting actions as end-effector poses or joint angles, but they often struggle to capture the 3D spatial structure and contact geometry central to dexterous manipulation. We introduce Point World Action Model (PointWAM), a 3D world action model that decomposes the world into a scene (i.e., environment) and hands (i.e., actor), and jointly forecasts both as 3D point trajectories within a shared space-time coordinate frame. This explicit, disentangled representation enables effective pre-training on large-scale human demonstration videos without requiring any task-specific object or keypoint selection. Given a colored point cloud and a language instruction, PointWAM predicts how the scene and hands co-evolve in 3D space over time, then retargets the forecast hand motion to robot actions. Pre-training on human videos improves average DexJoCo success by 56.9 percentage points, and scene-trajectory supervision adds 10.9 points over forecasting the hands alone. With both, PointWAM surpasses the prior state of the art on ten DexJoCo tasks by 11.7 points and outperforms strong VLAs on a real robot.

</details>


### [158] [Permutation Robustness Is Not Enough: Action Collapse in Multi-Agent Transformer Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02848)
*Amit Thakur,Mukesh Singhal*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Transformer multi-agent policies are inherently order-sensitive due to permutation mismatch, and low permutation error can be misleading because agents may all output the same action. The authors propose evaluating robustness with permutation-consistency metrics plus action-collapse diagnostics (action diversity, same-action fraction, max action frequency), finding that weak equivariance regularization preserves diverse behavior for N=3, while N=4 needs much smaller weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer policies are appealing for multi-agent robot learning since self-attention models inter-agent interactions. However, multi-agent teams are unordered sets whereas transformers process ordered token sequences. This mismatch affects cooperative navigation under agent-order permutations, and existing evaluation can be misleading: apparent permutation robustness may stem from action collapse (all agents picking the same action) rather than genuine equivariance.

Method: The paper studies cooperative navigation policies under agent-order permutations using transformers. It evaluates policies with two complementary families of metrics: (1) permutation-consistency metrics and (2) action-collapse diagnostics, comprising action diversity, same-action fraction, and maximum action frequency. Baselines/regularization variants include a PPO-ID baseline, strong equivariance regularization, and weak equivariance penalty, tested with teams of N=3 and N=4 agents.

Result: Low permutation error alone can be misleading because policies may appear robust simply due to all agents selecting the same action. The PPO-ID baseline yields non-collapsed behavior but remains order-sensitive. Strong equivariance regularization can still induce homogeneous (collapsed) behavior. A weak equivariance penalty improves robustness while preserving more diverse actions for N=3 agents, whereas N=4 teams require substantially smaller regularization weights.

Conclusion: Multi-agent transformer policies should be evaluated not only by return and permutation robustness, but also by whether they maintain non-collapsed, differentiated multi-agent behavior. Permutation error must be interpreted jointly with action-collapse diagnostics to avoid falsely concluding order-invariance.

Abstract: Transformer policies are attractive for multi-agent robot learning because self-attention can model interactions among agents. However, multi-agent teams are unordered, while transformers typically process agents as ordered token sequences. We study how this mismatch affects cooperative navigation policies under agent-order permutations. Our results show that low permutation error alone can be misleading: policies may appear robust simply because all agents choose the same action. We therefore evaluate policies using both permutation-consistency metrics and action-collapse diagnostics, including action diversity, same-action fraction, and maximum action frequency. A PPO-ID baseline yields non-collapsed behavior but remains order-sensitive, while strong equivariance regularization can still induce homogeneous behavior. A weak equivariance penalty improves the robustness while preserving more diverse actions for teams with \(N=3\) agents, whereas teams with \(N=4\) agents require substantially smaller regularization weights. These findings suggest that multi-agent transformer policies should be evaluated not only by return and permutation robustness, but also by whether they maintain non-collapsed, differentiated multi-agent behavior.

</details>


### [159] [SceneFactory-3D: Lifting 2D Traffic Scenes into 3D Physical Counterfactuals for Scalable Physically Grounded Safety Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02874)
*Yicheng Zhu,Linfeng Tian,Tianmu Zhao,Yang Chen,Fan Zuo,Tao Li,Zilin Bian*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SceneFactory-3D is a GPU-batched, physics-grounded multi-agent driving simulator that models suspension- and friction-limited forces at each wheel-contact point, enabling matched physical counterfactuals to be run in parallel. An empirical study across 21 friction and grade conditions shows learned controllers degrade sharply (safe work-zone clearance dropping 6-90 percentage points) as friction falls from 1.0 to 0.18.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scalable driving simulators typically execute vehicle commands using prescribed behavioral or kinematic rules, ignoring tire-road interface physics. This limits their ability to capture how adverse road and environmental conditions alter vehicle execution and propagate through traffic.

Method: SceneFactory-3D executes acceleration and steering commands via suspension- and friction-limited forces evaluated at each wheel-contact point. Spatially varying friction, per-world 3D heightfields, gravity, and rigid contact govern wheel motion and chassis collisions. Per-world terrain isolation and GPU batching allow matched physical counterfactuals to run in parallel, holding traffic scenario setup and vehicle controllers fixed while varying only road condition. Evaluation covers three learned-policy families on 1,024 matched 12-vehicle worlds per condition and two classical planners on a shared 32-world subset, across 21 friction and grade conditions.

Result: When friction drops from 1.0 to 0.18, the share of vehicles that safely clear the work zone falls by 6 to 90 percentage points across learned policies (18-19 percentage points for classical planners), and near-collision situations become more frequent for every learned policy.

Conclusion: Physics-grounded, counterfactual simulation reveals that learned vehicle controllers are markedly sensitive to road conditions, with safety performance degrading substantially under low friction—an effect that kinematic-rule simulators would fail to capture.

Abstract: Scalable driving simulators typically execute vehicle commands using prescribed behavioral or kinematic rules, overlooking the physics of tire-road interfaces, thereby limiting their ability to capture how adverse road and environmental conditions alter vehicle execution and propagate through traffic. To address this limitation, we present SceneFactory-3D, a GPU-batched, physics-grounded multi-agent driving simulator. Vehicles execute acceleration and steering commands via suspension- and friction-limited forces evaluated at each wheel-contact point. Spatially varying friction, per-world 3D heightfields, gravity, and rigid contact consistently govern wheel motion and chassis collisions. Per-world terrain isolation and GPU batching enable SceneFactory-3D to run matched physical counterfactuals in parallel: traffic scenario setup and vehicle controllers remain fixed while only the road condition changes, enabling the resulting closed-loop effects to be evaluated across parallel worlds. To demonstrate the advantage of the SceneFactory-3D-enabled counterfactual evaluation, we conduct an empirical study on vehicle controllers' sensitivity to road conditions. We study three learned-policy families on 1,024 matched 12-vehicle worlds per condition, and two classical planners on a shared 32-world subset, across 21 friction and grade conditions. When friction drops from 1.0 to 0.18, the share of vehicles that clear the work zone safely falls by 6 to 90 percentage points across learned policies (18-19 for classical planners), and near-collision situations become more frequent for every learned policy. Code: https://github.com/SmallWorldLab/SceneFactory_3D

</details>


### [160] [MixVLA: Adaptive Mixing of Non-Invariant Information for Generalizable Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02898)
*Pingrui Zhang,Yu Zhang,Pengyuan Wu,Bin Wang,Haoming Song,Xianqiang Gao,ZhaxiZhuoma,Zhigang Wang,Dong Wang,Bin Zhao,Xuelong Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MixVLA is a model-agnostic training framework that improves zero-shot OOD generalization of VLA models by stochastically mixing non-invariant representations (AMI), requiring no extra OOD data or architecture changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models generalize poorly under out-of-distribution (OOD) conditions in robotic manipulation. They entangle task-relevant invariant structure with environment-specific non-invariant factors, leading policies to rely on spurious appearance cues during action prediction.

Method: MixVLA introduces Adaptive Mixing of Non-Invariant Information (AMI), which stochastically mixes non-invariant representations to regularize distribution-specific variability while preserving complementary predictive cues. The mixed non-invariant features are fused with invariant representations for final action prediction, improving robustness without sacrificing policy expressiveness. The framework is model-agnostic and needs no additional OOD data or architectural modifications.

Result: Extensive experiments on LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, the RoboTwin perturbation suite, and real-world tasks show that MixVLA improves overall zero-shot robustness while retaining strong in-domain performance.

Conclusion: MixVLA offers a simple, model-agnostic training strategy that enhances zero-shot OOD robustness of VLA models without extra data or architectural changes, while maintaining in-domain performance.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have achieved remarkable advances in robotic manipulation, yet their zero-shot generalization under out-of-distribution (OOD) conditions remains limited. These models often entangle task-relevant invariant structure with environment-specific non-invariant factors, causing policies to rely on spurious appearance cues during action prediction. In this work, we propose \textbf{MixVLA}, a model-agnostic training framework that improves the generalization of VLA models without requiring additional OOD data or architectural modifications. The key component of MixVLA is \textbf{Adaptive Mixing of Non-Invariant Information (AMI)}. AMI stochastically mixes non-invariant representations to regularize distribution-specific variability while preserving complementary predictive cues. The mixed non-invariant features are then fused with invariant representations for final action prediction, resulting in improved robustness without sacrificing policy expressiveness. Extensive experiments across challenging manipulation settings, including LIBERO, LIBERO-Plus, the RoboTwin perturbation suite, and real-world tasks, demonstrate that MixVLA improves overall zero-shot robustness while retaining strong in-domain performance.

</details>


### [161] [From Language Priors to Field Adaptation: Preference Learning for Traversability Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02974)
*Simon Schwaiger,David Seyser,Alessandro Scherl,Zlatan Ajanović,Wilfried Wöber,Gerald Steinbauer-Wagner*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This work estimates robot traversability using von Mises-Fisher mixture prototypes in a frozen vision-language feature space, where natural-language rule priors and sparse relative image annotations enable sample-efficient domain adaptation without end-to-end training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Image-based traversability estimation depends heavily on the robot platform, deployment domain, and mission preferences, so purpose-trained models generalize poorly. Domain adaptation is difficult because it normally requires many annotations in the target domain.

Method: Traversability is represented via von Mises-Fisher mixture prototypes in a frozen vision-language feature space. Relative natural-language rules serve as a commonsense prior, while sparse relative image annotations adapt prototype directions and utilities to the target domain through computationally and sample-efficient fine-tuning.

Result: On WayFAST, the approach achieves accuracy competitive with end-to-end trained estimators while enabling sample-efficient image-based adaptation. Qualitative experiments show the language prior's zero-shot applicability and improved dense prediction of the fine-tuned estimator on semantic maps. Learned prototypes are semantically interpreted by dissecting semantically close natural-language prompts.

Conclusion: Traversability can be estimated sample-efficiently by combining language priors with sparse relative annotations in a frozen vision-language space, supporting platform- and domain-adaptive traversability estimation and providing interpretable prototypes.

Abstract: Image-based traversability estimation is inherently dependent on the robot platform, deployment domain, and mission preferences, which limits the applicability of purpose-trained models. To facilitate domain adaptation, this work aims to reduce the number of required annotations in the target domain using sample-efficient preference learning. Our method represents traversability through von Mises-Fisher mixture prototypes in a frozen vision-language feature space. Relative natural-language rules provide a commonsense prior, while sparse relative image annotations adapt the prototype directions and utilities to a target domain through computationally and sample-efficient fine-tuning. Experiments on WayFAST demonstrate accuracy competitive with end-to-end trained estimators while enabling sample-efficient image-based adaptation. Qualitative experiments further demonstrate the language prior's zero shot applicability and the fine-tuned estimator's improved dense prediction on semantic maps. Evaluation is complemented via semantic interpretation of learned prototypes by dissecting semantically close natural language prompts. Code and trained estimators available at https://resireg.github.io

</details>


### [162] [On Representational Alignment among Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02985)
*Fulvio Mastrogiovanni*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper introduces "relational sufficiency," a formal condition determining when heterogeneous agent representations are aligned enough for a given interaction, and derives a first-order lower bound on the number of scalar anchors needed to resolve interaction-relevant ambiguity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied agents interacting with the same physical process often hold heterogeneous, asynchronous, and observer-relative representations. Conventional approaches assume such representations must be globally aligned, which is unnecessarily costly. The paper asks instead which distinctions must actually be resolved for coherent interaction, aiming for a task-relative stopping condition rather than full alignment.

Method: The authors formalize the problem via relational sufficiency: an interaction-specific relational-evidence map defines the representational ambiguity left unresolved by comparison, and sufficiency holds when each residual ambiguity fiber lies within an interaction-equivalence class. When this fails, additional sensorimotor anchors must separate precisely the ambiguities affecting interaction outcomes. For smooth systems, they derive a local first-order lower bound on the number of independent scalar anchors required, given by the dimension of directions invisible to relational evidence but visible to the interaction-relevant map. The theory is instantiated in two variants of an asynchronous hand-off scenario sharing identical agents, representations, transports, and relational evidence.

Result: In the two hand-off variants, relative synchronization requires no additional anchor, whereas hand-off at an absolute time requires exactly one, matching the derived lower bound.

Conclusion: The analysis yields a task-relative stopping condition for representational alignment: residual disagreement need not be eliminated when it is invariant with respect to the interaction. Alignment effort can thus be minimized by anchoring only the ambiguity directions that matter for the task.

Abstract: Embodied agents interacting with the same physical process may maintain heterogeneous, asynchronous, and observer-relative representations. Rather than assuming that such representations should always be globally aligned, we investigate which distinctions among them must actually be resolved for coherent interaction. We formalize this question through relational sufficiency: an interaction-specific relational-evidence map defines the representational ambiguity left unresolved by comparison, and sufficiency holds when each residual ambiguity fiber lies within an interaction-equivalence class. When this condition fails, additional sensorimotor anchors must separate precisely the ambiguities that can change the interaction-relevant outcome. For smooth systems, we derive a local first-order lower bound on the number of inde- pendent scalar anchors required, given by the dimension of directions that are invisible to relational evidence but visible to the interaction-relevant map. We instantiate the theory in two variants of an asynchronous hand-off scenario that share the same observer agents, representations, transports, and relational evidence. Relative synchronization requires no additional anchor, whereas hand-off at an absolute time requires exactly one. The analysis there- fore provides a task-relative stopping condition for representational alignment: residual disagreement need not be eliminated when it is invariant with respect to the interaction.

</details>


### [163] [Subject-Specific Predictive Musculoskeletal Simulations of Lower-Limb Exoskeleton Assistance: Metabolic and Biomechanical Effects of Joint Assistance Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02991)
*Neethan Ratnakumar,Mariya Tohfafarosha,Xianlian Zhou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Predictive musculoskeletal simulations in six subject-specific models show that combined hip-knee-ankle assistance yields the largest metabolic savings, while hip-ankle assistance is the most efficient two-actuator configuration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing energy-optimal lower-limb exoskeleton assistance is difficult due to inter-individual variability in anthropometry and biomechanics, so optimal joint-assistance targets need systematic investigation.

Method: Subject-specific musculoskeletal models of six able-bodied subjects (BMI-based muscle strength scaling) were used in predictive simulations of self-selected-speed gait with ideal actuators providing joint assistance at 25 Nm and 50 Nm peak levels across different joint combinations. Outcomes were assessed via cost of transport (COT), joint kinematics, muscle activations, assistive torques, and joint-level power.

Result: At 50 Nm, combined H+K+A assistance gave the greatest mean COT reduction of 48.50 +/- 4.55% (individual range 42.22%-54.65%). Hip assistance was the most effective single joint (31.77 +/- 5.21%), with knee and ankle smaller and comparable (19.17% and 17.02%). H+A was the best two-joint configuration (44.60 +/- 7.08%). Higher torque bounds increased positive assistive power mainly at hip and ankle, while knee assistance was largely insensitive and delivered positive power near pre-swing.

Conclusion: H+A assistance is supported as an efficient two-actuator target, and the knee's atypical pre-swing power strategy is identified as a candidate for targeted experimental validation.

Abstract: Lower-limb exoskeletons have made considerable progress in reducing energy expenditure during walking. However, designing optimal assistance strategies remains challenging, particularly given inter-individual variability in anthropometry and biomechanics. This study explores energy-optimal lower-limb joint-assistance strategies using predictive simulations with musculoskeletal models. Subject-specific models of six able-bodied subjects, with BMI-based muscle strength scaling, were used in predictive simulations to generate gait at self-selected walking speeds. Ideal actuators were incorporated to simulate various combinations of joint assistance at peak levels of 25 Nm and 50 Nm to examine the effects of assistance on gait and metabolic savings. The effects of each assistance configuration were assessed through cost of transport (COT), joint kinematics, muscle activations, assistive torques, and joint-level power metrics to characterize the biomechanical and energetic impacts of different assistance strategies. At 50 Nm, combined H+K+A (hip-knee-ankle) assistance resulted in the greatest mean COT reduction of 48.50 +/- 4.55%, with individual reductions ranging from 42.22% to 54.65% across the subjects. Among single-joint conditions, assisting the hip was most effective, reducing COT by 31.77 +/- 5.21%; the knee and ankle produced smaller, comparable reductions (19.17% and 17.02%). H+A (hip-ankle) assistance (44.60 +/- 7.08%) emerged as the most effective two-joint assistive configuration. Increasing the torque bound increased positive assistive power primarily at the hip and ankle, while knee assistance showed little sensitivity and delivered positive power near pre-swing. These results support H+A assistance as an efficient two-actuator target, while identifying the knee's atypical pre-swing power strategy as a candidate for targeted experimental validation.

</details>


### [164] [Safe Streaming Flow Planning by Aligning Sampling Dynamics with Execution Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03132)
*Seunghwan Jang,Jeongyong Yang,Siddharth Ancha,SooJean Han*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SafeStreamingFlow is a goal-conditioned generative planner that aligns flow sampling dynamics with execution dynamics via sequential state-vector-field integration and hierarchical state prediction, enforcing safety only on the executed step using high-order control barrier functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world deployment of diffusion/flow-matching generative planners requires strict safety-constraint satisfaction during execution and fast online replanning. Prior safe diffusion/flow planners synthesize the entire trajectory at once and repeatedly perturb intermediate states for safety, which is computationally intensive and induces distribution shift because the learned sampling dynamics differs from the actual execution dynamics.

Method: SafeStreamingFlow is a goal-conditioned planner that aligns flow sampling dynamics with execution dynamics by sequentially integrating a learned state vector field together with hierarchical state prediction. Safety constraints are enforced only for the executed step via high-order control barrier functions.

Result: Across navigation, racing, and locomotion benchmarks, SafeStreamingFlow reduces planning latency and improves safety relative to existing methods while maintaining competitive goal-reaching success.

Conclusion: Aligning sampling with execution dynamics and applying high-order control barrier functions only to the executed step yields a streaming, safety-aware generative planner that is faster and safer than full-trajectory generation with intermediate-state perturbation.

Abstract: Generative planners based on diffusion/flow matching can learn to synthesize long-horizon trajectories from demonstrations. However, real-world deployment requires (i) enforcing safety constraints during execution and (ii) tight online replanning at fast execution rates. Prior safe diffusion/flow planners generate the agent's full trajectory at once, while repeatedly perturbing intermediate states to satisfy safety constraints. This approach is not only computationally intensive, but also introduces distribution shift since the learned sampling dynamics is distinct from the system's execution dynamics. We propose SafeStreamingFlow, a goal-conditioned planner that aligns flow sampling dynamics with execution dynamics by sequentially integrating a learned state vector field with hierarchical state prediction. Importantly, we need to enforce safety constraints only for the executed step via high order control barrier functions. Across navigation, racing, and locomotion benchmarks, SafeStreamingFlow reduces planning latency and improves safety compared to existing methods, while maintaining competitive goal-reaching success.

</details>


### [165] [Beyond Reward Hacking: Proxy Divergence Across Four Layers of a Staged Humanoid Learning Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03196)
*Arunabh Bora*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RL pipelines for legged robots are built from four proxies (reward, curriculum gate, evaluation statistic, reference motion); all suffer specification failure, and each can be repaired by a specific reformulation, demonstrated on a PPO humanoid grown over four stages and 13,500 iterations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional accounts localise specification failure (reward hacking) solely in the reward signal, treating curriculum gates, evaluation statistics, and reference motions as unproblematic scaffolding. This paper argues that all four components are proxies in the same formal sense and can each diverge from their intended target, so a complete treatment of specification failure must address every layer rather than the reward alone.

Method: For each of the four layers the paper states the traditional formulation, derives the divergence condition from the target, and proposes a reformulation: first-order (L1) costs where quadratic kernels are flat; peak and outcome statistics where curriculum gates average; gate reachability and information checks; deterministic, phase-desynchronised evaluation; curriculum state treated as part of the model; feasibility-first reference design with residual feed-forward; and function-preserving input widening enabling one policy to grow rather than be retrained.

Result: Measurements from one continuous lineage of a PPO policy for a simulated 1.91 m humanoid, grown over four stages and 13,500 iterations on a single laptop GPU, illustrate the divergences: a curriculum gate built on averaged error advanced at its rate limit on every check while the skill it gated was absent, and a batched push test with synchronised resets that aliased the gait phase ranked a 0.5 m/s push as more dangerous than a 2.0 m/s push.

Conclusion: Specification failure is a pipeline-wide property rather than a reward-only pathology. Each of the four proxies has a characteristic divergence mechanism and an admissible corrective reformulation, and diagnosing them jointly improves both the reliability and the interpretability of legged-robot RL pipelines.

Abstract: A reinforcement-learning (RL) pipeline for a legged robot is assembled from proxies. A reward stands in for intended behaviour, a curriculum gate stands in for competence, an evaluation statistic stands in for robustness, and a reference motion stands in for an achievable skill. The traditional view treats only the first of these as optimised against, and so locates specification failure (reward hacking) in the reward alone. I argue that all four are proxies in the same formal sense, that each has a characteristic divergence mechanism, and that each admits a reformulation that closes it. For every layer I state the traditional formulation, derive the condition under which it diverges from its target, and give the alternative: first-order (L1) costs where quadratic kernels are flat, peak and outcome statistics where curriculum gates average, gate reachability and information checks, deterministic and phase-desynchronised evaluation, curriculum state treated as part of the model, feasibility-first reference design with residual feed-forward, and function-preserving input widening that lets one policy grow instead of being retrained. The arguments are illustrated by measurements from one continuous lineage of a PPO policy for a simulated 1.91 m humanoid, grown over four stages and 13,500 iterations on a single laptop GPU. Among them, a curriculum gate built on averaged error advanced at its rate limit on every check while the skill it gated was absent, and a batched push test whose synchronised resets aliased the gait phase ranked a 0.5 m/s push as more dangerous than a 2.0 m/s one.

</details>


### [166] [DexJoCo-X: Benchmarking Action Representations for Multi-Hand Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03278)
*Xiangwei Jiang,Yao Mu,Lixin Duan,Wen Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DexJoCo-X is a benchmark and toolkit for controlled cross-embodiment dexterous manipulation, spanning 7 hands, 6 tasks, and 2,100 demonstrations. Testing three policies shows that a shared action interface alone is insufficient; effective multi-hand learning requires joint alignment of action coordinates, pretraining, and architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dexterous hands are proliferating, making per-morphology data collection and policy training impractical. Scalable cross-embodiment learning needs a unified representation capturing shared manipulation structure while preserving morphology-specific control. However, differences in hands, tasks, datasets, and control interfaces across prior studies prevent isolating the effects of representation, pretraining, and architecture.

Method: The authors introduce DexJoCo-X, a benchmark and toolkit providing a matched multi-hand, multi-task protocol with common scenes, success criteria, and execution interfaces. It covers seven representative dexterous hands, six single-arm and bimanual tasks, and 2,100 balanced demonstrations, using redesigned glove-to-hand mappings and an automated pipeline that expands reviewed demonstrations across randomized scenes. Three policies—π_{0.5}, Ego-Pi, and Being-H0.5—are evaluated to test whether a shared action interface suffices for multi-hand learning.

Result: Expanding π_{0.5} to an 80-dimensional bimanual output yields near-zero success. Ego-Pi preserves the pretrained action head via interleaved prediction and supports per-hand multi-task learning but is ineffective for seven-hand joint training. Being-H0.5, combining cross-embodiment pretraining, a unified action space, and embodiment-aware experts, lets one policy control all seven hands. Within it, function-aligned action slots reach 47.7% mean success versus 47.0% for native coordinates and 33.1% for DexLatent.

Conclusion: A shared action interface alone is insufficient for cross-embodiment learning. Effective cross-embodiment representation depends on the entire learning system: action coordinates, pretraining, and architecture must jointly separate shared manipulation structure from embodiment-specific control.

Abstract: As dexterous hands proliferate, collecting data and training policies separately for every morphology becomes increasingly impractical. Scalable cross-embodiment learning therefore requires a unified representation that captures shared manipulation structure while preserving morphology-specific control. Differences in hands, tasks, datasets, and control interfaces prevent existing studies from isolating the effects of representation, pretraining, and architecture. We introduce DexJoCo-X, a benchmark and toolkit for controlled comparison across seven representative dexterous hands, six single-arm and bimanual tasks, and 2,100 balanced demonstrations. DexJoCo-X provides a matched multi-hand, multi-task protocol with common scenes, success criteria, and execution interfaces, redesigned glove-to-hand mappings, and an automated pipeline that expands reviewed demonstrations across randomized scenes. Using $π_{0.5}$, Ego-Pi, and Being-H0.5, we examine whether a shared action interface is sufficient for multi-hand learning. Expanding $π_{0.5}$ to an 80-dimensional bimanual output yields near-zero success. Ego-Pi preserves the pretrained action head through interleaved prediction and supports per-hand multi-task learning, but remains ineffective for seven-hand joint training. By contrast, Being-H0.5 combines cross-embodiment pretraining, a unified action space, and embodiment-aware experts, enabling one policy to control all seven hands. Within this architecture, function-aligned action slots achieve 47.7% mean success, compared with 47.0% for native coordinates and 33.1% for DexLatent. These results show that cross-embodiment representation depends on the entire learning system: action coordinates, pretraining, and architecture must jointly separate shared manipulation structure from embodiment-specific control.

</details>


### [167] [HexVIO: Towards All-Day Stereo-Inertial Tracking Through Commodity DSPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03283)
*Patrick Wolf,Mateo de Mayo,Daniel Cremers*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HexVIO offloads the visual frontend of a stereo-inertial odometry system to the Hexagon DSP while keeping the backend on the CPU, achieving 67% lower power or 86% higher throughput on a commodity smartphone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Localization within surroundings is fundamental for spatial computing, and VIO offers a cost-effective and accurate solution. Efficient VIO implementations enable cooler, lighter, cheaper devices with longer battery life and better user experience for robots, wearables, XR devices, and drones.

Method: Leverages the Hexagon DSP, a commodity co-processor in modern smartphones and XR devices. The visual frontend of a stereo-inertial odometry system is offloaded to the DSP while the backend remains on the main CPU, with the implementation optimized for the DSP architecture.

Result: On a commodity smartphone, HexVIO achieves a 67% reduction in power consumption or an 86% increase in throughput, sustaining long-term real-time 30 fps tracking at 0.83 W, corresponding to ~18 hours of tracking on the test device.

Conclusion: Commodity DSPs show strong potential for enabling all-day visual-inertial tracking in robotics and mobile devices.

Abstract: The ability of a device to localize itself within its surroundings is a fundamental prerequisite for spatial computing. Visual-inertial odometry (VIO) has proven to be a cost-effective and accurate solution for this task. Robots, wearables, XR devices, and drones can benefit significantly from efficient implementations of VIO since they allow for cooler, lighter, and cheaper devices with longer battery life and a better user experience. In this work, we propose to enhance the efficiency of a VIO system by leveraging the Hexagon DSP, a commodity co-processor present in many modern smartphones and XR devices. Our approach offloads the visual frontend of a stereo-inertial odometry system to the DSP while keeping the backend on the main CPU. By optimizing the implementation for the DSP architecture, we achieve significant reductions in power consumption and latency compared to CPU-only execution. Our system, HexVIO, demonstrates a 67% reduction in power consumption or an 86% increase in throughput on a commodity smartphone, with the ability to sustain long-term real-time 30 fps tracking for 0.83 W, corresponding to ~18 hours of tracking on the testing device. These results highlight the potential of commodity DSPs for enabling all-day visual-inertial tracking in robotics and mobile devices.

</details>


### [168] [Equivariant Visual-Tactile Diffusion Policy for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03333)
*Lik Hang Kenny Wong,Yiyao Ma,Xiu-Shen Wei,Zelong Tan,Zhuheng Song,Dongsheng Xie,Kai Chen,Qi Dou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VISTA is a workspace-level equivariant visuotactile diffusion policy that improves data efficiency for contact-rich imitation learning by fusing visual and tactile observations into spherical tokens and applying end-effector rotation to an equivariant harmonic representation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contact-rich manipulation imitation learning depends on high-quality expert data that is costly to collect, making sample-efficient policy learning a critical problem.

Method: VISTA projects visual and tactile observations into spherical tokens, injects tactile contact cues into visual spherical directions via permutation-equivariant spherical fusion, and rotates the fused harmonic representation using the end-effector orientation. This representation conditions an equivariant diffusion policy to predict spatially consistent actions.

Result: Extensive simulation and real-world robotic experiments show VISTA substantially improves data efficiency over strong visuotactile imitation learning baselines.

Conclusion: VISTA demonstrates that workspace-level equivariance and visuotactile fusion can yield data-efficient contact-rich imitation learning policies.

Abstract: Imitation learning for contact-rich manipulation requires high-quality expert data that is expensive to obtain. This makes learning a sample-efficient policy a key issue. To address this, we propose VISTA, a workspace-level equivariant visuotactile diffusion policy for data-efficient contact-rich imitation learning. VISTA projects visual and tactile observations into spherical tokens, injects tactile contact cues into visual spherical directions through permutation-equivariant spherical fusion, and rotates the fused harmonic representation using the end-effector orientation. The resulting representation conditions an equivariant diffusion policy to predict spatially consistent actions. Extensive experiments in both simulation and real-world robotic settings show that VISTA substantially improves data efficiency over strong visuotactile imitation learning baselines. Project website: https://vista-paper.github.io/

</details>


### [169] [KungfuAthleteBot: learning high-dynamic humanoid motion from video with unified robust recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03388)
*Zhongxiang Lei,Lulu Cao,Xuyang Wang,Tianyi Qian,Jinyan Liu,Xuesong Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: KungfuAthleteBot (KAB) is a framework for learning high-dynamic humanoid skills from video by repairing three failure modes—physically inconsistent reconstructions, missing force/actuation information, and absent failure-recovery signals—using physics-guided trajectory correction, pseudo-low-kinetic-energy initialization, and a unified direct-training policy that also learns disturbance rejection and fall recovery (≈0.7 s, fastest reported).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video is an abundant, low-cost source of human motion rich in extreme athletic behaviors, but it cannot be used for humanoid robots by simply retargeting reconstructed trajectories. Video-derived motion has three core problems: (1) it is physically inconsistent, (2) it contains no actuation/force information, and (3) it provides no information about failure or recovery. The motivation is therefore to treat learning high-dynamic motion from video as the central problem and resolve each of these failure modes in turn.

Method: The framework, KungfuAthleteBot (KAB), addresses three issues, one per component. (C1) It builds the KungfuAthlete dataset from videos of national-level martial artists and applies a physics-guided parabolic trajectory correction that removes height floating, ground penetration, and high-frequency jitter from reconstructed aerial and landing phases. (C2) Since video carries no force information, strict trajectory tracking is dynamically infeasible; to avoid error-driven initialization from infeasible aerial poses, KAB introduces physics-driven pseudo-low-kinetic-energy (LKE) sampling that biases initialization toward dynamically feasible states so the policy can discover feasible actuation patterns rather than imitate infeasible ones. (C3) A direct training paradigm learns disturbance rejection and fall recovery within the same policy that tracks the video motion, requiring no recovery reference data and no manual mode switching.

Result: On a humanoid robot, KAB learns dynamic skills from video and recovers from arbitrary falls in about 0.7 s, reported as the fastest recovery for a unified policy. Ablations on the unified policy confirm the necessity of its components.

Conclusion: The results support the view that repairing and compensating video data, rather than only collecting more of it, is what unlocks high-dynamic humanoid skills.

Abstract: Video is an abundant, inexpensive source of human motion data that is rich in extreme athletic behaviors. Making it usable for humanoid robots, however, is not a matter of simply retargeting a reconstructed trajectory: video-derived motion is physically inconsistent, devoid of actuation information, and says nothing about failure or recovery. We present KungfuAthleteBot (KAB), a framework that treats learning high-dynamic motion from video as the central problem and resolves each of these three failure modes in turn. (C1) We build the KungfuAthlete dataset from videos of national-level martial artists and introduce a physics-guided parabolic trajectory correction that removes height floating, ground penetration, and high-frequency jitter from reconstructed aerial and landing phases. (C2) Because video carries no force information, strict tracking of a reconstructed trajectory is dynamically infeasible, and error-driven initialization keeps re-launching the policy from infeasible aerial poses. We introduce physics-driven pseudo-low-kinetic-energy (LKE) sampling, our central mechanism for making such references learnable: it biases initialization towards dynamically feasible states, letting the policy discover feasible actuation patterns instead of imitating infeasible ones. (C3) Finally, we introduce a direct training paradigm in which disturbance rejection and fall recovery are learned inside the same policy that tracks the video motion, requiring no recovery reference data and no manual mode switching. On a humanoid robot, KAB learns dynamic skills from video and recovers from arbitrary falls in about 0.7 s, the fastest reported recovery for a unified policy. Ablations on the unified policy confirm the necessity of its components, supporting the view that repairing and compensating video data, rather than only collecting more of it, is what unlocks high-dynamic humanoid skills.

</details>


### [170] [I2CD: Direct Image-to-Convex Decomposition for Simulation-Ready Collision Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03453)
*Qian Wang,Liam Merz Hoffmeister,Brian Scassellati,Daniel Rakita*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: I2CD predicts a compact convex decomposition directly from a single RGB image by training only a 38M-parameter cross-attention head on a frozen Hunyuan3D-2 backbone, producing physics-engine-ready convex polytopes in ~0.5s without post-processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Physics simulators and motion planners require convex collision geometry, but image-to-3D generative models output dense, frequently non-manifold visual meshes. Bridging them currently requires a slow, brittle reconstruct-then-decompose pipeline of repair, decimation, and approximate convex decomposition.

Method: I2CD freezes the pretrained Hunyuan3D-2 image-conditioned diffusion transformer and shape decoder and trains only a lightweight cross-attention head (38M parameters, under ten GPU-hours) whose learned "convex-slot" tokens emit the halfplane parameters of K convex polytopes.

Result: On 227 held-out OmniObject3D and Google Scanned Objects instances, I2CD attains the highest volumetric IoU among eight reconstruct-then-decompose pipelines while running 6-37x faster end-to-end. In a cross-simulator study across MuJoCo, PyBullet, Genesis, and Isaac Sim, every engine uses I2CD geometry as delivered, whereas raw generated meshes are silently replaced by different collision shapes or need seconds to minutes of preprocessing. On a physical xArm7, I2CD produces planner-ready geometry for a 20-object cluttered scene in 11s versus 328s for the strongest baseline (85 vs. 90 of 100 pick-and-place trials).

Conclusion: I2CD delivers compact, convex-by-construction collision geometry that loads into physics engines without post-processing at ~0.5s per image, enabling dramatically faster planner-ready scene generation at comparable manipulation success.

Abstract: Physics simulators and motion planners require convex collision geometry, yet image-to-3D generative models output dense, frequently non-manifold visual meshes. Bridging the two today takes a slow, brittle reconstruct-then-decompose pipeline of repair, decimation, and approximate convex decomposition. We present I2CD, which predicts a convex decomposition directly from a single RGB image. Rather than train a new image-to-3D model, I2CD freezes the pretrained Hunyuan3D-2 image-conditioned diffusion transformer and shape decoder and trains only a lightweight cross-attention head (38M parameters, under ten GPU-hours) whose learned "convex-slot" tokens emit the halfplane parameters of $K$ convex polytopes. The output is compact, convex by construction, and loads into physics engines without any post-processing, in ${\sim}0.5$s per image. On $227$ held-out OmniObject3D and Google Scanned Objects instances, I2CD attains the highest volumetric IoU among eight reconstruct-then-decompose pipelines while running $6$-$37\times$ faster end-to-end. In a cross-simulator study in MuJoCo, PyBullet, Genesis, and Isaac Sim, every engine uses I2CD geometry as delivered, whereas raw generated meshes "load" everywhere but are silently replaced by a different collision shape in most cases or need seconds to minutes of per-object preprocessing. On a physical xArm7, I2CD produces planner-ready geometry for a $20$-object cluttered scene in $11$s versus $328$s for the strongest baseline, at comparable pick-and-place execution success ($85$ vs. $90$ of $100$ trials).

</details>


### [171] [MobiAgent: Dual-Loop Recursive Policy Self-Improvement for Long-Horizon Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03476)
*Chenzhi Liu,Yue Zhang,Jiehong Lin,Jianan Wang,Bo Wang,Zhongrui Wang,Xiaojuan Qi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MobiAgent is a dual-loop agentic framework for long-horizon mobile manipulation that combines hierarchical planning with composable atomic skills during deployment (Inner Loop) and autonomous lifelong skill discovery via rollout recycling (Outer Loop).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon mobile manipulation suffers from compounding execution errors and capacity interference between locomotion and arm control. Existing VLA models lack the hierarchical reasoning needed for multi-stage objectives, and prior hierarchical agents rely on rigid sub-task mapping, inflexible replanning, and no continuous learning.

Method: MobiAgent uses a dual-loop design. The Inner Loop decouples high-level reasoning from low-level control via composable atomic skills: VLMs perform receding-horizon planning and visual reflection to dynamically compose skills for error recovery, executed by specialized flow-matching experts sharing a unified VLM backbone to maximize reusability and mitigate capacity interference. The Outer Loop enables automated lifelong learning by segmenting and verifying deployment rollouts, clustering them to discover atomic skills, and continuously fine-tuning the skill library without human annotation.

Result: Evaluated on RoboCasa, BEHAVIOR-1K, and real-world tasks. MobiAgent exceeds π_0.5-TA by 22.5 percentage points on BEHAVIOR-1K, enables robust recovery from execution failures, and via autonomous data recycling raises success from 7.50% to 27.50% on RoboCasa and 32.5% to 57.5% on Astribot S1.

Conclusion: MobiAgent effectively addresses long-horizon mobile manipulation by coupling flexible hierarchical deployment with recursive, annotation-free policy self-improvement, yielding strong gains in success rate and failure recovery across simulated and real-world benchmarks.

Abstract: Long-horizon mobile manipulation presents significant challenges due to compounding execution errors and capacity interference between locomotion and arm control. While recent Vision-Language-Action models excel at short-horizon tasks, they lack the hierarchical reasoning required for multi-stage objectives. Furthermore, existing hierarchical agents suffer from rigid sub-task mapping, inflexible replanning, and a lack of continuous learning. To address these limitations, we introduce MobiAgent, a dual-loop agentic framework that bridges robust deployment execution and recursive policy self-improvement. During deployment, the Inner Loop decouples high-level reasoning from low-level control through highly composable atomic skills. It employs Vision-Language models for receding-horizon planning and visual reflection, dynamically composing skills to ensure robust error recovery. These skills are executed by specialized flow-matching experts that share a unified VLM backbone, maximizing reusability while mitigating capacity interference. Concurrently, the Outer Loop drives automated lifelong learning by autonomously segmenting and verifying deployment rollouts, clustering them to discover atomic skills, and continuously fine-tuning the skill library without human annotations. Evaluations on RoboCasa, BEHAVIOR-1K, and real-world tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of MobiAgent. It outperforms $π_{0.5}$-TA by 22.5 percentage points on BEHAVIOR-1K and enables robust recovery from execution failures. Through autonomous data recycling, success improves from 7.50% to 27.50% on RoboCasa and from 32.5% to 57.5% on Astribot S1.

</details>


### [172] [Detect and Suppress: A Mechanistic Defense against Adversarial Patches in VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03498)
*Yukiya Horiba,Koshiro Aoki,Shunsuke Yasuki,Bum Jun Kim,Taiki Miyanishi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper mechanistically analyzes VLA models with a sparse autoencoder to find a feature correlated with adversarial patches, then suppresses it via a probe-triggered inference-time intervention that improves robustness under intermittent attacks without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adversarial patches disrupt VLA models and cause robot control failures, but the internal mechanisms behind these failures and how targeted interventions could mitigate them remain poorly understood.

Method: The authors use a sparse autoencoder (SAE) to mechanistically analyze VLA representations and identify a feature whose activation strongly correlates with adversarial patch presence. They then suppress this feature at inference time only when a linear probe detects an attack.

Result: Evaluated against VLA adversarial patch attacks on LIBERO-10, conditional intervention improves success rate under intermittent attacks, whereas continuously applying the same intervention substantially degrades policy performance.

Conclusion: Attack-related internal representations can serve as useful targets for VLA adversarial defense, and controlling when to intervene is important for limiting disruption to nominal policy behavior—achieving robustness without fine-tuning the VLA.

Abstract: Adversarial patches can disrupt Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models by manipulating visual observations, leading to failures in robot control. However, it remains poorly understood which internal mechanisms underlie these failures and how targeted interventions can mitigate them. In this work, we mechanistically analyze VLA representations using a sparse autoencoder (SAE) and identify a feature whose activation strongly correlates with the presence of an adversarial patch. Based on this analysis, we suppress the identified feature at inference time only when a linear probe detects an attack. This intervention improves robustness without the cost of fine-tuning the VLA. We evaluate our method against VLA adversarial patch attacks on LIBERO-10. Conditional intervention improves success rate under intermittent attacks, whereas continuously applying the same intervention substantially degrades policy performance. These results show that attack-related internal representations can provide useful targets for VLA adversarial defense and that controlling when to intervene is important for limiting disruption to nominal policy behavior.

</details>


### [173] [XGenAct: Geometry-Enhanced World Action Models through Cross-Task Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03516)
*Tingting Du,Ziyao Wang,Guoheng Sun,Ang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: XGenAct is a world action model that encodes RGB observations, robot actions, metric depth, surface normals, and functional role segmentation all as RGB videos via deterministic codecs, then trains a single video diffusion transformer with one objective over sampled perception and action tasks, achieving 52% success on five RLBench tasks versus 26% for the best baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world action models predict RGB and action futures but do not explicitly address the spatial understanding required for robot manipulation. Prior work adds only a limited set of spatial prediction tasks via specialized heads or branches, leaving both the spatial supervision range and the model architecture fragmented.

Method: The model represents RGB observations, robot actions, metric depth, surface normals, and functional role segmentation as RGB videos using deterministic codecs. During training it samples perception and action tasks, and uses a single video diffusion transformer with one unified objective to learn temporal prediction across these representation spaces, without modality-specific learned heads.

Result: On held-out RLBench tasks, structured perception training improves average closed-loop success over RGB-only training. XGenAct reaches 52% success on the five-task external comparison, versus 26% for the strongest evaluated baseline. It also predicts future depth and segmentation more accurately than evaluated pipelines that generate RGB first and then apply a frozen perception expert.

Conclusion: Unifying diverse spatial supervision and action prediction within a single video diffusion transformer and objective yields a more capable world action model for robot manipulation, improving closed-loop control and future spatial prediction over RGB-centric and two-stage alternatives.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) have advanced robot control by predicting how observations and actions evolve over time. Despite this progress, RGB and action based future prediction does not explicitly address the spatial understanding needed for robot manipulation. Existing efforts often add a limited set of spatial prediction tasks through specialized heads or branches, leaving both the range of spatial supervision and the model architecture fragmented. We introduce XGenAct, a world action model that represents RGB observations, robot actions, metric depth, surface normals, and functional role segmentation as RGB videos through deterministic codecs. By sampling perception and action tasks during training, XGenAct uses one video diffusion transformer and one objective to learn temporal prediction across these spaces without modality specific learned heads. On held out RLBench tasks, structured perception training improves average closed loop success over RGB only training, and XGenAct achieves 52% success in the five task external comparison, versus 26% for the strongest evaluated baselines. It also predicts future depth and segmentation more accurately than the evaluated pipelines that generate RGB first and then apply a frozen perception expert.

</details>


### [174] [DR-IPC: Disturbance-Resilient Integrated Planning and Control for LiDAR-Based Quadrotor Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03530)
*Peng Liu,Jingyan Wang,Qipeng Ye,Wen Li,Jinya Su,Zuo Wang,Shihua Li,Yunda Yan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DR-IPC integrates lightweight path guidance with NMPC to directly output angular velocity and thrust for disturbance-resilient quadrotor navigation, unifying motion generation and control onboard at 100 Hz.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LiDAR-based quadrotor navigation in cluttered environments is difficult under external disturbances, especially when obstacle-aware motion generation and disturbance-rejection control are designed as separate layers.

Method: Combines lightweight path guidance with nonlinear model predictive control (NMPC) to directly generate angular velocity and thrust. An interconnected extended Kalman filter and nonlinear disturbance observer provide filtered state estimates and reconstructed disturbances for NMPC prediction. The formulation unifies nonlinear quadrotor dynamics, actuator constraints, local motion generation, and penalised safe-flight-corridor residuals, eliminating a separate trajectory-optimization stage.

Result: Validated in Gazebo and MARSIM simulations plus indoor and outdoor experiments under wind, suspended payloads, narrow passages, ball impacts, and reactive dynamic-obstacle avoidance. In multi-goal navigation with disturbances, completed missions rose from 1/10 to 9/10 in Gazebo, and altitude RMSE dropped from 0.34 to 0.01 m in experiments. The full system runs onboard at 100 Hz.

Conclusion: DR-IPC effectively unifies planning and disturbance-rejection control, substantially improving navigation robustness and accuracy under disturbances while remaining computationally feasible for onboard real-time operation.

Abstract: LiDAR-based quadrotor navigation in cluttered environments remains challenging under external disturbances, particularly when obstacle-aware motion generation and disturbance-rejection control are handled in separate layers. This article presents disturbance-resilient integrated planning and control (DR-IPC), which combines lightweight path guidance with nonlinear model predictive control (NMPC) to directly generate angular velocity and thrust. An interconnected extended Kalman filter and nonlinear disturbance observer jointly provide filtered state estimates and reconstructed disturbances for NMPC prediction. The resulting formulation unifies nonlinear quadrotor dynamics, actuator constraints, local motion generation, and penalised safe-flight-corridor residuals without requiring a separate trajectory-optimization stage. Gazebo and MARSIM simulations, together with indoor and outdoor experiments, validate DR-IPC under wind, suspended payloads, narrow passages, ball impacts and reactive avoidance of a dynamic obstacle. In multi-goal navigation with disturbances, DR-IPC increases the number of completed missions from 1/10 to 9/10 in Gazebo and reduces the altitude RMSE from 0.34 to 0.01 m in experiments. The complete system operates onboard at 100 Hz. Supplementary videos are available on the project page https://drpp316.github.io/DR-IPC-Page/, and the source code will be released.

</details>


### [175] [Autonomous Robotic Navigation for Endovascular Brain-Computer Interface Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03537)
*Harry Robertshaw,Weijie Qi,Nikola Fischer,Alejandro Granados,Thomas C. Booth,Sam E. John*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: First demonstration of autonomous robotic navigation for endovascular BCI access in the cerebral venous system, using Soft Actor-Critic controllers trained in silico and validated in silico and in vitro, with online failure prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Endovascular BCIs avoid craniotomy but require precise device delivery through anatomically variable cerebral veins, motivating autonomous navigation with human oversight.

Method: Soft Actor-Critic controllers trained in silico for two sequential tasks from the right internal jugular vein to the superior sagittal sinus, using geometric augmentation of one training anatomy. Evaluated on a training anatomy and an anatomically unseen hold-out model across 250 in silico episodes and five fluoroscopy-guided in vitro robotic runs per task-anatomy condition (1,000 simulated episodes, 20 physical runs). Task recurrent predictors assessed for online detection of impending navigation failure.

Result: In silico success rates for Tasks A and B were 85.6% and 98.4% (training anatomy) and 42.0% and 91.6% (hold-out anatomy). 14/20 physical runs succeeded (70%), including 80% for Task B in the hold-out phantom. Predictors detected 99.3-100.0% of in silico failures with false-alarm rates of 0.8-6.7%. In vitro, predicted risk rose before failed episodes, but elevated probabilities during some successful runs indicated reduced calibration after transfer.

Conclusion: Autonomous cerebral venous access is feasible, and online failure prediction could support human oversight; anatomical generalization and sim-to-real calibration are priorities before preclinical translation.

Abstract: Endovascular brain-computer interfaces (BCIs) avoid craniotomy but require precise device delivery through anatomically variable cerebral veins. This work presents the first demonstration of in vitro autonomous robotic navigation for endovascular BCI access in the cerebral venous system. Soft Actor-Critic controllers were trained in silico for two sequential tasks spanning the right internal jugular vein to the superior sagittal sinus, using geometric augmentation of one training anatomy. Navigation was evaluated in a training anatomy and an anatomically unseen hold-out model over 250 in silico episodes and five fluoroscopy-guided in vitro robotic runs per task-anatomy condition, comprising 1,000 simulated episodes and 20 physical runs overall. Task recurrent predictors were also evaluated for online identification of impending navigation failure. In silico success rates for Tasks A and B were 85.6% and 98.4% in the training anatomy and 42.0% and 91.6% in the hold-out anatomy, respectively. Fourteen of 20 physical runs were successful (70% overall), including 80% success for Task B in the hold-out phantom. In silico the predictors detected 99.3-100.0% of failures with false-alarm rates of 0.8-6.7%. During in vitro evaluation, predicted risk increased before failed episodes, but elevated probabilities during some successful runs showed reduced calibration after transfer. These results demonstrate the feasibility of autonomous cerebral venous access and show how online failure prediction could support human oversight, while also identifying anatomical generalization and sim-to-real calibration as priorities before preclinical translation.

</details>


### [176] [RATE: Risk-Aware Tactile Encoding for Contact-rich Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03538)
*Yuyao Jiang,Haichao Liu,Jiarui Zheng,Zihan Ding,Weihao Yuan,Ziwei Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes Risk-Aware Tactile Encoding (RATE), a tactile representation learning method that encodes task-conditioned interaction risk to disambiguate similar tactile observations with different risk implications, improving robotic manipulation success in simulation and real-world settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tactile sensing is valuable for contact-rich robotic manipulation, and prior work has advanced tactile representation learning. However, similar tactile observations may stem from interaction conditions with very different task-risk implications, including sensor noise, task-necessary variations, and emerging undesirable contact. Without context-grounded risk information, these cases remain ambiguous to downstream policies, causing unnecessary corrections to benign variations or delayed responses to genuinely risky contact.

Method: The authors propose Risk-Aware Tactile Encoding (RATE), which learns tactile representations encoding task-conditioned interaction risk. It uses history-conditioned prediction to capture interaction context and alert supervision to associate this context with task-conditioned risk. The learned risk-aware representation is combined with conventional tactile features via a lightweight residual adapter.

Result: Experiments in both simulation and the real world show substantial improvements in task success. Controlled ablations confirm the complementary benefits of alert-guided learning and predictive temporal modeling.

Conclusion: RATE addresses the ambiguity of similar tactile observations with differing risk implications by learning risk-aware, task-conditioned tactile representations, and demonstrates effectiveness and complementarity of its components across simulation and real-world manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Tactile sensing is particularly valuable for contact-rich robotic manipulation. Recent work has made substantial progress in tactile representation learning for robotic manipulation. However, similar tactile observations can arise from interaction conditions with very different task-risk implications, such as sensor noise, task-necessary variations, and emerging undesirable contact. Without context-grounded risk information, these cases can be ambiguous to downstream policies, leading to unnecessary corrections to benign variations or delayed responses to genuinely risky contact. To address this limitation, we propose Risk-Aware Tactile Encoding (RATE), which learns tactile representations that encode task-conditioned interaction risk. Specifically, history-conditioned prediction captures interaction context, while alert supervision associates this context with task-conditioned risk. The learned risk-aware representation complements conventional tactile features through a lightweight residual adapter. Experiments in both simulation and the real world demonstrate substantial improvements in task success, with controlled ablations confirming the complementary benefits of alert-guided learning and predictive temporal modeling.

</details>


### [177] [A Unified Framework for Empowerment and Predictive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03563)
*Wooyoung Chung,Tristan Shah,Volodymyr Makarenko,Stas Tiomkin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper reformulates empowerment for sampling-based MPC by using a single policy for both probing and acting, yielding a first-order objective that standard MPC solvers can optimize, and shows that combining it with task costs matches or exceeds the success rate of either objective alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling-based MPC performance relies heavily on informative cost functions that demand substantial domain knowledge. Empowerment offers a goal-agnostic objective derived from system dynamics, but existing empowerment-based controllers have two limitations: (1) empowerment is estimated with a virtual probing policy distinct from the executed control policy, obscuring the link to resulting behavior; and (2) its computation typically requires second-order derivatives of the dynamics, limiting compatibility with standard MPC methods.

Method: The authors formulate empowerment using a single policy for both probing and acting, directly linking the empowerment objective to the executed behavior. This reformulation requires only first-order derivatives and can be optimized with standard MPC solvers, either alone or combined with a task-specific cost.

Result: Evaluation on classical control tasks shows that combining empowerment with task costs matches or exceeds the success rate achieved by either objective alone.

Conclusion: The proposed formulation provides a practical bridge between standard MPC and intrinsically motivated control.

Abstract: Sampling-based model predictive control (MPC) is a powerful approach to trajectory optimization, but its performance depends on an informative cost function that often requires substantial domain knowledge to design. An alternative is to derive objectives directly from the system dynamics. Empowerment, defined as the channel capacity between an agent's inputs and its future state, provides such a goal-agnostic objective and has been shown to produce useful behaviors across a range of domains. However, existing empowerment-based controllers have two key limitations: empowerment is estimated using a virtual probing policy distinct from the executed control policy, obscuring its connection to the resulting behavior; and its computation typically requires second-order derivatives of the dynamics, limiting compatibility with standard MPC methods. We formulate empowerment using a single policy for both probing and acting, directly linking the empowerment objective to the executed behavior. The resulting objective requires only first-order derivatives and can be optimized with standard MPC solvers, either alone or in combination with a task-specific cost. We evaluate the proposed approach on classical control tasks and show that combining empowerment with task costs matches or exceeds the success rate achieved by either objective alone. Our formulation provides a practical bridge between standard MPC and intrinsically motivated control.

</details>


### [178] [AVL-JEPA: Preventing Causal Dynamics Information Collapse In Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03587)
*Yikang Qiao,Ling Zhang,Ziying Song,Duan Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper identifies "causal dynamics information collapse" in JEPA world models—where high-dimensional visual info is preserved but action–consequence dynamics are discarded—and proposes Action-grounded Vision-invariance Latent (AVL) to prevent it, improving robotic control under visual perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: JEPAs predict future latent representations without reconstructing observations, letting world models focus on high-level semantic dynamics. However, a JEPA can retain high-dimensional visual information while discarding information about the physical consequences of actions—a failure mode the authors term causal dynamics information collapse. This undermines planning, which depends on causally relevant action dynamics.

Method: AVL uses two components: (1) the executed action as an auxiliary dynamics anchor that encourages the model to preserve dynamics information, and (2) a vision-invariance pathway that aligns perturbed and clean latent predictions without discarding dynamics information, forcing the model to fully understand and utilize causal dynamics information.

Result: Validated on four robotic control tasks (TwoRoom, PushT, OGBench Cube, Reacher), AVL substantially improves success rates under visual perturbations while preserving clean-environment performance. Additional evaluations cover physical consequence alignment, clean-noisy dynamics consistency, and the causal effect of targeted transition subspace erasure.

Conclusion: Results indicate that dynamics information causally relevant to planning is preserved from collapse under AVL.

Abstract: Joint embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) predict future latent representations without reconstructing observations, enabling world models to focus on high-level semantic dynamics. However, a JEPA can preserve high dimensional visual information while discarding information about the physical consequences of actions. We call this failure mode causal dynamics information collapse and propose action-grounded vision-invariance latent (AVL) to prevent this collapse. We first use the executed action as an auxiliary dynamics anchor that encourages the model to preserve dynamics information, and then use a vision-invariance pathway which aligns perturbed and clean latent predictions without discarding dynamics information, forcing the model to fully understand and utilize causal dynamics information. We validate AVL on four robotic control tasks (TwoRoom, PushT, OGBench Cube, and Reacher), showing that it substantially improves success rates under visual perturbations while preserving clean-environment performance. We further evaluate physical consequence alignment, clean-noisy dynamics consistency, and the causal effect of targeted transition subspace erasure. Collectively, these results indicate that dynamic information causally relevant to planning is preserved from collapse under AVL.

</details>


### [179] [World Action Learning via Interaction-Centric Spectral Latent Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03607)
*Zhiming Liu,Yikun Miao,Ying Chen,Hongrui Yin,Fangqi Zhu,Xiaoyi Pang,Quanxin Shou,Zhengyang Yan,Haodong Wang,Song Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WING transfers interaction knowledge from egocentric human videos to robot policies by isolating hand-object interaction from ego-motion and aligning slowly varying spectral components across embodiments, achieving 99.2% on LIBERO, 93.8% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 57.7% on RoboCasa-GR1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot policies require large-scale real-world interaction data, but robot demonstrations are expensive and hard to scale. Egocentric videos provide abundant human interaction experience with task-relevant semantics, yet direct transfer to robots is hindered by two issues: latent actions inferred from frame reconstruction are dominated by nuisance variation like ego-camera motion, and human versus robot behaviors exhibit differing temporal dynamics.

Method: WING (World Action Learning via INteraction-Centric Spectral Latent Guidance): (1) disentangles observer-induced motion from hand-object interaction, distilling only the interaction-centric component into latent actions; (2) leverages the observation that cross-embodiment task semantics reside in slowly varying temporal structure, identifying shared low-frequency components between egocentric latent actions and robot behaviors in the spectral domain and using them to guide action generation.

Result: Average success rates of 99.20% on LIBERO, 93.80% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 57.7% on RoboCasa-GR1, with strong performance on four real-world manipulation tasks across diverse generalization settings.

Conclusion: Interaction-centric spectral guidance offers an effective and scalable route for transferring physical interaction knowledge from human egocentric video to robot control.

Abstract: Learning general-purpose robot policies requires large-scale real-world interaction data, yet collecting robot demonstrations remains expensive and difficult to scale. Egocentric videos offer abundant human interaction experience with task-relevant semantics for robotic manipulation, but direct transfer is challenging for two reasons: latent actions inferred from frame reconstruction can be dominated by nuisance variation such as ego-camera motion, and human and robot behaviors often exhibit different temporal dynamics. We propose WING (World Action Learning via INteraction-Centric Spectral Latent Guidance), a framework for transferring interaction knowledge from egocentric videos to robot policies. WING first separates observer-induced motion from hand-object interaction and distills the interaction-centric component into latent actions. It then exploits the observation that cross-embodiment task semantics are concentrated in slowly varying temporal structures, identifying shared low-frequency components between egocentric latent actions and robot behaviors in the spectral domain and using them to guide action generation. WING achieves average success rates of 99.20% on LIBERO, 93.80% on RoboTwin 2.0, and 57.7% on RoboCasa-GR1, and also performs strongly across four real-world manipulation tasks under diverse generalization settings. These results show that interaction-centric spectral guidance provides an effective and scalable way to transfer physical interaction knowledge from human egocentric video to robot control. Project page: https://mikuz12.github.io/wing/

</details>


### [180] [Bridging Frontier Reasoning and Robot Execution: From Autonomous Demonstration Generation to Dense Language Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03615)
*Bosung Kim,Alexander Trevithick,Ruiyi Wang,Prithviraj Ammanabrolu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper bridges frontier-model reasoning with low-latency robot execution via (1) autonomous demonstration generation with corrective segments and (2) dense multi-granularity language supervision for a fast local policy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier models can enable robot manipulation from few demonstrations, but their high inference latency prevents real-time control. The work aims to retain frontier-level guidance while shifting execution to a low-latency local policy, addressing the reliability bottleneck that emerges once execution is offloaded.

Method: Two complementary approaches are studied.
