First, a frontier model autonomously generates demonstrations, augmented with corrective segments showing physical-error recovery, to supplement human data for training a fast local policy; at deployment a harness pairs frontier instructions with the local policy. Second, dense language supervision is introduced at three nested granularities (primitive, atomic, composite) with multi-aspect descriptions at each level to improve instruction following.

Result: Generation time and cost decrease as successful examples accumulate in context. On long-horizon RoboCasa 365 and BEHAVIOR-1K tasks with changing instructions, combined dense supervision achieves the highest performance under both oracle and frontier-model instructors. A crossword task combining semantic planning and manipulation under a fixed time budget evaluates both bridges jointly.

Conclusion: Autonomous demonstration generation and dense language supervision are complementary components for connecting frontier reasoning to low-latency local execution.

Abstract: Recent advances in frontier models enable robot manipulation from only a few demonstrations, but high inference latency limits their use for real-time robot control. To bridge this gap, we study two complementary approaches that connect frontier reasoning with low-latency local execution. First, we use a frontier model to autonomously generate demonstrations that supplement human demonstrations for training a fast local policy. We augment its in-context examples with corrective demonstration segments that show how to recover from physical errors, improving generation reliability. Generation time and cost decrease as successful examples accumulate in context, suggesting a path toward more efficient data collection. At deployment, a harness combines frontier-generated instructions with a fast local policy, enabling efficient execution while preserving the frontier model's ability to guide and correct actions. With low-latency execution delegated to the local policy, the bottleneck shifts to its capacity to reliably follow the frontier model's diverse instructions. Our second bridge introduces dense language supervision across three nested granularities---primitive, atomic, and composite---with multi-aspect descriptions at each level. Across long-horizon tasks in RoboCasa 365 and BEHAVIOR-1K, where instructions change as execution progresses, the combined supervision achieves the highest performance under both oracle and frontier-model instructors, demonstrating more reliable instruction following through the policy's language interface. Finally, we evaluate both bridges together on a crossword task that combines semantic planning and manipulation within a fixed time budget. These results support autonomous demonstration generation and dense language supervision as complementary components for connecting frontier reasoning to low-latency local execution.

</details>


### [181] [LLA-MPC on Embedded Hardware: Rapid Adaptive Control with Thousands of Parallel Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03616)
*Henry Z. Liao,Maitham F. AL-Sunni,John M. Dolan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The authors present a generalized, modular implementation of Look-Back and Look-Ahead Adaptive MPC (LLA-MPC) and validate it in real time on the F1TENTH platform under constrained computation and noisy state estimation, showing it outperforms a fixed nominal model on low-friction surfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original LLA-MPC formulation—a learning-free framework for real-time adaptive system identification and control—was only demonstrated in simulation for autonomous racing with a fixed model structure, leaving its practicality on real hardware unverified.

Method: The authors generalize LLA-MPC with modular dynamics and integrators, and deploy it on the F1TENTH platform. The system performs online tire-parameter identification by evaluating thousands of candidate models in real time on an embedded computer, operating under constrained computation and noisy state estimation.

Result: Experiments with low-friction tires across changing surfaces show that LLA-MPC completes high-speed tracking tasks where a fixed nominal model fails.

Conclusion: LLA-MPC is a practical, learning-free approach for real-time adaptive identification and control on resource-constrained hardware, demonstrated to generalize to real robotic platforms.

Abstract: We present a generalized implementation of Look-Back and Look-Ahead Adaptive Model Predictive Control (LLA-MPC), a learning-free framework for real-time, rapid adaptive system identification and control. The original formulation was demonstrated only in simulation, for autonomous racing with a fixed model structure. Our implementation has modular dynamics and integrators, and we validate it on the F1TENTH platform under constrained computation and noisy state estimation. The system identifies tire parameters online by evaluating thousands of candidate models in real time on an embedded computer. Experiments with low-friction tires across changing surfaces show that LLA-MPC completes high-speed tracking tasks where a fixed nominal model fails. Code, videos, and our related work are available at: https://lla-control.github.io.

</details>


### [182] [CORNAV: Construction-Aware Reasoning for Robot Navigation on Active Worksites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03622)
*Parastoo Ali Pour,Deepak Prakash Kumar,Tommy Zhou,Pramod Khargonekar,Mohammad Abdullah Al Faruque*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CORNAV is a blueprint-grounded, schedule-aware navigation framework for construction robots that uses 2D CAD drawings and project schedules (no BIM required) to ground object queries, enforce time-varying safety constraints, and plan safe paths, raising task success from 13.0% to 72.2% and eliminating hard-zone violations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The construction industry suffers from persistent labor shortages, low productivity costing the global economy over $1.6 trillion annually, and among the highest injury rates of major industries, motivating autonomous robots for efficiency and worker safety. However, existing language-grounded navigation systems rely solely on semantic scene understanding, lacking construction-specific context such as architectural plans, evolving work schedules, and safety constraints; they therefore localize permanent building features unreliably and cannot safely navigate active jobsites.

Method: CORNAV operates from 2D CAD drawings and project schedules without a Building Information Model. It (1) aligns architectural blueprints against hierarchical open-vocabulary 3D scene graphs to ground object queries, (2) converts project schedules into time-varying navigation constraints, (3) validates requests through an LLM-based safety module that escalates hazardous zones before planning, and (4) uses an A* planner that enforces mandatory exclusion zones while preferentially avoiding higher-risk areas.

Result: Across an indoor office and a real construction site, blueprint grounding raised task success from 13.0% to 72.2% over semantic retrieval alone, schedule awareness eliminated all hard-zone violations, and the safety module correctly rejected hazardous requests arising from mislabeled project schedules.

Conclusion: Grounding navigation in construction-specific context (blueprints and schedules) rather than semantic scene understanding alone substantially improves task success and safety, enabling reliable and safe autonomous navigation on active jobsites without requiring a BIM.

Abstract: The construction industry faces persistent labor shortages, low productivity that costs the global economy over $1.6 trillion annually, and one of the highest injury rates among major industries. These factors motivate the use of autonomous robots to improve efficiency and worker safety. Existing language-grounded navigation systems, however, rely on semantic scene understanding alone and lack access to construction-specific context such as architectural plans, evolving work schedules, and safety constraints. As a result, they localize permanent building features unreliably and cannot safely navigate active jobsites. We present CORNAV, a blueprint-grounded, schedule-aware navigation framework that operates from 2D CAD drawings and project schedules without requiring a Building Information Model. CORNAV aligns architectural blueprints against hierarchical open-vocabulary 3D scene graphs to ground object queries, converts project schedules into time-varying navigation constraints, and validates requests through an LLM-based safety module that escalates hazardous zones before planning. An A* planner then enforces mandatory exclusion zones while preferentially avoiding higher-risk areas. Across an indoor office and a real construction site, blueprint grounding raises task success from 13.0% to 72.2% over semantic retrieval alone, schedule awareness eliminates all hard-zone violations, and the safety module correctly rejects hazardous requests arising from mislabeled project schedules.

</details>


### [183] [EyeRobot 2.0: Active Gaze for Precise Manipulation without Wrist Cameras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03710)
*Kush Hari,Justin Kerr,Nidhya Shivakumar,Samarth Mahapatra,Carmelo Sferrazza,Jiahui Lei,Jitendra Malik,C. Karen Liu,Ken Goldberg,Angjoo Kanazawa*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EyeRobot 2.0 enables fine-grained bimanual manipulation with only a single stereo camera by actively swiveling its viewpoints to fixate on 3D points, then processing images foveally, achieving performance comparable to or better than setups with wrist cameras.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wrist cameras are commonly used for fine-grained bimanual manipulation, but removing them degrades standard policies severely (real-world success drops from 52% to 27% with only passive stereo). The authors aim to recover this lost capability using a single stereo camera by mimicking human active gaze, avoiding reliance on wrist cameras.

Method: The framework uses Active Visual Fixation (AVF): two eye viewpoints swivel to center gaze on a 3D fixation point, and images are processed foveally with more visual tokens at the center. Gaze is coordinated hierarchically—first a low-level gaze servoing policy trained with a dense geometric reward, then a target selector emitting fixation goals, co-trained with a BC gripper policy. Gripper information is canonicalized into a fixation-relative SE(3) frame to compact the action distribution.

Result: Across 7 real and 6 simulated tasks with over 1000 physical and 1800 simulated trials, EyeRobot 2.0 outperforms passive stereo by 40% (real) and 20% (sim). It matches ego+wrist policies when wrist views are clear (69% vs. 64%) and more than doubles their success when objects occlude wrist cameras (48% vs. 22%).

Conclusion: Active gaze with a single stereo camera can substitute for wrist cameras in fine-grained bimanual manipulation, and even surpasses ego+wrist setups under occlusion, demonstrating the value of active fixation and fixation-relative action representation.

Abstract: Inspired by human vision, we introduce a framework using active gaze to enable fine-grained bimanual manipulation with only a single stereo camera. EyeRobot 2.0 physically attends to a 3D fixation point in the scene by swiveling two eye viewpoints to center their gaze on it. The resulting images are processed foveally by allocating more visual tokens to the image centers, focusing computation on task-relevant features. Such Active Visual Fixation (AVF) requires carefully coordinated gaze during task execution, which we accomplish hierarchically by first training a low-level gaze servoing policy conditioned on a goal object, then training a target selector which emits fixation goals based on task progress. Both modules are trained with RL on real-world data: the first is trained with a dense geometric reward and the second co-trains with the BC gripper policy which allows it to discover fixation sequences that can resemble a human's fixation sequence while performing the task. EyeRobot 2.0 further takes advantage of fixation by canonicalizing gripper information into a fixation-relative SE(3) frame, which compacts the size of the action distribution to learn. We collect teleoperation data for 7 real-world and 6 simulated tasks, and conduct over 1000 physical and 1800 simulated robot trials comparing EyeRobot 2.0 against passive stereo and ego + wrist camera policies trained on the same data. Removing wrist cameras is costly for standard policies: with only passive stereo, real-world success drops from 52% to 27%. EyeRobot 2.0 closes this gap with only stereo, outperforming passive stereo by 40% in real and 20% in sim. It matches ego + wrist policies when their wrist views are clear (69% vs. 64%), and more than doubles their success when grasped objects occlude the wrist cameras (48% vs. 22%)

</details>
